<meta>This page describes the physical exterior of the manuscript, focusing on its binding and decorative features. There is no continuous text from a previous page as this represents the front cover.</meta>

# Front Cover of the Manuscript

The front cover is crafted from brown mottled leather, showing a distinct marbled pattern. It features **blind-tooled** <note>A decorative technique where patterns are impressed into the leather using heated tools without the application of gold leaf or ink.</note> geometric decorations. The design consists of a large central diamond (rhombus) shape defined by triple-ruled lines. 

This diamond is inscribed within a rectangular frame, also marked by triple-ruled lines. Additional diagonal lines extend from the corners of the rectangle to the points of the central diamond, creating a series of triangular compartments across the surface. 

The **spine** <note>the back edge of the book where the pages are gathered and joined</note> features five **raised bands** <note>horizontal ridges formed by the cords used to sew the sections of the book together</note>. In the second compartment from the top of the spine, there is a small red leather title label, which still retains partial gilded lettering.

<unclear>You</unclear> <note>original: "TE"</note>
<unclear>MAE</unclear> <note>original: "MAE"; likely a fragment of a name or a word such as "maestas" (majesty)</note>
</margin>

<meta>This is the title page of the work following the initial page markers. The text introduces Jan Hus's famous treatise on the Church.</meta>

# AN EXCELLENT BOOK
### BY HUS ON THE UNITY OF THE CHURCH,
->Whose author perished at the Council<-
->of Constance.<-
<gloss>Hus.</gloss> <note>The author is Jan Hus (c. 1370–1415), a Czech reformer whose trial and execution at the Council of Constance became a pivotal moment in the lead-up to the Protestant Reformation.</note>

---

->**I pray you, my fair reader, pay attention not to who**<-
->**is speaking, but to what is being said.**<-
<note>original: "Tu, quæſo, candide mi Lector, non quis, ſed quid dicatur, attende." This is a common scholarly plea for objectivity, asking the reader to judge the arguments on their own merit rather than the author’s controversial reputation.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for Jan Hus's influential treatise "On the Unity of the Church," featuring a subtitle that references his execution at the Council of Constance and an appeal for objective reading.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Hus, De Unitate Ecclesiae, Council of Constance, church reform, martyrdom, ecclesiology</keywords>

<column-break/>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

->The Catholic Church is one, consisting of things in heaven and things on earth, obtaining diverse names from Holy Scripture according to its dignity.<-

### CHAPTER I

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring a winged putto or child figure amidst foliage.</image-desc>SINCE EVERY pilgrim <note>original: "viator." In medieval theology, a "wayfarer" or "pilgrim" is a person currently living on Earth, traveling toward their eternal destination.</note> ought to faithfully believe in the holy Catholic Church, just as he ought to love the Lord Jesus Christ, the groom of that church, and the Church itself as his bride. But he does not love his spiritual mother unless he has at least known her through faith; therefore, he ought to know her through faith and thus honor her as his principal mother. For some knowledge of her, it must be noted that "Church" first signifies the house of God, made for this purpose, that in it the people may worship their God, as in 1 Corinthians 11: "Do you not have houses for eating and drinking?" etc. Or, "Do you despise the Church of God?" (according to Augustine, that is, the house of prayer).

<margin>The Church is understood in many ways.</margin>
<margin>Augustine.</margin>

Secondly, "Church" signifies the ministers belonging to that house, and in this way the clergy belonging to one material church call themselves "the Church." But because "Church" according to the Greeks is called a "congregation" contained under one governance, as Aristotle teaches in the second book of the *Politics*, chapter 7, where he says: "But the <term>assembly <note>original Greek: ἐκκλησία (ekklesia)</note></term> is shared by all." Therefore, according to this meaning, the congregation of all people is called the "Church." According to that passage in Matthew 25: "When the Son of Man comes in his majesty, and all his angels with him, then he will sit upon the seat of his majesty, and all nations will be gathered before him." Behold, a great... <note>The text continues on the next page with "magna" (great).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the alphabetical index of Jan Hus's treatise and begins Chapter I, where he defines the "Church" in three ways: as a physical building, as the clergy, and as the universal assembly of all people.</summary>
<keywords>Church, Jan Hus, definitions of the Church, Aristotle, Augustine, Vicar of Christ, assembly, ecclesia, wayfarer, Catholic Church</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>The previous page established the topical index and the beginning of Chapter I, where Hus defines the word "Church" (Ecclesia) as a "calling out" or assembly. This page continues that definition, specifically distinguishing between the "Church of the righteous" and the "Church of the wicked," while introducing Hus's central theological concept of predestination.</meta>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...the great assembly of all men under the rule of Christ the King. And because not all of that assembly is the holy Church, he therefore adds: *And he will separate them from one another, as a shepherd separates the sheep from the goats.* <note>original: "Et separabit eos ab inuicem, sicut pastor segregat oues ab hædis." This refers to the Last Judgment in Matthew 25.</note>

<margin>The church of sheep and the church of goats.</margin>
From this it is clear that one is the Church of Sheep, and the other is the Church of Goats; one is the church of the saints, and the other the church of the reprobate <note>The "reprobate" are those whom Hus believes are destined for rejection by God, the opposite of the "predestined."</note>. Likewise, the Church of the righteous is one thing when it is Catholic—that is, universal—which is not merely a part of another, and about which we shall now treat. It is another thing when it is particular, which is a part of the other, according to that saying of the Savior in Matthew 18: *Where two or three are gathered in my name, there am I in the midst of them.* From this it is clear that two righteous people gathered in the name of Christ, with Christ as the head, are a particular holy Church; similarly three, four, and so on up to the inclusive number of all the predestined. And in this way "the Church" is often understood in Scripture, as the Apostle says in 1 Corinthians 1: *To the Church which is at Corinth, sanctified in Christ Jesus.* Likewise in Acts 20: *Watch over yourselves and the whole flock, in which the Holy Spirit has placed you as Bishops, to rule the Church of God, which he acquired with his own blood.* And in this way, the righteous now living under the rule of Christ in the Archdeaconry of Prague—and especially the predestined—are the holy Church of Prague. The same reasoning applies to other particular churches of the saints, of which it is said in Ecclesiasticus 23: *In the assemblies of the Most High she opened her mouth.* And in the 30th chapter of the same: *The whole church of the saints will tell of his alms.*

<margin>The universal Church.</margin>
The holy Catholic Church, however—that is, the universal Church—is the entirety of all the predestined, which includes all the predestined of the present, the past, and the future. This opinion is clear through Augustine's writing on John, and in his opinion found in the Decree <note>Hus refers to Gratian's "Decretum," the standard collection of canon law, specifically Case 32, Question 4, Chapter "Recurrat."</note>, it is held how the same Church of the predestined ran from the beginning of the world until the Apostles, and from then until the Day of Judgment. For he says: The Church that birthed Abel, Enoch, Noah, and Abraham, she also birthed Moses and the prophets

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER I

later in time, before the coming of the Lord; and the same Church birthed those [prophets], and the apostles, and our martyrs, and all good Christians. For she birthed all who appeared as born at different times, but they are held together by the society of one people. And the citizens of the same city have experienced the labors of this pilgrimage; some experience them now, and others will experience them until the end. Behold how clearly this saint shows the holy Catholic Church; and correspondingly, he speaks there immediately of the "Church of the Wicked" <note>original: "Ecclesia malignantium."</note>. This church, he says, birthed Cain, Ham, Ishmael, and Esau; the same birthed Dathan and others like them among the same people; and the same church birthed Judas, the false apostles, Simon Magus, and all other "Pseudo-Christians" up to these times. Likewise, those stubbornly hardened by animal-like affection, whether they are close in unity or dissent by open separation. This is from Augustine. From his opinion, it is clear that there is only one holy universal Church, which is the entirety of the predestined from the first righteous person inclusive to the last person to be saved in the future inclusive. It encloses all who are to be saved, who fulfill the number; concerning the completion of this number, the saints slain under the altar received a divine response: that they should wait yet a little while until the number of their fellow servants and brothers was fulfilled (Revelation 6). For God is all-knowing, who has placed all things in measure, weight, and number, and has pre-defined exactly how many people are finally to be saved.

<margin>The Catholic Church is the Bride, the Dove, the Strong Woman, and the Queen.</margin>
That universal Church, therefore, is the Bride of Christ, of whom the Song of Songs speaks; and of whom Isaiah 61 says: *As a bridegroom decked with a crown, and as a bride adorned with her jewels.* She is the "only dove" of whom Christ says in Song of Songs 6: *My dove, my perfect one, is only one.* This is the "strong woman" whose household is clothed in double garments (Proverbs 31). This is the "queen" of whom the Psalm says: *The queen stood at your right hand in golden clothing.* This is Jerusalem our mother, the temple of the Lord, the kingdom of heaven, and the city of the Great King; all of which, Augustine says in his *Enchiridion* [chapter] 41, is to be understood not...

<summary>Hus defines the "Holy Catholic Church" as the total body of those predestined by God for salvation, spanning from the beginning of the world to the end of time. He distinguishes this spiritual body from both local "particular" churches and the "Church of the Wicked" (the reprobate).</summary>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

<margin>The Church is partly in heaven and partly on earth.</margin>
The Church is not only that part which journeys as a pilgrim <note>Hus uses "pilgrim" to describe the Church on earth, often called the "Church Militant."</note> from the rising of the sun to its setting, praising the name of the Lord <note>original: "a solis ortu usq; ad occasum laudans nomẽ domini"</note>, and singing a new song after the captivity of its old nature; it is also that part which has always remained in the heavens since it was created, joined to God, and has never experienced any evil from a fall. This part persists in blessedness among the holy angels and assists its pilgrim part as is fitting, because they shall be one in the fellowship of eternity, and even now are one in the bond of charity. The whole Church was instituted for the worship of God; therefore, neither the whole nor any part of it wishes to be worshiped in the place of God. So says Augustine.

<margin>Why the Church is placed in the Creed after the confession of the Trinity.</margin>
This is the holy Catholic Church which Christians confess immediately after their faith in the Holy Spirit. First, because according to Augustine (as cited above), the Church is the highest creature; therefore, it is placed immediately after the uncreated Trinity. Second, because by the love of the Holy Spirit, the Church is joined to Christ in a perpetual marriage. And third, because once the Trinity is established, it is fitting that the Trinity have a temple in which to dwell. Whence Augustine infers, saying: "God therefore dwells in his temple—not only the Holy Spirit, but also the Father and the Son." This also applies to his body, through which he was made the head of the Church of God, which exists among men, so that he may hold the primacy in all things. He himself said, "Destroy this temple, and in three days I will rebuild it" <note>original: "Soluite templũ hoc, & in triduo reædificabo illud." See John 2:19.</note>. Thus says Augustine. From his words it is gathered: first, that there is one universal Church praising God from the beginning of the world until the end. Second, that the holy angels are part of the holy Catholic Church. Third, that the pilgrim or "militant" part of that Church is helped by the "triumphant" Church <note>The Church Triumphant refers to those already in heaven.</note>. Fourth, that the militant and triumphant parts are united by the bond of charity. Fifth, that the whole Church and every part of it ought to worship God, and neither the Church itself nor any part of it wishes to be worshiped as God. From this it follows consequently that the faithful ought not to believe *in* the Church, since it is not God but the house of God, as Augustine says in his exposition of the Creed <note>original: "symboli." This refers to the Apostles' Creed.</note>. Rather, they ought to believe that the holy Catholic Church is the bride of the Lord Jesus Christ—a chaste bride, I say,

<margin>Augustine</margin>
<margin>Ambrose on the Epistles</margin>
<margin>The whole Church wants God to be worshiped</margin>
<margin>The Church is a bride, not an adulteress.</margin>

incorrupt, and incapable of finally committing adultery. For the blessed Cyprian, bishop and glorious martyr, says: "The Church is one, which is extended far and wide into a multitude by the increase of its fruitfulness." And he adds: "Yet there is one head, and one origin, and one mother rich in fruitfulness. The bride of Christ cannot be defiled; she is incorrupt and chaste. She knows but one Lord; she guards the sanctity of one bedchamber with chaste modesty." Thus says he. That holy Church is therefore the vineyard of the master of the house, about which Gregory says in a Homily: "Our Creator has a vineyard, namely the universal Church, which has produced as many branches as it has brought forth saints, from Abel the just even to the last of the elect who shall be born at the end of the world." Regarding this, Saint Remigius <note>Remigius of Auxerre (c. 841–908), a Benedictine monk and scholar.</note> also says in a Lenten Homily on that word, "The men of Nineveh will rise in judgment with this generation and condemn it" <note>original: "Viri Niniuitæ surgent in iudicio cũ generatione ista, & cõdemnabunt eã." See Matthew 12:41.</note>: "The holy Church," he says, "is gathered from two parts: one of those who have not sinned, and the other of those who have ceased to sin." Thus says he. Regarding this Church, Isidore also says in *The Supreme Good*, chapter 14: "The holy Church is called Catholic for this reason: that it is universally diffused throughout the whole world." Regarding this Church, therefore, Augustine and Ambrose say in their song of praise to God <note>This refers to the *Te Deum* hymn.</note>: "The holy Church throughout the whole world confesses thee" <note>original: "Te per orbem terrarũ sancta confitetur Ecclesia."</note>. Regarding this Church, Ambrose speaks for himself: "What house is more worthy of the entrance of Apostolic preaching than the holy Church? Or who is to be preferred above all others more than Christ? He who was accustomed to wash the feet of his guests, and whoever he receives into his house, he does not allow them to dwell there with polluted tracks—that is, with evil works." Thus says Ambrose. Regarding this Church, Pope Pelagius cites Augustine, saying: "There cannot be two Churches," and later saying: "It is certain that there is one Church which is the body of Christ, as has often been said, which cannot be divided into two or more." Regarding this Church, Jerome says in the decree on penance: "The Church of Christ is not...

<summary>Hus defines the Holy Catholic Church as a single, incorruptible body consisting of both angels in heaven and the faithful on earth from all of history. He distinguishes between the Church and God, arguing that while the Church is the "highest creature" and the "Bride of Christ," it is a house of God to be believed in as an article of faith, not worshiped as a deity.</summary>

<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>A sinner is not of the Church of Christ.</margin> [The Church] has neither spot, nor wrinkle, nor anything of this sort; therefore, whoever is a sinner, or is stained by any filth, cannot be called part of the Church of Christ.

<margin>The Church is the body of Christ.</margin> Therefore, the universal holy Church itself is the mystical body of Christ, as the Apostle <note>Commonly referring to St. Paul.</note> says in Ephesians 1: *He gave him to be the head over all the Church, which is his body.* And in Colossians 1: *He is the head of the body, the Church.* And again: *For his body's sake, which is the Church.* And in Ephesians 5: *Christ is the head of the church, and he is the savior of his body.* And below: *Christ loved the church and gave himself for it, that he might sanctify it, cleansing it with the washing of water in the word of life, that he might present it to himself a glorious church, not having spot or wrinkle, or any such thing, but that it should be holy and without blemish.* The holy Doctors <note>The "Doctors of the Church" are authoritative theologians like Augustine and Gregory.</note> lean upon this text. <margin>Augustine</margin> Augustine, in *On Christian Doctrine*: "Christ is the head of the church, which is his body, destined to be with him in the kingdom and eternal glory." <margin>Gregory</margin> Gregory in the *Moralia*, book 35, chapter 9: "Because Christ and the Church are the same head and body, they are one person." And in the 15th homily on Ezekiel: <margin>Bernard</margin> "The Church is one substance with Christ its head." And Bernard, in his 12th sermon on the *Canticles* <note>The Song of Solomon.</note>, says: "The Church is the beloved body of Christ, <margin>Paschasius</margin> that which he delivered up to death." And Paschasius, in his book *On the Sacrament of the Body of Christ*, says as it is found in the holy scriptures: "The Church of Christ, or the bride of God, is called the body of Christ, certainly because the general Church of Christ is his body, and Christ is the head, and all the chosen ones <note>original: "electi"</note> are called members. From these, one body of the Church is gathered into a perfect man, and the measure of the fullness of Christ. Moreover, the body of Christ—namely, the bride of God—is rightly called the Church, according to what the Apostle says: *And they shall be two in one flesh.* This, he says, *is a great mystery in Christ and the church.* For if Christ and the Church are one flesh, they are surely one body, one head, one bridegroom, while the individual chosen ones are members one of another." Thus says Paschasius. From these sayings of the saints, it is clearly drawn out that the holy universal Church is the number of all the predestined <note>term: <term>predestined</term> (Latin: *praedestinatorum*). In Hus’s theology, this refers to those God has chosen for salvation from eternity; only these are the "true" Church.</note>, and the mystical body of Christ (of which he himself

<meta>catchword: is the</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II</header>

is the head) and the bride of Christ, whom he redeemed with his blood out of the greatest love, so that he might finally possess her in glory, not having the wrinkle of mortal sin, nor the spot of venial sin, nor anything else making her base, but that she might be holy and immaculate, eternally embracing Christ her bridegroom.

### ->The one universal Church is divided into three particular parts. CHAPTER II<-

<margin>The Church Militant.</margin> Having said what the universal holy Church is, and that it is only one—just as there is only one number of all the predestined—and that it is spread throughout the world according to its members: it should be known that the holy Universal Church is divided into three parts, namely the Church Triumphant, Militant, and Sleeping.

The Church Militant is the number of the predestined while they are on their journey here toward the fatherland <note>original: "vians ad patriam"; a common medieval metaphor for life on earth as a pilgrimage toward heaven.</note>. And it is called "militant" because it carries out the soldierly service <note>original: "militiam"; the root of the English "militant."</note> of Christ against the flesh, the world, and the devil.

<margin>The Church Sleeping.</margin> The Church Sleeping is the number of the predestined suffering in purgatory. It is called "sleeping" because, while existing there, it no longer earns merit for blessedness, because by God’s preceding and helping grace it earned merit in the present life so that it might be rewarded in the fatherland after the satisfaction of purgatory. <margin><insert>The threefold Church</insert></margin>

<margin>The Church Triumphant.</margin> The Church Triumphant consists of the blessed resting in the fatherland, who, having held fast to the soldierly service of Christ against Satan, have finally triumphed. However, there will be one great Church made of all these on the day of judgment. As a sign of this threefold part of the universal church, the Doctors say the sacrament of the Eucharist is divided into <margin>Why the eucharist is divided into three parts.</margin> three parts. They say the first part, dipped in the liquid sacrament, signifies the Church Triumphant, which is absorbed and intoxicated by the steeping of the divine nature—as the head of the Church speaks in Canticles 5, gladdening his citizens and table-guests: *Be intoxicated, my dearly beloved.* The other two parts in the hand [of the priest]... <note>In the medieval Mass, the priest broke the Host into three parts, a practice Hus uses here as a symbolic map of the Church's structure.</note>

...of the Lord, and it is rightly signified that the Church must be purged. Through those two parts [of the consecrated bread] which the priest holds in his hands, the larger part placed underneath signifies the **Church militant** <note>The "Church Militant" refers to the Christians currently living on earth, who are "at war" against sin and the devil.</note>, and the smaller part resting upon the one underneath signifies the **Church waiting in purgatory**. For that very part leans upon the prayers of the Church militant. And for these two parts, we double our prayers to the Lamb, who is the head of the Church, that he may have mercy on us. But for the third part, toward whose place and rest we aspire <note>This refers to the "Church Triumphant" in Heaven.</note>, we ask that the same Lamb of a triple nature <note>Likely referring to Christ's divinity, soul, and body.</note> finally grant us peace. And for this reason, Christ, according to his humility, visited three places of the Church: namely, the **navel of our inhabited world** <note>A traditional medieval description of Jerusalem/Judea as the center of the world.</note>, dwelling for thirty-three years in Judea and Jerusalem; then **Limbo**, in which the fathers were purged, by extracting a small portion of his church in his soul; and finally, ascending into heaven for the third time, he led captivity captive, which he crowned after his triumph by placing it at the right hand of God. This, therefore, is the three-fold part of the one universal or Catholic Church, even though there are particular Churches everywhere.

<margin>Why the Lamb of God is sung in three ways.</margin>

<margin>The Church as a virgin.</margin>
Furthermore, this universal Church is a **virgin**, the bride of Christ the Virgin, from whom we are spiritually born as from a true mother. I say a virgin, "all fair, in whom there is no spot" (Song of Songs 4). "Not having wrinkle or spot" (Ephesians 5), both holy and immaculate, and thus most chaste according to her whole self in the fatherland <note>The "fatherland" refers to the afterlife or the Kingdom of Heaven.</note>. Yet, this Church is partially corrupted by committing fornication with the adulterous devil and with his many members through her crimes. Nevertheless, she is never received as a bride to be embraced, blessedly at the right hand in the bed of the bridegroom, until she has become a pure virgin, entirely without wrinkle. For Christ is the bridegroom of virginity; since he lives forever, it is not permitted for the bride to depart from him by spiritual fornication. Therefore, it is said of the multitude of heavenly citizens in Revelation 14: "For they are virgins, and they follow the Lamb wherever he goes." In the beginning of the world, Christ was the bridegroom of the predestined Church, and in the confirmation of the angels, he gave a dowry to a part of the bride.

bride. And thus, in the confirmation of the righteous Abel and other saints up until the Incarnation, the same betrothal continued. But in the **Incarnation**, he made a **second wedding**, creating a certain queenly part of the whole Church which is properly called the **Christian Church**. For then our leader and familiar lawgiver, addressing his bride (as the Apostle says in Hebrews 1), puts on our armor through the assumption of humanity; like a giant, he overcomes the enemies of the church and teaches how the part of the Church ought to follow him like a jealous bride. Thus, the entire Christian doctrine stands upon that prayer of the Church in which we ask the bridegroom, through his coming in the flesh, to teach us to **despise earthly things and love heavenly ones**. To "despise" means to set them aside in our affection, and to love Christ the bridegroom above all things.

<margin>The Incarnation of Christ as the second wedding.</margin>
<margin><unclear>The whole Christian doctrine is in the prayer...</unclear></margin>

From these things, it is clear that the holy universal Church is the only bride of Christ, a virgin who is ultimately most chaste, whom the Son of God joined to himself in marriage out of eternal love and the grace of adoption. We firmly believe in her, saying in the Creed: "I believe in **one, holy, Catholic Church**," and to which is added in the second creed <note>The Nicene Creed.</note>, "**and Apostolic**." It is called Apostolic because the apostles are full parts, purged in the Spirit, of that same mother Church which they themselves planted by the doctrine of Christ and their own blood, and by whose doctrine and authority their Vicars <note>Those acting in the place of the apostles, such as bishops and the Pope.</note> now rule the young girl who is currently seeking the bridegroom of the Church. And thus the Decree (23, question 1) speaks: it advises, says Pope Leo, to seek a privilege wherever judgment is brought from the fairness of the law itself. For he himself resides in heaven, seeing and providing that God binds and looses. Wherefore Boniface VIII, in the decree *Unam Sanctam*, says: "By the urgency of faith, we are compelled to believe and hold to **one, holy, Catholic, and also Apostolic Church**."

It emphasizes the Church's identity as the "One, Holy, Catholic, and Apostolic" bride of Christ, unified by God's eternal predestination.</summary>

<meta>The previous page established that the Church is the mystical body of Christ, a perfect unity of the predestined that is without spot or wrinkle, contrasted with the "Church of Goats" or sinners who are not truly part of this holy body. This page continues to explore the nature of that unity and addresses the common objection that the Church on earth seems to contain both saints and sinners.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: all</meta>

...finally united in bliss. Even in the present, its unity consists in the unity of faith and virtues, and in the unity of charity, as **Augustine** <note>original: "Augu."</note> explains in his commentary on the Gospel of **John** regarding those words in John 17: *That they all may be one.* And in his letter to Dardanus, explaining the verse, *It is expedient that one man should die for the people*, he says that **Caiaphas** <note>Caiaphas was the High Priest who presided over the trial of Jesus; the author notes that Caiaphas unintentionally prophesied God's plan.</note> prophesied that God would gather his children into one. He did not mean, Augustine says, into any one physical place, but that he gathers them into one spirit and one body, of which the one head is Christ. The Apostle touches upon this unity, saying in **Ephesians 4**: *Careful to keep the unity of the spirit in the bond of peace; one body, one spirit, one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one God and Father of all.* There is no doubt that without this union, as stated before, there is no salvation.

### Objection: that every Christian, even the foreknown <note>term: "foreknown" (original: "praescitus") refers to those whom God knows from eternity will eventually be damned, as opposed to the "predestined" who are chosen for salvation.</note> or those finally to be damned, is a part of the Church. An elegant solution to this objection through notable points. CHAPTER III.

<margin>
Extra <note>This likely refers to the "Extravagantes," a collection of Papal decrees in Canon Law.</note>
Dragnet
Matthew 13
Gregory
Matthew 22
</margin>
But an objection is raised against what has just been said. First, if that opinion holds, no "foreknown" person would be part of the holy mother universal Church. This consequence seems false, since every Christian is a part of that Church, according to **Matthew 13**: *The kingdom of heaven is like a dragnet <note>original: "sagena"; a large fishing net.</note> cast into the sea, which gathered every kind of fish.* Regarding this, Saint **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), a primary authority for medieval theologians.</note> says in his Homilies: "The Holy Church is compared to a dragnet cast into the sea because it is committed to fishers, and through it, everyone is drawn from the waves of the present age to the eternal kingdom, lest they be drowned in the depths of eternal death."

This is confirmed, secondly, by **Matthew 22**: *The kingdom of heaven is like a king who made a marriage feast for his son, and sent his servants to call those invited to the wedding.* Those who went out gathered all

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER III</header>
<sig>C ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: his</meta>

all whom they found, both good and bad, and the wedding was filled with guests. As **Gregory** says in his homily: "Behold, by the very quality of the guests it is clearly shown that the present Church is designated by this king’s wedding, in which the evil meet with the good, indeed a mixed diversity of children."

<margin>Matthew 13</margin>
Thirdly, this is confirmed by **Matthew 13**: *The Son of Man shall send his angels, and they shall gather out of his kingdom all scandals, and those who work iniquity.*

Likewise by **Matthew 5**: *Whosoever therefore shall break one of these least commandments, and shall teach men so, he shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven.* In both Gospel sayings, according to **Gregory** in his 12th Homily, the "kingdom of heaven" is said to be the **present Church**.

<margin>Luke 3</margin>
Fifthly, it is confirmed by **Luke 3**: *He will baptize you with the Holy Spirit and with fire; his winnowing fan is in his hand, and he will purge his threshing floor <note>original: "aream"; the floor where wheat is separated from the waste.</note>, and gather the wheat into his barn, but the chaff he will burn with unquenchable fire.* Here, the "threshing floor" designates the Catholic Church, as the doctors explain, and especially <margin>Augustine</margin> **Augustine**, who says in the last chapter of *On Faith to Peter*: "Hold most firmly, and do not doubt in any way, that the threshing floor of God is the Catholic Church, and that in it, until the end of the age, the chaff is mixed with the wheat." And this saying of Augustine has confirmation through those words of Christ in **Matthew 13**: *The kingdom of heaven is like a man who sowed good seed in his field.* And later: *Let both grow until the harvest.*

<margin>Solution to the objection</margin>
To understand these points and those to be discussed, it must be assumed from the Apostle’s words that Christ is the head of the holy universal Church, and she is his body, and every predestined person is his member, and consequently a part of that Church, which is the **mystical body of Christ**—that is, the "secret" body—regulated by the power and influence of Christ the head, and joined and connected by the bond of predestination. This assumption is clear from that saying of the Apostle in **Ephesians 1**: *He gave him as head over the whole Church, which is his body...*

<vocab>Church, unity of faith, the foreknown, dragnet, kingdom of heaven, mystical body of Christ, predestination, Augustine, Gregory, Matthew</vocab>

<summary>This page addresses the theological tension between the Church as a perfect body of the saved and the observable reality of the "present Church" containing both good and bad people. Using parables like the dragnet and the threshing floor, the author argues that while the earthly Church is mixed, the true "mystical body" is defined by predestination and union with Christ.</summary>

This page continues to explore the nature of that unity and addresses the common objection that the Church on earth seems to contain both saints and sinners.</meta>

<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<note>original header: "I. H. DE ECCLESIA" — likely referring to Ioannes Hus (John Hus), the author of this treatise.</note>

...of himself. Likewise from that place where he speaks in the person of the predestined in **Romans 12**: *We, being many, are one body in Christ.* And **Ephesians 4**: *He gave some indeed to be apostles, and some prophets, and others evangelists, and others pastors and teachers, for the perfecting of the saints, for the work of the ministry, for the edifying of the body of Christ.* And further down: *But performing the truth in love, let us grow up in him in all things, who is the head, even Christ. From whom the whole body, being compacted and fitly joined together by what every joint supplieth, according to the operation in the measure of every part, maketh increase of the body unto the edifying of itself in love.*

<margin>Why Christ is called the head of the church.</margin>
Furthermore, it should be noted that Christ is called the head of the Church because he is the most worthy person in the human race, bestowing motion and sense upon all its members. For just as in a man the head is the most excellent part, bestowing motion and sense to the body itself and its parts—without which neither the body nor any of its members can naturally live—so Christ is the person who is true God and man, bestowing spiritual life and motion to the Church itself, and to every member of it, without whose influence it cannot live or feel. And just as all the senses are located in the head of a man, so in Christ are hidden all the treasures of the wisdom and knowledge of God, as is clear from **Colossians 2**. Wherefore the above-mentioned sentiment is implied by the Apostle <note>The author uses "the Apostle" as a standard title for St. Paul.</note> in the same place in **Colossians 1**, when he says: *All things were created through him and in him, and he is before all, and all things consist in him, and he is the head of the body of the Church, who is the beginning, the firstborn from the dead, that in all things he may hold the primacy; because it well pleased the Father that in him all fullness should dwell, and through him to reconcile all things unto himself.* Truly, the Apostle shows the unity of the body of the Church in **1 Corinthians 12**, showing first that the division of graces, ministries, and operations proceeds from one spiritual Lord, who works in all. For it is necessary

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER III</header>

for grace to come first, which is the principle of ministering in the clergy and of working in the laity. For the Spirit gives grace, the Lord receives the ministry, and God requires the ministration. To one, he says, is given through the Spirit the word of wisdom; to another, the word of knowledge according to the same Spirit. To another, faith in the same Spirit; to another, the grace of healing in the one Spirit; to another, the working of miracles; to another, prophecy; to another, the discerning of spirits; to another, kinds of tongues; to another, the interpretation of words. And these nine things the Apostle seems to express consequently in their order in the men who receive those gifts. God, he says, has placed some in the church: first apostles, second prophets, third teachers, then miracles, then the graces of healing, helps, governments, kinds of tongues, and interpretations of words—which nine gifts seem to be adapted to the previous ones. And in the same place, making a comparison of the body of the church and its members to the natural body of a man, the Apostle says: *For as the body is one, and hath many members, and all the members of the body, whereas they are many, yet are one body, so also is Christ.*

<margin>The similarities and differences of the human body to the mystical body of Christ.</margin>
A threefold agreement and a threefold difference between the members of the mystical body <note>term: "mystical body" (Latin: corpus mysticum) — a theological concept describing the spiritual union of all Christians with Christ as their head.</note> and the human body is to be noted. For just as members constitute one body to which a soul is joined; and again, just as every member is necessary to every other member, since they help one another reciprocally in their duties, so it is with the members of the church, by the power of communion and the bond of charity <note>In this context, "charity" (Latin: charitas) refers to the highest form of Christian love.</note>. Thirdly, truly, just as the members conduct themselves in their governance, so also do the members of the Church. Since, according to **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople and Church Father known for his preaching.</note> in *The Imperfect Work*, man is a certain book in which the entire Christian religion is inscribed. Therefore, just as there is an affinity of the head to the feet, so reason and affection are connected. Also, just as every member, noble or ignoble, serves the spirit without contention, so every member of the church serves Christ without contention over status or obedience. And just as the pre-eminent members do not grow proud of their nobility, but act...

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...perform their office according to the governance of the soul for the benefit of each individual, so it ought to be concerning the members of the Church. And just as the eyes and face lack a covering in their operations, lest they be disfigured by a veil and prepared for ruin, so Christ and the Apostles, out of the fervor of charity <note>original: *charitas*; the selfless, divine love of God</note> and their distance from the heat of greed, were not worldly-involved in temporal affairs; it is such "eyes" that all the clergy, their vicars, ought to be. But the less noble members are covered, such as the private parts, and so it is with the contemptible ones through whom <margin>The diversity of members in the Church and the body.</margin> the dregs of the Church are purged. The diversity between the members of these bodies is noted as follows. First, since the parts of the Church are joined through grace, they do not regard their physical position or placement of site as the members of a man do. The second difference: since the members are mystical, it is not contradictory but fitting that one member should have offices of different kinds. For a man is, as it were, a totality <note>original: *universitas*; implying a person is a "little universe" containing many functions</note>, and therefore it suits him to act urgently as much as he can. The third difference is that although the members of the Church have virtues infused by Christ—just as the members of the body have virtues infused by the soul, by which they are established in their being as members—nonetheless, the primary influence and the operation of the members is voluntary, gracious, and meritorious.

<margin>To be in the body and yet not be part of the body.</margin> Furthermore, it should be noted that just as there is something in the human body that is not part of that body—such as spit, phlegm, dung, an abscess, or urine—and that thing is not "of" the body because it is not a part of it. But another thing is in the human body as its part, such as every limb. In this same way, something is in the mystical body of Christ, which is the Church, and yet is not "of" the Church, since it is not a part of it. In this way, every "foreknown" Christian <note>original: *praescitus*; referring to those whom God knows from eternity will ultimately reject him and be lost</note> is of the body itself only as dung that is finally to be cast out. And <margin>Not everyone who is in the Church is of the Church.</margin> thus, it is one thing to be *of* the Church and another to be *in* the Church. It is clear that the following does not follow: "If any wayfarers <note>original: *viantes*; those currently living their earthly life</note> are in the Church, then they are of the Church." Rather, the opposite is true. For we know that weeds grow among the wheat, the raven feeds in the same area as the dove, and the chaff

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER III

is gathered into the barn among the grains; yet there is an uncommunicating distinction between these things, as was exemplified in the human body. Thus we must imagine the Holy Mother Church, and the text of **1 John 2** goes toward this when it says: *Now many antichrists have appeared; they went out from us, but they were not of us. For if they had been of us, they would surely have remained with us.* For just as superfluity <note>waste or excess matter</note> proceeds from food and solid members while not being "of" them, so the purgings of the Church are the foreknown; they proceed from her, but they were not of her as parts. Since no part of her finally falls away from her, because the charity of predestination <note>the bond of love for those chosen by God for salvation</note> that binds her does not fail, according to the Apostle in **1 Corinthians 13**. This the Apostle proves in **Romans 8**, saying: *We know that all things work together for good to those who love God, to those who are called saints according to his purpose (namely, of predestination).* For those whom he foreknew, he also predestined to be conformed to the image of his Son, that he might be the firstborn among many brothers. Moreover, those whom he predestined, these he also called; and those whom he called, these he also justified. And he concludes for the predestined from a long proof, saying: *I am certain that neither death nor life, nor angels, nor principalities, nor powers, nor things present, nor things to come, nor fortitude, nor depth, nor any other creature will be able to separate us from the charity of God, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord.*

It should further be noted that, as many say, there is a fourfold relationship of wayfarers to Holy Mother Church. For some <margin>Wayfarers exist in four distinct states.</margin> are in the Church by name and in reality, such as the predestined, obedient Catholics. Some are there neither in reality nor by name, such as the foreknown pagans. Some are there in name only, such as foreknown hypocrites. And some are there in reality, although they may seem to be outside by name, such as predestined Christians whom the satraps <note>officials or governors; here used derisively for Church leaders</note> of Antichrist seem to condemn in the face of the Church. For in this way, the High Priests and Pharisees [condemned] our Redeemer as a blasphemer and, consequently, a heretic, though he was the predestined Son.

<summary>Hus distinguishes between the institutional Church (being "in" it) and the spiritual Church of the predestined (being "of" it), comparing sinners and hypocrites to bodily waste that will eventually be expelled. He argues that true members are bound by an unbreakable bond of divine love and predestination that ensures they can never be truly separated from God.</summary>

# I. HUS ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: And it</meta>

...the Son of God, [as written in] Romans 1, they condemned to a most shameful death.

<margin>We are made members of the Church not by human election, but by divine predestination.</margin>
It should further be noted that no place or human election makes a person a member of the holy universal Church, but rather divine predestination <note>The theological concept that God has willed the salvation of certain souls from eternity.</note>, with respect to anyone who perseveringly follows Christ in charity. Moreover, according to Augustine in *On the Predestination of the Saints*, predestination is "the election of the divine will through grace." Or, as it is commonly said: Predestination is the preparation of grace in the present and of glory in the future. Furthermore, in the *Decretum* <note>original: *de pœni. dist. iiii. §. hinc propheta*; a reference to Gratian's Decretum, the collection of Canon Law used in the Middle Ages</note>, a twofold predestination is established. One, by which someone is preordained here to righteousness and to receiving the remission of sins, but not to obtaining the life of glory; the second definition mentioned above does not apply to this predestination. The other is the predestination by which someone is predestined to obtain eternal life in the future, and the first type follows from this one, but not the other way around. For if someone is predestined to eternal life, it follows consequently: "Therefore he is predestined to righteousness." And if he attains eternal life, therefore he has attained righteousness; but the reverse is not true. For many become participants in righteousness in the present, but because of a failure of perseverance, they do not become participants in eternal life. Whence it is said in the *Decretum*: "Many seem predestined by the merit of present righteousness, but not by the predestination of eternal glory." And Gratian founds this position on the word of the Apostle in Ephesians 1: *Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, who has blessed us with every spiritual blessing in the heavens in Christ. Just as he chose us in him before the foundation of the world, that we should be holy and spotless in his sight in charity, who predestined us unto the adoption of sons through Jesus Christ in himself according to the purpose of his will, to the praise of the glory of his grace, in which he has made us accepted in his beloved Son, in whom we have redemption through his blood unto the remission of sins.*

<column-break/>

<page-num>17</page-num>
### CHAPTER III.
<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: can</meta>

<margin>Men are of the Church in two ways.</margin>
And it is further clear that people can be of the holy mother Church in two ways: either according to predestination to eternal life—by which way all who are finally holy are of the holy mother Church—or according to predestination only to present righteousness, as are all those who receive the grace of the remission of sins but do not persevere to the end.

<margin>Double grace.</margin>
And it is further clear that grace is twofold: namely, the grace of predestination to eternal life, from which the one preordained cannot finally fall away. The other grace is according to present righteousness, which is present now and absent at another time, because it now happens and now falls away. And the first grace makes the sons of the holy universal Church, and makes a man, in a certain way, infinitely more perfect than the second, because it confers an infinite good to be enjoyed forever, which the second does not. And the first makes sons of the eternal inheritance, but the second makes temporal officials acceptable to God. Whence it seems probable that just as Paul was at once a blasphemer according to present unrighteousness and yet of the holy mother Church, and along with this, faithful and in grace according to the predestination of eternal life; so Iscariot <note>Judas Iscariot</note> was at once in grace according to present righteousness, and yet never of the holy mother Church according to the predestination of eternal life. Since that predestination was lacking to him, and so Iscariot—although he was an apostle or bishop chosen by Christ (which is a name of an office)—was nevertheless never part of the holy universal Church. Just as Paul was never a member of the devil, although he performed certain acts similar to the acts of the "Church of the Malignant." <note>original: *Ecclesiæ malignantis*; a term often used for the community of the wicked or those opposed to God's will.</note> Likewise Peter, who fell into the grace-less state of perjury by the Lord's permission so that he might rise again more strongly; for into such sins, as Augustine says, it is expedient for the predestined to fall.

<margin>Double division from the church</margin>
And it is clear from these things that there is a twofold division from the holy Church. The first is "un-loseable," in the way that all the foreknown <note>original: *præsciti*; those God knows will not be saved (the reprobate).</note> are divided from the Church. And the second is "loseable," in the way that some heretics are divided from that same holy Church through a sin that can be lost, and they can...

<summary>Hus distinguishes between belonging to the institutional Church and being a true member of the "Universal Church" based on God's eternal predestination. He argues that even figures like Judas Iscariot, though holding the office of Apostle, were never truly part of the Church because they were not predestined for eternal life.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: The previous page discussed the nature of the Church as the mystical body of Christ and the role of divine predestination in determining membership. It established that one is not made a member by human election or office, but by God’s eternal will.</meta>

...they can nevertheless come through the grace of God to the fold of the Lord Jesus Christ. For regarding such people, he himself says in John 10: *I have other sheep, which are not of this fold, and those I must bring.* <note>original: "Alias oues habeo, quæ nō sunt ex hoc ouili..." (John 10:16)</note> He had "other sheep" according to predestination who were not yet part of this fold or of his Church according to their present state of righteousness, whom he brought to life through his grace. And this distinction between predestination and present grace is very much to be noted. For some are sheep according to predestination, yet "ravenous wolves" according to their present state of injustice, as Augustine explains in his commentary on John. Similarly, some are children according to predestination but not yet according to present grace; Augustine touches on this distinction for both sides in his commentary on John 11, where it says: *To gather into one the children of God who were scattered.* <note>John 11:52</note> Caiaphas, he says, prophesied only concerning the nation of the Jews, in which there were sheep of whom the Lord himself says, *I was not sent except to the lost sheep of the house of Israel.* <note>Matthew 15:24</note> But the Evangelist knew there were other sheep who were not of this fold, who had to be brought in. And therefore he adds: *and not only for the nation, but to gather into one the children of God who were scattered.* Now, these things were said according to predestination. For they were not yet his sheep nor the children of God. Thus far Augustine. 

Hence, regarding these matters, it is said in the *Decretum* <note>original: "de pœni. dist. iiij. §. Hoc ergo"; a reference to Gratian’s collection of Canon Law regarding penance</note>: Therefore, in this way, they are not "children" unless they are sharers of eternal happiness. And it adds that people are called "children" in three ways: First, by predestination alone, like those of whom John says, *To gather into one the children of God who were scattered.* Second, by predestination and the hope of eternal happiness, like those to whom the Lord says, *Little children, I am with you only a little while longer.* <note>John 13:33</note> Third, by the merit of faith and present righteousness, but *not* by the predestination of eternal glory, like those of whom the Lord says, *If his children forsake my law and do not walk in my judgments.* <note>Psalm 89:30</note>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV</header>
<sig>D ii</sig>
<meta>catchword: queen</meta>

->CHAPTER IV.<-

Christ is the Head of the Church from the beginning of the world until eternity, and all the righteous are members of his body; however, those foreknown [to be lost] <term>presciti: literally "the foreknown," referring to those God knows will ultimately be damned</term> never were, nor will be, members of that body.

### CHAPTER IV

<margin>Christ is the proper head</margin>
From what has already been noted, it is first concluded that Christ alone is the head of the universal Church, who is not merely a part alongside another. This is evident, because if anyone is the head of that universal church, then he is made "better than the angels" and every blessed created spirit, which belongs to Christ alone. Hebrews 1. For it is necessary for him to be the "firstborn among many brothers." Colossians 1. Consequently, he must be the captain by the dignity of the right of primogeniture <note>The legal and spiritual right of the firstborn to inherit leadership</note>. The deduction is clear through the Apostle in Colossians 1 <note>The author is likely synthesizing Ephesians 1:20-23 and Colossians 1:18</note>, where he says: *What God worked in Christ, raising him from the dead and seating him at his right hand in the heavenly places, above every principality, power, virtue, and dominion, and every name that is named, not only in this age but also in the one to come; and he put all things under his feet, and gave him as head over the whole church, which is his body.* 

From this it is clear that if any Christian were to be the head of the universal church alongside Christ—since the Church cannot be a monster having two heads equally, as is stated in the *Extravagante* <note>A term for papal decrees; here citing Boniface VIII’s famous bull *Unam Sanctam* (1302)</note> of Boniface VIII beginning *Unam Sanctam*. Therefore, he says, "Of the one Church there is one body and one head, not two heads like a monster"—one would have to concede that the Christian who was the head of that Church was Christ himself, or otherwise concede that Christ would be inferior to him and his humble member. Since this is impossible, the conclusion is evident. Hence the holy Apostles harmoniously confessed themselves to be servants of this head and humble ministers of his bride. But never did any of the Apostles presume to assert that he was the head or the bridegroom of the said Church, because this would be to commit adultery with the queen.

<margin>The church cannot be two-headed.</margin>
<margin>The Apostles are servants of their own [Lord's] bridal chamber.</margin>

<summary>Hus explores the distinction between those who are "children of God" by eternal predestination versus those who only appear so by present faith, concluding that Christ alone—not any human—can be the head of the universal Church.</summary>

<page-num>20</page-num>
# JOHN HUS ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: And fur[ther]</meta>

...queen of heaven, and to assume a title of dignity and office. <note>The text likely refers to the Church or the Virgin Mary in a comparative sense here, following the discussion of predestination.</note> A title of dignity regarding eternal predestination, and of office to which God, by eternal decree, appointed Christ to supremely rule his bride. This is also evident through Blessed Augustine in his letter to Dardanus. She herself, he says, has only one head—namely, the one who rules her, who excels and serves as a model for all, governing both spiritual and secular matters as one.

<margin>One head [according to] Augustine</margin>
<margin>The internal and external head of the church</margin>
Wherefore, the "head" of the church can be understood in two ways: either internal or external. An **internal** head is the chief person within that church. This can be meant in two ways: either someone who presides over the physical goods of that church, or someone who presides over its spiritual goods through ruling. An **external** head is a person presiding over subjects of their own nature but who stands outside their number; when they rule by influencing those subjects, they are called their "head." In this way, Christ is the *extrinsic* <note>meaning: originating from outside the human group</note> head of any particular church and of the universal church according to his divinity. However, he is the *intrinsic* <note>meaning: sharing the same nature as the members</note> head of the universal church according to his humanity. These two natures—divinity and humanity—are the one Christ, who is the unique head of his bride, the universal church, which is the community of the predestined. For she is the "man" who descends from heaven and ascends into heaven, as stated in John 3. <note>John 3:13</note> This is not meant of the whole body itself, but regarding the head: his "descent" was not a change of physical location, but his incarnation or "emptying." <note>original: "inanitio"; a reference to the Greek 'kenosis', Christ's act of setting aside his divine glory to become human.</note> His "ascent" was a physical movement by which he draws the other parts of the body along with him.

<margin>A particular church can have many subordinate heads.</margin>
From this it is clear that it is not contradictory for a particular church to have multiple heads. Indeed, it can have three: divinity, Christ's humanity, and a <term>Captain</term> <note>original: "Capitaneus"; here referring to an earthly leader like a bishop or pastor appointed by God.</note> ordained by God for its governance. But these heads are subordinate: divinity is the highest, Christ's humanity is the middle, and the Captain is the lowest. The universal Church, however, as has been said, has two heads: one extrinsic, which is divinity, and the other intrinsic, which is humanity.

---

<page-num>21</page-num>
## CHAPTER IV.
<meta>catchword: humanly,</meta>

<margin>Christ as extrinsic and intrinsic head.</margin>
And further, it appears from these points that from the very beginning of the world, Christ was the extrinsic head of the church according to his divinity until the incarnation. From the incarnation onward, he is the intrinsic head of the church according to his humanity. Thus, the entire holy catholic church always had and still has Christ as its head, from whom it cannot fall away, since it is a bride bound to the head itself by perpetual love. For in Jeremiah 31, the bridegroom says to the church: *I have loved you with an everlasting love, and therefore I have drawn you, taking pity on you.* <note>original: "In charitate perpetua dilexi te..." (Jeremiah 31:3)</note> Therefore, the bridegroom was always present to the whole church from the beginning according to his divinity, just as he was to come to the holy fathers according to his humanity. 

Wherefore Augustine, commenting on Psalm 36 regarding the verse *I have been young, and now am old*, <note>Psalm 37:25 in modern bibles, 36:25 in the Vulgate</note> speaks to the point: "For the Lord himself in his heart, which is the Church, was younger than the first men. And behold, now he has grown old; you know, recognize, and understand this because you have been placed in this state and have believed that Christ is our head and we are the body of the head. Are we the only ones? Are not those who were before us also part of it? All who were righteous from the beginning of time have Christ as their head. For they believed that he was to come, whom we believe has now come; they were healed by faith in him just as we are. Thus he himself is the head of the entire city of Jerusalem, with all the faithful counted together from the beginning to the end, joined even with the legions and armies of angels. Thus there is one city under one king, and a single province under one Emperor—happy, in perpetual peace and health, praising God, blessed without end." 

But the body of Christ, which is the church, was "younger" like a single man. And behold, now at the end of the age it is in a "ripe old age." For it was said of her: *They shall be multiplied in a ripe old age*; <note>Psalm 92:14</note> she has been multiplied through all nations, and the voice is his own. These are the words of Augustine. In these words, it is clear how Christ is the head of the holy church—the one in whom the fathers believed would come according to his humanity, so that he might be their head humanly.

<summary>This page explains the dual nature of Christ's leadership over the Church, distinguishing between his divine (extrinsic) and human (intrinsic) roles. It argues that the Church has existed since the beginning of the world, united under Christ’s headship through both the Old and New Testaments.</summary>

Here, he begins to argue that those whom God knows will ultimately be lost (the "foreknown") cannot be considered true members of the Church.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
# ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: church</meta>

...divinely, who was present to them through his divinity. In this head, all the elect <note>The "elect" refers to those chosen by God for salvation.</note> are united with the holy angels.

<margin>Solution to the prior objections.</margin>
Secondly, as an objection to what has already been said, it is gathered that no **foreknown** person <term>foreknown: *præscitus*, referring to those God knows from eternity will eventually be damned</term> is a member of the holy mother Catholic Church. For there is only one unique holy mother Catholic Church from the beginning of the world, which is embraced without any mixture of the wicked by the right hand of the Bridegroom through perpetual charity after the Day of Judgment, as is clear from what was said above and from the preceding authority of Augustine. And since she will have no other members after the Day of Judgment than those she has and will have before the Day of Judgment—and since all who are to be saved after the Day of Judgment are the **predestined**—it follows that none of those who are to be saved were "foreknown" before the Day of Judgment. Consequently, no foreknown persons were members of the Church, the Bride of Christ. Since the reasoning is the same for one foreknown person as for any other, it follows that no foreknown person is a member of the holy mother Catholic Church.

Likewise, it is impossible for Christ ever *not* to love his Bride, or any part of her, since he necessarily loves her as himself. But it is impossible for him to love any foreknown person in this way; therefore, it is impossible for any foreknown person to be a member of that Church. The premise is evident from that famous principle: *God cannot know or love anything anew* <note>original: "Quod non potest deus quidquam de nouo cognoscere seu diligere"</note>, as Augustine says in the sixth book of *On the Trinity*. For God cannot begin or cease to understand anything, nor can he perform a new act of will. This is partly because he is immutable (unchanging), and partly because divine knowledge or volition does not depend on anything outside of himself.

<margin>Christ loves the church as himself</margin>
From this, it appears that Christ loves the whole Church as himself, because at some point he will love her in this way—specifically after the Day of Judgment, when she will reign with him, as is clear from the text of the *Song of Solomon*. <note>original: "Cant. canticorum"</note> Otherwise, it would not be a true marriage based on the perpetual charity of Christ consenting to the divine nuptials; nor would the Bridegroom, who is one person with the Bride, love her as himself. For regarding this, the Apostle says in Ephesians 5: *Christ loved the church*

<column-break/>

<page-num>23</page-num>
# CHAPTER 4
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

*the church and gave himself up for her, to sanctify her, cleansing her by the washing of water with the word of life, so that he might present the Church to himself in splendor, without spot or wrinkle or any such thing, that she might be holy and without blemish.* <note>original: "non habentem maculam aut rugam... ut sit sancta & imaculata"</note> On this, Bernard of Clairvaux says in his 12th sermon on the *Song of Solomon*: "The Church is the body of Christ, dearer to him than the life he gave up to death." And it is clear from these points that Christ must always love his Bride, the holy Church, just as he will love her after the Day of Judgment. By the same token, he hates every foreknown person just as he will ever hate them after the Day of Judgment. Since God fully knows what kind of end any foreknown person will make with him, and how much penance any predestined persons who fall will perform (becoming perpetually pleasing to God thereafter), it is clear that he loves any sinful predestined person more than any foreknown person, regardless of what temporary grace <note>Hus distinguishes between "temporary grace" (acting rightly for a time) and "predestination" (being chosen for eternal life).</note> that foreknown person may have. This is because he wills the predestined person to have perpetual beatitude, while he wills the foreknown person to have perpetual fire. As is clear in the Psalm: *You hate all who work iniquity.* <note>original: "Odisti omnes qui operantur iniquitatem"</note> Therefore, because the pride of the foreknown (by which they hate God) always ascends after their final lack of repentance, they are not of the body of Christ. For Blessed Augustine says in his 52nd sermon *On the Words of the Lord*: "Far be it that there should be a humble head and a proud member." He who loves pride does not wish to be of the body of Christ the head. And in his 50th sermon, according to the same Augustine, Christ—speaking truly about all pastors, since he holds all good pastors within himself—says truly: *I am the truth; all who are in unity with me are one.* A foreknown person, however, since he is a member of the devil, is not joined in an orderly way to this head. Even Augustine himself, in *On Christian Doctrine* book 3, chapter 32, after showing that Christ and his body, which is the Church, are one person, rebukes Tyconius <note>Tyconius was a 4th-century North African theologian who wrote rules for interpreting Scripture.</note> for his second rule, in which he calls the whole human race the "bipartite body of the Lord." Augustine says: "He should not have called it so. For in truth, that which will not be with him in eternity is not the body of the Lord." Instead, one should speak of the "true and mixed" body of the Lord.

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the true Church consists only of the predestined, excluding the "foreknown" (those God knows will be damned) because God’s eternal love and knowledge are unchanging. He cites Augustine and Bernard of Clairvaux to claim that while the wicked may appear to be in the Church now, they are not truly part of Christ’s mystical body.</summary>

Following the previous discussion on Christ as the head of the Church, the author now uses the authority of St. Augustine and St. John Chrysostom to prove that those who ultimately fall away—even if they appear holy for a time—were never truly members of Christ's mystical body.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>J. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: we perceive [cernimus]</meta>

<margin>The wicked are not of the Lord’s body</margin>
...either truly or by pretending, because it must be said that not only for eternity, but not even now, are hypocrites with him. Behold how clearly that holy man <note>Referring to St. Augustine.</note> shows that the **foreknown** <term>foreknown: *præsciti*, those God knows will eventually fall into sin and be lost</term> are not truly of Christ’s church. And from the *Decretum* <note>original: "De pœni. dist. iiij"; a reference to Gratian’s Decretum, a collection of canon law</note>, it is deduced that no one is of the kingdom of Christ, which is the Church, unless they are truly a son whom the Father gave to him. Concerning whom it says in John 3: "It is necessary that he should not perish, but have eternal life." It is well said where he states above: "Let it not move us that he does not give this **perseverance** to certain sons." Far be it from being so, if they were of those predestined and called according to his purpose, who are truly the children of the promise. But while these people live piously, they are called children of God; yet those who will live impiously and die in that same impiety, he does not call "sons." And again in the same place, treating that word from 1 John 2: "They went out from us, but they were not of us," he says: "They were not of the number of sons, even when they were in the faith of sons." For those who are truly sons are foreknown and predestined, conformed to the image of the Son of God, and are called holy according to his purpose so that they might be the **elect** <note>The "elect" are those chosen by God for salvation.</note>. For the son of promise does not perish, but the son of perdition does. These people were, therefore, from the multitude of those "called," but they were not from the "fewness" of the elect. Later in the same place: "For he knew from the beginning who were the believers and who was to betray him." And he said, "Therefore I said to you, that no one comes to me unless it has been given to him by my Father." Because of this, many of his disciples went back and no longer walked with him. Were these not called "disciples" in the speaking of the Gospel? And yet they were not truly disciples, because they did not remain in his word, according to that which is written: "If you remain in my word, you are my disciples." Therefore, because they did not have perseverance, just as they were not truly disciples of Christ, so they were not truly children of God, even when they seemed to be and were called so.

<margin>Who the elect disciples and children of God are.</margin>
We therefore call ourselves "elect," and "disciples of Christ," and "children of God," which those whom we perceive <note>The catchword "nimus" from the previous page completes the word "cernimus," meaning "we perceive" or "we discern."</note> to live piously after being reborn should be called.

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV</header>
<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: proves [probat]</meta>

...But they are only truly what they are called if they remain in that for which they are so named. If, however, they do not have perseverance—that is, if they do not remain in that which they began to be—they are not truly called what they are called, and they are not [those things]. For in the eyes of Him to whom it is known what they will become—that is, from good men becoming evil—these things are not so. These are the words of the blessed Augustine. Behold, he clearly shows how there are many in the Church who are called "sons" by men, who nevertheless are not of the Church, since they are not truly children of God predestined to the life of glory.

And the same is evident through Saint Chrysostom in his *Incomplete Work* <note>original: "de opere imperfecto"; a famous commentary on Matthew long attributed to John Chrysostom</note>, Homily 9, who says: "Those who were of God cannot perish, because no one can fall away from God." And that is evident from the tenth chapter of John: "My sheep hear my voice, and I know them, and they follow me. And I give them eternal life, and they shall not perish forever, and no one shall snatch them out of my hand." Where afterwards Christ, the best teacher, proves from the immensity of the Father's gift—which is the Holy Spirit—that no one can do this, because his Father is almighty, from whose hand no one can tear anything away. But because Christ and his Father are one with the Holy Spirit (who is his gift, by which the Church is joined to himself), therefore no one can snatch his sheep from his hand. For he himself eternally chose every member of the Church as a bride. Therefore, he will desert no such person, because otherwise he would have chosen them for glory without foresight. And to this point goes the logic of the great philosopher <note>referring to St. John the Apostle</note> in 1 John 2, when he says of the foreknown, who remained for a time in grace: "If they had been of us, they would certainly have remained with us." For this conditional statement is not impossible or heretical, since it was formed by the Holy Spirit. According to that in Matthew 10: "For it is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you."

The Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul.</note> also supports this in Romans 8, as I inserted above, where speaking of himself and of the predestined, who are members of the Church, he proves...

The author cites Augustine and Chrysostom to distinguish between those who are merely called "sons of God" by men and those who are truly chosen by God.</summary>
