<meta>This page continues Hus’s argument regarding the composition of the Church. He is refining the distinction between those who are "of the Church" by eternal election (predestination) and those who only appear to be members through temporary righteousness (present justice). He responds here to the logical objection that a person cannot be both a member of the Church and a sinner simultaneously.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

...proves that no creature will be able to separate them from the love which is in Christ Jesus, binding his members together in an unshakeable way, since the love of predestination never fails (1 Corinthians 13). Whence in Romans 8 the Apostle says: "But you are not in the flesh, but in the spirit, if indeed the spirit of God dwells in you. But if anyone does not have the spirit of Christ, he is not his," and he understands this to mean that such a person is not a part of his body.

<margin>Objection concerning a foreknown person existing in grace.</margin>
And if it is objected through these points that a **foreknown** <term>foreknown: *præscitus*, one whom God knows will ultimately be lost</term> person existing in **charity** <note>original: "charitate"; here meaning the state of grace or divine love</note> temporarily possesses this bond, and consequently a union with Christ; and likewise, a **predestined** <term>predestined: *prædestinatus*, one eternally chosen by God for salvation</term> person existing in a state of crime lacks this bond, and consequently the union with Christ. It is evident that just as in the human body there is "flowing moisture" and "radical moisture," so in the mystical body of Christ, there is "grace according to present justice" and "consummated grace." <note>Medieval medicine taught that "radical moisture" was the essential, life-sustaining fluid of an organism, while "flowing moisture" was temporary and changeable. Hus uses this to distinguish between eternal election and temporary piety.</note> Whence, just as **abscesses** <note>original: "apostemata"; swellings or tumors</note> are attached by flowing moisture but are not truly integrated because of their different nature, so it is with the members of the devil, even though for a time they are attached according to present justice.

<margin>The predestined have radical grace</margin>
The predestined, however, although they may be deprived of "flowing grace" for a time, nevertheless possess "radical grace" from which they cannot fall away. Thus, the predestined who are currently righteous, possessing a twofold grace, are bound by a twofold bond.

<margin>Objection.</margin>
But an objection is raised against these things. For it follows from what has been said that one must concede that the same person is simultaneously and at once righteous and unrighteous, faithful and unfaithful, a true Christian and a heretic, in chief grace and **graceless** <term>original: *ἄχαρις* (acharis); without grace</term>, and so with other contrary designations, from which a manifest contradiction follows.

<margin>Solution.</margin>
To this it is said that it ought to be conceded that the same person is simultaneously and at once righteous and unrighteous, but it is a contradiction that they be simultaneously and at once righteous and unrighteous *in the same respect*. Just as it is impossible for contraries to exist in the same thing at the same time in the same respect; and thus the aforementioned designations are not contrary because of **equivocation** <note>meaning the terms are being used in different senses or categories</note>. Since, according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>, only one thing is opposed to one thing. And thus the same man—

<meta>catchword: man</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV</header>

<margin>Peter was at once righteous and unrighteous.</margin>
—a man is righteous by the grace of predestination, and unrighteous by a "deprivable vice" <note>a sin that removes temporary grace but not eternal election</note>, such as Peter was in denying Christ, and Paul in persecuting him. For they had not then fallen from the charity of predestination. And consequently, according to that, they were in grace, and thus righteous; and because they were then in a state of crime, and thus deprived of flowing temporal grace, they were thus unrighteous. And if it is inferred, "Therefore they were then *not righteous*, and consequently they were not righteous," this is answered by denying the first consequence. For a move from **privation** to **negation** <note>a logical distinction: "unrighteous" (privation of a quality) does not strictly mean the same as "not righteous" (negation of the category) in this theological context</note> is not valid except with limitation, as in this proposition: Peter and Paul were unrighteous, therefore according to *present* grace they were not righteous, and the conclusion is true. Thus it is truly conceded that they were righteous according to the grace of predestination, and were not righteous according to present grace. Similarly, Paul was faithful according to predestination, and unfaithful according to **perfection** <note>his temporary state of conduct</note>, and was an Israelite according to predestination, and a blasphemer according to present justice, and was in the charity of predestination, and **graceless** <term>original: *ἄχαρις*; without grace</term>, that is, without the charity of present justice.

<margin>Not beloved, beloved.</margin>
And to confirm this, Paul’s own quote from Hosea in Romans 9 is useful, where it is said: "I will call my not-people my people, and her who was not beloved, beloved, and her who had not obtained mercy, one who has obtained mercy. And it shall be in the place where it was said to them, 'You are not my people,' there they shall be called children of the living God." Whence they do not understand this scripture and similar ones, except those who know that there is no contradiction unless opposites are in the same thing in the same respect and for the same instant. Whence those who know how to discuss this concede that Christ, during the holy three days, was both dead and alive. Indeed, as Ambrose says, he was dying and not dying, because he lived in spirit and was dead in the flesh. He was dying as a man, and was not dying as God; and the Apostle in 1 Timothy 5 says that a widow living in luxury is dead while she lives, because she lives in the flesh but does not live in the spirit; and it is clear that neither contraries nor contradictions follow. From what has already been said, it is finally clear that no one—

<margin>Christ dead and alive</margin>

<summary>Hus addresses the logical paradox of how a person can be both a saint and a sinner. He argues that the Elect (like Peter or Paul) remain "just" through God's eternal predestination even when they fall into temporary, "unjust" sin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hus’s argument regarding the composition of the Church. He is refining the distinction between those who are "of the Church" by eternal election (predestination) and those who only appear to be members through temporary righteousness (present justice). He responds here to the logical objection that a person cannot be both a member of the Church and a sinner simultaneously.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

no **foreknown** <term>foreknown: *præscitus*, one whom God knows will ultimately be lost</term> person is truly a part of the holy mother Church. If **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas, the influential 13th-century theologian</note> or anyone else should call a foreknown person who is currently in a state of grace a "member of the Church," he then retracts this in accordance with **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, whose views on predestination heavily influenced Hus</note> and Holy Scripture, looking instead toward the common way of speaking and the reputation of the **Church Militant** <term>Church Militant: the living body of believers on earth currently "at war" with sin and the devil</term>. Hence, it was said above what Augustine says: "If they truly do not have perseverance—that is, if they do not remain in that which they began to be—they are not truly called that which they are called, and they are not. For in the eyes of Him to whom it is known what they will become—that is, evil people instead of good—these things do not exist." This saying of Augustine ought to stand against all objections in which a retraction is to be noted.

### Response to the authorities cited in the objection above.

#### CHAPTER V

<margin>On being in the Church according to "unformed faith."</margin>
Now we must speak to the authorities proposed to the contrary in the third chapter. To understand them, it must be noted that men are said to be in the holy Church in various ways. For some are said to be in it only according to **unformed faith** <term>unformed faith: *fides informis*, a scholastic term for a belief in God that is not accompanied by active love or "charity," and thus does not save</term>, such as foreknown Christians entangled in crimes, to whom the Lord says in Luke 6: "Why do you call me 'Lord, Lord,' and do not do what I command you?" And in Matthew 7 He says of them: "Many will say to me on that day, 'Lord, did we not prophesy in your name, and cast out demons in your name, and perform many miracles in your name?' And then I will declare to them: 'I never knew you. Depart from me, all you who work iniquity.'"

<margin>According to "present faith."</margin>
Others, however, are in the Church only according to present faith and grace, such as the righteous who are foreknown, who are not in the Church according to **predestination** <term>predestination: *prædestinatio*, God's eternal choice of those who will be saved</term> to eternal life.

<margin>According to predestination.</margin>
Some are in the Church according to predestination only, such as the unbaptized children of Christians, and pagans or Jews who are future Christians. Some are there according to unformed faith and according to predestination, such as predestined Christians currently living in crime, but who will return to grace. Some are there according to predestination and present

<column-break/>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V.</header>
<meta>catchword: grace</meta>

grace, as are all the elect Christians who imitate Christ in their conduct, who are still able to fall from that state of grace in this life. Others, however, are in the **Church Triumphant** <term>Church Triumphant: the Church in heaven, consisting of those who have already defeated sin and death</term>, having been confirmed in grace. All people, however, are divided into the foreknown and the predestined; the former are ultimately members of the devil, and the rest are members of the **mystical body** <note>original: "corpus mystici"; the spiritual union of all true believers with Christ as their head</note>, which is the holy Church, the bride of the Lord Jesus Christ. Hence, in the first authority regarding the **dragnet** <note>referring to the parable of the net in Matthew 13:47–50</note>, the predestined are signified by the good fish, and the foreknown by the bad fish which were cast out.

<margin>The Church is compared to a dragnet, etc.</margin>
Regarding this, **Blessed Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD)</note> says: "The holy Church is compared to a dragnet cast into the sea, because it has been committed to fishermen." Behold the first similarity: through it, each person is drawn from the waves of the present world toward the eternal kingdom—namely, by the "call"—so that they are not drowned in the depths of eternal death. Behold the second similarity: it gathers from every kind of fish, because it calls the wise and the foolish, the free and the slave, the poor and the rich, the strong and the weak to the forgiveness of sins. Behold the third similarity. Therefore, let the **False Scribe** <note>likely a reference to an opponent who misinterprets texts</note> beware of inferring that because the holy Church gathers from every kind of men through the "call," all who are called to the faith of Christ are members of the holy Church, the bride of Christ. Hence, Blessed Gregory shows conclusively the distinction between the elect and the reprobate, saying: "From which end, namely, the good fish are selected, while the bad are cast out; because each of the elect is received into eternal tabernacles, while the reprobate, having lost the light of the eternal kingdom, are dragged away to the outer darkness." For now, the "net of faith" contains the good (that is, the elect) and the bad (that is, the foreknown) mixed together. Behold the fourth similarity. But the "shore" of the holy Church indicates the net it was pulling—namely, through the call to faith. And Blessed Gregory adds: "And though the fish of the sea which were captured cannot be numbered, we who are evil are captured, but we are changed into goodness," by which he hints that the predestined who are currently evil are finally changed into goodness.

<summary>Hus categorizes different ways a person might be considered "in" the Church, distinguishing between temporary appearance and eternal reality. He uses the parable of the dragnet from Gregory the Great to explain that while the visible Church calls everyone, only the "predestined" are its true members.</summary>
<keywords>predestination, foreknown, Augustine, Gregory the Great, unformed faith, Church Militant, Church Triumphant, dragnet parable, membership, grace</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding who truly belongs to the Church. He is now systematically addressing biblical passages and the writings of Church Fathers (authorities) that seem to suggest the Church is a mix of both good and bad people. Hus argues that while they may appear together on earth, they are not united in God's eternal plan.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: as if</meta>

...is restored. Therefore, the voice of **Blessed Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory I (c. 540–604), a key doctor of the church</note> is the voice of the **predestined** <term>predestined: those chosen by God for salvation from the beginning of time</term>, who, having been struck down by malice, are called back from the holy church into goodness through baptism or through penance.

<margin>Solution regarding the authority on the wedding.</margin>
From these points, the explanation of the second authority regarding the wedding feast becomes clear. In this wedding, both the good and the bad are gathered through faith and are mixed together in the holy church. However, the bad are not true sons, just as they are not true friends, because they lack the wedding garment, which is the **charity of predestination** <note>Hus defines the "wedding garment" as the love that flows from being one of God's chosen, rather than just outward belonging</note>. Hence, the King of the wedding says to them as if to a single person: "Friend, how did you enter here, not having a wedding garment?" Regarding this, Gregory says: "It is truly a wonder, dearest brothers, that he calls this man both a 'friend' and 'rejected,' as if he were more aptly saying: 'Friend and not a friend; a friend through faith, but not a friend through his actions.'" These are the words of Gregory.

<margin>Regarding the third authority.</margin>
The explanation of the third authority is also clear: for from the kingdom of the holy church they will gather all stumbling blocks—that is, those who commit iniquity, meaning those who practice a wicked lack of repentance. Behold, there the **foreknown** <term>foreknown: *præsciti*, those whom God knows will ultimately reject Him and be lost</term> are touched upon.

<margin>Regarding the fourth authority.</margin>
Regarding the fourth authority, which says, "Whoever therefore breaks one of the least of these commandments," **St. Augustine** speaks relevantly in his commentary on the final chapter of John. On that passage where Simon Peter hauled the net to land full of 153 large fish: "Whoever therefore breaks one of the least of these commandments and teaches men so, shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven. But whoever does them and teaches them shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven." That person, therefore, could belong to the number of the large fish. But the "least" man—the one who undoes by his deeds what he teaches with his words—can be in such a church as that first catch of fish signifies, containing both good and bad. For that church itself is called the "kingdom of heaven," which is why He says: "The kingdom of heaven is like a **dragnet** <note>original: *fagenæ*</note> cast into the sea, gathering every kind of fish," and so on. There He also wants us to understand both the good and the bad, whom He leads to the shore—that is, to the end of the age—to be separated. Furthermore, to show that these "least" ones are the **reprobrate** <term>reprobate: those rejected by God for their persistent sin</term>, who teach good things by speaking but undo them by living badly, and that they will not be

<column-break/>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V</header>

as if they were the "least" in eternal life, but rather will not be there at all. When He had said "he shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven," He immediately added: "For I say to you, unless your righteousness exceeds that of the **scribes and Pharisees** <note>Hus often compared the corrupt clergy of his own day to the biblical Pharisees</note>, you shall not enter the kingdom of heaven." Certainly, those are the scribes and Pharisees who sit in the chair of Moses, and of whom He says: "Do what they say, but do not do what they do. For they speak and do not act." They teach with words what they undo with their habits. It follows, therefore, that he who is "least" in the kingdom of heaven—of the kind that exists now in the Church while teaching what he undoes—will not belong to the society of those who do what they teach, and therefore he is not in the number of the large fish. For "whoever does and teaches shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven." And because he was "great" here, he will be there, where that "least" man is not. Indeed, they will be so great there that he who is "lesser" there is greater than those who are "great" here—that is, those who are in the kingdom of heaven where the net gathers the good and the bad. Hence, those who do the good they teach will be the greater ones in that eternity of the kingdom of heaven; these the fish indicate, belonging to the right hand and to the resurrection of life. This is the view of Augustine. From his words, we have the same opinion as that of Gregory: first, that the church gathers both the elect and the foreknown in faith. Second, it is held that those who teach in the church, and hold seats of dignity, yet break the commandments of God, are reprobate. For he says: "Finally, to show that these least ones are reprobate, who teach good things by speaking which they undo by living badly, they will not be as if 'least' in eternal life, but will not be there at all." Third, it is held that the faithful Christians who fulfill the commandments of God by their work are the "great" in the holy church of God, and the prelates who preside but break the commandments are the "least"; and if they are foreknown, then they will not be in the kingdom of God. Therefore, let the disciples of the **Antichrist** <note>A term Hus frequently used for those who opposed Christ's teachings while claiming to lead His Church</note> blush, who, living 

<margin>To teach and not to do.</margin>
<margin>The greatest in the church are often the least.</margin>

->for Christ<-

<summary>Hus interprets the parables of the Wedding Feast and the Dragnet to argue that while the visible Church contains both the "predestined" and the "foreknown," only the former truly belong to it in eternity. He emphasizes that clergy who teach the law but live wickedly (like the Pharisees) will have no place in the kingdom of heaven.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's theological argument regarding the membership of the Church. He is refuting the idea that being physically present within the institutional Church or holding high office makes one a true member of the mystical Body of Christ. He relies heavily on Saint Augustine to distinguish between those who are "in" the Church and those who are "of" the Church.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH
## CHAPTER IV

Contrary to Christ, they say they are the greatest in the holy church of God and the proudest, publicly flourishing in the greed and pomp of the world; they are called the heads and the body of the holy Church, though according to Christ’s Gospel they ought to be called the least. Fourthly, it is held that the **one hundred and fifty-three great fish** <note>original: "cliij. in dextera nauigij capti" - a reference to the miraculous catch of fish in John 21:11</note> caught on the right side of the ship signify the **predestined** <term>predestined: those chosen by God for salvation from the beginning of time</term>, among whom those who taught and kept the commandments of God are, all else being equal, the greater.

<margin>Solution to the fifth objection.</margin>
To the fifth confirmation of the Gospel: *He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit*, etc. He grants that the holy church is the **Lord’s threshing floor** <note>original: "area domini" - a biblical metaphor for the Church where the good and bad are mixed before judgment</note>, in which there are, according to faith, both good and bad, predestined and **foreknown** <term>foreknown: "praesciti," those whom God knows will ultimately reject Him and be lost</term>, currently mixed together—the predestined like wheat, the foreknown like chaff. The first are to be gathered into the granary of the heavenly homeland; the rest are to be burned with unquenchable fire, as the Gospel and the exposition of **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), the most cited authority in Hus's work</note> say. But just as chaff always remains chaff, so the foreknown always remains foreknown. And even if at some time he is in a state of grace according to **present justice** <note>Hus distinguishes between temporary righteousness and the eternal state of being chosen by God</note>, nevertheless he is never part of the holy church. And just as wheat always remains wheat, so the predestined always remains predestined and a member of the church, even if he should sometimes fall away from **adventitious grace** <note>grace that comes and goes based on current behavior</note>, but never from the grace of predestination. This is well-supported by the opinion of Augustine (32. q. 4. C. Recurrat), where it is held: "Therefore, whether they seem to be inside, or are openly outside, he who is dearer, is dearer. Whether they remain in their sterility on the threshing floor, or are carried away like by the wind on the occasion of temptation: that which is chaff, is chaff, and is always divided from the truth of that church which is **without spot or wrinkle** <note>a common biblical description of the perfected Church in Ephesians 5:27</note>. Therefore, he who is mixed with the congregation of the saints in carnal hardening [is still chaff]." Yet one must despair of no one, whether he appears as such within, or is an open adversary without. These are the words of Augustine. From this, John the Baptist pertinently says: *He will purge*, namely on the day of judgment, *his threshing floor*, that is, the holy church, and *he will gather the wheat into the granary*, that is, the predestined into the homeland, but the chaff—that is, the foreknown—he will burn with 

<column-break/>

with unquenchable fire. Whence Augustine, in *On Faith to Peter*, the last chapter, as has been cited, says: "Hold most firmly and by no means doubt that the threshing floor of God is the Catholic Church, and in it, until the end of the age, the chaff is contained mixed with the wheat. Here also the bad are mixed with the good in the **communion of the sacraments**; and in every profession, whether of clerics or of laity, there are both good and bad together." And below: "In the end of the age, the good are to be separated from the bad even in body, when Christ shall come having the winnowing fan in his hand, and he will thoroughly cleanse his floor, and gather the wheat into the granary, but the chaff he will burn with unquenchable fire, when by a just judgment he will segregate the just from the unjust, the good from the bad, the upright from the perverse. He will place the good on the right, the bad on the left, and from his mouth the sentence of eternal judgment, everlasting and unchangeable, shall be brought forth. All the wicked shall go into eternal burning, but the just into eternal life. The wicked always to burn with the devil, the just however to reign without end." These are Augustine's words. And it is clear from the exposition of the saints how in the parables of Christ the foreknown are signified by the bad fish, by the bad guests of the wedding, by the man at the wedding not clothed in a wedding garment, by the chaff, by the **tares** <note>original: "çiçania" - weeds that look like wheat</note>, by the bad seed, by the bad tree, by the foolish virgins, and by the goats. Conversely, in the opposite way, the predestined are signified by the good fish, by the good guests, by the man clothed in a wedding garment, by the wheat, by the good seed, by the good tree, by the wise virgins, and by the sheep.

<margin>The good mixed with the bad.</margin>
<margin>The foreknown are signified.</margin>
<margin>The predestined are signified.</margin>

Therefore, the faithful person ought to beware of this logic: "The foreknown are in the holy church of God, therefore they are part of it." For it has been said that it is one thing to be *in* the church, and another to be *of* the church, or to be a part or member of the church. Just as it does not follow: "The chaff or the tares are in the wheat or mixed with the wheat, therefore the chaff is wheat," so it is in this proposition. Just as it does not follow: "Dung or an ulcer is in the body of a man, therefore it is part of it." Again, this consequence does not [follow]...

<vocab>Ecclesia (Church), Praedestinati (the predestined), Praesciti (the foreknown/reprobate), Augustinus (Augustine), Triticum (wheat), Palea (chaff), Iudicium (judgment), Area Dei (the threshing floor/field of God)</vocab>

<summary>Jan Hus uses agricultural and biblical metaphors to argue that the institutional Church contains both the "predestined" (true members) and the "foreknown" (reprobates), who are mixed together like wheat and chaff until the final Judgment. He emphasizes that being physically present or holding high office in the Church does not guarantee one is a true part of Christ's spiritual body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from page 33, where he distinguishes between the institutional visible Church and the mystical "Holy Catholic Church." He continues to argue that true membership in the Church is determined by God's predestination rather than by human office or temporary states of grace.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
-># JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH<-

...this logic does not hold: "This person is in a state of grace according to [present] justice, therefore he is a part or a member of the holy catholic church." However, this logic *does* hold: "This person is in the grace of **predestination** <term>predestination: the theological concept that God has chosen certain souls for salvation from eternity</term>, therefore he is a part or member of the holy church." And again, this logic does not hold: "Peter is in a state of sin, therefore he is not a part or member of the holy church." Rather, it follows correctly that he is simply not in the church according to the grace of present justice. These subtle points of logic are understood by weighing what it means to be "in the church" versus what it means to be "a member" or "part of the church." Being a member of the holy catholic church is created by <margin>What is predestination</margin> **predestination**, which is the preparation of grace in the present and glory in the future; it is not created by a place of high rank, nor human election, nor any sensible sign. For even though the devilish **Iscariot** <note>original: "Scarioth" - Judas Iscariot</note>, despite Christ's election and the temporary spiritual gifts given to him for his Apostleship or Bishopric, was regarded by the people as a true disciple of Christ, he was not a true disciple. Instead, he was a wolf covered in a sheep's skin, as **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo, a primary influence on Hus's theology</note> says; and consequently, he was not predestined, and thus not a part of the church that is the Bride of Christ.

From this it is clear that it would be a great presumption to assert, without a special revelation or without fear, that one is a member of that holy church. For no one is a member of that church unless they are predestined and, in their own time, "without spot or wrinkle." No one could assert without fear or revelation that they are predestined and holy, without spot or wrinkle. Therefore, this is the conclusion.

Thus, it is quite marvelous with what audacity those who are more devoted to the world, who live more secularly and lawlessly, and who are far removed from following Christ’s life—being more barren in fulfilling Christ’s counsel and commandments—would assert without fear that they are the heads, or the body, or the principal members of the church which is the Bride of Christ. Do we really believe that they are without the "spot" of mortal sin, or without the "wrinkle" of venial sin? Their desertion of Christ’s counsel, their idleness in their holy office, and their worldly works suggest the opposite, especially since the Groom of the church says: *By their fruits you shall know them* (Matthew 7). And

<column-break/>

<page-num>35</page-num>
-># CHAPTER V<-

(Matthew 7) and in John 10 he says: *Believe the works.* And in Matthew 23: *Do not do according to their works, for they say and do not do.*

But an objection is raised against these points. First: by this reasoning, any cleric distinguished by a character or sensible sign <term>character: a permanent spiritual mark believed to be imprinted on the soul during sacraments like ordination</term> through a prelate in the eyes of the church would be a part of the holy mother church; [if not,] then Christians would not recognize their own mother. Indeed, if she were unknown, they would not pay their bodily debts, such as offerings and **tithes** <term>tithes: a tenth of one's income paid as a tax to the church</term>, and an exceedingly great confusion would follow in the **militant church** <term>militant church: the church on earth currently struggling against sin and the devil</term>.<margin>Objection that every cleric is part of the church.</margin>

To this, we reply by denying the premise. For an example is given in Judas, who was elected to the ministry of the Bishopric by Christ, who could not err. Yet he, being **foreknown** <term>foreknown: "praescitus," those who God knows will ultimately be lost</term>, was never a true disciple of Christ, as Augustine proves. Rather, he was a wolf covered in sheep’s skin, and was always chaff and the grain of weeds or tares. Similarly, the second part of the premise is denied. For the church is called the Bride of Christ <gloss>in the strictest sense</gloss> <note>original: "ἀντονοματικῶς" (antonomastically) - using a title to refer to the highest or most proper example of a category</note>, which is the totality of the predestined, as has been said. For if that totality is the most principal Bride of Christ, then that is the church which is "holy" in the proper sense, since she is the "only dove" and the "queen standing at the king's right hand," to whom the young virgins shall be brought. Therefore, just as in the time of the pilgrimage of the clergy with whom Christ lived, all those things—the rank of the priesthood, the observance of the traditions they established, their claims that "God is our father" and that they were "the seed of Abraham" and "never slaves to anyone"—did not make that clergy the holy church in the true and proper sense. Christ said of them in Matthew 15 that the disciples should let them be offended, because *they are blind and leaders of the blind.* Thus it stands that a particular multitude of clergy does not become the holy church, in the proper sense, simply because that multitude affirms itself to be that holy church. And it is clear that such arguments do not hold. In the first, it was inferred that otherwise... <margin>Solution.</margin>

<summary>Hus argues that true membership in the Church is defined by God's eternal predestination rather than external clerical status or "present justice." He refutes the objection that this makes the Church "unknowable," asserting that institutional rank and "sensible signs" do not guarantee one is a true member of the Bride of Christ.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "Holy Catholic Church" as a body of the predestined. Hus is addressing the problem of how a Christian can "know" the Church if its true membership is hidden in the mind of God.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header># JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

Otherwise, it follows that Christians would not know their own mother. For we must know our mother by faith. Just as we know by faith the **Triumphant Church** <term>Triumphant Church: the part of the Church already in heaven with God</term>, Christ her head, and his apostles together with the blessed angels and many saints. However, we know those who are still **pilgrims on the way** <note>original: "viantes" — those currently living on earth</note> and those who are sleeping <note>referring to those in purgatory</note> in a rather general and imperfect way. But "when that which is perfect comes, that which is in part shall be done away," because in our heavenly homeland we shall clearly behold our mother along with all her members. Nor should the faithful murmur, but rather rejoice in the truth that our holy mother the Church is so unknown to us here on our journey, because upon this fact stands the merit of Christian faith. For according to the Apostle in Hebrews 11: "Faith is the substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen"—that is, things not visibly apparent to us here on our journey.

<margin>In heaven we shall see our mother the church with her members.</margin>

Furthermore, by reason of **predestination** or that charity which never fails—which is the "wedding garment" distinguishing a member of the church from a member of the devil—we do not perceive these things visibly here. For according to **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD)</note>, "The act of faith is to believe what you do not see." And the opposite of the second conclusion is evident. For we pay our debt to the holy church when we have Christ as our supreme pontiff and his ministers; those whom we hold to be ministers or fathers through a general faith based on their works, and whom we generally suppose to be members of Christ, we provide with temporal support for the body.

<margin>Objection.</margin>

And if it is objected that any **layman** <term>layman: a member of the church who is not a priest or of the clergy</term> is bound to believe concerning his **prelates** <term>prelates: high-ranking church officials like bishops or abbots</term> that they themselves are heads of the church and parts of the church, either according to predestination or according to their present state of righteousness.

<margin>Solution.</margin>

To this it is said: That a layman is not bound to believe anything about his superior except what is true. This is evident because no one is bound to believe anything unless God moves him to believe it. But God does not move a person to believe a falsehood. For even if a good outcome occasionally arises from a false faith, and God moves the substance of the act, nevertheless God does not move a person in such a way that they are deceived. Thus, if a layman

<column-break/>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>### CHAPTER V.</header>

were to believe concerning his prelate that he is a holy member of the church, and it were not so, his faith or his believing would be false. A pastor, therefore, is bound to move his subjects to believe he is such a person through the instruction of virtuous works. Hence, if a subject does not recognize the virtuous works of his superior, he is not bound to believe that the superior is a member of the church according to present righteousness; and he should believe only with fear and conditionally that he is such a member simply—that is, according to predestination. If, however, he clearly recognizes his superior's crime, then he ought to suppose from the work that the man is not then righteous, but an enemy of Jesus Christ. And it is clear that the consequence of the third objection is false. For there is no confusion in the **Militant Church** <term>Militant Church: the church on earth currently struggling against sin and the devil</term> arising from the fact that, without revelation, we do not distinctly know the members of the mystical body of Christ who are currently on their journey.

<margin>Objection</margin>

But a further objection is raised: Since grace makes one a child of the church, just as sin makes one a member of the devil or an unbeliever, it seems that a person can become a member of the church after having been an unbeliever, just as a member of the church can become a member of the devil. For who doubts that **Iscariot** <note>Judas Iscariot</note>, when he was a true apostle, was a member of the church? Just as **Paul**, when he was a blasphemer, was divided from the holy mother church.

<margin>Solution</margin>

To this it is said that "church" is understood in two ways: truly and by reputation (or by name). **Truly**, as has been said, it refers to the predestined. **By name**, the congregation of the **foreknown** <term>foreknown: those God knows will ultimately be damned (Latin: praesciti)</term> is also called the church. Although, due to the simple error of those on the journey, such a person may be "reputed" to be of the holy mother church. Thus, many are called heads or members of the church according to worldly fame, even though according to God's foreknowledge they are members of the devil, who believe for a time and later fall away, or are even now and always unbelievers. And in this way, as was said above according to Augustine, they were "disciples" of Christ who went back and no longer walked with him. Similarly, Iscariot was a false disciple of Christ; of him Augustine says, commenting on John and showing that the sheep heard the voice of Christ: "But what," he says...

<summary>Hus argues that true membership in the Church is known only by faith and God's predestination, not by outward appearance or church office. He distinguishes between the "True Church" of the saved and the "Reputative Church" which includes those who may appear holy but are ultimately not chosen by God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Church, specifically contrasting those who are merely "reputed" to be members with those who are truly predestined for salvation. It transitions into Chapter VI, which discusses the "Church of the Wicked" and Satan as its head.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: to be [esse]</meta>

...he says, do we think those who heard were sheep? <note>original: "inquit" — likely referring to a quote from Augustine continued from the previous page</note> Behold, Judas heard, and he was a wolf; he followed, but covered in sheepskin, he lay in wait for the shepherd. Thus he spoke. In this way, therefore, many are said to be of the church by reputation—even according to their present righteousness—but they are not truly of the church according to their predestination to glory. Who these people are, **Augustine** teaches in his commentary on John, saying: "The Lord knows those who are his; he knows who will remain for the crown, and who will remain for the flame; he knows the wheat in his threshing floor, he knows the chaff, he knows the grain, he knows the weeds; but to everyone else, it is unknown who are the doves and who are the ravens."

### Just as Christ is the head of the elect, so Satan is the head of the Synagogue of the wicked. CHAPTER VI.

<margin>The Devil is the head of the malicious.</margin>
Having spoken of the Holy Catholic Church, which is the mystical body of Christ and of which he is the head, we must now speak briefly of the **Church of the Malicious** <note>original: "Ecclesia malignantium" — a term often used in the Middle Ages to describe the assembly of the wicked or those destined for damnation</note>, which is the body of the devil, and of which he himself is the head. For **Blessed Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD)</note> says in Book 4 of his *Moralia*, chapter 9: "Just as the Redeemer is one person with the congregation of the good (for he is the head of the body, and we are the body of this head), so the ancient enemy is one person with the entire collection of the reprobate, because he presides over them in iniquity as their head." From this it is clear that all the **foreknown** <term>foreknown: those whom God knows eternally will reject His grace and face damnation</term> constitute one body. Indeed, Christ said in John 8 to the Jews, the chief priests, and the Pharisees—who were called the heads of the church: "You are of your father the devil, and you wish to do the desires of your father."

<margin>The wicked exist in a threefold degree, just as the good do.</margin>
From this it is clear that there must be one generation which was bad among the common people, worse among the secular leaders, but worst of all among the **prelates** <note>prelates: high-ranking church officials like bishops or abbots; Hus is famously critical of corruption in the Church hierarchy</note>. Just as the generation of the righteous corresponds to them proportionally in three opposite degrees. If, therefore, there is one generation of the perverse, it follows that there is one "wicked man" who possesses parts that are members of the devil. And since a thing cannot

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VI.</header>

be a member or a head except in relation to a whole, complete body, it is manifest that there is one body of the devil. Furthermore, while the body of Christ is called a "mystical body" because of the mystery of the heavenly marriage between Christ and the church, the body of the devil is not mystical in this way, but rather "obscure." For being joined to the devil does not directly signify a mystery, but rather a scourge. Thus, that body of the devil has a **natural existence** <note>referring to the physical/ontological reality of the people themselves</note>, since according to **Augustine** in his book *On the Nature of the Good*: "Every evil must be grounded in something good." Therefore, every mortal evil is grounded in the good of nature. And beyond this natural existence, that body of the devil has an "existence of vice," just as the mystical body of Christ has an "existence of virtue." Because of this, **Blessed Augustine** denies in Book 3 of *On Christian Doctrine*, chapter 32, that there can be a single body composed of the members of Christ and the members of the devil.

And if it is asked: what "form" unites the members of the devil in that body? It is said that there is an external form and an internal deformity. The external form is the eternal **foreknowledge of God**, who knows and ordains that all the foreknown members of that diabolical body be bound together for perpetual punishment. The internal deformity, however, is **final disobedience** or pride, which the saints call the "sin of final impenitence," or the **sin against the Holy Spirit** <note>traditionally understood as the persistent refusal to repent until death</note>. And so this same sin both joins them together and sets them apart. For it joins the members of the devil by binding them in **Tartarus** <note>a classical term for the deepest abyss of hell</note> in their malice, and it separates them from the company of the blessed. It is like heat: when it dissolves a mixture, it gathers **those of the same kind** <note>original Greek: "τῶ ὁμογενῆ" — homogeneous things</note>, because it causes each dissolved element to seek its own place. But it separates **those of a different kind** <note>original Greek: "τῶ ἑτερογενῆ" — heterogeneous things</note>, because it destroys the harmonious balance by resolving the parts of the mixture back into their separate, simple natures. For on the day of judgment, through the coldness of the devil's body and the heat of charity in Christ's body, the body must be dissolved into two parts according to the law of the final form. Then the light parts will fly away with their head—who is a consuming fire—to their natural dwellings among the saints. But the leaden parts, by their earthly affection...

<vocab>Church of the Malicious, body of the devil, the foreknown, Augustine, Gregory, sin against the Holy Spirit, final impenitence, homogeneous, heterogeneous</vocab>

<summary>Hus defines the 'Church of the Wicked' as the body of the devil, contrasting it with Christ’s mystical body. He argues that the wicked are joined by their final impenitence and God's foreknowledge of their punishment, ultimately to be separated from the righteous at the Last Judgment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter regarding the "Church of the Malicious." It addresses the complexities of how Christ can be considered the "head" of all humanity while the wicked are simultaneously members of the "body of the devil." The text transitions into Chapter VII, which challenges the contemporary view of the Roman Papacy's role in the Universal Church.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>ON THE CHURCH</header>

...earthly ones, they shall descend to the underworld. Whence **Job** [21:13] says, "In a moment they descended to the infernal regions." <note>original: "In puncto ad inferna descenderunt."</note>

<margin>Objection</margin>
But an objection is raised through **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Dominican friar and philosopher</note> concerning Christ, where he says that Christ is the head of all men—both of the faithful, who are united to him in act through grace, and of the unfaithful, who are his members only in potential. And he later subdivides this according to the **predestined** <note>those chosen by God for salvation</note> and the **foreknown** <note>original: "praescitos" — those whom God knows will ultimately be damned</note>, who, when they depart from this world, cease entirely to be members of Christ. And he resolves it by saying: regarding this testimony of St. Thomas, it seems to me that he speaks truly by saying that Christ, according to his divinity, is the extrinsic head of the whole human race, which can be called one "natural body" collectively, to which Christ does good just as he does to the whole world. According to his humanity, by the power of Christ's passion, a certain secondary perfection is acquired for the whole world; and thus, according to his humanity, he does good to the whole human race, even when he punishes all the damned—whether for their lack of faith (as in those who did not believe in the Lord Jesus Christ), or for their despair (through which they abandoned the heavenly things they ought to have aspired to), or thirdly, for their rash judgment (through which they abandoned the charity with which they ought to have finally clung to the Lord Jesus Christ).

From these things it is clear how Christ is the head of all men, and how he is the head of the predestined, and how it is not contradictory to call the **body of the devil** (which is the **synagogue of Satan**) the "church of Christ" by reason of its creation, the benefits it receives, and its preservation—but *not* by reason of a union of charity. In that sense, she is called the Church of Christ whom he loved, so that he might present her to himself as a bride without spot, to be embraced forever. However, which is the Church of Christ and which is the synagogue of Satan—whether among men or even among the angels—we shall know more fully after Christ the Lord has performed the final judgment. For he himself says in **Matthew 7**: "Enter through the narrow gate; for wide is the gate and spacious is the way that leads to perdition, and there are many who enter through it; for narrow is the gate and strait is..."

<margin>The way of Christ is narrow.</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII</header>

"...strait is the way that leads to life, and there are few who find it." On this, **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an important Early Church Father</note> says in his 18th homily: "The way of Christ is called strait and narrow because Christ does not receive into himself anyone except those who have stripped themselves of all sins, laid down the entire burden of the world, and have become refined and spiritual." These are his words. Almighty Lord, you who are the way, the truth, and the life, you know how few walk in you in these times; how few imitate you, their head, in humility, poverty, chastity, hard labor, and patience. The way of Satan is open, and many go through it; help your little flock, so that it may not desert you, but follow you through the way of hardship unto the end.

->That the Roman Pontiff with the cardinals<-
->is not the whole body of the universal church, but<-
->a part; nor is the Pope the head, but Christ. Likewise,<-
->the Roman church can be conceded to be<-
->the catholic church in a certain sense. CHAPTER VII<-

<margin>It is asked whether the Roman church is the catholic church.</margin>

Because it has been said that Christ alone is the head of the Holy Universal Church, and all the predestined (past and future) are his mystical body, and each of them is a member of that same body, it remains to see briefly if the **Roman church** is that universal holy church, the bride of Christ. And it seems that it is, because the Holy Catholic Apostolic Church is unique; but that is none other than the Roman Church; therefore, the statement is true. The first part is clear through **Pope Boniface**, who says in the *Extravagante* <note>original: "Extrauagante" — referring to the collection of canon law; specifically the bull *Unam Sanctam* issued by Boniface VIII in 1302</note>: "We are compelled by faith to believe and to hold to one holy catholic and apostolic church." And the second part is similarly clear through the same Decretal, where it is said: "Of the one and only Church there is one body and one head—not two heads, as if it were a monster—namely Christ, and Christ's vicar, Peter, and the successor of Peter; for the Lord said to Peter himself: 'Feed my sheep,' he said..." <note>original: "pasce oves meas"</note>

<summary>This page discusses Christ's role as the head of all humanity (including the wicked) and transitions into a critique of the Roman Church's claim to be the sole "Universal Church." The author argues that Christ is the only true head of the Church, challenging the idea that the Pope serves as a second head.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s examination of the nature of the Church. Having previously discussed how Christ is the head of all humanity in different ways, he now moves into a critique of the Roman institutional Church's claim to be the sole "Universal Church." He specifically engages with the legal and theological definitions of the Papacy and the "body" of the Church.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

...generally, not specifically these or those. It is clear that he [Peter] is understood to have been entrusted with all of them. Therefore, whether the Greeks or others say that they have not been entrusted to Peter and his successors, they must necessarily confess that they are not among Christ's sheep, since the Lord says in the Gospel of John: "There shall be **one fold and one shepherd**." <note>original: "Unum ovile & unum esse pastorem." John 10:16</note> Behold, from these words it appears that the holy Roman Church is that universal Holy Church, because all of Christ's sheep are one fold under one shepherd. And this is the intent of the aforementioned **Decretal** <note>A papal decree; specifically the bull *Unam Sanctam* (1302) issued by Pope Boniface VIII</note>, which concludes thus: "Furthermore, we declare, say, and define that it is absolutely necessary for the salvation of every human creature to be subject to the Roman Pontiff." <note>original: "Porro subesse Romano Pontifici omni humanae creaturae declaramus, dicimus & diffinimus omnino esse de necessitate salutis."</note> If, therefore, every man is by necessity subject to the Roman Pontiff by that definition, it follows that the proposition would be true.

On the opposite side, it is argued that the Roman Church is the church of which the Pope is the head and the Cardinals are the body, together constituting that church. But that church is not the "Holy Catholic and Apostolic Church"; therefore, the claim is a false doubt. The first part is evident through the sayings of certain doctors, among which it is stated that the Pope is the head of the Roman Church, and the college of Cardinals is truly the body. And the second part is manifest from the fact that the Pope with the Cardinals is not the **totality of all the predestined** <note>Hus argues that the true Church consists only of those God has chosen for salvation (the predestined), not just the high-ranking clergy in Rome.</note>.

For an understanding of the present matter, we must weigh that Gospel passage where Simon Peter said: "**You are the Christ, the Son of the living God.**" And Jesus answering said to him: "**Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah, for flesh and blood has not revealed this to you, but my Father who is in heaven. And I say to you, that you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it. And I will give to you the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.**" <note>Matthew 16:16–19</note> In this Gospel, Christ's church, its faith, its foundation, and its power are made known. The "Church" is there in the words: *I will build my church.* The "Faith" is there: *You are the Christ, the Son of the living God.* The Foun-

<margin>
Church.

<header>CHAPTER VII</header>

<margin>
Foundation.

-dation is there: *Upon this rock I will build.* The "Power" is there: *I will give to you the keys of the kingdom of heaven.* And regarding these four—namely, the Church, the Faith, the Foundation, and the Power of the church—we shall briefly touch upon them.

<margin>
The Church is spoken of in three ways: first, purely by name.
</margin>

Regarding the first, it can be supposed from what was declared above that, besides the Church spoken of by reputation or by name, the word "Church" is used in three ways. First, it is a certain congregation or convocation of the faithful according to a specific condition, or for a time, or purely according to "present justice." <note>Hus uses "present justice" to describe people who are currently behaving righteously but might not be chosen for final salvation.</note> In this way, the <term>foreknown</term> <note>Latin: "praesciti" — those whom God knows will ultimately be lost/damned</note> are of the church for the time in which they are in a state of grace. However, that church is not the mystical body of Christ, nor the Holy Catholic Church, nor a part of it.

<margin>
Second, in a mixed sense.
</margin>

Secondly, the Church is taken in a mixed sense, for both the **predestined** <note>those chosen for salvation</note> and the **foreknown**, while they are in grace according to present justice. And this church communicates in part, but not in its entirety, with the holy church of God. This church is called "mixed"—grain and chaff, wheat and tares, a net cast into the sea gathering every kind of fish, and a kingdom of heaven like ten virgins, five of whom were foolish and five wise, as was said above. **Tyconius** <note>A 4th-century Donatist theologian whose rules for interpreting Scripture were influential on St. Augustine</note> erroneously called this church the "bipartite body of the Lord," as is clear in the third book of *On Christian Doctrine*, chapter 32. For the foreknown are not the body of the Lord, nor a part of it.

<margin>
Third, for the predestined; and this is the truly Catholic church, an article of faith.
</margin>

But in the third way, the Church is taken for the convocation of the predestined, whether they are in grace according to present justice or not. And in this way, the Church is an **article of faith** <note>referring to the line "I believe in the Holy Catholic Church" in the Apostles' Creed</note>, of which the Apostle speaks in Ephesians 5, saying: "**Christ loved the church and gave himself up for her, that he might sanctify her, cleansing her by the washing of water in the word of life, so that he might present the church to himself in splendor, without spot or wrinkle or any such thing, that she might be holy and without blemish.**" And the Savior calls this Church *his* church in the cited Gospel passage, when he says: "**Upon this rock I will build my church.**"

<margin>
The Church of Christ.
</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the true "Holy Catholic Church" consists only of the predestined, distinguishing it from the institutional Roman hierarchy and the visible "mixed" church of both the saved and the damned. He challenges the notion that subjection to the Pope is the sole marker of being one of Christ's sheep, grounding his definition of the Church in the Gospel of Matthew and the writings of St. Paul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the identity of the Church. Building on the previous page’s discussion of the Roman Pontiff, Hus now uses the Gospel of Matthew and various legal commentaries to argue that the "Church" is not defined by institutional rank (Pope and Cardinals) but by "predestination"—the divine selection of the faithful. He begins to interpret the famous "Upon this rock" passage from a reformist perspective.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>The gates of hell shall not prevail against it.</margin>
That this refers to her <note>The Holy Church</note> is immediately clear from the words added: For it follows, *And the gates of hell shall not prevail against it.* Since Christ is the rock and the foundation of that church in which it is founded according to **predestination**, it cannot finally be overcome by the gates of hell—that is, by the powers or the assaults of persecuting **tyrants**, or of evil spirits. For Christ, the King of Heaven and the bridegroom of that church, is more powerful than the prince of this world. Therefore, to demonstrate His power, His foreknowledge, and His predestination, by which He powerfully builds, protects, foreknows, and predestines His church, and to give hope of perseverance to His church, He added: *And the gates of hell shall not prevail against it.* Regarding this, <term>Lyra</term> <note>Nicholas of Lyra (c. 1270–1349) was a famous Franciscan biblical commentator whose work Hus frequently cited for its literal and historical insights.</note> says: "From this it is clear that the church does not consist of men by reason of their power or their ecclesiastical or secular dignity, because many princes and **Supreme Pontiffs** and others of lower rank have been found to have apostatized from the faith." Thus he says. And this statement is partially evident in the case of Judas Iscariot, the apostle and bishop, who was even present while Christ said, *Upon this rock I will build my church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it.* For he himself was not built upon Christ the rock according to predestination; therefore, the gates of hell finally prevailed against him.

<margin>Peter represented the person of the universal church.</margin>
It appears, however, from the aforementioned words of the Lord, that the church is understood in a special way as all those who, after His resurrection, were to be built up in Him through faith and through consuming grace. For to Peter—who represented the person of the **universal church** and confessed this faith, *You are the Christ, the Son of the living God*—He said in praise: *Blessed are you, Simon Bar-jona.* This praise applies to Peter and to the whole holy church, which is initially blessed in its journey by confessing humbly, obediently, courageously, and constantly that Christ is the Son of the living God. Moreover, flesh—that is, worldly wisdom—and blood—that is, pure philosophical knowledge—did not reveal that faith concerning such a hidden article, but

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

but God the Father alone. Therefore, on account of a confession so clear and firm, the **Rock** <note>Christ</note> said to Peter: *And I say to you, that you are Peter*—that is, a confessor of the true Rock, which is Christ—*and upon this rock which you have confessed*, that is, upon Me, *I will build my church*—through firm faith and consuming grace—meaning, the gathering of the **predestined** who, after their labor, are ordained for glory. Therefore, *the gates of hell shall not prevail against it.*

Furthermore, from the words of the Savior it is gathered: first, that the church is **one**, where He says "church" (in the singular); second, that it is **Christ’s**, where He says "my"; third, that it is **holy**, where He says "and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it." From this it is concluded that there is one holy church of Christ, which is called <term>catholic</term> <note>original Greek: "καθολική"</note> in Greek, "universal" in Latin, and <term>apostolic</term> <note>original Greek: "ἀποστολική"</note>, because it was strengthened and founded upon the rock of Christ by the words and deeds of the Apostles, as Jerome says in his prologue to the Apocalypse. Hence, I suppose that it is called the **Holy Roman Church**. For the <term>Decretum</term> <note>The *Decretum Gratiani*, a collection of canon law that served as the legal textbook for the medieval Church.</note> says (26. d.): "Although there is one bridal chamber of Christ across the whole universal catholic church, nevertheless the holy Roman, catholic, and apostolic church has been preferred above other churches by many synodal decrees," which it proves through that passage in Matthew 16: *You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church.* And later it calls that church the **Roman Church**, the first Seat of the Apostle, *not having spot or wrinkle.* This church cannot be understood as **this Pope** with **these Cardinals** and their household. Hence, upon this text, the **gloss** <note>A standard marginal or interlinear commentary on a legal or biblical text.</note> says: "It is an argument," it says, "that wherever the good are found, there is the Roman Church." And this argument would give full confidence to the faithful in knowing where the Roman Church is. And thus the *Decretum* (23. q. 1., *A recta*) shall be understood, where the canon speaks of the Roman Church thus: "This is the holy and apostolic mother of all churches, the church of Christ, which through the grace of Almighty God is proven never to have strayed from the path of apostolic tradition, nor [to have yielded] to heretical novelties."

<margin>The Church without spot is not the pope with the cardinals.</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the true Church is the "gathering of the predestined" built on Christ as the Rock, rather than the institutional hierarchy of the Pope and Cardinals. He uses the biblical image of the church "without spot or wrinkle" to distinguish the spiritual body of the faithful from fallible church officials.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hus’s critique of the institutional Church. Having argued that Christ, not Peter, is the ultimate rock, Hus now addresses the specific claim of papal infallibility. He uses historical examples and canon law to argue that "the Church" which cannot err is the universal body of the faithful, rather than the Pope and the College of Cardinals, who are prone to human sin and error.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>The Pope can err</margin>
...succumbed to being depraved. Here it is known that this cannot be understood as referring to any particular pope and his household; therefore the <term>gloss</term> <note>A 'gloss' is a marginal or interlinear commentary on a legal or biblical text, which held significant authority in medieval scholarship.</note> speaks thus. Therefore I ask: regarding which church do you understand it said that it cannot err? For it is certain that the Pope can err, as seen in <note>original: "xix. d. Anastasius, & .xl. d. Si Papa"</note> Distinction 19, *Anastasius*, and Distinction 40, *Si Papa*. <note>These are references to the Decretum Gratiani, the primary collection of canon law, citing instances where popes were judged for heresy or error.</note> Thus, neither the pope himself nor his family is that church of which it is said here that it cannot err. Whence the gloss says: *The congregation of the faithful itself is called this church.* And thus also is understood the saying of Blessed Jerome found in Case 24, Question 1, *Hæc est fides* (This is the faith): "Holy," he says, "is the Roman church, which has always remained immaculate through the providence of the Lord and the assistance of the Blessed Apostle Peter, and in the future it shall remain without any insult from heretics, and shall persist as the first and immovable for all time." This cannot be understood as referring to every Pope with his College of Cardinals. For they are very often stained by wicked deception and sin, as

<margin>A woman was pope. In the year of our Lord 858.</margin>
in the time of Pope John, an English woman, who was called Agnes. <note>This refers to the medieval legend of 'Pope Joan,' whom Hus and many of his contemporaries believed was a historical woman who disguised herself as a man and was elected pope.</note> How then did that Roman church—that Agnes, Pope John, with her college—remain always immaculate, she who gave birth? And the reasoning is the same regarding other Popes who were heretics and were deposed because of manifold enormities.

<margin>The Roman Church has primacy, but it is not the entire mother and universal church.</margin>
Since, therefore, according to the Decretals, the Roman church has primacy and dignity before God over all others, it is clear that she is the total <term>Church Militant</term> <note>The 'Church Militant' refers to the Christian community currently living on Earth, struggling against sin and the world.</note>, which God loves more than any of its parts. And thus it clearly follows from the faith that not that college [of Cardinals], but the whole mother church dispersed among every nation and tongue, is that holy Roman church of which the Laws and the holy doctors speak. Therefore, to impress this sentiment upon us through the blessed Augustine and Ambrose, this chant was ordained for the church itself: *The holy church throughout all the world acknowledges thee.* <note>A line from the 'Te Deum', a famous Christian hymn.</note> And in the <term>Canon of the Mass</term> we offer first and foremost for the holy Catholic Church, that God might deign to grant her peace, to guard, and to unite her throughout the whole world. Whence, without doubt, prayer is made for the most principal church militant, which I suppose to be

<meta>catchword: to be</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

<margin>The Pope with his college is the chief part in the dignity of the church.</margin>
to be the Roman church. Nevertheless, among its parts in comparison of greatness, the Pope and his college are the chief part in dignity, provided they follow Christ more closely and, by abandoning pride and the ambition for primacy, serve their mother more effectively and humbly. For by doing the opposite, they are turned into an idol of desolation and into a college contrary to the humble college of the apostles and of the Lord Jesus Christ.

<margin>The Roman, Antiochian, Alexandrian, and Constantinopolitan Churches.</margin>
It must be noted, however, that the Roman church was properly called the congregation of Christ's faithful existing under the obedience of the Roman Bishop. Just as the Antiochian church was called the congregation of Christ's faithful under the Antiochian bishop. And so it is with the Alexandrian and Constantinopolitan churches. And in that manner, Peter, the Apostle of Christ and Roman bishop, names the church, saying in the last part of First Peter to the faithful of Pontus, Galatia, Cappadocia, Asia, and Bithynia: *The church that is gathered in Babylon greets you.* <note>Hus follows the tradition that 'Babylon' in 1 Peter 5:13 is a code name for Rome.</note> Behold, there 'Church' is taken to mean the faithful in Christ who were at Rome with the blessed Peter. Hence also the Apostle called particular churches in that manner, as when writing from Corinth to the Romans in the last chapter, he says: *All the churches of Christ greet you.* And afterwards: *I, Tertius, who wrote this epistle, greet you in the Lord. Gaius, my host, and the whole church, greet you.* There, 'the whole church' is understood as all the faithful in Christ who were serving with Paul in Corinth. Likewise, in the first part of First Corinthians: *To the church of God which is at Corinth, to those sanctified in Christ Jesus.* And in the first part of First Thessalonians, he says: *Paul, Silvanus, and Timothy, to the church of the Thessalonians.* And often elsewhere. Thus, they are properly called particular churches, each of which are parts of the universal church, which is the Church of Jesus Christ.

<margin>The Church began in Judea.</margin>
The Christian church, however, took its origin from Judea, and the first was called the church of Jerusalem. Whence in Acts 8 it says: *And on that day a great persecution arose against the church which was...*

He uses the legend of Pope Joan to prove that the hierarchy is fallible, concluding that "the Church" is a global community with its historical roots in Jerusalem.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s exploration of the definition of the "Roman Church." Having previously argued that the Pope is not the infallible "rock" of the Church, Hus here examines the historical and theological reasons why the universal Church of Christ came to be associated with the city of Rome, while cautioning against a purely institutional or power-based definition.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

...was in Jerusalem, and all were scattered through the regions of Judea and Samaria except the Apostles. <note>original: "erat Hierosolymis..." Hus is quoting Acts 8:1 to show that the earliest church was centered in Jerusalem, not Rome.</note>

<margin>The second church at Antioch.</margin>
The second church was Antioch, in which the Apostle Peter resided, and there that name, **Christian**, first became known. Wherefore the faithful were first called disciples and brothers, and afterward they were called Christians. For it is read in Acts 11: *Now there were Apostles and brothers who were in Judea.* And at the end of the chapter it says how Barnabas brought Paul to Antioch, and they stayed in the church for a whole year and taught a great multitude, so that the disciples were first called Christians at Antioch.

In a second way, the **Roman Church** is understood to mean whatever Pope, with whatever Cardinals, wherever they may dwell, whether living evilly or well. 

And thirdly, it is taken to mean the Pope [alone]; but these two modes have been extorted by scholars. <note>Hus argues that defining "the Church" simply as the Pope or the hierarchy is a forced interpretation used to justify institutional power.</note> For it is not a good reason to call the Roman Church our mother because of pride, or because of the favor of the Emperor who endowed the church, <note>A reference to the 'Donation of Constantine,' a document (later proved forged) claiming the Emperor gave vast lands and authority to the Pope.</note> nor because of the height of the Pope’s pomp regarding his share of the empire through primacy or lordship. Nor thirdly, so that it may be believed that every Christian must necessarily recur to him, and recognize him as the head and most holy father for the necessity of salvation; rather, it is for other reasons. For since the "Roman Church" is a term established outside of a foundation in Holy Scripture, it is enough to have a probable reason for it. 

<margin>See how much the author gives to the Roman church.</margin>
For the holy church of Christ flourished first in Jerusalem in the time of the Apostles dwelling with Christ, and afterward in Antioch, and afterward in Rome in the time of the preaching and martyrdom of Peter and Paul. And thus is understood the saying of the Savior in Matthew 12: *Truly the kingdom of God has come upon you.* And that in Luke 17: *The kingdom of God is within you*, for wherever the body is, there the eagles will be gathered. <note>Hus uses these verses to suggest that the Church exists wherever the faithful gather, rather than being confined to one geographical location.</note>

For although the Christian church began in Judea, and Christ, the head of the church, was martyred in Jerusalem, yet it is ra-

<column-break/>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII</header>
<sig>H</sig>

<margin>Three reasons why the church of Christ is called the Roman church</margin>
-tionally called the church of Christ—according to a certain preeminence—the **Roman Church**, for three reasons. First, because Christ knew the Gentiles were to be grafted in under the Roman Empire in place of the unfaithful Jews who departed, as the Apostle says in Romans 9. The second cause is because a greater multitude of martyrs triumphed there than in any other city. For just as a man takes a name from where he is born from the womb and triumphs gloriously, so it is here. Since, therefore, the holy church was born in Rome according to its many parts, gathered from the womb of the Synagogue, and triumphed there while growing among the nations, it was therefore fitting that it should take its name from the metropolis of the nations, which is Rome. Whence in <term>Distinction 22, *Sacrosancta*</term> <note>A reference to the Decretum Gratiani, the standard collection of Catholic canon law.</note>, it is said that Peter and Paul consecrated the holy Roman church on one and the same day, and preferred it above all other cities in the whole world as much by their presence as by their venerable triumph. The third cause is so that it may be known that it is not location or antiquity, but *formed faith* <note>In medieval theology, 'formed faith' (fides formata) is faith animated by love/charity, as opposed to mere intellectual belief.</note> that founds the church of Christ. For as to person and as to time, the church of Christ existed earlier in its previous seats. And in this sense it is said in 2 Maccabees 5: *The Lord did not choose the people for the sake of the place, but the place for the sake of the people.* 

<margin>The church of Christ takes names according to different places.</margin>
And from this, I believe it is permitted to call the church of Christ by the name of whatever place the just and faithful inhabit. Just as Christ is called a Nazarene because of his conception, which took place in Nazareth, and can be called a Bethlehemite because of his birth, and a Capernaumite because of the working of miracles which he performed in Capernaum, and a Jerusalemite because of his most glorious passion.

<margin>The responding conclusion.</margin>
From what has already been said, it is clear what must be said regarding the doubt raised at the beginning of this chapter. For it ought to be conceded that the Roman church is the **holy mother catholic church, the bride of Christ.**

<margin>Regarding the argument to the contrary.</margin>
To the argument to the contrary—by which it is argued that the Roman church is the church whose head is the Pope and whose body is the Cardinals—it is answered by conceding this, if one takes "church" in the second sense, namely for...

<summary>Hus outlines three reasons why the universal Church is called "Roman": the inclusion of the Gentiles, the density of martyrs in Rome, and the presence of Peter and Paul. He distinguishes this spiritual "Roman Church" from the institutional hierarchy of the Pope and Cardinals.</summary>
