--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from page 48 regarding the nature of the Church. Here, he transitions from a critique of the institutional "Roman Church" to a theological definition of the true Church as the body of the predestined, emphasizing that Christ, not the Pope, is its head. He then begins Chapter 8, which explores the nature of faith as the Church's foundation.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>BOOK II. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>Christ, not the Pope, is the head of the church.</margin>
...and the Pope, along with whatever Cardinals there may be, wherever they may live. But it is denied that this specific institutional church is the "holy catholic and apostolic church." Thus, even if both parts of the argument are granted, the conclusion is denied. And if it is said: "I posited that the Pope is holy along with all twelve Cardinals living with him," which, once posited and admitted as a high possibility, it would then follow that the Pope and his Cardinals are the holy catholic and apostolic church. Here too, the conclusion is denied. Instead, it follows logically that a holy Pope with holy Cardinals are *a* holy church, which is merely a *part* of the holy catholic and apostolic church. For the faith of Christ’s followers is firm regarding the first [the universal Church], but not the second [the specific group of leaders]. Of the first, that word of Christ is verified: *The gates of hell shall not prevail against it.* <note>original: "Portæ inferi non præualebunt aduersus eam." Quoting Matthew 16:18, a cornerstone text for defining the Church’s authority.</note> Regarding the second, however, it is uncertain to me and to any other wayfarer <note>The term "wayfarer" (viator) was commonly used in medieval theology to describe humans living on earth, traveling toward their eternal destination.</note>, unless divine revelation should enlighten someone. Therefore, neither is the Pope the head, nor are the Cardinals the whole body of the holy universal catholic church. For Christ alone is the head of that church, and his <term>predestined</term> <note>Hus followed the teachings of John Wycliffe, believing the true Church consists only of those God has chosen for salvation from the beginning of time.</note> are the body, and each individual is a member. For the bride herself is one person with Christ Jesus.

### Faith is understood in many ways, and which faith is the foundation of the Church. CHAPTER VIII

As for the second point, namely the Faith, which is touched upon in the passage: *You are the Christ, the son of the living God*, it must be noted that "faith" is sometimes used for the act of believing by which one believes. At other times, it is used for the "habit" <note>In medieval philosophy, a "habit" (habitus) is a stable disposition or quality of the soul that makes it easy to perform certain actions.</note> of believing through which one believes, and sometimes for the truth which is believed, as Augustine teaches in book thirteen of *On the Trinity*.

Secondly, it should be noted that there is one kind of faith which is the <term>explicit</term> <note>Explicit faith refers to truths a believer understands and confesses clearly.</note> belief of a faithful person. And there is another which is <term>implicit</term> faith, such as when a Catholic—having the habit of faith either infused by God or acquired—explicitly believes in the catholic church in general, and within that belief, he 

<column-break/>

<header>CHAPTER VIII</header>
implicitly believes whatever specific truth is contained under the authority of holy mother church. Similarly, by believing whatever Christ wanted to be believed about himself, and by not believing about Christ what He did not want believed, a person in this way believes every article about Christ that is believable, whether affirmative or negative. Peter held this faith implicitly while he was expressly confessing Christ to be true God and true man, saying: *You are the Christ, the son of the living God.* And yet, that same Peter explicitly contradicted Christ and his Gospel; for when Christ said, "All of you will suffer an offense because of me this night," Peter, disbelieving, said: "Even if everyone else is offended by you, I will never be offended." So too, many of the faithful believe every truth of scripture in general and implicitly; and when an unknown truth is proposed to them, they ask if it is placed in Holy Scripture, and once this is shown, they immediately grant the assent that the Holy Spirit demands.

<margin>Faith in general, shaped by charity, is enough for salvation.</margin>
Therefore, whoever holds faith <term>formed by charity</term> <note>Medieval theologians distinguished between "unformed" faith (mere intellectual belief) and faith "formed by charity" (belief given life and merit by the presence of divine love/grace in the soul).</note> in general, it is sufficient for salvation when combined with the virtue of perseverance. For God, who gave the initial faith, will give a clearer faith to his soldier, unless that soldier places an obstacle in the way. For God does not demand that all His children be continually engaged, during their earthly journey, in the particular act of thinking about every single detail of faith; rather, it is enough that, having set aside laziness, they possess faith as a "formed habit."

<margin>Unformed faith.</margin>
Here it should be known that Faith is twofold. One is "unformed," which is when "the demons believe and tremble." <note>A reference to James 2:19.</note> The other is faith formed by charity. This second kind, with perseverance, saves a person, but the first does not. Therefore, regarding the second kind, it is said in John 3: *Whoever believes in the son of God has eternal life.* And to Peter, who held and confessed that faith, the Savior said: *Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah.* And that faith is the foundation of the other virtues in which the Church of Christ dwells.

Likewise, it should be noted that since faith concerns things not apparent to the senses, but rather hidden things—and hidden things are difficult to believe—therefore...

<meta>catchword: therefore</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that Christ, not the hierarchy, is the head of the Church and explores the distinction between "explicit" faith and "implicit" faith, emphasizing that faith must be animated by love (charity) to lead to salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Head of the Church, Predestination, Explicit Faith, Implicit Faith, Faith formed by Charity, Unformed Faith, St. Peter, St. Augustine</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s theological exploration from his work *On the Church*. Having previously argued that the true Church consists only of those predestined for salvation, Hus now pivots to define "faith"—the foundational virtue of this Church. He distinguishes between the certain knowledge held by those in heaven and the "obscure" faith required of "wayfarers" (humans still on their earthly journey).</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>Faith is concerned with truth.</margin> For something to be truly believed, two things are necessary for faith. The first is truth, which enlightens the mind. The second is authority, which strengthens the soul. From this, we derive a specific property of faith: that it is concerned only with truth, to the exclusion of falsehood. A believer must defend this truth even unto death.

<margin>Faith is concerned with things unseen.</margin> The second property of faith is that, falling short of absolute demonstration or direct personal knowledge, it remains obscure to believers; for we do not say we "believe" that which we see plainly with our eyes. The saints in the fatherland <note>original: "patria." In medieval theology, "the fatherland" refers to heaven, the final home of the soul, as opposed to "the way" (earth).</note>, who clearly see those articles of faith which we know only obscurely, are not said to believe them, but to see them. In place of faith, they possess clear vision, and in place of hope, they possess perpetual fruition <note>term: "fruition" (fruitionem). This refers to the soul's ultimate, joyful enjoyment of God's presence.</note>.

<margin>Faith is the foundation.</margin> The third property of faith is that it is the foundation for the wayfarer <note>term: "wayfarer" (uiatori). A standard theological term for a human being currently living on earth, traveling toward eternity.</note> to reach the peaceful dwelling of the things believed. Therefore, the Apostle says in Hebrews 11: *Faith is the substance—that is, the foundation—of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen* <note>original: "fides est substantia... argumentum nō apparentium." A direct quote from the Vulgate Bible, Hebrews 11:1.</note>—namely, not apparent to the senses. For we hope for our blessedness and we believe in it, but we do not see it with carnal eyes. And because *without faith it is impossible to please God* (Hebrews 11), it is necessary that everyone who is to be saved must, first of all, be a believer. A believer is one who has faith infused by God, without any contrary trembling or doubt mixed into their faith.

<margin>All who live in sin are unbelievers.</margin> Furthermore, all those living in crime—according to their present injustice—are unbelievers, since it is impossible for anyone to sin mortally except to the extent that they fail in faith. For if one were to contemplate the punishment to be inflicted on such sinners, and fully believe it, and had faith in God’s knowledge—how He clearly knows all things and is present with those who sin—one would undoubtedly not sin in such a way at that moment.

<margin>One fails in faith in three ways.</margin> Now, a person can fail in faith in three ways. In the first way, through weakness: in this manner, he fails who wavers in his believing and does not persevere until death in the defense of the faith. In the second way, one fails in faith because, while he firmly believes many things that ought to be believed, he remains in many other beliefs as if in empty holes...

<meta>catchword: ...holes.</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VIII</header>

...and thus fails in faith, possessing a shield of faith that is full of holes. In the third way, someone fails in faith in the application of that shield. This happens when a person has a firm habit of believing what should be believed, yet fails in the act of meritorious work because of a disordered life. Hence it is said of such people in Titus 1: *They profess to know God, but by their deeds they deny him.* <note>original: "Confitentur se nosse deum, factis autem negant." Quoting Titus 1:16.</note> Whoever, therefore, fails in faith in any of these ways lacks the firmness of faith.

<margin>Faith differs from hope in three ways.</margin> It must also be considered that faith differs from hope. First, in this: that hope is only concerned with obtaining a future reward. Faith, however, concerns the past—such as that God created the world and that Christ was incarnated, and so on; the present—such as that God exists, that the saints are in the fatherland, and that Christ sits at the right hand of the Father; and the future—such as that Christ will come to judge, that all who have not yet risen will rise on the day of judgment, and that God will finally reward with blessedness all the saints who end this life in a state of grace. Second, hope does not reach the same level of certain knowledge as faith regarding the thing hoped for, but rests in a middle state between doubt and belief. Thus, there are many things proposed to the faithful which, setting aside specific distinctions, they ought neither to doubt, concede, nor deny, but rather to hope for. For example, if it is proposed to me, "You will be saved," I ought not to concede it (for I do not know if it is true), nor ought I to deny it (for I do not know if it is false), nor ought I to doubt it; rather, I ought to hope for it.

<margin>Many things are hoped for which should not be conceded, denied, or even doubted.</margin> Third, they differ in that hope is only concerned with a possible good for the one hoping; faith, however, is concerned with both evil and good. For we believe in the remission of sins, which is a great good for all who are to be saved; but we also believe that the sin of blasphemy shall not be forgiven, neither in this age nor in the one to come.

<margin>What it is to believe.</margin> And because "to believe" is the act of faith (which is to trust), know therefore that to believe—which is necessary for a human to reach blessedness—is to adhere firmly and without hesitation to truth as if spoken by God. For the sake of this truth, because of its certainty, a person ought to expose their life to the danger of death. And in this way, everyone is bound...

<sig>H iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...of Christ</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus defines faith as a firm adherence to truth based on divine authority, distinguishing it from the clear vision enjoyed by saints in heaven. He also contrasts faith with hope, noting that while faith covers past, present, and future truths (both good and bad), hope is specifically the expectation of a future reward.</summary>
<keywords>faith, hope, wayfarer, heaven, truth, sin, salvation, Hebrews 11, Jan Hus, theology</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s theological exploration from his work On the Church. Having defined faith as an obscure but certain foundation for the "wayfarer" (the Christian on earth), Hus now moves to distinguish between the absolute authority of Scripture and the fallible authority of the papacy, eventually defining Christ himself—rather than the Pope—as the true "Rock" and foundation of the Church.</meta>

<page-num>54, 55</page-num>
<header>BOOK ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>To what extent one should believe the word of man.</margin>
A Christian is bound to believe, either explicitly or implicitly <note>term: "explicitly or implicitly" (explicite uel implicite). In medieval logic, to believe "explicitly" is to understand a specific doctrine, while "implicitly" means to believe everything the Church teaches as a whole, even if one does not know the specific details.</note>, every truth that the Holy Spirit has placed in the Scriptures. In this way, a person is not bound to the sayings of the saints outside of Scripture, nor to believe Papal Bulls, except for what they might say based on Scripture, or what is implicitly founded in Scripture. Instead, a person may believe Bulls as a matter of opinion, because both the Pope and his Court can be mistaken due to ignorance of the truth. Indeed, it is verified of that court that "it deceives and is deceived" <note>original: "fallit & fallitur."</note>. Profit deceives the Pope, and he is deceived because of ignorance. Moreover, experience—which is the mistress of all things—teaches how one ought to believe the letters of princes, the documents of Notaries, and the stories of men. For experience itself teaches that these three often fail.

<margin>Believing God is different from believing the Pope.</margin>
Therefore, one believes God in one way, for He can neither be deceived nor deceive. One believes the Pope in another way, for he can both be deceived and deceive. Thus, one believes Sacred Scripture in one way, and a humanly devised Bull in another. For it is not permitted to disbelieve or contradict Sacred Scripture; however, it is sometimes permitted both to disbelieve and to contradict Bulls, especially when they recommend or promote the unworthy, or when they savor of greed, or magnify the unjust and oppress the innocent, or implicitly contradict the commands or counsels of God.

From what has been said, it is clear what kind of faith is the foundation of the Church, by which it is founded and built upon the rock, Christ Jesus: it is that faith by which the Church confesses that Christ is the Son of the living God. For Peter spoke for all the faithful: "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God." This, says John in 1 John 5, is the victory that overcomes the world: our faith. For who is it that overcomes the world, except the one who believes that Jesus is the Son of God?

### In what way Christ is the foundation of the church and the apostles are also its foundations. Likewise, praise of Peter for his extraordinary virtues, which his vicars ought to imitate.

->CHAPTER IX.<-

<column-break/>

### CHAPTER IX.

The "foundation," which was the third point in our proposal, was touched upon in the phrase: "Upon this rock I will build my church." And because Popes use this saying of Christ most frequently in their pronouncements—wishing to conclude that they themselves are the rock or the foundation upon which the Church stands, just as it was said to Peter, "You are Peter"—therefore, for the understanding of the word of the Lord, it must be noted that the foundation of the church *by* which it is founded is touched upon there in the word "I will build." The foundation *in* which it is founded is touched upon in "Upon this rock." And the foundation *through* which the church is founded is touched upon in "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God."

<margin>The foundation of the church: by whom, in whom, and through what.</margin>
The foundation *by* whom first and *in* whom first the holy Catholic Church is founded is Christ Jesus; and the foundation *through* which it is founded is faith, which works through love <note>original: "fides, quæ per dilectionem operatur." A reference to Galatians 5:6, emphasizing that true faith must manifest in works of charity.</note>, which Peter put forward, saying, "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God." Therefore, Christ is the foundation of the church, who says in John 15: "Without me you can do nothing"—that is, without me, the first and most principal foundation.

<margin>Christ builds the church upon himself, the rock.</margin>
Christ founds and builds his church upon himself, the rock, while he disposes her to hear and perform his words; for then the gates of hell do not prevail against her. Hence, in Luke 6, Christ says: "Everyone who comes to me and hears my words and does them, I will show you what he is like. He is like a man building a house deep, who laid the foundation upon a rock. When a flood arose, the stream beat against that house and could not move it, for it was founded upon a rock." And the Apostle Paul shows this foundation in 1 Corinthians 3, saying: "No other foundation can anyone lay than that which is laid, which is Christ Jesus." And in 1 Corinthians 10, he says: "And the rock was Christ." Therefore, in this foundation, and upon this rock, and by this rock, the holy church is founded, because he says: "Upon this rock I will build my church."

And upon this foundation the Apostles built the church...

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that Jesus Christ, rather than the Apostle Peter or any subsequent Pope, serves as the true and singular foundation of the Christian Church. Hus draws heavily on the Epistles of St. Paul and the commentaries of St. Augustine to dismantle the claim that the institutional papacy holds the same "Rock" status as Christ.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>The Apostles built the church upon Christ.</margin>
...of Christ. For they did not call the people to themselves, but to Christ, who is the first necessary and most effective foundation. Therefore, the Apostle says that *no one can lay another foundation* <note>original: "fundamentum aliud nemo potest ponere" (1 Corinthians 3:11)</note>. Thus, when the Apostle himself saw the Corinthians erring regarding the foundation, he rebuked them in the first chapter of First Corinthians, saying: *Each of you says, "I belong to Paul," or "I belong to Apollos," or "I belong to Cephas" <note>term: "Cephas." The Aramaic name for the Apostle Peter, meaning "Rock."</note>, or "I belong to Christ." Is Christ divided? Was Paul crucified for you, or were you baptized in the name of Paul?* <note>1 Corinthians 1:12-13</note>. It is as if he were saying, "No." Therefore, neither Peter, nor Paul, nor anyone else besides Christ is the principal foundation or head of the Church. Thus, in the third chapter of First Corinthians, the holy Apostle subsequently says: *What then is Apollos? What is Paul? They are the ministers of Him in whom you have believed, as the Lord has given to each to minister to the Church. I,* he says, *planted,* specifically through preaching; *Apollos watered,* specifically through baptism; *but God gave the growth,* specifically through the foundation of faith, hope, and charity. Therefore, neither he who plants, like Paul, nor he who waters, like Apollos, is anything—meaning they are not that upon which the church can be founded—but He who gives the growth, God Himself, is the foundation of the church. And it follows: *But let each one take care how he builds upon it, for no one can lay another foundation than that which is laid, which is Christ Jesus.*

<margin>The Rock.</margin>
Furthermore, this foundation is the "rock of justice" about which Christ speaks in the Gospel when saying to Saint Peter: *You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church* <note>Matthew 16:18</note>. Regarding this, Saint Augustine, in his thirteenth sermon on the words of the Lord, says: Our Lord Jesus Christ said to Peter, *You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church.* Upon this rock which you have confessed, upon this rock which you have recognized when saying, *You are the Christ, the Son of the living God,* I will build my church—that is, I will build my church upon myself, the Son of the living God. I will build you upon me, not me upon you. For those men who wished to be built upon men used to say, "I belong to Paul," "I belong to Apollos,"

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

"I belong to Cephas," who is Peter himself. But others, who did not wish to be built upon Peter, but upon the Rock <note>original: "petram." Hus and Augustine emphasize the distinction between the person Peter (Petrus) and the foundation-rock (petra).</note>, said, "I belong to Christ." So much for that. And in his final homily on the Gospel of John, Augustine speaks thus: Peter the Apostle, because of the primacy of his apostleship, acted as a representative of the community in a figurative generality. For as far as pertains to him personally, by nature he was one man, by grace one Christian, and by more abundant grace one and the same first apostle. But when it was said to him, *I will give to you the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven,* he signified the universal church. The church in this world is shaken by various temptations, like rains, floods, and tempests, yet it does not fall because it is founded upon the Rock <note>original: "Petram"</note>, from which Peter took his name. For the Rock does not take its name from Peter, but Peter from the Rock, just as "Christ" does not come from "Christian," but "Christian" from "Christ." For this reason the Lord said, *Upon this rock I will build my church,* because Peter had said, *You are the Christ, the Son of the living God.* Upon this rock, He said, which you have confessed, I will build my church. For the Rock was Christ <note>1 Corinthians 10:4</note>. Therefore the church, which is founded on Christ, received from Him the keys of the kingdom of heaven in the person of Peter—that is, the power of binding and loosing sins. For what the church is in Christ through its own property, Peter is in the Rock through representation; by this representation, Christ is understood as the Rock and Peter as the church. Therefore this church, which Peter represented, as long as it lives among evils by loving and following Christ, is delivered from evils; moreover, it follows Him most closely in those who contend for the truth even unto death. Throughout all this, Augustine teaches in harmony with the Apostle that Christ alone is the foundation and the rock upon which the church is founded. To this the Apostle Peter calls us in the second chapter of First Peter, saying: *Coming to him as to a living stone, rejected indeed by men but chosen and honored by God, you also, like stones...*

<margin>The Rock is not from Peter, but Peter is from the Rock.</margin>

<margin>Christ [is] the stone and the rock.</margin>

<meta>catchword: stones</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus uses the writings of St. Paul and St. Augustine to argue that Christ is the only true "Rock" and foundation of the Church. He interprets the famous "You are Peter" passage as Christ building the Church on Himself and the faith Peter confessed, rather than on Peter's person or office.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s theological argument regarding the true foundation of the Church. Building on the previous page's use of Pauline scripture, Hus now incorporates the testimony of the Apostle Peter himself and early medieval commentators like Remigius and Haymo to prove that Christ is the "Rock" mentioned in the Gospels.</meta>

### BOOK II. ON THE CHURCH / CHAPTER IX

As living stones, be built up into spiritual houses to offer a holy sacrifice of spiritual victims acceptable to God, through JESUS CHRIST. <note>original: "lapides uiui ſuperædificamini in domos ſpirituales..." (1 Peter 2:5)</note>

For this reason, Scripture contains: Behold, I place in Zion a chief cornerstone, chosen and precious, and whoever believes in him will not be put to shame. Therefore, honor is yours who believe; but for those who do not believe, the stone which the builders rejected has become the head of the corner, and a stone of stumbling and a rock of offense to those who stumble at the word and do not believe in that which they were set. <note>1 Peter 2:6-8, quoting Isaiah 28:16 and Psalm 118:22</note> And Paul says in Romans 9: Israel, by not following the law of righteousness, did not reach the law of righteousness. Why? Because it was not by faith, but as if by works. For they stumbled over the stumbling stone, as it is written: Behold, I place in Zion a stumbling stone and a rock of offense, and everyone who believes in him will not be put to shame. <note>Romans 9:31-33</note> Behold how these two Roman apostles and bishops, Peter and Paul, prove from Scripture that the Lord Jesus Christ himself is the stone and the fundamental rock. For in Isaiah 28, the Lord says: Behold, I will send a cornerstone into the foundation of Zion, tried, precious, and firmly established in the foundation. <note>Isaiah 28:16</note> And in the Psalms: The stone which the builders rejected has become the head of the corner. <note>Psalm 118:22</note>

<margin>Christ is the foundation of the apostles and the church</margin>
Therefore, Christ himself is the foundation of the Apostles and of the entire church, in whom the church itself is constructed. Wherefore the Apostle says in Ephesians 2: You are no longer strangers and foreigners, but you are citizens with the saints and members of God’s household, built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets, with Christ Jesus himself as the chief cornerstone, in whom the whole structure, being joined together, grows into a holy temple in the Lord. <note>Ephesians 2:19-21</note> Regarding this, Saint Remigius <note>Remigius of Auxerre (c. 841–908), a Benedictine monk and prolific commentator on the Bible.</note> says: The foundation of the apostles and prophets and of all the faithful is Christ, because they are stabilized and founded in his faith. Just as he himself said: Upon this rock—that is, upon

<column-break/>

upon me—I will build my church, which consists of angels and righteous men. For everyone who has faith in Christ is founded upon him, upon Christ Jesus himself, the chief cornerstone. Christ is the foundation and the highest stone because faith begins from him, and in him and by him faith itself is perfected and completed; therefore, all the elect are founded in him. These are the words of Remigius and Haymo <note>Haymo of Auxerre, another influential 9th-century Carolingian commentator often cited alongside Remigius.</note>.

From these things it is clear that Christ alone is the principal foundation of the church, and the Apostle weighed that foundation so heavily that he dared to speak of nothing except what was founded in Him. Wherefore he says in Romans 15: For I do not dare to speak of any of those things which Christ has not accomplished through me, in obedience to God, in word and in deeds, and in the power of signs and wonders, in the power of the Holy Spirit. Moreover, I have preached this gospel in this way: not where Christ was named, lest I should build upon another's foundation. <note>Romans 15:18-20</note> Behold, this apostle, who was a "chosen vessel," says he does not dare to speak anything except what Christ spoke through him, because otherwise he would not be founding or building upon the most effective foundation, Christ, if he said, commanded, or did anything that did not have its foundation in Jesus Christ. From which it is clear that not Peter, but the rock <note>term: "petra." The Latin word for rock, used by Hus to distinguish between the person of Peter (Petrus) and the foundation of Christ (petra).</note>, Christ, is designated in Christ's gospel when Christ says: *Upon this rock I will build my church.*

<margin>Objection: That Peter is the foundation of faith</margin>
But an objection is raised through Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 339–397), Bishop of Milan and one of the four original Doctors of the Church.</note> in the fiftieth Distinction, where he says: Peter was made more faithful after he wept for having lost his faith, and thus he found greater grace than he had lost. As a good shepherd, he received the flock to be protected, so that he who had been weak in himself might become a firmament <note>term: "firmamentum." In this context, a source of stability or a pillar of strength for others.</note> for all; and he who had changed under the temptation of questioning might found others in the stability of faith. Finally, for the solidity of the devotion of the churches, he is called a rock, just as the Lord says: *You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church.* For he is called a rock because he first laid the foundations of faith through imitation, and just as...

<summary>Jan Hus argues that Christ is the unique and primary foundation of the Church, citing the writings of Peter and Paul to show that even the Apostles viewed Christ as the "Cornerstone." He then addresses a counter-argument from St. Ambrose which suggests Peter might be considered a foundation through his role as a shepherd.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from the previous chapter. He is currently resolving a potential conflict in church tradition: how both Peter and Christ can be called the "rock" or "foundation" of the Church. He contrasts the views of Saint Ambrose and Saint Augustine to conclude that Christ is the primary foundation, while the Apostles are secondary foundations whose authority supports the faithful.</meta>

60
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: Foundation.</meta>

an immovable rock, might hold together the structure and mass of Christ's entire work.
<margin>Solution.</margin> These are the words of Ambrose, showing why Peter is called a rock.
Here I say that the exposition of Augustine, that most excellent interpreter of Scripture, seems to be—and indeed is—more effective, because it is founded on the explicit words of Scripture. Scripture says that Christ is the rock and the cornerstone, and the most effective foundation. Nowhere, however, is it read expressly in Scripture that Peter is the rock; nor did Christ, who could easily have said it, say: "You are the rock, and upon you, the rock, I will build my church." Instead, he said: "You are Peter [Petrus]," <note>original: "Tu es Petrus"</note> that is, the confessor of the true rock. And "upon this rock [petram] which you have confessed, I will build my church." <note>Matthew 16:18. Hus highlights the linguistic shift from the name 'Peter' (Petrus) to the word for 'rock' (petra).</note>

<margin>The church is built upon Christ through faith, hope, and charity.</margin> Moreover, Christ builds the church upon himself through faith, hope, and love. Hence we believe and hope in Christ, and not in Peter; and we ought to have greater love or affection for Christ than for Peter. For in this way the fathers of the Old Testament did not believe or hope in a future Peter, but in the Rock. Similarly, the saints of the New Testament do not believe or hope in Peter, but in Christ, who is the objective focus of our faith and hope.

<margin>The Apostles are foundations of the church, but in a different way than Christ.</margin> It must, however, be granted that the Apostles are foundations of the church, but not in the same way that Christ is the foundation. For he himself is the Foundation of foundations, just as he is also the Holy of Holies. Hence Blessed Augustine, [commenting] on that Psalm, *Its foundations are in the holy mountains*, <note>Psalm 87:1</note> shows that the most principal foundation of the New Jerusalem—the city of Zion—is Christ the cornerstone, and the "mountains" are the prophets and Apostles in whom the foundations of the Church exist. He says: "You should know that Christ is the first and greatest foundation." "No one," says the Apostle [Paul], "can lay any other foundation than that which is laid, which is Christ Jesus." <note>1 Corinthians 3:11</note> How then are the Prophets and Apostles foundations, and how is Christ Jesus the foundation, beyond which there is nothing further, as we believe? For just as he is openly called the Holy of Holies, so figuratively [he is called]

<column-break/>

61
<header>CHAPTER IX</header>
<sig>I iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Second.</meta>

the Foundation of foundations. Therefore, consider the sacraments: Christ is the Holy of Holies. If you consider the flock subject to him: Christ is the Shepherd of shepherds. If you consider the building: Christ is the Foundation of foundations. And afterward, showing the reason why the Prophets and Apostles are called foundations of the structure of the city of Jerusalem, [Augustine asks]: Why are the prophets and Apostles foundations? Because their authority carries our weakness. Why are they gates? Because through them we enter; yet we enter through Christ, for he himself is the door. And the twelve gates of Jerusalem are mentioned, and Christ is the one gate, and Christ is the twelve gates, because Christ is in the twelve gates. This is Augustine's view. And the gloss on that word in Revelation 21, *The wall of the city having twelve foundations*, <note>Revelation 21:14</note> says: "that is, the prophets, in whose faith the Apostles were founded; for from them faith passed on to the Apostles, and that same preaching of the Apostles gained its credibility from the prophets, who said the same thing." Or let us understand the Apostles as the foundations in whom the entire fortification of the church is founded. Likewise in the same place: *The foundations of the wall of the city were adorned with every precious stone, and the first foundation was jasper.* <note>Revelation 21:19</note> The gloss says: "foundations," that is, the prophets or Apostles adorned in themselves with every kind of virtue.

<margin>How Christ is the foundation of the church, and how the apostles are foundations.</margin> Behold, from these things it is clear how Christ is the foundation of the church, and how the Apostles are foundations: Christ by authority, for the constitution of the church begins from him, is finished in him, and is completed through him. The Prophets and Apostles, however, are foundations because their authority carries our weakness. And Ambrose also intends that meaning in his saying, stating that Peter is called a rock for this reason: that he was the first to lay foundations for those who would imitate the faith, and—as an immovable stone, namely by his final constancy—he might hold together the structure and mass of Christ's entire work. The foundation, however, by which the church is founded in Christ is the faith which Peter confessed, saying: "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God." And of this foundation Paul speaks in 1 Corinthians 3.

<summary>Jan Hus argues that while the Apostles (and Peter specifically) can be called "foundations" or "rocks" because their authority supports the weak, Jesus Christ remains the primary, unique foundation of the Church. He uses Augustine's linguistic analysis of Matthew 16 to show that the Church is built on the faith Peter confessed, rather than on the person of Peter himself.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Hus, De Ecclesia, Saint Augustine, Saint Ambrose, Apostle Peter, foundation of the church, petra vs petrus, Christ as cornerstone, New Jerusalem, authority of the apostles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the true foundation of the Church. Having previously discussed St. Augustine’s view that the "Rock" mentioned in the Gospels is Christ himself rather than Peter’s person, Hus now examines how the Apostles built upon this foundation and addresses traditional titles given to Peter by Church Fathers and Popes.</meta>

62
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: sheep</meta>

According to the grace that was given to me, as a wise architect I have laid the foundation, specifically by teaching the faith of Christ. And he adds: Another, however, builds upon it, meaning they perform good works upon that faith. But let each one see how they build upon it, namely, their spiritual life in Christ. For he adds: For no one can lay any other foundation except that which has been laid, which is Jesus Christ. But if anyone builds upon this foundation gold, meaning the doctrine of the deity and celestial things; silver, meaning the doctrine of humanity and created things; or precious stones, that is, the virtues adorning the soul and its powers—that person is without doubt founded in Jesus Christ. The Apostles built in this way, while they fervently and clearly taught the doctrine of the deity, the humanity, and the virtues, and living in the flesh, they planted Christ’s church with their own blood. But as to which of them built more upon Christ, and planted the church in Christ, when we come to our homeland <note>The "homeland" (patria) refers to Heaven in medieval theology.</note> by the Lord’s granting, then we shall know without a doubt. It is conceded, however, that Peter received from the "Rock" of the church—which is Christ—his humility, poverty, firmness of faith, and consequently, his blessedness.

But regarding that word of the Gospel, "Upon this rock I will build my church," <note>Matthew 16:18</note> that Christ intended to build the entire militant church <note>The "militant church" refers to the Christian community currently living on Earth, struggling against sin.</note> upon the person of Peter—the faith of the Gospel combined with Augustine’s exposition and reason itself speak against this. For Christ was to build his church upon the Rock, which is Christ, from whom Peter received his firmness; since Christ is the head and foundation of the whole church, but Peter is not.

<margin>
James and Peter, the summits <note>original: "akrotas"</note> of the most ancient theologians. <note>original Greek: Ἰάκωβος καὶ πέτρος κορυφαῖα τῶν πρεσβυτάτων θεολόγων ἀκρότας.</note>
</margin>

But on the contrary, the Blessed Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential early Christian mystical writer.</note> in his book *On the Divine Names*, chapter 3, calls the blessed Peter the "vertical" one, that is, the chief or the Captain. And in the booklet he wrote *On the Death of the Apostles Peter and Paul*, he addresses Titus thus: When they were being separated from each other, led to the places of execution, Paul addressed Peter in these words: "Peace be with you, the foundation of the churches, and the shepherd of the sheep...

63
<header>CHAPTER IX</header>
<meta>catchword: Whence</meta>

<margin>
Note also the mention of James and Peter.
</margin>
...of the sheep and lambs of Christ." Likewise, Augustine in *On Questions of the Old and New Law* says that Peter was the first among the Apostles. Likewise, in the Canon Law [23. q. 1. *Rogamus*], Pope Marcellus speaks thus: "We ask you, brothers, that you teach nothing else than what you have received from the Blessed Peter and from the rest of the Apostles. For he himself is the head of the whole church, to whom the Lord said: You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church." Likewise, in distinction 21, chapter *In novo*, Pope Anacletus says: "In the New Testament, after Christ the Lord, the priestly order began from Peter, because to him first the pontificate in the church of Christ was given, the Lord saying to him: 'You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church.' He, therefore, was the first to receive from the Lord the power of binding and loosing, and the first to lead the people to the faith by the power of his preaching." Yet the other Apostles received honor and power in equal fellowship with him. Likewise, it is commonly said: "That Peter was the head of the church because he is called Cephas, which is interpreted as 'head.'" <note>Hus is addressing a common medieval false etymology that linked the Aramaic "Cephas" (Rock) to the Greek "Kephale" (Head).</note>

<margin>
Peter is not the head of the church, but the captain of the apostles.
</margin>
Supposing those things which we said in chapters 1, 2, 3, and 4—that there is only one holy universal church, which consists of all the predestined who are to be saved, and that Christ alone is the head of that church, just as he alone is the most worthy person in that church, bestowing upon it and its members movement and sense into the life of grace—then it is manifest that Peter was not, nor is he, the head of the holy catholic church. And the saying of the Blessed Dionysius is true, that Peter was the captain among the Apostles, and was the foundation of the churches, as was recently said of the Apostles. And Augustine’s saying is also true, that Peter was first among the Apostles according to a certain prerogative. And Marcellus’s saying is true, that Peter was the head of the whole church which he ruled by doctrine and example. He was not, however, a person more worthy than the Mother of Christ, nor equal to Christ, nor was he appointed the ruler of the angels, who at that time constituted the triumphant church. <note>The "triumphant church" refers to the saints and angels in Heaven.</note>

->Whence<-

<vocab>Petrus (Peter), Christus (Christ), Ecclesia (Church), fundamentum (foundation), Dionysius, Augustinus (Augustine), Marcellus Papa (Pope Marcellus), Anacletus papa (Pope Anacletus), Cephas, capitaneus apostolorum (captain of the apostles)</vocab>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that while Peter held a primary "captaincy" among the Apostles and served as a foundation for local churches, he is not the "head" of the universal Church, a title reserved solely for Christ. Hus reconciles papal decrees and patristic writings by interpreting Peter's leadership as one of example and administrative priority rather than ontological headship over all the predestined.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from Chapter IX regarding the leadership of the Church. Having established that Christ is the true foundation or "Rock," Hus now clarifies that while Peter held a position of leadership, he was not the "Head" of the Church in the same sense as Christ, nor did he claim such a title for himself.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH
## CHAPTER IX

<sig>K</sig>
<meta>catchword: Christ, Peter</meta>

<margin>Peter did not dare to assert himself as the head of the church.</margin> Therefore, it is not a matter of great doubt to a simple believer that Peter did not dare to assert that he was the head of the holy catholic church, because he neither ruled over the whole of it, nor did he excel so much in dignity, nor was he the spouse of that same catholic church. Even John the Baptist—about whom, according to the testimony of Truth in Matthew 11, "None has arisen greater among those born of women"—did not dare to call himself the spouse of the church, but instead humbly confessed himself to be its friend. Thus, in John 3, when his disciples were jealous on his behalf and said: "Rabbi, he who was with you across the Jordan, to whom you bore witness, look, he is baptizing and everyone is going to him." John answered and said: "A man cannot receive anything unless it has been given to him from heaven; you yourselves bear witness to me that I said, 'I am not the Christ,' but that I was sent before him. He who has the bride is the bridegroom. It is enough for me that I am the friend of the bridegroom, standing and hearing with joy the voice of the bridegroom." This is also what he says in John 15: "You are my friends if you do what I command you." And so it is clear that it would be too great a presumption and foolishness for any man other than Christ to call himself the head or the spouse of the holy catholic church.

However, the reason Christ appointed Peter as the captain and pastor after him was the preeminence of his virtues for ruling the church. Otherwise, the Wisdom of the Father <note>A traditional title for Christ, the second person of the Trinity.</note> would have acted without foresight in appointing him as the bishop of his church. And since all moral virtues are generally connected, it is clear that Peter had a certain preeminence in every kind of virtue. There were, however, <margin>Three virtues excelled in Peter. The first was faith.</margin> three virtues in which Peter excelled: Faith, humility, and charity <note>Charity (caritas) in this context refers to divine love.</note>. Faith, which must be the foundation of the church, excelled first in Peter. For this reason, the Great Teacher ordained in Matthew 16 that, regarding the question he asked about himself—"Who do men say the Son of Man is?"—Peter answered for them all. He said: "You are the Son of the living God." Here he confesses the humanity in Christ, by which he signified that Christ

<column-break/>

was the Messiah promised to the fathers. And the second part of his answer confesses Christ as the natural Son of the living God; thus, Peter confesses Christ to be both true God and true man. Among all the articles of faith, this one is most relevant to the building up of the church. For according to the Blessed John in 1 John 4 and 5, the Son of God overcomes the world. "Who is it," he asks, "who overcomes the world, except the one who believes that Jesus Christ is the Son of God?" For once this foundation is held, as much as it is believed, everything that Christ did or taught must be accepted by the whole church without objection. Therefore, Peter heard from the Lord: "Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah, for flesh and blood has not revealed this to you, but my Father who is in heaven." And from this faith, Peter received the burden of the church's leadership. "And I say to you," he said to the Rock <note>Hus emphasizes that Christ is the "Rock" (Petra) speaking to Peter (Petrus).</note>, "that you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church." Therefore, because of this, the Vicars of Peter and the overseers of the church are bound to preach the faith of the church. Thus, in Luke 22, the Savior says: "I have prayed for you, that your faith may not fail; and once you have turned back, strengthen your brothers." Thus, praying for his faith, he was heard because of his reverence (as in Hebrews 5). Secondly, the Lord enjoined upon him the primacy <margin>The office of primacy is enjoined upon Peter.</margin> of office: "I will give you, after my death, the keys of the kingdom of heaven," that is, of the church, which I will strengthen against the "church of the wicked," giving you the power of binding and loosing, so that you do not occupy the keys of the church in vain. I gave these to you by reason of your meritorious confession of my humanity and deity, by which you said, being taught in a heavenly manner by the Father: "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God." Therefore, by reason of such a firm and profound confession, in John 1 he is called *Cephas*, which is interpreted as Peter. Thus, a man skilled in sacred languages <note>original: "Hieroperitus linguarum," likely referring to St. Jerome.</note> says that *Cephas* is interpreted as "Peter" or "firmness," and it is Syriac, not Hebrew. From this, the solution to the final objection is clear. For *Cephas* does not mean "head" (as some interpret the Gospel and Jerome), but *Cephas* is interpreted as "stone" or "Peter." <note>Hus is correcting a common medieval pun where "Cephas" was wrongly linked to the Greek word "Kephale" (Head). He clarifies it is Aramaic/Syriac for "Rock".</note>

Regarding the second virtue, which is humility, since Peter... <margin>The second virtue of Peter: humility.</margin>

<summary>Hus argues that Peter never claimed to be the "Head" of the Church, a title reserved for Christ alone, and explains that Peter's leadership was based on his preeminent virtues of faith, humility, and love. He also clarifies the linguistic origin of the name "Cephas," debunking the idea that it means "head" to prove that Peter's authority was that of a firm foundation, not a replacement for Christ's headship.</summary>

Here, he begins to enumerate the specific virtues—humility, charity, poverty, and patience—that qualified Peter for his leadership role, contrasting these with the conduct of the contemporary papacy.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: this</meta>

...Peter heard from his master in Matthew 11: "Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart." <note>original: "Discite a me, quia mitis sum & hu. corde."</note> And in the 20th chapter of the same book: "Whoever wants to be great among you, let him be your minister; and whoever wants to be first among you, shall be your servant." <note>original: "Quicũq; uoluerit inter uos mã. fieri, sit mi. ue. & qui uo. inter uos pri. esse, erit ues. ser."</note> He heard the same in Matthew 22, Mark 10, and Luke 22: that he should not be above others except in the sense of being more humble, having received this particular privilege from the Lord Christ Jesus. Whence it is reasonably said that Peter questioned and answered humbly, just as he humbly offered himself before the others to perform the ministry of the church. For in Acts 8, being sent to Samaria by the apostles, he went humbly with John. Likewise in Acts 8 <note>Hus likely intends Acts 9:38-43</note>, being called to Joppa, he went humbly, and there for many days he stayed with Simon the Tanner. <note>Staying with a tanner, a trade considered "unclean" by many, was a sign of Peter's humility and break from strict Jewish ritualism.</note> Likewise in Acts 10, called by Cornelius from Joppa to Caesarea, he went humbly. Likewise in Acts 15, in the Council of the Apostles and the church, after his own sermon, when James was defining the matter and saying: "Hear me; Simon has related..." etc. And it follows: "Because of this I judge that those who are converted to the Lord from the Gentiles should not be troubled." <note>Hus emphasizes that Peter submitted to the judgment of James at the first church council.</note> Likewise in Acts 9, it is held how Peter went through all parts, namely preaching the word of God humbly. Likewise, when he was sharply rebuked by Paul in Galatians 2, he endured it humbly. Moreover, he practiced all these things not for the sake of worldly honor or advantage, but humbly, obediently, and for the sake of upholding the honor of the law of Christ. Whence in these laws, the greatness of the apostle Peter must be measured according to the humility of his service, as is clear from the definition of the master in Luke 14 and 16, and Matthew 23, saying: "He who humbles himself shall be exalted." <note>original: "Qui se humiliat exaltabitur."</note>

<margin>The third virtue of Peter: charity.</margin>
As for the third virtue, which is charity, it is clear that he possessed it in a certain way above the others, as is evident from the fervor of his works, which must proceed from a greater love. And this is confirmed by the fact that Peter would otherwise be too ungrateful, unless he loved his master—who loved him so singularly, wiping away his great blasphemy and graciously setting him over his sheep—with a correspondingly greater love. And thirdly, it is confirmed by this:

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
### CHAPTER IX
<sig>K ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: given</meta>

this: that otherwise the Master, in the last chapter of John, would have asked pointlessly: "Simon, son of John, do you love me more than these?" and immediately committed his sheep to him to be fed.

<margin>Multiple reasons for loving Christ.</margin>
But it should be noted here that there are multiple reasons for loving Christ. For some love Christ more than others do, but by reason of his divinity—as is believed of John the Evangelist. Some love Christ no less according to the reason of his humanity—as is believed of Philip. And some according to the reason of his body, which is the church; and so for many other reasons, for which they say of any saint that the word of Ecclesiasticus 44 is verified: "There was not found one like him who kept the law of the Most High." And Peter's preeminence is clear by reason of his faith, humility, charity, and indeed his poverty and patience. Regarding poverty: for in Acts 3, he said to the man asking for alms: "Silver and gold I have none; but what I have, this I give to you." And because he heard from the Master in Luke 21: "In your patience you shall possess your souls," it seems probable that Peter, following his denial of the Master, stood more ready to suffer martyrdom; especially because, recognizing his own weakness from that denial, he had a fresher memory of his own fragility. Because of this, he condescended humbly to others and was readier to suffer imprisonment even unto death for the Lord he had denied. There is no doubt that he humbly endured Herod’s prisons in Jerusalem, the prisons of Theophilus in Antioch, and Nero’s prisons in Rome. <note>Theophilus of Antioch refers to a legendary figure from the Clementine literature, a governor who supposedly imprisoned Peter before being converted.</note>

<margin>The patience of Peter.</margin>

<margin>The Vicar of Peter ought to walk in the ways of Peter.</margin>
If one called the Vicar of Peter walks in the ways of the virtues just mentioned, we believe that he is his true vicar and the chief Bishop of the church which he rules. But if he goes in contrary ways, then he is the messenger of the Antichrist, contrary to Peter and to the Lord Jesus Christ. Whence the blessed Bernard, in his fourth book to Eugenius, writes thus: "Among these things, you, the Pastor, proceed gilded, surrounded by so many [riches]..." <note>Hus cites St. Bernard of Clairvaux’s "De Consideratione," a famous 12th-century treatise warning Pope Eugenius III against the corrupting influence of wealth and power.</note>

<insert>the gilded pope</insert> <note>original: "papa d'auratꝰ"</note>

<vocab>Petrus (Peter), Ioannes Euangelista (John the Evangelist), Philippus (Philip), Bernardus (Bernard of Clairvaux), Vicarius Petri (Vicar of Peter), humilitas (humility), charitas (charity), paupertas (poverty), patientia (patience), Antichristus (Antichrist), Roma (Rome), Antiochia (Antioch)</vocab>

<summary>Hus outlines the virtues of Peter—humility, charity, poverty, and patience—citing numerous biblical examples to argue that apostolic authority is based on service rather than worldly power. He concludes by stating that a Pope is only a true Vicar of Peter if he emulates these virtues; otherwise, he is an agent of the Antichrist.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from Chapter IX of De Ecclesia. On the previous page, Hus established that Peter’s leadership was defined by service and humility rather than worldly rank. Here, he cites Saint Bernard of Clairvaux and other Church Fathers to argue that a Pope who lives in luxury succeeds the Emperor Constantine rather than the Apostle Peter.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
# I. H. ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: Likewise</meta>

...given by variety. What do the sheep receive? If I dared to say it, these are pastures for demons rather than for sheep. This is not how Peter sanctified, nor how Paul played. <note>original: "ludebat" - Hus uses this to contrast the serious ministry of the apostles with the perceived frivolity or lack of seriousness in the contemporary clergy.</note> And it follows: Either deny you are a shepherd to the people or show yourself to be one. You will not deny it, lest you deny that you are the heir to the one whose seat you hold. This is Peter, who is not known to have proceeded adorned with gems or silks, nor covered in gold, nor carried on a white horse, nor surrounded by soldiers, nor with attendants clamoring around him; yet without these things, he believed he could sufficiently fulfill the saving command: "If you love me, feed my sheep." <note>John 21:17</note>. In these [worldly] things, you have succeeded not Peter, but Constantine. <note>Hus refers to the "Donation of Constantine," a document—later proved to be a medieval forgery—that claimed the Emperor Constantine granted the Pope temporal power and imperial regalia. Hus argues this wealth is the root of church corruption.</note> So says Bernard. That saint knew that Pope Eugenius ought to be a vicar in poverty and humility, not in pride, but in the feeding of the sheep, following Peter. For a man is a true vicar of the one whose place he holds, and from whom he legitimately receives delegated power.

<margin>Peter, the highest of the high pontiffs, walked without pomp.</margin>

<margin>He who truly acts in the place of Christ</margin>

But no one truly and acceptably to Christ acts in the place of Christ or Peter unless he follows him in character, since there is no other relevant following, nor does he receive delegated power from God under any other condition. And therefore, for such an office of vicar, both a conformity of character and the authority of the institutor are required; and to this person the Savior, commending the institution of the venerable Sacrament at the Last Supper and appointing his disciples as <term>vicars</term> <note>A "vicar" is someone who acts in the place of another; here, Hus argues the title requires acting like the person being represented.</note> for this purpose—that they should do so in his memory—said in John 13: "I have given you an example, so that just as I have done to you, you also should do likewise." And in Matthew 11: "He who does and teaches, this one shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven." <note>This citation is actually Matthew 5:19.</note> Wherefore on this point Saint Jerome says to Heliodorus, and it is placed in the *Decrees*, distinction 40: It is not easy to hold the chair of the saints Peter and Paul, who reign with Christ, because of this saying: "They are not the sons of the saints who hold the places of the saints, but they who perform their works." And Blessed Gregory says the same: It is not places or ranks that make us close to our Creator, but either our good merits join us to Him, or our bad ones separate us.

<column-break/>

<page-num>69</page-num>
# CHAPTER IX
<sig>K iij</sig>

<margin>Many are priests in name, few in work.</margin>

Likewise Chrysostom: Many are priests, and few are priests; many in name, and few in work; see therefore how you sit upon the chair, because it is not the chair that makes the priest, but the priest the chair; it is not the place that sanctifies the man, but the man the place. Not every priest is holy, but every saint is a priest. He who sits well upon the chair does honor to the chair; he who sits badly does injury to the chair. Therefore, a bad priest acquires a crime from his priesthood, not dignity. So says he. Likewise, in the same place from the deeds of Boniface the Martyr, it is said thus: If the Pope, negligent of his own and his brothers' salvation, is found to be useless and slack in his works, and moreover silent—which is most harmful to himself and the sheep—he nonetheless leads innumerable people in crowds with himself to the first slave-pen of Gehenna <note>original: "geennæ" - a reference to hell or eternal punishment.</note>, to be beaten with many stripes for eternity along with him. Nor is it necessary to cite many saints, since the High Pontiff, the Saint of Saints, says in John 10: "All who have come [before me] are thieves and robbers." And in Matthew 5, he says to the disciples: "You are the salt of the earth; but if the salt loses its flavor, with what shall it be salted? It is good for nothing further, except to be thrown out and trampled by men." And in Luke 14 he says: "Salt is good, but if it has lost its flavor, with what shall it be seasoned? It is useful neither for the soil nor for the manure pile, but it shall be thrown out." And wishing to impress this judgment of the most pious Savior and best teacher upon the minds of men, he immediately added: "He who has ears to hear, let him hear." Let every priest see, therefore, if he has entered well, being clean from crime, with a sincere intention for the honor of God and the benefit of the church, and if he conducts himself worthily, holding worldly honors and the gain of the age in low esteem; otherwise, he is a liar, an Antichrist, and the higher his office, the greater an Antichrist he is. Let the humble traveler look to Christ, who says in John 14: "I am the way, the truth, and the life." Behold, he who wishes to go has a way, because Christ is the way. Where he wishes to go, Christ is the truth, and where he wishes to remain, Christ is the life.

<summary>Hus argues that the legitimacy of a Pope or priest depends on their imitation of Christ's humility and poverty rather than their official title or "chair." He warns that a corrupt leader does not inherit the authority of Peter but instead leads the faithful toward spiritual ruin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from the previous chapters regarding the nature of the papacy and the clergy. Having previously argued that the Pope must follow Peter in humility rather than Constantine in worldly power, Hus now moves into a systematic definition of "power" (potestas) to explain what Christ actually gave to Peter and the priesthood.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
# E. II. ON THE CHURCH

### Concerning the manifold power of men, and what kind of power was conferred on Peter and all priests in these words: "Whatever you bind on earth," etc. CHAPTER X

Now we must speak concerning the power of Christ, which was handed down to his Vicars, and which is touched upon in that passage: *I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven* <note>original: "Tibi dabo claues regni cœlorum" — Matthew 16:19</note>, which is to say, the power of binding and loosing sins. This is how Augustine describes it in his final inquiry on the Gospel of John. The effect of this power is shown when Christ adds: *And whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.*

<margin>Spiritual power.</margin>
And because the aforementioned power is a spiritual power, it should be noted that spiritual power is the power of the spirit, ordained immediately by itself so that a rational creature may be directed according to the goods of grace. Moreover, every human being is a spirit, since they consist of two natures <note>Meaning the physical body and the spiritual soul.</note>, as the Savior speaks in Luke 9, saying to the disciples: *You do not know what kind of spirit you are.* And in 1 John 4 it is said: *Every spirit that dissolves Jesus is not from God* <note>original: "Omnis spiritus qui soluit Iesum" — usually translated as "every spirit that does not confess Jesus." Hus interprets this as the spirit of a subtle heretic.</note>; in that context, "spirit" refers to a subtle heretic who denies that Jesus is true God and true man. Thus it is clear that whether power is considered in relation to God and rational creatures, or restricted to the powers of humans and angels, it is true that every spiritual power is a power of the spirit. And although a man does not "give" grace himself, he nevertheless administers the sacraments so that the subject may be directed toward the goods of grace.

<margin>Bodily power.</margin>
Bodily power, on the other hand, although it exists for the sake of the goods of grace, is immediately concerned with the ordering of God's creature according to natural goods or the goods of fortune <note>Wealth, status, or physical health.</note>. And thus it seems that every human has
<meta>catchword: has</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>71</page-num>
# CHAPTER X 71

has a dual power, since every human ought to have power over the actions of their own limbs, and for that reason has the power of "traveling" <note>Hus uses the term "viandi," referring to the "viator" or the person on the journey of life toward God.</note> in grace. Therefore, spiritual power admits a manifold subdivision, since some is the Power of Order, and some is Common Power.
<margin>Manifold spiritual power.</margin>

The Power of Order <term>Power of Order: The specific authority granted to a priest through the sacrament of Holy Orders.</term> is called that spiritual power which a Cleric has for administering the Sacraments of the church, so that he may be spiritually useful to himself and to the laity; such is the power of consecrating <note>Performing the Eucharist.</note>, absolving, and administering other sacramentals. For the power of consecrating is ordained by itself and immediately so that the priest may consecrate, just as habits of moral virtue are ordained for the sake of better habitual acts; and since the priest is directed according to the goods of grace so that he may consecrate worthily, the above definition is clear.

Common Spiritual Power is that power which every parishioner has in exercising spiritual works, both in themselves and in others, which are remembered in this verse: *Teach, counsel, reprove, comfort, forgive, endure, pray.* <note>original Latin mnemonic: "Doc. consul. castig. solare remitte fer ora" — These are the seven Spiritual Works of Mercy.</note> For as many as received Christ through faith, he gave them the power to become children of God, so that it might be permitted for them to direct themselves and their brothers in the way of their father, Christ, and by correcting them charitably, as is clear in Matthew 18: *If your brother sins against you,* says Christ, *reprove him between you and him alone.*

<margin>Dual secular power.</margin>
Secular power is twofold, namely civil and common. Civil authoritative power belongs only to a civil lord. But civil "vicar" power belongs to officials or ministers. Common secular power, however, is the power by which a human can direct themselves and their own according to the goods of nature and fortune. And thus, just as a human cannot be whole without soul and body, nor an adopted son of God without the goods of nature and grace, so it is not possible for a "traveler" <note>viatore</note> to exist unless they have both common secular and common spiritual power. In infants and the dead, however, this power is bound.

<margin>Spiritual power is everywhere more perfect.</margin>
Spiritual power, however, is everywhere more perfect, and the priestly power itself exceeds royal power in terms of dignity, because according to
<meta>catchword: according to</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus defines and subdivides different types of power—spiritual, bodily, civil, and priestly—arguing that while all people possess a "common" spiritual power to do good, the priestly "Power of Order" is a specific sacramental authority that exceeds secular royalty in spiritual dignity.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual power, secular power, Power of Order, sacraments, works of mercy, priesthood vs. kingship, binding and loosing, Jan Hus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument in Chapter X of De Ecclesia, where he is defining the nature of spiritual power. In the preceding section, he began explaining the "keys of the kingdom" given to Peter, transitioning here into a comparison between the authority of the priest and the authority of the king.</meta>

# ON THE CHURCH [left] ->CHAPTER X<- [right]
L
<meta>catchwords: lis [left], bent [right]</meta>

...according to the Apostle in Hebrews 7: *The one who blesses is greater, and the one who is blessed is lesser.* <note>original: "Maior est qui benedicit, & minor qui benedicitur." — Hebrews 7:7</note> From this, it follows that the spiritual power of the priest exceeds royal power in antiquity, dignity, and utility. It exceeds in **antiquity** because, at God’s command, the priesthood was instituted first, as is clear in Exodus 28. Afterward, through the priesthood and at God’s command, the royal power was instituted, as is clear in Deuteronomy 17 and 1 Samuel 12. It exceeds in **dignity**, as has been said, because as the greater party, the priest blesses, consecrates, and anoints the king. Its greater **utility** is evident from this: spiritual power is sufficient by itself to rule the people, as is clear from the case of Israel, which was healthfully governed without royal power until the time of Saul. Therefore, since spiritual power concerns the highest goods and is sufficient in itself, it exceeds earthly power, since the latter is worth nothing without spiritual power principally directing it. But on the contrary, spiritual power can be sufficient by itself without the earthly; and for this reason, priests who abuse such a high power through pride or some other crime fall more deeply into Gehenna <note>Gehenna: a term for Hell or eternal punishment.</note> with the devil, according to the rule of Saint Gregory and other saints: *The higher the status, the heavier the fall.* <note>original: "Quanto status altior, tanto casus grauior."</note>

And it must be noted that "power" refers now to the absolute capacity for ruling, and now to that same capacity combined with authentic notification or promulgation.

<margin>All power is from God.</margin>
And it is clear that it is not contradictory to say that there is no power except from God, and yet it is another thing for someone other than God to "give" power—that is, to provide an authentic promulgation in the face of the Church that a created person holds this kind of power from God. Indeed, such a combined status is given by man in part, but only if God principally authorizes it.

And from this, it can be further conceived that power is not expanded or constricted, increased or diminished, regarding its **essence**, but rather regarding the **execution of the work** that proceeds from it; and this is permitted to be done, but only if there is a reasonable cause 

<column-break/>

in the eyes of God. This is the sense explained by the Canon Law <note>original: "Canon. xxiij. q. pri, Miramur" — refers to the Decretum Gratiani, a collection of Church law.</note>: "It is one thing," it says, "to have the power of office, and another to have its execution." For the most part, in the case of monks and others, the power of office is retained, just as in the case of those who are suspended: while their ministry is forbidden, their power is not taken away. In a similar sense, it is granted that natural power—which is **free will**—can now be expanded through grace and now restricted. In this way, the apparent disagreements among the Doctors <note>Doctors: highly authoritative Christian theologians.</note> are resolved; some of them, like Anselm, say that free will cannot be lost, increased, or diminished. Others, however, like Augustine in his *Enchiridion*, say that free will can be lost through sin and increased by grace.

<margin>The power of office and its execution differ.</margin>
Regarding the granting, withdrawal, or restriction of power, there is a great dispute in the Church. However, it is known that when God and reason compel a man to act for the benefit of the Church, then—and only then—does God give, withdraw, or restrict this power. Hence, when Christ says to Peter, *I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven* <note>original: "Tibi dabo claues regni cœlorū"</note>—the power of binding and loosing sins—He spoke to the entire Church Militant <note>Church Militant: the body of Christians living on earth, as opposed to those in heaven or purgatory.</note> in the person of Peter. This does not mean that every person in that Church holds those keys indifferently, but that the whole Church holds those keys according to its individual parts that are fit for this purpose. Furthermore, those **keys** are not material objects, but spiritual power and the knowledge of Gospel science; it is because of this power and knowledge that Christ is believed to name them "keys" in the plural. Hence, the Master of the Sentences <note>The "Master of the Sentences" is Peter Lombard, whose textbook was the standard for theological study in the Middle Ages.</note> says in Book 4, Distinction 18, Chapter 2: "He says 'keys' in the plural because one is not enough. These keys are the science of discerning and the power of judging, by which the ecclesiastical judge must receive the worthy and exclude the unworthy from the kingdom."

<margin>There is a dispute regarding the granting, restriction, etc., of power.</margin>
<margin>What the keys of the church are.</margin>
And it must be noted that although the Trinity alone possesses this kind of authentic and principal power, the humanity of Christ alone possesses this kind of "sub-authentic" principal power from within, since Christ is simultaneously God and man. Nevertheless, the prelates of the Church possess...

<margin>Spiritual power is held differently by God, Christ’s humanity, and Church prelates.</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues for the superiority of spiritual power (the priesthood) over secular power (kingship), basing his claim on historical precedence in the Bible and the spiritual nature of the "keys of the kingdom." He distinguishes between the essence of a priest's power and its "execution," noting that while humans may grant the office, only God grants the true authority.</summary>

In the preceding section, he compared priestly and royal power, arguing that the spiritual is greater in antiquity and utility.
