# I. H. ON THE CHURCH [left] ->CHAPTER X<- [right]
<page-num>74</page-num>
<meta>catchword: tia</meta>

...they possess the instrumental or ministerial power entrusted to them, which is a **judicial power** <note>original: "potestas iudiciaria"</note> consisting principally of two things: namely, the power of investigative knowing <note>original: "arbitrarie cognoscendi" — the ability to examine a case or a conscience.</note> and the power of judicial judging. The first of these, in the forum of penance, is called the **key of conscience**, serving as a preparatory stage for the second, more principal power. This is because no one may lawfully deliver a definitive sentence unless they first have the power to know the cause upon which the judgment is made. Therefore, the first "key" is not merely an act or habit of knowing, but the power to conduct such an investigation beforehand.

Thus, every power of the priestly order to instrumentally open the "door"—which is Christ—to a person, or to close said kingdom to a subordinate, is the **key of the church** given to Peter and the others. This is clear in Matthew 18, where the Savior says: *Amen, I say to you, whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.* <note>Matthew 18:18</note> And in John 20: *Receive the Holy Spirit; whose sins you forgive, they are forgiven them, and whose sins you retain, they are retained.* <note>John 20:22-23</note> And in Matthew 16, it was said to Peter, and to the church through him: *Whatever you bind...* etc. <note>Matthew 16:19</note> Because of a lack of understanding, these words terrify many Christians, causing them to fear in a slavelike manner, while others are deceived by them, presuming a **fullness of power** <note>original: "plenitudine potestatis" — a technical term often used to describe the absolute authority claimed by the Papacy.</note>.

Therefore, it must first be understood that the Savior's statement is necessarily true in its literal sense, because it is impossible for a priest to loose or bind anything unless that loosing or binding occurs in heaven. This refers not just to the "heavenly body" that contains the sublunary world and everything in it, but to the approval of God and the angelic nature, which constitute the "heavens."

It should be noted that in the soul of one who sins mortally, there is guilt, and grace is seized or ceases to exist. Because of this, the mortal sinner is bound by the debt of eternal damnation if they do not repent. While remaining in this guilt, they are separated from the company of those living in grace...

<column-break/>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X</header>
<sig>L ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: contra</meta>

<margin>Penance as the remedy for sin</margin>
...separated. However, they can find a remedy in **penance**, through which guilt is deleted, grace is conferred, the bond of damnation is broken, and the human being is reunited with the church. This penance is completed through **contrition, confession, and satisfaction**. <note>These are the three traditional parts of the Catholic Sacrament of Penance.</note> Contrition, which is sadness or full grief for the sin committed, must include a dislike for the sin—both what was done and what might be done. In a moment of necessity, this alone would be enough for a person’s salvation. Hence, the Savior, knowing that the mind of the adulteress was in sorrow in John 8, enjoined the second part upon her, saying: *Go, and sin no more.* <note>John 8:11</note> On this point, Saints Augustine, Ambrose, and Gregory agree when they say that "to repent is to bewail the evils committed, and to not wish to commit again what must be bewailed."

<margin>Infinite power is required for the justification of the ungodly.</margin>
Secondly, it must be noted that the **justification of the ungodly** <note>original: "iustificationem impij" — the theological process by which a sinner is made righteous before God.</note> requires infinite power, by which God purges the soul of its stain and grants grace. Secondly, it requires God's mercy, by which He releases the offense against His majesty and the eternal punishment that would be due if the person did not repent. For this reason, the church frequently prays: *Almighty and merciful God...* <note>original: "Omnipotens & misericors deus"</note> which acknowledges God’s infinite power and mercy. That infinite power is required for the justification of the ungodly is clear, because according to **Augustine**: *It is easier to create the world than to justify the ungodly.* <note>A famous quote from St. Augustine’s Tractates on the Gospel of John.</note> If the first requires infinite power, then so does the second. The reason is that the justification of the ungodly requires the active giving of the Holy Spirit, which can belong to no one but God, as Augustine proves in many places (as I have noted in my discourse on Indulgences). And the **Master of the Sentences** <note>Peter Lombard, whose "Sentences" was the standard medieval theology textbook.</note> concludes in Book 1, Distinction 14, from Augustine's words: "Therefore, men—no matter how holy—cannot give the Holy Spirit." The same logic applies to the active remission of sins.

<margin>Man does not forgive sins, but God does.</margin>
Hence, the Baptist spoke uniquely of Christ in John 1: *Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world.* <note>John 1:29</note> Regarding this, Augustine says in his *Homilies on John* (Homily 4): "Let no man claim for himself and say that he himself takes away the sins of the world." Now pay attention to those who...

<summary>Jan Hus defines the "power of the keys" as a ministerial role where the priest acts as God's instrument, emphasizing that only God possesses the infinite power required to actually forgive sins and justify a sinner.</summary>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH
<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: sins</meta>

...against those proud ones toward whom John [the Baptist] pointed his finger; they were not yet heretics, and yet they were already being pointed out when he cried out against them from the river. Hence the Jews often charged Christ with blasphemy for this reason: because they judged him to be a mere man <note>original: "purum hominem"</note>—though this was false—and they said he could not simply forgive sins, because a sin is not simply forgiven unless the offense against God is released. But who forgives an injury except the one to whom it was done or to his own subordinate? For God, in granting this kind of power, would first forgive the injury against Himself before His **Vicar** <note>In this context, the priest acting in Christ's name.</note> would forgive it. Regarding this, **Ambrose** <note>St. Ambrose of Milan, a 4th-century Father of the Church.</note> says: "He alone forgives sins who alone died for us." The same Word of God forgives sins; the Priest is the judge, and the priest indeed performs his office, but he exercises no path of [independent] power. This is found in the Decree on Penance, Distinction 1, under the heading 'The Word of God' <note>original: "de pœni. diſt. j. Verbū dei" — a reference to the Decretum Gratiani, a collection of Canon Law.</note>. **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome, the 4th-century translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> is of the same opinion, as cited by the **Master of Sentences** <note>Peter Lombard, whose *Sentences* was the standard theological textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> in the place mentioned above, as are Gregory [the Great] and Paul. And the reasoning is the same regarding the retention and binding of sins. Hence the Master [Lombard] in Book IV, Distinction 18, Chapter 4, concludes from the cited authorities and reasons: "Therefore, God alone cleanses a man inwardly from the stain of sin and from the debt of eternal punishment." He then concludes the section thus: "By these and many other testimonies it is taught that God alone through Himself forgives sins, and just as He forgives some, so He retains the sins of certain others."

<margin>Question</margin>
But someone will say: if God alone can forgive or retain sins, why then did He say to the Apostles and their vicars, "Whatever you loose," etc., and "Whose sins you retain," etc.? <note>Matthew 16:19 and John 20:23.</note> What, then, is it for a priest to loose or bind sins, to remit or retain them?

<margin>Solution.</margin>
To the first question, the Master of Sentences responds in the place cited above, saying: "Priests also bind when they impose the **satisfaction** <note>The penance or corrective actions performed by a penitent.</note> of penance upon those confessing; they loose when they remit something of that satisfaction, or through it admit those who have been purified to the communion of the sacraments." To the second question, **Richard** <note>Richard of Saint Victor, a 12th-century mystical theologian.</note> speaks beautifully in his booklet on the *Power of Binding and Loosing*. For he says: "What is it to remit

<column-break/>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X</header>
<sig>L iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: sti</meta>

sins? It is nothing other than to absolve by relaxing the sentence of severity which is owed for sins, and in absolving, to release it. And what is it to retain sins, but to refuse to absolve those who are not truly repentant? For many of those confessing seek absolution who nevertheless do not wish to entirely abandon their crimes. Many promise caution for the future but do not wish to make satisfaction. All such people, because they do not truly repent, undoubtedly ought not to be absolved. For this is to truly repent: to grieve for past transgression with a firm purpose of confessing, making satisfaction, and guarding oneself with all caution. Those who repent in this manner deservedly ought to be absolved, and otherwise, they are dismissed without absolution—and this is to retain sins."

<margin>What it is to truly repent.</margin>
Behold, from these things already said, we can clearly understand in the resolution of sins what the Lord does through Himself, what He does through His minister, and what He does through Himself together with the office of His ministers. Through Himself alone, He certainly looses the bond of stubbornness; through Himself and His minister, He looses the debt of eternal damnation; and through the minister, He looses the debt of future purgation. For He reserves the power of the first resolution to Himself alone; the second resolution He performs through Himself together with His ministers. However, the third the Lord is accustomed to perform not so much through Himself as through the minister. It is rightly said, then, that the Lord absolves the true penitent from the bond of damnation. It is nonetheless rightly said that the priest does this along with the Lord: the Lord indeed works toward the conversion of the heart, and the priest toward the confession of the mouth. For the confession of the heart alone suffices for the salvation of the soul for one who is truly repentant; however, the "point of necessity" <note>original: "articulus necessitatis" — a legal and theological term for an emergency situation where a priest is unavailable.</note> excludes the confession of the mouth and the absolution of the priest. Thus says Richard.

<margin>How God and the minister absolve the sinner</margin>
From what has been said, it is held that God, who predestines eternally, first completes the absolution of the one to be saved in time, and the remission of their sin, before that person is absolved by a minister of the church here on earth. It is evident, secondly, that a minister of Christ's church cannot...

<margin>God absolves before the priest.</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the power to forgive sins belongs primarily to God, who cleanses the soul inwardly before any priestly action. He defines the priest's role as "ministerial," focusing on imposing penance and admitting the repentant to the sacraments rather than possessing the ultimate power of salvation.</summary>

The text emphasizes that if God has not already "loosed" a soul in heaven, the priest’s words on earth are ineffective.</meta>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH
<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: but</meta>

...of Christ <note>original: "sti Vicarius"</note> to absolve or bind sins, to remit or retain them, unless God does so first, is clear through that passage in **John 15**: *Without me,* he says, *you can do nothing.* The "vessel of election" <note>A traditional title for St. Paul, referring to Acts 9:15.</note> knew this, which is why he says in **2 Corinthians 3**: *Not that we are sufficient to think anything of ourselves, but our sufficiency is from God.* If, therefore, we are not sufficient even to think unless God has created that thought, how are we sufficient to bind or loose unless He Himself has first bound or loosed? And the **Philosophers** <note>Referring to Scholastic thinkers who blended Aristotelian logic with Christian theology.</note> recognize this, saying that no **secondary cause** <term>secondary cause: a created being or force that acts only because it is moved by God, the "Primary Cause"</term> can act without the cooperation of the **primary cause**. And it is further clear that a person cannot be loosed from sin, nor receive the remission of sins, unless God has loosed them or given them remission. Hence **John the Baptist** said in **John 3**: *A man can receive nothing unless it has been given to him from heaven.* Therefore, just as the eternal Lord first forgives the sin committed against Him in the soul before He allows this to be proclaimed by Himself or through another, so it is necessary for God to act.

<margin>The priest binds or looses nothing unless Christ has first bound or loosed it.</margin>
Thus, those priests are madly insane <note>original: "uesane insaniunt"</note> who think or say that they loose or bind at their own whim <note>original: "ad suum uotum"</note> without the prior absolution or binding of Jesus Christ. For the binding or loosing of God is simply first. Therefore the Gospel says that what is bound on earth shall be bound in heaven, but it does not say that it is bound in heaven *afterward*, rather than *before*. Hence the ignorant think that the priest binds and looses first in time or in the moment, and God follows after him; it is foolishness to believe this. But **Logicians** know well that there are two types of Priority: namely, the **Priority of Origin**, taken from the **material cause**, and the **Priority of Dignity**, taken from the **final cause**. And these two priorities commonly concur at the same time, and thus the binding or loosing by the **church militant** <note>The church on earth, currently "fighting" against sin.</note> is prior in a certain way to the binding or loosing of the **church triumphant** <note>The church in heaven.</note>, and vice versa. But the binding or loosing of God is simply first, and it is clear that it would be blasphemy to assert that a human remits an offense made against so great a Lord, with the Lord Himself merely "approving" it. From the uni-

<column-break/>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X</header>
<meta>catchword: hypo-</meta>

-versal sovereignty of this Lord, it is necessary that He Himself first absolves or binds if any **Vicar** <note>A representative, such as a priest or the Pope.</note> does so. No other article of faith should be more certain to us than this: that it would be impossible for anyone of the church militant to absolve or bind, except insofar as he conforms to the Head of the Church, our Lord Jesus Christ.

<margin>Christ does not always bind or loose what the Pope binds or looses.</margin>
Hence the faithful person must beware of this saying: "If the Pope or another claims to loose or bind by any sign, then by that very act the person is loosed or bound." For whoever grants this must consequently grant that the Pope is **impeccable** <term>impeccable: incapable of sinning or making a moral error</term>, and thus is God; otherwise, he could err and act out of alignment with the **Key of Christ**. And it is established that just as it is impossible for the shape of a physical key to open a lock if the substance of the key is missing, so it is impossible for Christ’s Vicar to open or shut unless he conforms to the Key of Christ, which first opens or shuts. For just as Christ—the firstborn among many brothers and the firstfruits of those who sleep—first entered the kingdom, so He alone could as the Head earn the spiritual kingdom, which was universally shut by the transgression of our first parents until His coming; and so it must be said of every particular opening or shutting regarding the heavenly homeland. And this is also clear in those things which concern the faith and the **keys of the church**, as those who study the **Chronicles** know, since even **Peter**, the first Vicar of Christ, sinned in this way.

Likewise, it is God alone who cannot be ignorant of to whom sin should be remitted, and He alone is the one who cannot be moved by a perverse affection or judge unjustly. But any Vicar can be ignorant of to whom sin should be remitted, and can be moved by a perverse affection to bind or loose; therefore, if a priest, moved by anger or greed, refused to apply absolution to one who was truly penitent and confessing, he would not by that act bind that person in their guilt. Similarly, suppose that in another case someone approaches confession deceitfully, as happens very often, and the priest, being ignorant...

<summary>Jan Hus argues that priests and Popes have no independent power to forgive sins; they only act effectively when their judgment mirrors God’s prior decision. He warns against the "blasphemous" idea that human ministers can compel God’s grace through ritual acts alone.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues Hus’s argument regarding the limits of the clergy's power to forgive sins. In the previous section, he established that God is the primary actor in forgiveness; here, he argues that a priest's "keys"—the power to bind and loose—are ineffective if the person is a hypocrite or if the priest's judgment contradicts God's truth.</meta>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...hypocrisy, and [the priest] applies the words of absolution, then without a doubt he does not absolve, as the Scripture says in **Wisdom 1**: *The holy spirit of discipline will flee the deceitful.* <note>original: "Sanctus spiritus disciplinæ effugiet fictum"</note> Behold, in the first case the Vicar <note>Vicar of Christ, a title here referring generally to the priest or Pope acting as Christ's representative.</note> pretended to bind or remit sins and did not; and in the second case he pretended to loose or remit and did not. It is clear what a great illusion occurs, both on the part of those applying the keys and on the part of those who do not truly repent. For it is necessary that one who wishes to be absolved first be disposed in his will, so that he grieves for his fault and intends to sin no more. Thus, all the priests and Vicars together cannot absolve a person from a crime if that person intends to sin criminally and does not wish to grieve for the sin.

<margin>The righteous cannot be bound.</margin>
Just as all of them together cannot bind a righteous person or retain their sins while that person is humbled with their whole heart and has a contrite heart, which God does not despise. Hence **Saint Jerome** <note>Jerome (c. 347–420 AD) was a Church Father and translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> on that passage in **Matthew 16**—*I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven; whatever you loose on earth will be loosed in heaven, and whatever you bind, etc.*—says that some, not understanding this passage, take on something of the arrogance of the **Pharisees**, <note>A Jewish sect described in the Gospels, often used by Hus as a metaphor for legalistic or hypocritical clergy.</note> thinking they can condemn the innocent or loose the guilty, whereas with God it is not the sentence of the priests but the life of the accused that is sought. To these words the **Master of the Sentences** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose book "The Sentences" was the standard theology textbook for centuries.</note> adds in Book 4, Distinction 18, Chapter 6: *Here it is clearly shown that God does not follow the judgment of the church when it judges through deception or ignorance.* And he adds in Chapter 8: *For sometimes he who is cast out (that is, outside the holy Church by a priest) is actually inside; and he who is outside according to the truth appears to be kept inside through the false judgment of the priests.*

<margin>The keys must be used cautiously.</margin>
And in Distinction 19 of Book 4, he says that a priest who binds and looses should be discerning and just; otherwise, he often kills souls that do not die and gives life to those who do not live. In this way, he falls under the judgment of the curse, as found in **Malachi 2**: *I will curse your blessings, and I will bless your curses.* <note>original: "Maledicā benedictionibus uestris, & maledictionibus benedicā."</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X</header>

...Therefore, the **Vicars of Christ** must be careful not to presume rashly to loose or bind whatever they wish.

<margin>Objection.</margin>
But an objection is raised via the Canon *Solitæ*, regarding seniority and obedience, where **Pope Innocent** <note>Pope Innocent III (r. 1198–1216), who strongly asserted the legal and political authority of the Papacy.</note> says: "The Lord said to Peter, and in Peter he said to his successors: 'Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven.'" Innocent says that by saying "Whatever," he excluded nothing.

<margin>Solution. Peter could not loose many things.</margin>
Here it must be noted that after that statement "Whatever you loose," **Peter** could not loose <term>loose: in this context, to unbind, annul, or break</term> the Scripture, because Christ Jesus our Savior said in **John 10**: *The Scripture cannot be broken.* Second, he could not loose one who was unwilling to repent; hence he said to **Simon** in **Acts 8**: *Repent of this wickedness of yours and pray to God, if perhaps it may be forgiven you.* Third, he could not loose a marriage, because the Savior said in **Matthew 19**: *What God has joined, let no man separate.* Fourth, he could not loose **Judas** from sin, because the Savior said: *None of these perished except the son of perdition, that the Scripture might be fulfilled.* If, therefore, Peter had pretended—by that saying of Christ "Whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven"—that he was able to loose the four things mentioned above, would they have been loosed in heaven? Certainly not. For the will of God would stand in the way regarding Scripture, marriage, and Judas; and in the case of the person unwilling to repent, their own stubbornness and the will of God would stand in the way.

<margin>How the words of Christ "Whatever you loose," etc., are to be understood.</margin>
Therefore, it does not follow that because this Vicar thinks he looses or binds whomever he wishes, he actually does so. Regarding this, **Saint Augustine** <note>The influential 4th-century Bishop of Hippo.</note> speaks as follows in *On True and False Repentance*: "God offered **Lazarus**, whom He had already raised from the dead in the tomb, to the disciples to be loosed, thereby showing the power of loosing granted to priests. He said: 'Whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.' That is: I, God, and all the ranks of the heavenly host, and all the saints praising in that glory, confirm with you those whom you bind and loose. He did not say 'those whom you *think* you bind and loose,' but those 'in whom you perform works of justice or mercy...'"

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the spiritual authority of priests and Popes is limited by God's truth and the spiritual state of the believer. He contends that an unjust or ignorant judgment by a priest—such as absolving an unrepentant person or condemning an innocent one—is invalid in the eyes of God.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Hus, De Ecclesia, Vicar of Christ, absolution, power of the keys, repentance, St. Augustine, St. Jerome, Peter Lombard, Pope Innocent III, church law vs divine law</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from the previous section, specifically focusing on the "power of the keys" (binding and loosing sins). Hus is quoting traditional authorities like Saint Augustine and Richard of Saint Victor to prove that a priest's spiritual power is not absolute or arbitrary, but must align with God's justice.</meta>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH
->82<-

...of mercy; other works of yours among sinners I do not recognize. <note>original: "alia autem opera vestra in peccatores non cognosco." Hus is quoting Saint Augustine to show that God only recognizes priestly actions that align with His mercy and justice.</note> Thus says **Augustine**, stripping away the empty reputation of clerics in the matter of loosing or binding. Likewise, **Richard of Saint Victor**, in his book *On the Power of Binding and Loosing*, speaks thus: But you still persist and say, "If I cannot bind and likewise absolve whomever I wish, or remit the sins of whomever I wish, or retain them, what does it mean when it is said generally to **Peter**, *Whatever you bind, whatever you loose*? <note>original: "Quodcunque ligaveris, quodcunque solveris." This refers to Matthew 16:19, the biblical basis for priestly authority.</note> Just as it is proposed generally, which is said to all the apostles in common, *Whose sins you remit, they are remitted to them; whose sins you retain, they are retained.*" <note>original: "Quorum remiseritis, remittuntur eis, quorum retinueritis retenta sunt." From John 20:23.</note> This question would rightly trouble you if the Lord had said to Peter, "Whatever you **wish** to bind shall be bound, and whatever you **wish** to loose shall be loosed." But He does not say this, nor did He wish it to be understood that way. If someone wishes to bind what they cannot bind, will it therefore be bound? Who would say this? Therefore, He does not say "whomever you wish to bind," but "whatever you bind shall be bound in heaven." That person is truly bound who, according to the character of their own confession, is held by a just debt of satisfaction. <term>Satisfaction</term> <note>In the medieval church, "satisfaction" refers to the penance or actions performed to show true repentance after confessing a sin.</note> That person is truly absolved by the priestly office whose sin is justly remitted through worthy satisfaction; therefore, the priest binds and absolves those who justly deserve to be absolved by the priest's sentence. Their sins are undoubtedly retained—not those to whom absolution is denied unjustly, but those to whom it is denied **justly**. Therefore, what the Lord says to Peter carries the same weight as if He said in other words, "What has been bound and loosed by you shall be bound and loosed with me. He who is constrained by you through the imposition of a due satisfaction is held by me as a debtor of that same satisfaction. And he who has earned from you a due absolution for his sins shall not be able to be further restricted by my judgment in this regard." In this same way, we must also understand what He says to all the apostles: *Whose sins you remit, they are remitted to them, and whose sins you retain, etc.* For indeed, the sins of those who transgress against the Lord will be remitted or retained when they are justly remitted or retained by His ministers, the priests; and both are then truly done by them when they proceed toward any

<meta>catchword: execution</meta>

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER X
->83<-

execution according to **Canonical order**; <note>The "Canonical order" refers to the established laws and traditions of the Church, implying that a priest cannot simply make up his own rules.</note> but they can do neither of these according to their own whim, but only according to merit and the instituted order. Thus says **Richard**.
<margin>There is a difference between "whatever you loose," etc., and "whatever you wish to loose."</margin>
And it is clear from the declaration of **Augustine** and **Richard** himself that it does not follow that because Christ said to Peter, or to any of his Vicars, "Whatever you loose on earth (that is, in the **Church Militant**) shall be loosed also in heaven (that is, the **Church Triumphant**)," <note>The "Church Militant" refers to Christians currently living on earth, while the "Church Triumphant" refers to those in heaven.</note> that therefore "whatever you **wish** to loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven." And it is clear that in the first case, the distribution applies to any truly repentant person. And there, in "Whatever you bind," the distribution applies to the unrepentant; for absolution pertains to any truly repentant person, and binding pertains to the unrepentant. And the logic is the same regarding retaining and remitting.

Therefore, the disciple of Christ must beware of the **Fallacy of the Antichrist** when he argues in this way: "Whatever the Vicar of Christ binds on earth shall be bound also in heaven; but he binds this faithful layman who is unwilling to give him money for absolution; therefore, he is bound in heaven." Similarly, when he argues: "Whatever the Vicar of Christ looses on earth shall be loosed also in heaven; but he looses this unrepentant man who is willing to give money; therefore, he is loosed in heaven." Similarly, if it is argued: "Whatever the Vicar of Christ looses on earth shall be loosed in heaven; but he looses this man—noting he is one of the **foreknown**—in the agony of death; <term>foreknown</term> <note>original: "praescito." In Hus's theology, the "foreknown" are those God knows will ultimately be damned, regardless of earthly priestly rituals.</note> therefore, he is loosed also in heaven." In these arguments, the minor premise is faulty in its substance; for unless the said man has bound himself through an evil will, or loosed himself through true contrition, the minor premise is false. And according to **Richard**, the argument should be rectified thus: "Whatever the Vicar of Christ binds **justly** on earth shall be bound also in heaven. But he binds this faithful layman who is unwilling to give money." Behold, the minor premise is false. The other arguments are rectified in the same way.
<margin>Objection</margin>
And if it is objected that a Christian ought to doubt when a priest binds or looses rightly and when he does not, it is said that the opposite follows...

<meta>catchword: [followed by]</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus uses the writings of Augustine and Richard of Saint Victor to argue that a priest's power to forgive or retain sins is not absolute; it only mirrors God's judgment if the priest acts "justly" and according to the sinner's true repentance. He warns against the "Fallacy of the Antichrist," where corrupt priests claim to bind or loose people based on the payment of money rather than spiritual merit.</summary>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's argument regarding the "power of the keys." He is demonstrating that a priest's or pope's power to forgive or retain sins is not absolute or arbitrary, but is valid only when it aligns with God's own judgment. He uses logical syllogisms to refute the idea that a pope can bind or loose anyone at will.</meta>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH
->84<-

...it is said, however, we must believe that he only binds or looses when he ministers according to the rules of Christ’s law; and when he deviates from this path, he may *claim* to bind or loose, but he does not actually do so. Then, regarding the statement of **Innocent** <note>Pope Innocent III (r. 1198–1216), a powerful pope who asserted supreme papal authority.</note>, "The Lord said to **Peter**, excepting nothing: *Whatever*," <note>original: "Quodcunque." Refers to Matthew 16:19: "Whatever you bind on earth will be bound in heaven."</note> if **Innocent** understands this "whatever" to mean that Peter or his vicar binds anything they *pretend* to bind, then **Innocent’s** meaning would be false. For then, by logical deduction, an absurdity would follow, arguing as follows: *Whatever Peter or his vicar binds on earth shall be bound in heaven. But he pretends to bind this holy man on earth; therefore, the man is bound in heaven.* The conclusion is false and impossible, and the minor premise is true (or can be true); therefore, the major premise, according to **Innocent’s** sentiment, would be false.

However, if **Innocent** conceives this "whatever" as **Richard**, **Augustine**, and **Gregory** <note>Hus is citing major Church authorities: Richard of St. Victor, St. Augustine of Hippo, and Pope Gregory the Great.</note> do—as referring only to that which is a valid loosing or binding—then it is true what the Lord says: *Whatever*, excepting nothing. For this holds true: *Whatever you loose on earth for a truly penitent person shall be loosed also in heaven.* Similarly: *Whatever you bind on earth for an unrepentant person shall be bound also in heaven.* And this is what the brief glosses of the **Decretals** <note>The collected laws of the Catholic Church.</note> suggest when they say: **When the key does not err** <note>original: "Clave non errante." A legal phrase meaning the priest's spiritual judgment is correct and aligns with God’s law.</note>; and consequently, as long as it is holy on earth, it will be confirmed in heaven. For whoever is rightly penitent and is loosed on earth by Christ’s vicar on earth is also loosed in heaven. Just as "he who believes and is baptized shall be saved." This means he who believes with love and perseverance. For "to believe" is understood there just as it is in John 3: *He who believes in the Son of God has eternal life.*

Hence, if it is argued: *Whoever believes in the Son of God has eternal life, and every Christian believes in the Son of God, therefore every Christian has eternal life.* Or again: *Whoever believes in the Son of God has eternal life; but this **foreknown** person <term>foreknown: "praescitus," a person who, despite currently appearing holy, God knows will ultimately be lost.</term> who is in a state of grace believes in the Son of God; therefore, this foreknown person who is in a state of grace has eternal life*—the conclusion is

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER X.
->85<-

false. Therefore, both of those logical sequences are invalid because "to believe" is used in one way in the first premise and in another way in the second. Thus, to clarify the intent, the argument should be stated this way: *Whoever believes with love and perseverance in the Son of God [has eternal life]*; then the conclusion will be valid, but the opponent must prove the second premise. Likewise, in the second logical sequence, let the substitution be made thus: *But this foreknown person who is in grace believes with love and perseverance in the Son of God*—now, this statement is false.

From what has been said, it is clear what the **power of the keys** of the church is. It must be believed as a Catholic truth that every priest of Christ, rightly ordained, has sufficient power to confer any sacraments belonging to him, and consequently to truly absolve a contrite person from sin—even though this power, regarding its execution, is reasonably "bound" (restricted) in many cases, as was shown near the beginning of this chapter. Moreover, that this power was equal among the **Apostles** is found in Distinction 21, in the Canon titled *In Novo*, where it is said: "The other Apostles received the same fellowship of honor and power as Peter." <note>original: "pari consortio, honore & potestate." A significant claim by Hus that the Pope is not inherently superior to other bishops in spiritual authority.</note> And further down: "In their place, the **Bishops** arose as their successors." Where the gloss says: "It is an argument that every Bishop is equal to an Apostle in terms of order and the nature of consecration." And in Distinction 23, Chapter 1, **Saint Cyprian** <note>An early Church Father and Bishop of Carthage (d. 258 AD).</note> says: "To all the Apostles after His resurrection, He gave equal power."

Hence, it would be foolish to believe that the Apostles received no spiritual gift from Christ except what was simply derived to them from Peter. For He said to all in Matthew 18: *Whatever you loose on earth.* And in John 20: *Receive the Holy Spirit; whose sins you remit, they are remitted to them.* And in Luke 22: *Do this in memory of me.* And in the final chapter of Matthew: *All power is given to me in heaven and on earth; go therefore and teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, teaching them to observe all things whatever I have commanded you; and behold, I am with you.*

<margin>
The Apostles were equals, therefore all priests and bishops are equals.
</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that priestly authority (the "power of the keys") is only valid when it reflects God's truth, rather than the priest's personal will. He uses canon law and church fathers to assert that all bishops and priests possess the same spiritual authority given to the original Apostles.</summary>

Having previously argued that a priest's power is not absolute but dependent on God's will, Hus now turns to the specific ways the clergy selectively read the Bible to justify their own authority while ignoring the duties of humility and service that Christ commanded.</meta>

<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<note>original: "uobiscum sum omnibus diebus usq; ad consummationem seculi."</note>...I am with you always, even to the end of the age. <note>Matthew 28:20.</note>

### How the clergy twists scripture to their own side and abuses the power granted to them.

->CHAPTER XI<-

Because many priests have neglected the imitation of Jesus Christ, the high priest, they make a display of the power handed down to the Church. Therefore, more must be said about this kind of power. For they squeeze out <note>original: "emungunt." Hus uses a vivid verb meaning to "wipe the nose" or "wring out," suggesting the clergy are forcing a meaning out of the text that isn't there.</note> from that passage in **Matthew 16**: <note>original: "Quodcunq; ligaueris supra terrã, erit ligatum & in cœlis."</note> *Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound also in heaven,* the idea that whatever they themselves have done, every person ought to totally approve. And from that saying of Christ in **Matthew 23**: <note>original: "Supra cathedram Moysi sederunt scribæ & pharisæi, quæcunq; dixerint uobis facite."</note> *The scribes and Pharisees sit on the chair of Moses; whatever they say to you, do it,* they squeeze out the claim that every subject must obey them in all things. And so, these priests clamorously claim for their own glory whatever sounds favorable to their desires in Christ’s Gospel, without providing any corresponding ministry of charity. But whatever sounds like labor, worldly rejection, or the following of Jesus Christ, they either despise or pretend to hold while they actually do not. 

Hence, because Jesus said to **Peter**: <note>original: "Tibi dabo claves regni coelorum."</note> *I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven,* and *whatever you bind on earth...* etc., they take those words very pleasantly for the sake of exalting their own power. But what the Lord said to **Peter** in **John 21**: *Follow me, and feed my sheep,* they flee from that as if it were poison. Similarly, what he said to the disciples in **Matthew 18**: *Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound also in heaven,* they gratefully receive and boast about. But what he said in **Matthew 10**: *Do not possess gold and silver,* they shun as something harmful. Similarly, what he said to the disciples in **John 20**: *Receive the Holy Spirit; whose sins you forgive, they are forgiven them; and whose sins you retain, they are retained,* they calmly accept. But what he 

<margin>Note the craftiness of the prelates</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XI</header>

said in **Matthew 11**: *Learn from me, for I am meek and humble in heart,*—that gentleness and humility which prepares a place for the Holy Spirit—they do not admit into their hearts. Likewise, what the Lord said to the disciples in **Luke 10**: *He who hears you, hears me,* they seize upon to demand obedience to themselves. But what he said in **Matthew 20**: *You know that the rulers of the Gentiles lord it over them, and those who are great exercise authority over them. It shall not be so among you; but whoever wants to become great among you must be your servant. And whoever wants to be first among you must be your slave. Just as the Son of Man came not to be served, but to serve.* This most serious saying they attack in both word and deed: in word, by saying they *ought* to rule; and in deed, because they refuse to serve the Church in the manner of Jesus Christ the Lord. 

To put it briefly, they chew on, proclaim, and extend far too widely every word of Scripture—and especially of the Gospel—that seems to suggest they should be rich, pampered, famous in the world, and suffer no insult for Christ's sake. However, whatever suggests the following of Jesus Christ, such as poverty, gentleness, humility, tolerance, chastity, labor, or patience, they suppress, or interpret <note>original: "glossant," referring to the practice of writing 'glosses' or commentaries that often explained away the plain meaning of a text.</note> according to their own desires, or expressly repudiate as if it were irrelevant to salvation. 

And the devil, who is the worst of sophists <note>A 'sophist' is a person who uses clever but false arguments.</note>, seduces them through their ignorance of logical consequences, arguing thus: "Christ gave power to Peter and the other Apostles, therefore he gave it to these men." From this they infer that they may do whatever they wish, and thus by the same evidence they are the most blessed fathers with Christ, judging the Church, and destined to be perpetually crowned afterward. But blessed be Christ the all-knowing, who said those things to his Apostles knowing that they would direct the power handed to them according to his good pleasure, for the service of his bride <note>The "Bride of Christ" is a traditional metaphor for the Church.</note>.

Regarding the power in which the clergy boast, it must be noted that "power" is sometimes used to mean a position of authority or true power...

<margin>The Devil as a Sophist.</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the clergy selectively use biblical passages to bolster their authority and wealth while ignoring Christ's commands regarding humility, poverty, and service. He characterizes this "cherry-picking" of scripture as a form of spiritual deception influenced by the devil.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's discussion of authority within the Church. He distinguishes between true spiritual authority granted by God and the "pretended" or "shadowed" power wielded by corrupt officials. He draws heavily on the imagery of the Book of Revelation to warn against those who use their office for personal gain rather than the imitation of Christ.</meta>

<page-num>88, 89</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH / CHAPTER XI.</header>

True power is as described in **Romans 13**: <note>original: "Omnis anima potestatibus sublimioribus subdita sit."</note> *Let every soul be subject to the higher powers.* Sometimes, however, the term is used equivocally to mean a "pretended" or "masked" power, as in **Luke 22**, where Christ says to his captors—who were sent by the power of the High Priests: <note>original: "Hæc est hora uestra & potestas tenebrarũ."</note> *This is your hour and the power of darkness.* And in **Revelation 6** it says: <note>original: "Ecce equus pallidus & qui sedebat super eũ, nomen illi mors, & infernus sequebatur eum, & data est illi potestas sup quatuor partes terræ interficere gladio, fame & morte, & bestijs terræ."</note> *Behold, a pale horse, and he who sat upon it, his name was Death, and Hell followed him; and power was given to him over the four parts of the earth to kill with the sword, with hunger, and with death, and with the beasts of the earth.* And in **Revelation 13** it is held how the dragon gave power to the beast, and they worshipped the beast, saying: <note>original: "Quis similis bestiæ, & quis poterit pugnare cum ea?"</note> *Who is like the beast, and who will be able to fight against it?* And further down: *It was granted to him to make war with the saints and to overcome them, and power was given to him over every tribe, people, tongue, and nation, and all who dwell on the earth shall worship it, whose names are not written in the book of life of the Lamb who was slain.*

What is this "beast" whom men worship because they fear its power? Let him who reads understand, and let him resist such pretended power. Nor should he fear it in the same way, because though it was granted to the beast to make war with the saints and to "overcome" them through the killing of the body, those who die for the law of Christ ultimately overcome the beast themselves. For the Savior said to them in **Matthew 10**: <note>original: "Nolite timere eos qui occidunt corpus."</note> *Do not fear those who kill the body.* And in **John 16** <note>OCR says "Iohan. j.", likely a scribal error for John 16:33.</note>: *In the world you will have tribulation, but have confidence, for I have overcome the world.* On this, **Augustine** comments in his work on John: *They looked and they overcame; in whom, if not in Him? For the world would not have been overcome if the world had overcome His members.* Hence the Apostle says: *Thanks be to God, who gives us the victory.* He immediately added: *Through our Lord Jesus Christ,* who said to his own: *Have confidence, for I have overcome the world.* These are the words of Augustine. Moreover, those who have the "power of predestination" overcome the power of the dragon and the beast. This is the chief power, of which **John 1** says: <note>original: "Dedit eis potestatẽ filios dei fieri"</note> *He gave them power to become the children of God.* This power is followed by "perfected power," which is the power God gives to the blessed in the Fatherland <note>original: "patria." A common theological term for Heaven, the true home of the soul.</note> to fully enjoy the Lord and every creature within Him.

<margin>Power is understood in various ways.</margin>
<margin>The Beast signifies pretended power.</margin>

<column-break/>

Therefore, true Christians who wish to reach that power must resist every "pretended" power that strives to remove them from the imitation of Christ, whether by force or by craft. For by resisting such power, one does not resist God’s ordinance, but rather the **abuse of power**.

<margin>Simoniacs.</margin>
Those who practice **simony** <note>term: <term>Simony</term> — The sin of buying or selling sacred things, church offices, or sacraments, named after Simon Magus in the Book of Acts.</note> exercise this abuse regarding the "power of the keys." These men pretend to either damn the innocent or release the guilty, and they do this because of a lack of blind obedience to them, or for the sake of their own profit. Of such people, the Lord says in **Ezekiel 13**: <note>original: "Violabant me ad populũ meum, propter pugillum ordei uel fragmen panis, ut interficerent animas, quæ non moriuntur, & uiuificarent animas, quæ non uiuunt, mentientes populo meo credenti mendacijs."</note> *They profaned me among my people for a handful of barley or a piece of bread, to kill souls that should not die and to save souls alive that should not live, lying to my people who believe their lies.* On this, **Gregory** says (and it is found in the Canon Law): *Rightly is it said through the prophet: "They killed souls that do not die and gave life to souls that do not live." For he kills one who is not dying who condemns a righteous man, and he strives to give life to one who is not living who attempts to release a guilty man from punishment.*

<margin>Selling bishoprics.</margin>
They exercise an abuse of power who buy and sell holy orders, who acquire or sell Bishoprics, Canonries, or Parishes through simony; those who demand payment for the sacraments in an improper way; and those who, living greedily, luxuriously, or criminally in any way, pollute the power of the priesthood. For even if they confess that they know God, they deny Him by their deeds (as in **Titus 1**). Consequently, they do not truly believe in God, and thus, like unfaithful children, they hold unfaithful views regarding the seven sacraments of the church, the "keys," the offices, the customs, the ceremonies, the sacred objects of the church, the veneration of relics, indulgences, and holy orders. This is evident because such men despise the name of God.

<margin>The greed of priests is criticized.</margin>
Hence it is said in **Malachi 1**: *But you, O priests, who despise my name, and you said: "In what have we despised your name?" You offer polluted bread upon my altar.* Is there anyone among you who will shut the doors and kindle [fire on my altar for nothing?]...

<summary>Jan Hus distinguishes between true spiritual authority and the "pretended power" of corrupt church officials. He argues that resisting the abuses of the clergy, such as simony (the selling of offices) and the misuse of excommunication, is a duty for true Christians and not an act of rebellion against God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's critique of the moral state of the clergy. He moves from discussing the nature of spiritual power to the specific ways in which corrupt priests desecrate the sacraments and the law of Christ, focusing on the "pestiferous clergy" who oppose reform.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>T. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: nibus (with...)</meta>

...gives my altar for free: I have no pleasure in you, says the Lord of hosts, and I will not receive a gift from your hand. <note>original: "non est mihi uoluntas in uobis, dicit dominus exercituum, & munus non accipiam de manu uestra." Malachi 1:10.</note> Behold, the Lord says to the wicked priests that they despise his name and offer polluted bread.

<margin>Communicating unworthily.</margin>
For this reason, in the first part of the decree, first question, chapter "many of the laity," <note>Referring to the *Decretum Gratiani*, a collection of canon law.</note> Gregory says, after the declaration of the sacraments and power: We pollute the bread, that is, the body of Christ, when we approach the altar unworthily; and being filthy, we drink the pure blood. And the Apostle says in **Hebrews 10**: *Anyone who sets aside the law of Moses dies without mercy on the evidence of two or three witnesses. How much worse punishment do you think is deserved by the one who has trampled the Son of God underfoot, and has treated the blood of the covenant as a polluted thing, in which he was sanctified?* <note>original: "Quanto magis putatis deteriora mereri supplicia, qui filium dei conculcauerit..."</note> Second, such people crucify the Son of God. For the Apostle says in **Hebrews 6**: *Crucifying the Son of God again for themselves and holding him up to contempt.* Third, such people act impiously against the law of Christ, as the blessed Jerome says in his commentary on the prophet Zephaniah: <note>original: "Sacerdotes qui Eucharistiæ seruiunt... impie agunt in legem Christi."</note> Priests who serve the <term>Eucharist</term><note>The sacrament of Holy Communion, the bread and wine.</note> and divide the blood of the Lord to his people act impiously against the law of Christ, thinking that the Eucharist depends on the words of the one praying, rather than their life; and that only the formal prayer is necessary, not the merits of the priest. Of whom it is said: *Whatever priest has a blemish, let him not approach to offer oblations to the Lord.* Fourth, the aforementioned blaspheme the majesty of the Lord. Thus in **2 Peter 2**: *Those who walk after the flesh in the lust of uncleanness and despise authority.* Then: *But these, like irrational beasts, made to be caught and destroyed, blaspheming in things they do not understand.* And regarding this, the blessed Augustine, on Psalm 144, "Praise the Lord for he is good," says: *If you exceed the due measure of nature by immoderate greed, and gorge yourself with wine, no matter how many praises of God your tongue sounds, your life blasphemes.* These are his words.

How then do the greedy, those guilty of <term>simony</term><note>The sin of buying or selling sacred things or church offices.</note>, the lustful, and those guilty of other crimes—

<column-break/>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XI</header>
<sig>N ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Ad ho (Toward...)</meta>

—with these things being the case, feel rightly toward the Lord in his goodness? They despise the name of God by their unfaithfulness to his sacraments, they pollute his bread, they crucify the Son of God Jesus Christ to themselves and hold him up to contempt, they act impiously against the law of God, and they despise and blaspheme authority. And it is clear that such people are the "pestiferous clergy," <note>Hus uses the term "pestifer" to describe clergy who act like a plague or infection upon the Church.</note> thinking unfaithfully regarding the seven Sacraments of the church, and the <term>keys</term><note>The "power of the keys" refers to the authority to forgive or retain sins.</note>, and other matters pertaining to the law of Christ. And it is clear that the statement of the Doctors—whose leader at that time was Stephen Paletz along with Stanislaus, drawing after them Peter of Ikoyma, John Heliae, Andrew Bioda, John Hildesen, Matthew the monk, Herman the Hermit, George Bota, and Simon Vuenda—placed as the matter of dissension, is verifiable in the clergy who live criminally. For they say at the beginning of their writing: *The matter of this dissension is manifest on the part of certain ones of the pestiferous clergy.* For because the priests of Christ preached against the crimes of the pestiferous clergy, therefore dissension arose; because the clergy, bringing the plague of scandal to the people and being unwilling to endure preaching contrary to their own plague, rose up maliciously to extinguish the preaching, acting against those preaching the Gospel and wishing to heal their plague through the word of the Lord. But it was the intention of the stated Doctors that those evangelizing against the malice of the pestiferous clergy held heretical views regarding the keys of the Church—which, by the Lord's help, they shall not prove as long as they live.

<margin>Behold the articles falsely imposed upon the Hussite by the Inquisitors of Heretics.</margin>

->Only Christ is the true Roman Pontiff, to whom every human creature is subject by necessity of salvation. Likewise, modern pontiffs differ from ancient ones in morals and life.<-

->CHAPTER XII.<-

<summary>Jan Hus argues that corrupt and immoral priests desecrate the Eucharist and the Law of Christ through their actions, effectively "crucifying Christ again." He defends his followers against the accusations of the "Doctors" (his academic rivals), asserting that the real source of conflict is the clergy's refusal to hear preaching against their own corruption.</summary>
