<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s theological argument regarding the nature of the Church. Having previously criticized the moral failures of the clergy, he now addresses the definition of the "Roman Pontiff" (the Pope), arguing that this title most truly belongs to Jesus Christ rather than the human occupant of the Roman see.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: Chapter 22.</meta>

To the honor of the Lord Jesus Christ—whose honor and whose name as the Christ the aforementioned doctors <note>Hus is referring to the faculty of the University of Prague who opposed his teachings.</note> did not mention anywhere in their writing—this conclusion is proven: **To be subject to the Roman Pontiff is a necessity for salvation for every human creature.** This is clear from the fact that no one can be saved unless they are meritoriously subject to Jesus Christ. But he himself is the Roman Pontiff, just as he is the head of the universal church and of every particular church; therefore, the conclusion is true. The logic is clear with the major and minor premises, and is evident from what was said above, and through that passage in **1 Peter 2**: *For you were once like sheep going astray, but you have now turned to the shepherd and bishop of your souls.* <note>original: "Eratis enim aliquando sicut oues errantes, sed conuersi estis nunc ad pastorem & episcopum animarum uestrarum."</note> And in **Hebrews 7**: *Jesus has become the guarantee of a much better covenant.* There were perhaps many other priests according to the law, because they were prevented by death from remaining in office.

<margin>Christ is the true supreme pontiff.</margin>
But this man, because he remains forever, has an eternal priesthood; therefore, he is able to save forever those who approach God through him, since he always lives to make intercession for us. For it was fitting that we should have such a High Priest: holy, innocent, undefiled, separated from sinners, and made higher than the heavens. He does not have a daily need, like those priests, to offer sacrifices first for his own sins and then for the people's; for he did this once for all by offering himself. <note>Hus is quoting Hebrews 7:22–27 to argue that Christ fulfills the role of the ultimate High Priest.</note> Behold, this is the most holy and supreme Roman Bishop and Pontiff, standing by God the Father and remaining with us, as he says in the final chapter of **Matthew**: *Behold, I am with you always, even to the end of the age.*

<margin>Christ the true pontiff administers the sacraments.</margin>
For Christ is that person who is present everywhere, since he is the true God, whose nature it is to be everywhere without being limited by space. He is the bishop who baptizes and who takes away the sins of the world (**John 1**). He is the one who joins marriages together, so that man may not separate them (**Matthew 9**): *What God has joined, let no man separate.* He makes priests (**Revelation 1**): *He has made us a kingdom and priests.* He himself performs the sacrament of the Eucharist, saying in **Luke**...

<column-break/>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII</header>
<sig>N ii</sig>
<meta>catchword: Too much</meta>

chapter 22: *This is my body.* He confirms his faithful in **Luke 21**, saying: *I will give you a mouth and wisdom which all your adversaries will not be able to resist or contradict.* He feeds his sheep with his word and example, and with the food of his own body. Moreover, he does all these things on his part without fail, because he is a holy, innocent, undefiled priest, separated from sinners and made higher than the heavens. He is the supreme bishop keeping watch over his flock, because he who keeps Israel neither slumbers nor sleeps. <note>A reference to Psalm 121:4.</note> He is the Pontiff who prepared an easy way for us to the fatherland. He is the Pope <note>term: Pope <gloss>Hus uses the title "Papa," which means "Father," and applies it here to Christ as the "Father of the future age."</gloss></note> because he is the wonderful Prince of Peace, the Father of the age to come. Behold, it was fitting that we should have such a pontiff, who, though he was in the form of God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but emptied himself, taking the form of a servant; he humbled himself, becoming obedient unto death, even death on a cross. Because of this, God also exalted him and gave him the name that is above every name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth. <note>Hus is quoting the famous Christological hymn from Philippians 2:6–10.</note>

From this follows the conclusion: **To be subject to the Roman Pontiff is a necessity for salvation for every human creature.** But there is no other such pontiff except the Lord Jesus Christ himself, our pontiff. This is because the humanity of Christ is subject to no other pontiff as a necessity for salvation, because God exalted him and gave him a name—which is his most worthy being—above every other name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee, every power, should bow in obedience to him: those in heaven (that is, the angels), those on earth (that is, all human beings), and those under the earth (that is, the devils). Furthermore, it is because the mother of Christ is a human creature, as are John the Baptist, the apostle Peter, and the other blessed ones; yet it is not a necessity for salvation for any of them to be subject to any other Roman pontiff besides Christ, since they have already been made safe <note>meaning: they are already in heaven/saved</note>, and the Roman Pontiff <note>referring here to the earthly Pope</note> cannot loose or bind them.

<summary>Jan Hus argues that Jesus Christ is the true "Roman Pontiff" and "Pope" to whom all people must be subject for salvation. By applying these titles to Christ, he challenges the absolute authority of the earthly papacy, noting that even the saints and Mary are subject only to Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Pontiff, Jesus Christ, Salvation, High Priest, Sacraments, Papal Authority, Head of the Church, Jan Hus</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of papal titles and authority. He has just established that Jesus Christ is the true High Priest. Now, he contrasts the humility of Christ and the Apostles with what he views as the arrogance of recent Popes, specifically citing a controversial decree by Pope Clement regarding the power to command angels.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

<margin>
Pope Clement commands the angels of paradise
</margin>
Therefore, Pope Clement <note>Likely referring to Clement VI (r. 1342–1352), who issued a famous Bull for the Jubilee Year of 1350.</note> had extended his power too far, commanding in his Bull to the angels of paradise that they should lead the soul of a pilgrim—who was traveling to Rome for <term>indulgences</term> <note>Certificates sold or granted by the Church to reduce the temporal punishment for sins.</note> and departing from purgatory absolved—into perpetual joys. This man essentially wished that the knee of the celestial angels should be bowed at his command. And when he added, "We do not wish that the pain of hell be inflicted upon him in any way," he thus commanded that the power or the knee of the infernal regions should be bowed at his command. The Apostles did not presume to act in this way. For John did not wish to command, but rather to worship at the feet of an angel. Whence in the final chapter of the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note> he says: *I, John, fell down to worship at the feet of the angel, and he said to me: See that you do not do this; I am your fellow servant, and of your brothers the prophets, and of those who keep the words of the prophecy of this book. Worship God.* <note>original: "Ego Ioannes cecidi ut adorarem pedes angeli, & dixit mihi, Vide ne feceris, conseruus tuus sum... Deum adora."</note> Behold, this great Apostle and beloved prophet of God, who without doubt exceeded modern Popes—and especially Clement who commands angels—did not wish to command anything to an angel, but falling down, wished to worship at his feet; yet the holy angel forbade him, showing him that he ought to worship God.

<margin>
The High Priest of the Old Law prefigures Christ.
</margin>
It should be noted, however, from the cited text of the Apostle in **Hebrews 7**, that many were made priests according to the law, because every High Priest of the Old Law prefigured Christ in all his legal acts. Therefore, <term>antonymatically</term> <note>A rhetorical term meaning to use a title or epithet instead of a proper name; here, meaning Christ is the "High Priest" in the most absolute sense.</note> and uniquely, He is called the High Priest and Bishop of souls. From this, that multitude of priests and their offices were consummated in the one Christ, as the Apostle teaches. And this is the reason why the Apostles did not call themselves "Most Holy Popes," "Heads of the Universal Church," or "Universal Pontiffs." Instead, having the High Priest with them until the end of the age, they called themselves servants of Christ, companions in tribulation, and ministers of the church. Whence that holy custom was preserved in the time of the Blessed Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great (r. 590–604).</note>, and according to the Decree in the ninety-second distinction: "Behold," he says in the preface of a proud letter: "you have taken care to print 

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER 12

the word of appeal, calling me 'Universal Pope,' which I ask your Sweetest Holiness <note>Gregory is writing to the Patriarch of Alexandria, who had addressed Gregory with this title.</note> not to do further; for that which is given to another more than reason demands is taken away from us. I do not seek to prosper in words, but in character; nor do I consider a man to be honored in a way that I know causes my brothers to lose their own honor. For my honor is the honor of the universal church; my honor is the solid strength of my brothers. Then am I truly honored, when the honor due to each individual is not denied. For if your Holiness calls me 'Universal Pope,' you deny yourself to be that which you acknowledge me to be: the whole. But far be this from us; let those words depart which puff up vanity and wound charity."

<margin>
Modern Pontiffs do not imitate Saint Gregory.
</margin>
From the words of this holy Pope, it is gathered how easily a man is puffed up when he is called "Most Holy Father"—even if he happens to be living in sin—and the one calling him so is struck either by the greed of flattery or lies out of ignorance. Whence Gregory notably says: "I do not seek to prosper in words, but in character." Alas, the Modern Pontiffs do not seek it so; they glory in the naked name without the character of virtue, pretending that by reason of their office or ecclesiastical dignity, the name of "holiness" belongs to them. But if there were logic to this, then Judas would have had to be called a "holy apostle." But blessed be the Lord, who, to remove such a cloak of pretense, said to the disciples in **John 6**: *Have I not chosen you twelve, and one of you is a devil?* And he said this before Iscariot had even betrayed his master.

Whence the more holy men are praised by others, the more they humble themselves, and the more they depress their minds with fear, lest the praise exceed their actual merit. Thus, in the **Acts of the Apostles**, Peter, the apostle of Christ, having been called by messengers to Cornelius the Gentile, went humbly; and when he had come to Cornelius, the latter came to meet him...

<summary>Jan Hus critiques the title "Universal Pope," using historical examples like Pope Gregory the Great's rejection of the term and Pope Clement VI's supposed overreach in commanding angels. He argues that holiness comes from character and following Christ, not from an ecclesiastical title.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Clement VI, Pope Gregory the Great, Universal Pope, humility, High Priest, Peter and Cornelius, indulgences, angels, purgatory</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that Christ, not the Pope, is the true head of the Church. Having previously discussed the pride of recent Popes who claim power over angels, Hus now contrasts the humility of St. Peter with the arrogance of his "modern" successors, citing early church law to argue against the exaltation of the Roman Pontiff.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

<margin>The mountains are touched.</margin>
Cornelius, having been assured by an angel of Peter's holiness, fell down and worshipped at Peter’s feet. But Peter, taught by God about Cornelius and certain of his own blessedness through revelation, did not permit Cornelius to lie prostrate at his feet. This is unlike modern Popes, in whom not a spark of holiness is seen; indeed, often conscious of their own crimes, they take even greater pride in the veneration shown to them. If their title of "pomposity" is omitted, they are quickly shaken by anger. Seeking to extinguish such pride, the Council of Africa <note>Referring to the Third Council of Carthage (397 AD), often cited in medieval canon law.</note> says in Distinction 99: "The Bishop of the first see shall not be called the prince of priests, or the high priest, or anything of this kind, but only the Bishop of the first See. Moreover, not even the Roman Pontiff should be called 'Universal.'" All these titles have now been prescribed out of pomp, flattery, and greed—and among the simple-minded, out of blind deception. Therefore, returning to our supreme High Priest, the humble Jesus Christ—who in Luke 14 commands that when one is invited to a wedding, he should sit in the lowest place—let us confess to him, according to his own command in Luke 17, that we are "unprofitable servants." For he says: *When you have done all that was commanded of you, say: We are unprofitable servants.* <note>original: "Cum feceritis omnia quae praecepta sunt uobis, dicite, Serui inutiles sumus."</note> For when we have humbled our souls before the supreme High Priest by fulfilling his commands, it may be possible that our Popes are "thieves and robbers." <note>A reference to John 10:8.</note> But he himself, the Shepherd of our souls, will not fail us in those things necessary for salvation; instead, as a truly pious pastor, he will feed, guard, and nourish his sheep.

### That the Pope is not the head of the church, but the vicar of Christ.

## CHAPTER XIII

Furthermore, the aforementioned doctors <note>Theologians at the University of Prague who opposed Hus.</note> assert in their writing that the Pope of the Roman Church is the head, and the college of Cardinals is the body. They claim these are the true successors of the prince of the Apostles, Peter, and of the college of the other Apostles of Christ in
<meta>Catchword: office</meta>

---

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII</header>

the ecclesiastical office of knowing and defining all matters of Catholic and ecclesiastical concern, correcting and purging errors regarding them, and, in all such matters, having care for all those churches and all of Christ's faithful. Since, for the government of the church throughout the whole world, there must always remain such manifest and true successors in that office of the prince of the Apostles, Peter, and the college of the other Apostles of Christ. Nor can any other such successors be found or given on earth except the Pope, who exists as the head, and the college of Cardinals, which exists as the body of the Roman Church mentioned above. These are very long-winded trifles <note>original: "Istae nugae multum longae." Hus uses 'nugae' (trifles/nonsense) to dismiss his opponents' arguments.</note> which I estimate proceeded for the most part from the head of Stanislaus <note>Stanislaus of Znaim, a former friend of Hus who became his fierce theological opponent.</note>, who was struck and terrified by the Roman <term>Curia</term> <note>The administrative and legal apparatus of the Papal court.</note>, and they involve many points.

Regarding these, I note that in their writing, "Church" is taken to mean all living Christians. They seem to imply this when they say that the community of the Clergy in the kingdom of Bohemia—not only with the community of the entire Clergy in the world, but also of all Christendom—always faithfully feels and believes just as the Roman Church does. Or secondly, these doctors call the Roman Church only the Pope with the Cardinals, when they say that they believe just as the Roman Church does and not otherwise, of which Roman Church the Pope is the head and the college of Cardinals the body. I estimate that the doctors denoted the church only in these ways in their writing.

<margin>What "Pope" signifies.</margin>
I suppose that "Pope" signifies that spiritual Bishop who bears the place of Christ in the highest and most similar way, just as Peter did after the Ascension. However, if any person whom the Western Church accepts as the Roman Bishop—to decide the cases of the church as its head and to command the faithful whatever he wishes—is called "Pope," it is an abuse of the term. For according to this, it would be necessary to concede in such a case that a most crude Layman, or a female <note>A reference to the medieval legend of "Pope Joan," a woman who supposedly disguised herself as a man and was elected Pope.</note>, or a heretic and the Antichrist would be the Pope.
<meta>Catchword: It is evident</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus critiques the exaltation of the Pope, arguing that true leadership follows the humility of Peter and Christ rather than the "pomp" of modern titles. He challenges the definition of the Roman Church as merely the Pope and Cardinals, warning that such a definition could logicially lead to a heretic or the Antichrist being called the "head" of the Church.</summary>
<keywords>humility, Council of Africa, titles of the Pope, St. Peter, Cornelius, Stanislaus of Znaim, Roman Church, Cardinals, Antichrist, Pope Joan</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the fallibility of the Roman Papacy. Having previously discussed the pride of popes, he now provides historical examples of "failed" popes—including laymen, a legendary female pope, and heretics—to prove that the office of the Pope is not immune to error or sin, and that a corrupt Pope may even be considered an Antichrist.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>A woman was Pope for two years.</margin>
This is evident, for Constantine II was an unlearned layman who, having been suddenly ordained a priest, was made Pope through ambition. He was later deposed, and all those he had ordained were declared "un-ordained" <note>Meaning their ordinations were declared invalid because Constantine II was considered an intruder into the office.</note> around the year of our Lord 707. The same is evident concerning Gregory <note>Likely referring to Gregory VI (1045–1046), who was accused of simony (buying his office).</note>, who, being unlearned in letters, consecrated another to serve alongside him. Since this displeased the people, a third Pope was brought in. While these three were arguing among themselves, the Emperor came to Rome, deposed them, and ordained another alone. Regarding the female pope, this is clear in the case of Agnes, who was called "John the Englishman." The Monk of Chester <note>Referring to Ranulf Higden (c. 1280–1364), author of the *Polychronicon*, a popular universal history.</note> writes of her in Book 5, Chapter 3: A certain woman sat in the papacy for two years and five months after Leo. This is said to have been a girl named Agnes, born in Mainz. She was brought to Athens by her lover in male clothing and named John the Englishman. She progressed so much in various sciences that when she came to Rome, she had great masters as her listeners while she lectured on the **Trivium** <note>The three basic liberal arts: grammar, logic, and rhetoric.</note>. Finally elected Pope, she became pregnant by her lover. While she was proceeding from St. Peter’s Church to the Lateran Church, she was overcome by the pains of childbirth and gave birth between the Colosseum and St. Clement’s. She died and was buried there shortly after. For this reason, it is said that the Popes commonly avoid this route, and she is not placed in the catalog of Popes.

Regarding a heretic, this is clear in Pope Liberius, of whom the Monk of Chester writes in Book 4: by the command of Constantius, he was exiled for three years because he refused to favor the **Arians** <note>Followers of Arianism, a fourth-century theological belief that denied that Jesus Christ was of the same substance as God the Father.</note>. In the meantime, the Roman clergy ordained Felix as Pope, who, having held a council, condemned and expelled two Arian priests, Ursacius and Valens. Hearing this, Liberius was recalled from exile. Overcome by the long duration of his exile and emboldened by the prospect of reclaiming the papacy, he consented to heretical depravity. Having cast Felix out, he held the churches of Peter, Paul, and Lawrence by force, so that the clerks and priests who favored Felix were slaughtered in the church—with Liberius not lifting a finger to stop it—and Felix was martyred.

<meta>catchword: And then</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII</header>

<margin>A perverse Pope is the Antichrist.</margin>
And then, concerning the Antichrist, it is clear that every Pope living in a manner contrary to Christ—just like any other perverse man—is commonly called an "Antichrist," according to that passage in 1 John 2: *Many Antichrists have arisen.* <note>original: "Antichristi multi facti sunt."</note> Nor will a faithful person stubbornly dare to deny that it is possible for the "man of sin" to sit in the holy place. Of this, the Savior prophesies in Matthew 24, saying: *When you see the abomination of desolation, which was spoken of by Daniel the prophet, standing in the holy place, etc.* <note>original: "Cum uideritis abominationẽ desolationis qđ dicta est a Daniele propheta in loco sancto &c."</note> And the Apostle says in 2 Thessalonians 2: *Let no one deceive you in any way, for that day will not come unless the rebellion comes first, and the man of sin is revealed, the son of perdition, who opposes and exalts himself above all that is called God or that is worshiped, so that he sits in the temple of God, showing himself as if he were God.*

<margin>From where the name "Pope" had its beginning.</margin>
It is also clear through the Chronicles how the Papal dignity grew. For the Emperor Constantine, around the year of our Lord 301, commanded that the Supreme Bishop should be called "Pope" by everyone, and that name also grew through the "Endowment" <note>Referring to the "Donation of Constantine," a document (later proven to be a forgery) which claimed Constantine gave the Pope authority over the Western Roman Empire.</note>. The Emperor Phocas also confirmed this same thing at the insistence of the clergy around the year of our Lord 600, as is read in his annals. Whence the Monk of Chester relates in Book 4, Chapter 14: How the excellence of the Roman Empire assisted the Papacy of its pontiff over others. The Council of Nicaea, he says, bestowed this privilege on the Roman Pontiff: that just as Augustus was held above other kings, so the Roman Pontiff should be held as the Chief Father and Pope. However, the origin of that name and excellence was in the endowment of the church, as specified in the **Decretum** <note>The *Decretum Gratiani*, a collection of canon law; Hus cites Distinction 96, "Constantinus."</note>.

Having noted these things to remove any objection, I suppose that the doctors should note in their writing that by the "Roman Church," they mean that church of which the Savior said to Peter: *Upon this rock I will build my church.* For so it is, as was said in Chapter 7: the Saints and the Decrees call it the Holy Roman Church, as in Distinction 21, the chapter "Although," and Question 23, Case 1, the chapter "This is the faith," and in the **Clementines** <note>A collection of decrees by Pope Clement V.</note> concerning the oath of the Roman princes.

<sig>O ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: ortho</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus presents historical "cracks" in the papacy—including the legend of Pope Joan and the heresy of Pope Liberius—to argue that the Pope is not inherently holy or infallible. He traces the title "Pope" to imperial decrees rather than divine mandate, suggesting that a corrupt pope fulfills the biblical prophecy of the Antichrist.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Joan, Agnes, Jan Hus, Antichrist, Liberius, Arianism, Donation of Constantine, Canon Law, Man of Sin, Roman Church, Papal History</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of the institutional claims of the Roman Church. On the previous page, he argued that popes are fallible human beings and that the office does not guarantee holiness. Here, he examines the specific legal and theological claims made by Church "doctors" regarding the authority of the Pope and the College of Cardinals.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH, CHAPTER XIII</header>
<sig>O iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: telligen (understanding)</meta>

...professors of the orthodox faith, venerating the Holy Roman Church—whose head is Christ our Redeemer—and the Roman Pontiff, the Vicar of that same Redeemer, with the fervor of faith and the readiness of clear devotion. This is found in the *Sext* <note>original: "in sexto"; referring to the *Liber Sextus*, a collection of Canon Law published in 1298</note>, in the decree "Alma Mater Ecclesia." Also in the *Extravagante* of Boniface VIII <note>original: "Extrauagante Bonifacij. viij."; referring to the decree *Unam Sanctam*, which famously asserted that there is no salvation outside the Roman Church</note>, "Sancta Romana Ecclesia," and in other sayings located elsewhere and cited above.

<margin>What the learned feel about the Roman Church.</margin>
Concerning these things, in the trifles of these learned doctors, I grasp these points. First: the Pope is the head of the Holy Roman Church. Second: the College of Cardinals is the body of the same Holy Church. Third: the Pope is the true successor of Peter, the Prince of the Apostles. Fourth: the Cardinals are the true successors of the college of Christ’s other Apostles. Fifth: for the governance of the Church throughout the entire world, it is necessary that such manifest, true successors always remain in the office of Peter, the prince of the apostles, and the other apostles of Christ. Sixth: no other such successors can be found or given on earth except the Pope, who exists as the head, and the College of Cardinals, which exists as the body of the Roman Church.

<margin>Arguments against the aforementioned points.</margin>
Against all six points now set forth, I argue in summary. Every truth in the religion of Christ that must be followed is either a truth known by bodily sense, discovered by infallible intelligence, known through revelation, or set down in the Divine Scripture. But none of these six points are truths known by bodily sense, discovered by infallible intelligence, known through revelation, or set down in Divine Scripture. Therefore, none of these six points is a truth in the religion of Christ that must be followed. The major premise is clear through Saint Augustine in his *Enchiridion* <note>A handbook on Christian faith, hope, and love</note>, chapter 3, where he speaks thus: "For these things are chiefly, indeed almost alone, to be followed in religion; he who speaks against them is either entirely a stranger to Christ’s name, or a heretic." And he follows: "These things are to be defended by reason, either starting from the senses of the body or...

<column-break/>

...discovered by the intelligence of the mind. However, regarding things we have neither experienced through bodily sense nor been able to reach with the mind: these must be believed without doubt based on the witnesses by whom the Divine Scripture—which has already earned its name—was composed. They were able to see or even foresee those things, being divinely aided either through the body or through the spirit." These are the words of Saint Augustine. The doctors cannot disprove the minor premise, nor was any of these six points divinely revealed to them. For those points are not evident from bodily sense, nor from reason, nor from Divine Scripture. Rather, it seems that by delivering those points with an authority as if they must be believed, the doctors fall under a curse <note>original: "anathema"; a formal curse or excommunication by the Church</note> through that authority of Augustine which they themselves cite in their own writing. If anyone venerates any writings as having authority other than those which the Catholic Church has received or handed down to be held as authoritative, let him be anathema. This is clear because they themselves have handed down their own writings as authoritative so that they might be believed, yet the Catholic Church has not received those writings, since they are placed neither in Divine Law nor in Canon Law. Therefore, it follows that the doctors themselves are, as such, under a curse. It is clear that religious faith should not be granted to them regarding those points unless they have proved them evidently, or founded them in Sacred Scripture or by lively reason. For Augustine says to Jerome in an epistle: "I have learned to yield this honor and fear only to those writers who are now called 'Canonical,' so that I dare not believe any of them to have erred in writing. Others, however, I read in such a way that, no matter what holiness or learning they possess, I do not think a thing is true simply because they felt it to be so, but because they were able to persuade me through Canonical or probable reasons which do not shrink from the truth." (Decree, Distinction 9, "Ego solum"). Since, therefore, these Doctors are not the authors of Sacred Scripture, even granted that they excel in holiness, the faithful are not to think what they feel is true unless they have persuaded us through other authors of Scripture, or through canonical or probable reasons, that those points do not deviate from the truth.

<margin>How authors ought to be read.</margin>

->Then<-

<vocab>Holy Roman Church, Pope, Cardinals, Peter, Apostles, Augustine, Enchiridion, Jerome, Canon Law, Anathema, Sacred Scripture</vocab>

<summary>Jan Hus outlines six claims made by Church doctors regarding the absolute authority of the Pope and Cardinals, then systematically rejects them. He argues that since these claims are not found in Scripture, reason, or direct revelation, they are not binding on Christians and may even be "anathema."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from the previous section, where he challenged the "trifles" of the church doctors regarding the authority of the Pope and Cardinals. Here, he moves to a direct critique of the claim that the Pope is the "head" of the Church, using historical examples and theological reasoning to argue that only Christ holds that position truly.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>That the Pope is not the head of the church.</margin>
Then, similarly, regarding the point that the Pope is the head of the Roman church, always holding uniformly that the Roman church is the bride of Christ built upon Christ, against which the gates of hell cannot prevail <note>original: "portæ inferi nō possunt ꝑualere"; a reference to Matthew 16:18</note>, it is argued as follows: no Pope is the most worthy person of the Catholic church except for Christ; therefore, no Pope is the head of that Catholic church except for Christ. It follows that no one, without a revelation, could reasonably assert of himself or another that he was the head of the church of holy power, although by living well he ought to hope that he is a member of the holy Catholic church, the bride of Christ. Therefore, concerning this, we ought not to contend over whether this person or that, whoever is living among us, is a member of a particular holy church, but rather to suppose from their works that if he is a superior ruling a particular holy church, then he is the superior in that particular church. This ought to be supposed regarding the Roman Pontiff, unless his works contradict it, because the Savior said: "Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves; by their fruits you will know them." <note>The text cites Matthew 11, but the quote is from Matthew 7:15-16</note> And in John 10 he says: "Believe the works." Likewise, it is not necessary to believe that this or that Roman Pontiff is the head of any particular holy church unless God has predestined him. This is evident, because otherwise the Christian faith would have to be deceived and a Christian would have to confess a lie, since the Church was deceived in the case of Agnes <note>original: "Agnete"; referring to the legend of Pope Joan, who was believed in Hus's time to have reigned as Pope Agnes for over two years</note>. And God permitted this for the sake of His own, without doubt for the better, so that one who is elected as Pope should not immediately be held as a saint, or as such a person as is claimed, without any fear or hesitation.

Therefore, I would wish that the doctors would teach the people publicly whether, when the entire clergy then living estimated that they had a true Pope, that same Agnes was the head of the Roman church. Or if the church was then **headless** <term>ἀκέφαλος; original: "akephalos", meaning without a head</term>—by the name of the Pope—for two years and five months while the Church Militant <note>The "Church Militant" refers to the body of Christians living on earth, as opposed to those in heaven or purgatory</note> existed. From this, the faithful can understand that just as it is not of the substance of the **universal** <term>καθολικῆς; original: "katholikēs", meaning catholic or universal</term> faith to believe explicitly that this Liberius, John, Boniface, Clement, or Urban is predestined or a member of the holy mother church according to the sentence stated above; so it is not of the necessity of sal-

<meta>catchword: te sa (continuation of "salvation")</meta>

---

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII</header>

-vation for all Christians living with him that they explicitly believe him to be the head of any church, unless his deeds and evangelical life move them more evidently to believe it. For it would be an excessive presumption to assert that we are the heads of any particular church which might be a part of the holy mother church. How then can any of us presume, without revelation, to assert of himself or another that he is the head, when Ecclesiastes 9 truly says that "no one knows whether they are worthy of love or hatred," as far as predestination is concerned. Likewise, if we look to the sense and movement which we influence in our subjects, and on the other side to the mirror of Scripture by which we should regulate our whole life, we would choose rather to be called servants and ministers of the church than heads. For it is well known that if we do not perform the office of a head, we are not heads; because according to Augustine in his book *On the Ten Strings* <note>original: "de decem chordis"; a famous sermon by St. Augustine on the Ten Commandments</note>, a perverse husband is not the head of his wife; much more so the Pope of the church—if he degenerates from Christ, he is not the head of that particular church. Hence, after Augustine had shown that a truly Christian woman ought to grieve over the fornication of her husband, not because of the flesh, but because of the charity and chastity owed to Christ the husband, he says consequently: Christ speaks in the hearts of good women where the husband does not hear, saying: "Grieve over the injuries of your husband, but do not imitate him; rather let him imitate you in what is good. For in that which he does evilly, do not think of him as your head, but think of me as your Lord." And he proves this ought to be done: "If," he says, "he is the head in that which he does evilly, and the body follows its head, they both go into the abyss." However, so that the Christian does not follow his evil head, let him hold himself to the head of the church, Christ; to Him you owe your chastity, to Him you shall bring honor. Let the private man be absent; let the man joined to mother church be present.

<margin>Note here those who are adulterers.</margin>
Blessed, therefore, be Christ, the head of the church, who cannot be separated from his bride, which is his mystical body—in the way that Popes have often been separated from the church itself through heresy. But some of the aforementioned doctors say that the Pope is the bodily head of the Church Militant, which must always be here with the church.

<margin>The Pope is the bodily head according to others.</margin>
<meta>catchword: clesia (church)</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Hus argues that the Pope cannot be the true head of the Catholic Church, a title reserved for Christ alone. He uses the historical legend of Pope Joan ("Agnes") to demonstrate that the institutional papacy is fallible and that a Pope's authority is conditional upon his moral conduct and adherence to Christ's teachings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s theological treatise, specifically his rebuttal against the "doctors" (the established church theologians of Prague) who argued for the supreme authority of the Pope and the College of Cardinals. Hus is currently challenging the claim that the Pope acts as the physical head of the church while Christ remains only its spiritual head.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: doctrinally</meta>

church; however, Christ is not a physical head in that way. Here it is said that the same difficulty remains for them to prove the first part. For it remains for them to prove that the Pope is the head of the holy church, which they have not proved. And then it remains first to prove that Christ is not the physical head of the church militant <note>The "Church Militant" refers to the Christian church on earth, which is seen as being at war against sin and the devil, as opposed to the "Church Triumphant" in heaven.</note>, since Christ is a physical person, for he is the man who is the head of the church militant. Christ is present with his church all days, even until the end of the age <note>original: "usque ad consummationẽ seculi"; a reference to Matthew 28:20</note>, according to his divine person. Similarly, he is present through grace, giving his own body to the church to be eaten sacramentally and spiritually. In what way is that bridegroom—who is the head of the church—not more present to us than the Pope, who is separated from us by two hundred miles <note>original: "CC. miliaria"</note> and is unable to influence our feeling or movement by himself, which is the duty of a head? It would therefore be sufficient to say that the Pope is the Vicar of Christ <note>A title meaning "representative" or "deputy" of Christ.</note>, and it would be well for him if he were a faithful minister predestined to the glory of the head.

### The college of cardinals is not the body of the holy Roman church, nor is any Pope whatever a true successor of Peter.

->CHAPTER XIV.<-

The second point is this: the College of Cardinals is the body of the holy Roman church. If this is so, then the College of Cardinals is the holy Roman church itself. This follows from the Apostle's words to the Ephesians, chapter 1: "He gave him to be the head over all the church, which is his body." <note>Ephesians 1:22-23</note> And since the church, which is the mystical body of Christ, cannot be damned—as Christ says in Matthew 16: "Upon this rock I will build my church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it"—it follows that the College of Cardinals cannot be damned. And since that conclusion is false, or at the very least doubtful to the doctors, it follows that they have doctrinally proposed something false or doubtful in that point

<column-break/>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIV</header>
<sig>P</sig>
<meta>catchword: witness</meta>

as if it were true; but what fruit is there in teaching Christians in such a way?

Likewise, the college of cardinals is either the true body of the holy Roman church, or a fictional one. It is not the second, according to the Doctors; therefore it is the first. Consequently, the college is predestined for glory; and since the doctors do not have a revelation of predestination concerning that college, it follows that they should not have asserted that the college itself is the body of the holy Roman church.

Likewise, all the predestined <note>Hus follows Wycliffe’s view that the "true" Church consists only of those chosen by God for salvation (the predestined), not the visible hierarchy of the clergy.</note> are the body of the holy Roman church, and they are not only that college. The first part is clear through the words of the Apostle, who speaks in the person of the predestined in Romans 12: "We, being many, are one body in Christ." And in 1 Corinthians 12, showing the unity of the body of the church, the Apostle does not state that the college of apostles is the body of the church. Instead, he says that God has placed in the church: first, apostles; second, prophets; third, teachers; then miracles, then gifts of healings, etc. And making a comparison of the body of the church and its members to the natural body of a man, he speaks thus: "For as the body is one and has many members, but all the members of the body, though they are many, are one body, so also is Christ." This is because he is one person with his holy church, which is his body. And the second part—that all those predestined are not that college—is manifest in itself. Therefore, the doctors would have spoken better if they had said that Christ is the head of the holy Roman church and the individual predestined are its members, and all together are the body which is the church, rather than saying that the Pope is the head of the Roman church and the college of Cardinals is the body. For in this way they would have agreed with the Apostle and with the saints cited in the first chapter, and especially with Blessed Augustine, who in book 3 of *On Christian Doctrine* <note>original: "lib. iij. de doc. Christia"</note> says: "For it is not truly the body of the Lord which will not remain with him for eternity." If, therefore, the college of cardinals will not remain with Christ for eternity (which is hidden from me), how is it the body of the holy Roman church or of Christ? Similarly, regarding the claim that the Pope with the aforementioned college is the holy Roman church, which cannot

<margin>
Christ is the head of the church and all the predestined are his body.
</margin>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that Christ is the true physical and spiritual head of the church militant, rejecting the claim that the Pope and Cardinals exclusively constitute the "body" of the Roman Church. He asserts that the true Church consists of all those predestined for salvation, as the hierarchy is fallible and not guaranteed eternal life.</summary>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from page 105. Hus is debating the definition of the Church and its "head." He is refuting the claims of the "Doctors" (the theological faculty at the University of Prague) who argued that the Pope is the head of the Church and the Cardinals are its body.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

...can [not] be overcome by the gates of hell. <note>original: "test a portis inferi superari"; This refers to Matthew 16:18. Hus is arguing that the "Rock" the church is built on is Christ or faith, not the person of the Pope, because a Pope can fall into sin, but the true Church cannot.</note>
Let us therefore speak more securely with Blessed Augustine, who in the prologue to his commentary on Psalm 79 <note>specifically on the verse "Give ear, O Shepherd of Israel"</note> says: "Finally, by this testimony, he acknowledges both Christ and the vineyard—that is, the head and the body, the king and the people, the shepherd and the flock, and the entire mystery of all Christians: Christ and the church." Behold, this Teacher of the holy church shows us the holy church together with its head. This stands in contrast to those doctors who, without scriptural authority, claim that the body of the holy Roman church is the College of Cardinals—though it would be a good thing for that college if its members were actually members of the holy church of Jesus Christ! We ought to consider how Blessed Augustine himself feared to call Christ a "lordly man" <note>original: "hominem dominicum"; A technical theological term Augustine eventually rejected because he felt it suggested Christ was two separate persons rather than one.</note> because that phrasing is not found in Scripture. How much more, then, should we fear to call any Christian the "head" of the holy church militant, lest Christ be blasphemed? For this name "Head" was reserved for Christ alone by the counsel of the Trinity as his own unique title. How then do the doctors teach, without showing any scripture, that the Pope is the head of that holy church and the college is its body? It is enough for a faithful Christian, with "formed faith" <term>formed faith: Faith that is not just intellectual belief, but is "formed" or made alive by love and good works.</term> and perseverance, to believe as an article of faith concerning the catholic church that it is the **one totality of the predestined faithful to be saved** by the virtue of Christ’s merit. Christ is the head of that holy catholic church, even if he has not explicitly come down to any particular Vicar <note>A "Vicar" is a representative or deputy; here referring to the Pope.</note> whom one might recognize as the chief head. For in this way, many were saved in Judea, in Asia, and in Ethiopia who believed in Christ according to the teaching of the apostles, without explicitly recognizing Peter—indeed, some believed without having ever heard anything of Peter at all.

<margin>What it means to believe in the catholic church</margin>

The third point is this: The Pope is the manifest and true successor of Peter, the prince of the apostles. This was mentioned near the end of chapter seven. However, it is still said that the Doctors have not proven this point. And because a vicar ought to act in the place of a superior, 

<meta>catchword: from whom,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

from whom he received his vicarial power, it is therefore necessary that he be conformed most closely in his works to the one whose place he takes; otherwise, the power in him would be void. From this, an argument is formed: A man is a Vicar to the one whose place he takes and from whom he lawfully receives representative power. But no one truly takes the place of Christ or Peter unless he follows him in his character, since no other form of following is more relevant; nor does he otherwise receive representative power from God. Therefore, for that vicarial office, both conformity of character and the authority of the one appointing him are required.

If, therefore, the Pope is a most humble man, weighing worldly honors and the profit of the world as little; if he is a shepherd who earns his name from the "pasture" of the Word of God (the pasture concerning which the Lord said to Peter in John 21, "Feed my sheep"); if he feeds the sheep with the Word and the example of virtues, being made a "pattern for the flock" from the heart as Peter teaches in 1 Peter 5; if he is mild, patient, chaste, and labors anxiously and solicitously in the ministry of the church, regarding all worldly temporal things as dung <note>original: "sterccora"; echoing Saint Paul in Philippians 3:8</note>—then without a doubt he is the true Vicar of Jesus Christ, manifest to God and men, at least as far as outward human judgment can perceive.

<margin>Note what the Pope must do to be a true successor of Saint Peter.</margin>

But if he lives in a manner contrary to these virtues—since there is no "fellowship of Christ with Belial" (2 Corinthians 6), and Christ says in Matthew 12, "He who is not with me is against me"—how is he a true Vicar of Christ or Peter? Is he not rather the **Vicar of Antichrist**, opposing Christ in his character and in his life? Thus, when Peter's will and words were contrary to him (Matthew 16), even after the promise of the giving of the keys, Christ called him "Satan," meaning an adversary, saying: "Get behind me, Satan, you are a stumbling block to me, for you do not mind the things of God but the things of men." 

<gloss>An evil Pope</gloss>
<margin>He is the Vicar of Antichrist.</margin>

If, therefore, Peter—the first Vicar of Christ, chosen by him and spiritually appointed to the church—was called "Satan" by Christ because out of carnal affection he tried to dissuade him from undergoing the suffering of death, why should another, whose life is even more contrary to Christ, not truly be called Satan, and consequently the Antichrist, or his vicar, or the chief minister of the Antichrist?

<sig>P ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Whence</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus defines the Church as the totality of the predestined and argues that the Pope's legitimacy as Christ's Vicar depends entirely on whether his moral conduct mirrors that of Christ and Peter.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument from the preceding chapters regarding the nature of the Church. Having previously argued that Christ—not the Pope—is the true head of the Church, Hus now moves to criticize the conduct of contemporary clergy. He uses the writings of Church Fathers like Bernard of Clairvaux and Augustine to distinguish between "true pastors" and "hirelings" who serve only for their own gain.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

Wherefore Blessed Bernard says in his commentary on the Song of Songs: "Iniquity has gone out from your elder judges, O Lord, who seem to rule your people. <note>original: "Egressa est iniquitas a senioribus iudicibus tuis domine"; Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153) was a highly influential mystic and reformer. Hus frequently cites him to show that his own criticisms of the clergy were shared by respected saints.</note> Alas, alas, O Lord God, for those who seem to hold the primacy and rule the leadership in your church are the first to persecute you." He also says: "All are friends and all are enemies, all are necessary and all are adversaries, all are members of the household and none are peacemakers, all seek their own interests. They are ministers of Christ, yet they serve the Antichrist." <note>The "Antichrist" in Hus’s context often refers to a system or a person within the Church who acts in direct opposition to Christ’s teachings, particularly through greed or the pursuit of power.</note> Behold, this holy man clearly shows how evil prelates are falsely the friends, household members, and ministers of Christ. But in truth, they are enemies of Christ and ministers of the Antichrist.

Likewise, Augustine, in his commentary on John, shows who are not true pastors but hirelings, <term>hirelings [mercenarii]</term><note>A "hireling" or mercenary is someone who performs a religious duty only for pay, rather than out of love for God or the "flock."</note> saying and proving (in the Canon Law, Case 8, Question 1): "There are certain leaders in the church of whom the apostle Paul says: they seek their own things, and not those of Jesus Christ. What does it mean, then, to seek one's own things? It means not loving Christ freely, not seeking God for His own sake, following after temporal advantages, panting for profits, and desiring honors from men. When these things are loved by a leader, and God is served for the sake of these things, whoever is such a person is a hireling; let him not count himself among the children of God. For of such people the Lord says: 'Amen, amen, I say to you, they have received their reward.'"

<margin>Peter is the model of the good; Judas the model of the evil.</margin>
Wherefore Augustine himself says that just as the apostle Peter bore the model of all good people, and especially of Bishops, so Judas bore the figure of all evil people, and specifically of Priests. Wherefore in his commentary on John, regarding that word in John chapter 12: "For the poor you will always have with you, but me you will not always have," he says: "What does he mean? How is it to be understood: 'Me you will not always have'? Do not be terrified; this was said to Judas. Why then did he not say 'you have' [singular], but 'you [all] have' [plural]? Because there is not just one Judas, one evil person; he signifies the body of the evil, just as Peter signifies the body of the good." And further down: "In the person of Peter, the good in the church are signified; in the person of Judas, the evil in the church are signified. To them it was said: 'But me you will not always...'"

<column-break/>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER 14</header>

"...have.' What is 'not always' and what is 'always'? If you are good, if you belong to the body signified by Peter, you have Christ, both in the present and in the future. In the present through faith, in the present through the sign [of the cross], in the present through the sacrament of baptism, in the present through the food and drink of the altar. <note>Hus is listing the ways a believer "possesses" Christ through the life of the Church: faith, ritual, and the Eucharist.</note> You have Christ in the present, but you have him always, because when you depart from here, you will come to Him who said to the thief: 'Today you will be with me in Paradise.' But if you behave badly, you may seem to have Christ in the present because you enter the church, you mark yourself with the sign of Christ, you are baptized with the baptism of Christ, you mingle yourself with the members of Christ; in the present you have Christ, but by living badly you will not have him always." These are the words of Augustine, showing that the true Vicars of Peter are the righteous, and the Vicars of Judas Iscariot are the evil, and especially priests who are greedy, hypocritical, and blasphemous. And he shows the same in his commentary on the Psalm: "O God, do not hold your peace from my praise." <note>Psalm 109:1 (Vulgate 108:1)</note> And Ambrose says (in Case 22, the last Question): "Beware, brothers, of a lie," and further down, "for it is a lie to call oneself a Christian and not to do the works of Christ. For it is a lie to profess oneself a bishop, a priest, or a cleric, and to act contrary to this order." And in Case 2, Question 1: "All leaders are not regarded as leaders, for the name does not make the Bishop, but the life." And in Distinction 4, under the Heading: "He is not truly a priest who is only named a priest." And Chrysostom says: "Many are priests, and yet few are priests." <note>This quote from St. John Chrysostom was a popular medieval proverb suggesting that many hold the title of priest, but few possess the necessary virtue.</note>

<margin>A greedy Pope is the Vicar of Judas.</margin>
Behold, from these and other points mentioned, it is shown that a Pope is not a manifest and true successor of the prince of the Apostles, Peter, if he lives in a manner contrary to Peter; and if he seeks after greed, then he is the vicar of Judas Iscariot, who loved the reward of iniquity and betrayed Jesus Christ.

<margin>Which Cardinals are successors of the Apostles.</margin>
And by equal evidence, the Cardinals are not manifest and true successors of the college of the other apostles of Christ, unless they live in the manner of the apostles, keeping the commandments and counsels of the Lord Jesus Christ. For if they "climb in by another way" <note>An allusion to John 10:1, referring to those who enter the sheepfold improperly.</note> than through the door of the Lord Jesus...

<sig>P iii</sig>

<vocab>Bernard, Augustine, Peter, Judas Iscariot, Antichrist, Prelates, Bishop, Priest, Pope, Cardinals, Vicar of Peter</vocab>

<summary>Hus argues that the legitimacy of Church leaders—from the Pope and Cardinals down to local priests—depends on their moral conduct rather than their official titles. He concludes that a greedy or sinful leader is not a successor of St. Peter, but rather a "vicar" of Judas Iscariot.</summary>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of the ecclesiastical hierarchy. He previously established that true leadership in the Church is defined by following Christ’s example rather than merely holding a title. Here, he applies this logic to the Pope and Cardinals, using the Gospel of John and the book of Revelation to distinguish between true vicars and those he calls "thieves and robbers."</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

...of Jesus Christ. For if they ascend by another way than through the door of the Lord Jesus Christ, then they are thieves and robbers. <note>original: "fures & latrones"</note> As the Savior himself pronounces, saying of all such people: "As many as came are thieves and robbers," in the tenth chapter of John. <note>John 10:8.</note> Therefore, whoever says they will be the true and manifest Vicars of Christ, while knowing themselves to be in a state of crime, they are lying. For this reason, the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note> says how those of the Synagogue of Satan claim to be Jews and are not, but they lie. Hence, if cardinals gather ecclesiastical benefits and trade them, and accept money for their sale either by themselves or through others—and thus luxuriously devour and consume the goods of the poor, yet neither perform miracles, nor preach the word of God to the people, nor pray devoutly, nor fulfill the role of Deacons (whom the Apostles established in the sixth chapter of Acts) because they neither perform their duties nor live their lives—
<margin>The luxury of Cardinals is noted.</margin>
in what, I ask, are they the Vicars of the Apostles? Is it in this: that they gather benefits or seek out gifts like Gehazi? <note>original: "Giezi"; Gehazi was the servant of the prophet Elisha who was cursed with leprosy for greedily taking secret payments (2 Kings 5).</note> Or that they approach the presence of the Pope very early in the morning in the most proud display, with a family of expensive cavalry—not because of the distance of the place or the difficulty of the journey, but to show their magnificence to the world and their opposition to Christ and his Apostles? For the Apostles went about cities, towns, and castles on foot in humble dress, evangelizing the kingdom of God. Are they truly and manifest Vicars of the Apostles in this: that they allow themselves to be venerated by men with a bended knee? Or that they surround the Pope when foreigners visit, while he sits on high in splendid finery, even extending down to his feet and indeed beyond the seat, so that they may humbly seek to kiss his blessed feet with bended knees—as if the holiness of the Father Pope himself bubbled up all the way to the soles of his feet? But do the sick receive health from those feet? For Christ endured, but did not presume, to have his feet kissed by a woman, as is clear in the seventh chapter of Luke. <note>Luke 7:37-38.</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII</header>

...the seventh chapter of Luke. Because devout contrition and the healing and washing of the feet of Christ—that is, the poor—erase the crimes of the traveler. Truly, that kiss profits neither party toward salvation; for the one kissing will be blamed for culpable greed, or fear, or flattery, or blind and deceptive devotion. He will be more anxious or reverent in bending his knees and approaching the feet than he would be before the sacrament of the Savior’s body. <term>Sacrament of the Savior’s body [sacramento corporis Saluatoris]</term> <note>Hus is pointing out the irony that people show more outward reverence to the Pope's feet than they do to the Eucharist (the bread and wine), which was considered the actual body of Christ.</note> On the part of the Pope being kissed, it is entirely blameworthy, because he cannot be made equal to Christ so as to receive such great dignity.

But the Apostles kept in memory the word of Christ in the fourteenth chapter of Luke: "When you are invited to a wedding, do not sit down in the first place, lest perhaps a more honorable man than you be invited by him, and he who invited both you and him comes and says: 'Friend, give place to this man,' and then you begin with shame to take the lowest place." <note>Luke 14:8-9.</note> It is established from the sayings of the saints that Christ speaks of a spiritual calling, placing, and dining, not a physical one; for by the "wedding" is understood the marriage of Christ and the Church, which will be completed in eternity at the final supper. To this wedding, many are called, but few are
<margin>To recline in the last place.</margin>
chosen, as Christ speaks in the twenty-second chapter of Matthew. He, however, reclines in the last place who piously thinks himself the least of the elect, just like the Apostle of Christ, who saw secrets that a man is not permitted to speak, yet reckoned himself the least of the Apostles. <note>A reference to St. Paul's humility in 2 Corinthians 12 and 1 Corinthians 15:9.</note> If, therefore, the Pope reckons himself the "Most Holy Father," or pleasingly receives that adjective from his subjects, how is he not presumptuously choosing the first place? Hence, if there were humility in him, as there was in Blessed Gregory, he would very quickly
<margin>The Pope cannot be "most holy."</margin>
destroy, or strive to destroy, that style of address. <note>original: "stylū"; Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604) rejected exalted titles, preferring "Servant of the servants of God."</note> For it is not because he holds the place of Peter, nor because he has a large endowment, that he is "most holy." But if he follows Christ in humility, patience, and labor, out of a great bond of charity, then he is holy. But far be it from him to be "most holy," <note>original: "sanctissimus"; the superlative form of "holy."</note> because then he would be God Almighty, and consequently he would not be [a man].

<meta>The catchword at the bottom "effet" is a common printer's error or archaic spelling for "esset" (would be).</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus criticizes the worldly pomp of Cardinals and the Pope, specifically the practice of kissing the Pope's feet, arguing that true holiness comes from humility and following Christ's example rather than exalted titles or wealth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that the legitimacy of Church leaders—specifically the Pope and Cardinals—depends on their behavior rather than their titles. He argues that the Church can be governed by holy priests without the "pomp" of the high hierarchy.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

...would be the Vicar of Jesus Christ, who did not wish to have his feet kissed by a woman <note>original: "osculari fœminam per pedes suos"; a reference to Christ’s instructions after the resurrection (such as "Do not touch me" to Mary Magdalene), which Hus uses to contrast Christ's humility with the elaborate rituals of the Papal court.</note> after his resurrection, being immortal, undoubtedly blessed, so that he might take away blasphemous presumption from those miserable men who sometimes falsely pretend to be Christ's Vicars. But the feet of Christ and of those ascending with Christ are blessed, and are not food for worms, a rotting member, or the stinking sweat of worldly affections. From these things it can be proven whether that fourth point—that Cardinals are the manifest and true successors of the apostles of Christ—holds any truth. For a tree is known by the fruits it bears.

The Church could be governed equally well without a Pope and Cardinals, and even better, since cardinals, through their pomp, cause scandal <note>original: "scandalizent"; in this context, 'scandal' means causing others to stumble or lose their faith.</note> rather than building up the faithful of Christ.

### CHAPTER XV.

<margin>The Church was governed for three hundred years without Cardinals.</margin>
The fifth point is this: for the governance of the Church throughout the whole world, it is necessary <term>oportet: Hus is examining the claim that the office of Cardinal is "necessary" for the Church's existence.</term> that such men always remain—namely, Cardinals as the manifest and true successors to that office of the prince of the apostles, Peter, and the other apostles of Christ. In this point, that word "it is necessary" does not imply a necessity on the part of God governing the Church, who can govern the Church dispersed throughout the whole world without such successors; nor does it imply a necessity on the part of the Church, which can be governed equally well by holy Priests, even if those twelve cardinals were removed. Just as He governed it for three hundred years and more after the ascension of Christ. Let it not perhaps be said that this word "it is necessary" speaks of a necessity which the Savior noted in Matthew 18, saying: "It is necessary that scandals come, but woe to that man through whom the scandal comes." For the Savior spoke these words after rebuking his disciples who were seeking greatness among themselves, where he had adjured them, saying: "Amen I say to you, unless you are converted and be-
<meta>catchword: -come</meta>

---

<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XV</header>

-come as little children, you shall not enter the kingdom of heaven. And lest they should cause those believing in Christ to stumble through pride, He added: "Whoever shall cause one of these little ones who believe in me to stumble, it would be better for him that a millstone <term>mola asinaria: A large millstone turned by a donkey, signifying a heavy and shameful weight.</term> be hung around his neck, and that he be drowned in the depth of the sea." Treating this word, the Blessed Gregory <note>Pope Gregory I (the Great), an influential theologian and Doctor of the Church.</note> says in his *Book of Pastoral Care*, chapter 2: "Pastors," he says, "who are perverse in their morals, what they preach with words, they attack with their behavior. Thus it happens, when the pastor walks along a precipice, the flock follows him to the drop-off; for when the laity recognize the words of the Prelates, they are led to ruin by their deeds." <margin>Bad Prelates are a stumbling block to the people.</margin> Hence it is written by the prophet: "Bad priests are the cause of the people's ruin." Of whom the Lord says through the prophet: "They have become a stumbling block of iniquity to the house of Israel." Indeed, no one does more harm in the church than one who acts perversely while holding the name or rank of holiness. For no one dares to rebuke this person when they go astray, and the fault is widely extended as an example when the sinner is honored out of reverence for their rank. Truly, those unworthy of such weight would flee these dangers if they carefully considered the sentence of truth: "Whoever shall cause one of these little ones who believe in me to stumble, it would be better for him that a millstone be hung around his neck, and that he be drowned in the depth of the sea." <margin>Millstone.</margin> For by the "millstone" the circuit and sorrow of a worldly life is expressed, and by the "depth of the sea," final damnation is designated.

Therefore, he who is brought to the appearance of holiness, yet destroys others either by word or example, it would certainly have been better if his earthly acts had constrained him to death under a secular garment; because sacred offices, when held in guilt, show him as changeable to others. Surely, if he fell alone, a more tolerable punishment of hell would torment him. That holy Pope <note>Referring back to Gregory the Great.</note> knew the conditions and dangers facing a Prelate, and especially the Roman Pontiff <note>The Pope.</note>, since his sin of commission or omission would be a scandal to the entire Christian people. For it is said in Distinction 4: "The goodness of the Pope..."
<meta>catchword: is as if</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the Church does not fundamentally require the office of the Papacy or the Cardinalate to function, as it flourished for centuries without them. He warns that high-ranking officials who live immorally cause more harm than ordinary sinners because their rank gives them the appearance of sanctity while they lead others to spiritual ruin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of the high Church hierarchy. He has just been arguing that the office of Cardinal is not strictly necessary for the Church to function, as the Church existed for its first three centuries without them.</meta>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...is like the salvation of all, and his perversity leads to the damnation of innumerable persons, as stated in the Decree, 40th Distinction, the chapter beginning *If the Pope* <note>original: "Dist. xl. Si papa"; a reference to the Decretum Gratiani, a collection of canon law. This specific chapter famously states that the Pope can be judged by no one unless he is found to have strayed from the faith.</note>. If, therefore, the Pope and Cardinals—through pompous processions on horseback, shining garments, and exquisite, wondrous displays; through excessive care in amassing church benefices or money; and through a manifest ambition for honor greater than that of secular laymen—should cause those who believe in Christ to stumble, how could they necessarily always remain for the governance of the universal church as manifest and true successors in the office of Peter the Apostle and the other apostles of Christ? 

<margin>Note the office of the Apostles.</margin>
The office of the apostles was never anything other than following Christ in their conduct, teaching the church, baptizing people, healing the sick, casting out demons, offering the sacrifice of Christ’s body <note>The Eucharist or Mass.</note>, and exercising the power enjoined upon them for the perfection of the church everywhere. If, therefore, the Pope with his cardinals exercises this office, he holds the office of Peter. But if he and the cardinals decline from this, who can doubt that he falls away from the true Vicarship <term>Vicariatu: the office of acting as a representative or deputy</term> of Christ and His apostles?

<margin>The sixth point</margin>
And with equal evidence, this sixth point is declared, which is: No other such successors can be found or given on earth except the Pope as the head and the college of cardinals as the body of the Roman Church. Regarding this, I first note that Christ is the most sufficient head, as He proved for three hundred years and more, during which His church prospered; and His law is most effective for settling ecclesiastical matters, since God produced it for this very end. For Christ, with His law, does not fail to rule the church when devout priests minister that same law to the people, according to the opinion of the holy Doctors which they produced by the instinct of the Holy Spirit—as is clear concerning Saints Augustine, Jerome, Gregory, and Ambrose, who were given to the church for its doctrine after the time of the Apostles. 

<margin>The holy Doctors benefited the church more than many Popes.</margin>
Whence there is no doubt that the blessed Augustine benefited the church more than many Popes, and in doctrine perhaps more than all the cardinals from the first ones down to those now living. For he understood the scripture of Christ in the governance of the church more deeply, and defined

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER XV.

defined Catholic matter, purging and correcting heretical errors from the church. How then were those four Doctors not true representatives <note>original: "Vicarij"</note> of the Apostles and their manifest successors? Indeed, they were truer and more upright in their service to the people than the modern Pope with his Cardinals, who shine before the people neither with a holy life nor with doctrine. Whence I boldly assert that on whatever point these four holy Doctors agree, the Pope with his Cardinals cannot lawfully define the opposite as a matter of faith for the people. And the same is true of other saints, such as 

<margin>Greek Doctors.</margin>
John Chrysostom, John of Damascus, and Dionysius the Areopagite, who, taught by the Holy Spirit, enlightened the church of Christ with their knowledge and their morals.

And the argument against that first point proceeds principally like this: God is omnipotent; therefore, God can provide other true successors of the apostles besides the Pope and Cardinals. Therefore, other true successors of the Apostles can be found or given who are not the Pope or Cardinals; thus, that point is false. The first consequence is proven thus: if God cannot give other true successors besides the Pope and Cardinals, it would follow that the power of Caesar—a man and not God—by instituting the Pope and Cardinals, would limit the power of God. This consequence is false, and the reasoning is proven because the 

<margin>Constantine instituted the Pope.</margin>
Emperor Constantine instituted the Pope after three hundred years. For the Roman Bishop was a companion to other bishops until the "Donation of Caesar," by whose authority he began to rule as head. Whence the Decree, 96th Distinction <note>Another reference to the Decretum Gratiani, citing the famous, though later proven forged, "Donation of Constantine."</note>, which we cannot deny for very shame, speaks thus: "The Emperor Constantine, on the fourth day of his baptism, conferred a privilege upon the Bishop of the Roman church, so that throughout the whole world bishops should have him as head, just as judges have a king." In which privilege, among other things, it is read: "We grant him power and vigor and imperial honor, decreeing that his leadership..."

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the spiritual authority of the Church resides in those who follow the Apostles' lifestyle and teaching, rather than in the legal offices of the Pope and Cardinals. He cites the "Donation of Constantine" to argue that the Papacy's supreme legal status was a human invention of the fourth century, not a divine necessity.</summary>
