<meta>This page continues Hus’s critique of the requirement for absolute obedience to the Roman hierarchy. He begins to dismantle the legalistic definition of obedience used by the Church "doctors" by contrasting it with the example of Christ, who obeyed even those lower than Himself.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>J. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

is Jesus Christ, whom they did not dare to include [in their list of authorities to be obeyed], perhaps because they forgot to cast the slander upon us regarding the disobedience of Jesus Christ—to serve whom is to reign <note>original: "cui servire regnare est." This is a famous liturgical phrase suggesting that true freedom and power come from serving God.</note>, and whose obedience is so powerful that it is not worth obeying anyone except insofar as that obedience does not result in disobedience to our God.

Regarding that saying of the Doctors: "The Apostolic See of the Roman Church and the prelates must be obeyed by those of lower rank in all things," etc.

<margin>What obedience is.</margin>
We must now speak of obedience. It should be noted that "obedience" is first understood analogically <note>Used here to mean that the word has different levels of meaning depending on what is being described.</note>, or very generally, as the compliance of any creature in respect to the divine will, which all creatures obey. This happens either without resistance—as a stone obeys by falling or moving downward, fire by rising, and the sun by giving light, and so with other creatures—or it obeys with resistance, like the devil or a damned man, who must obey by suffering the punishment he deserves. In this way the Saints speak when they say that all things obey their Creator; only the sinful man does not obey—that is, he does not submit to the Creator’s command without the resistance of his own will.

<margin>Obedience as a virtue.</margin>
But then obedience, as it is an act of virtue or a virtue itself, is described by some as follows: "Obedience is the subjection of one’s own will to the judgment of a superior in things lawful and honest." Or: "Obedience is the habit of voluntarily carrying out the command of a superior in things lawful and honest." The first description is defined by the action, the second by the habit. From these descriptions it follows that there is no obedience in unlawful things; thus "obedience" implies something good, just as "disobedience" implies something evil. But it seems to me that the first description fails because obedience is a broader concept than "subjection," since obedience applies even to God, but subjection does not. For God "obeyed" the voice of man; for Joshua 10 says: *There was no day so long before or after, when the Lord obeyed the voice of a man and fought for Israel.* Yet God the Trinity was not subject or subordinate to man as an inferior. Nor is all obedience according to the judgment of a superi-

<meta>catchword: or</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XVII</header>

<margin>A superior ought to be subject to an inferior by the example of Christ.</margin>
-or, since Christ (as Luke 2 says) was subject to his parents. And it is certain that, just as among those born of women none greater than John the Baptist has arisen, so Christ was infinitely greater than Joseph or Mary. Since Christ did nothing except what he ought, it is clear that a superior ought to be subject or obedient to an inferior; for whatever the Fountain of Religion <note>Hus uses this title for Christ to emphasize Him as the source of all faith.</note> has taught as the most fundamental principle must be held. Hence Christ, who was of two natures, was doubly obedient: he obeyed God the Father in all things, as one less than the Father according to his humanity (for he himself said in John 14, *The Father is greater than I*). Second, he obeyed his parents, who were his inferiors, as has been said. And he was obedient even to others, willingly enduring suffering from them; and he is obedient to true and holy Christians, supplying their needs and fulfilling their will. Thus it is clear that it does not follow that "he obeys him, therefore he is inferior to him." Similarly, it does not follow that "he serves him, therefore he is inferior to him." For Christ obeyed and served another man; hence Isaiah 43 says: *You made me serve in your sins, you gave me labor in your iniquities; I am he who blots out your iniquities for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins; bring me into memory so we may be judged together.* And Matthew 20 says: *The Son of Man came not to be served, but to serve.* And of him the Apostle says in Philippians 2: *Christ emptied himself, taking the form of a servant.* And in John 13, he girded himself with a towel, put water in a basin, and washed the feet of the disciples. Whence he is not falsely, but truly, a bishop, the "servant of the servants of God" <note>original: "servus servorum dei." While this was a formal title for the Pope, Hus applies it here to Christ to show that true leadership is based on service, not power.</note>, not only the Roman one, but the general bishop of all churches; he himself is the Bishop of Prague <note>By calling Christ the "Bishop of Prague," Hus is likely suggesting that Christ, not the local ecclesiastical hierarchy he was clashing with, is the true head of his local church.</note>. But just as he is a servant or minister, not by civil compulsion (because an exacted active life was not suited to him), so he is the bishop of souls, not of secular riches or possessions; since this very Bishop only once mounted the colt of a donkey, humble and gentle, as Zechariah chapter 9 testifies. And Matthew 8 says: *Foxes have holes, and birds of the air have nests, but the Son*

<meta>catchword: of man</meta>

<summary>Hus defines obedience, distinguishing between the general obedience of nature and the virtuous obedience of the will. He argues that obedience does not imply inferiority, using the example of Christ's submission to His parents and His service to humanity to challenge the absolute authority of the Church hierarchy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's theological exploration of the nature of obedience. Having previously argued that Christ—the ultimate authority—practiced a form of obedience even toward those "below" Him, Hus now systematically deconstructs the definitions of obedience used by the Church legalists (the "Doctors"). He moves from a general definition to a specific moral framework based on Scripture and the writings of St. Thomas Aquinas.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>J. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

"But the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head" <note>original: "filius autem hominis nõ habet ubi caput suũ reclinet." A reference to Matthew 8:20, emphasizing Christ's voluntary poverty and humility.</note>. The Apostle [Paul] gives the reason for this in 2 Corinthians 8, saying: "For you know the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, who for our sakes became poor, so that through his poverty we might become rich."

The second description [of obedience provided by the Church Doctors] also fails based on what has already been said. It claims that "obedience is the habit <term>habitus: a stable disposition or character trait developed through practice</term> of executing the command of a superior." However, not all obedience is in relation to a superior who is being obeyed, nor is it always in relation to a command. For sometimes obedience is shown toward an inferior, as has been stated. It is also sometimes in response to counsel <note>In medieval theology, a "counsel" (consilium) is a godly suggestion for perfection, like celibacy, which is not strictly commanded as a law for everyone.</note>, as when a person obeys the counsels of God to which they are not bound under penalty of mortal sin. It is also shown toward a petition [a request], as God obeyed the petition of Joshua by commanding the sun to stand still at Gibeon and not move toward its setting, as seen in Joshua 10. Thus Jerome says in Letter 113: "God sometimes seems to obey the prayers of the Saints." And it is clear

<margin>Obedience to command, counsel, petition, and persuasion.</margin>
that obedience is sometimes the fulfillment of a command, sometimes of counsel, and sometimes of a petition (which is neither command nor counsel). And sometimes it is even the fulfillment of persuasion, just as the devil persuaded Christ in Matthew 4 to go with him to the holy city and to a very high mountain; Christ most virtuously consented to him in this and fulfilled his will <note>Hus uses this provocative example to show that "fulfilling the will of another" (obedience) can be virtuous even in strange circumstances, as long as it serves a higher divine purpose.</note>. According to this distinction, it would be said that to obey is to truly fulfill the will of another. Thus, obedience always implies a relationship of one person to another. This is not the case for other virtues, such as continence or temperance, which focus on one's own self-control.

<margin>Threefold obedience.</margin>
From this it is gathered that there is a threefold obedience, just as there is a threefold humility: The obedience of a superior to an inferior (this is the greatest); the obedience of an equal to an equal (this is the middle level); and the obedience of an inferior to a superior (this is the lowest). It is to this last type that the first definition applies: "Obedience is the submission of one's own will to the judgment of a superior, in things both lawful and honest." It can also be described thus: Obedience is an act of the will of a rational creature by which they voluntarily and discreetly submit themselves to their purpose. This term carries a good meaning, just as "to disobey" carries an evil 
<meta>catchword: evil,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XVII</header>
<sig>V</sig>
meaning. Both terms, however, imply a rational creature in a state of subjection. Secondly, obedience implies as its foundation an action, a passion <term>passion: here meaning "suffering" or "undergoing" something</term>, silence, or something of this sort upon which the command falls. Just as every sin is either disobedience itself or accompanies it—for every virtuous person obeys God—so every sinner is disobedient.

<margin>Obedience exists in the reason and the will.</margin>
Obedience exists in the reason and the will. It is in the reason, which discerns that one must obey in certain matters, and in the will, which consents to the one giving the command. Its effects are found in the other powers of the soul and outwardly in the resulting action. Since Scripture speaks of both "good obedience" and "bad obedience," the "good" is obvious. Regarding "bad" obedience, Genesis 3 says to Adam: "Because you obeyed the voice of your wife more than mine, the ground is cursed in your work." And Matthew 15: "Why do you transgress the commandments of God for the sake of your traditions?" and Acts 5: "We must obey God rather than men." Thus, whenever one obeys 

<margin>Bad obedience.</margin>
a human more than God—as Adam obeyed Eve—that is always "bad" obedience. Consequently, everyone who obeys wrongly is being disobedient to God. Thus it stands that the same man can be both obedient and disobedient at the same time, in respect to different commanders or different commands. It does not follow that "Sortes <note>A common medieval shorthand for 'Socrates,' used as a generic name like 'John Doe' in logical examples.</note> is disobedient, therefore Sortes is not obedient." Rather, it follows that "Sortes is not obedient to the one whom he is disobeying," or "he is not obedient in respect to the specific command he is breaking." It is clear that to obey, in its general sense, is to fulfill the will of the one commanding. This is done "well" when a human or created spirit existing in grace fulfills the lawful will of the commander. It is done "badly" when someone in a state of crime fulfills the commander's will regarding a given command—such as when someone living in lust fasts because they were commanded to—or secondly, when they fulfill an evil command that goes against God.

From this it is clear that it is impossible for a rational creature to be morally virtuous unless they are obedient to their God.

It should further be known that there is a threefold obedience according to St. Thomas [Aquinas] (Summa Theologica II-II, Question 104, Article 5): namely, sufficient, perfect, and 
<margin>Sufficient obedience.</margin>
indiscreet <term>indiscreet: lacking discernment; blind obedience</term>. Sufficient obedience is that which obeys only in those things to which
<meta>catchword: it is bound</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that true obedience is the fulfillment of another's will for a good purpose, noting that even God and Christ exhibited forms of obedience toward humans. He distinguishes between "good" obedience (to God) and "bad" obedience (to humans over God), asserting that moral virtue is impossible without primary obedience to the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>obedience, Jan Hus, St. Thomas Aquinas, types of obedience, virtue vs sin, biblical authority, Christ's humility, hierarchy of commands</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Hus’s systematic examination of obedience. After critiquing the narrow definitions provided by contemporary Church "Doctors," Hus now builds a positive framework for obedience based on the hierarchy of laws—natural, secular, and divine—drawing heavily on the authority of the Apostle Paul and St. Thomas Aquinas.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>J. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: while</meta>

to which one is bound by natural law, and when it does not exceed its own established limits, this is obedience. It is "sufficient" obedience when a person obeys in those matters to which they are expressly bound, and clear examples of this are found in Holy Scripture. For children are held to obey their parents, according to that word of the Apostle in Colossians 3: "Children, obey your parents in all things" <note>original: "Filij obedite parētibus ueſtris p̃ omnia."</note>. This is understood only in those matters which pertain to the outward discipline of life and domestic care, as Thomas [Aquinas] says in the place cited above <note>Hus is referring to the *Summa Theologica*, specifically the section on Justice and Obedience.</note>. Similarly, servants are held to obey their masters, according to that word of the Apostle: "Obey your earthly masters in all things." And in 1 Peter 2: "Servants, be subject to your masters with all fear, not only to the good and gentle, but also to the difficult" <note>original: "discholis," meaning perverse or harsh.</note>, which is to be understood only in those matters which pertain to the performance of lawful services and works, as Thomas says in the place cited above. Wives are held to obey their husbands, according to that word of the Apostle cited above, and Peter in the same place: "Women, be subject to your husbands in the Lord," which is to be understood only regarding those things which pertain to an outwardly lawful marital life. Similarly, all Christians, each in their own rank, are held to obey secular powers, according to that word of the Apostle in [Titus] 3: "Remind them to be subject to princes and powers" <note>original: "Admone illos, principib⁹ & poteſtatib⁹ ſubiectos eſſe." Hus cites this as Timothy, but the text is from Titus 3:1.</note>. And in Romans 13: "Let every soul be subject to the higher powers, etc." where the Apostle proves that every person ought to obey those in authority both in secular and spiritual matters, because they are ministers of God ordained for the protection, purification, and praise of the good, but for the correction, punishment, and vengeance of the wicked; for there is no power except from God, and he who resists the power resists the ordinance of God. Thomas agrees in the *Summa* (II-II, question 104, article 6), and all such subjection or obedience is understood to apply to those ranks in which they justly preside, and when they command just things, and not otherwise. The *Ordinary Gloss* <note>The *Glossa Ordinaria* was the standard medieval commentary on the Bible, found in the margins of most study manuscripts.</note> agrees on the passage "those [powers] that exist are from God." The Master of the Sentences <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose *Sentences* was the standard textbook for medieval theology students.</note> also agrees in the second book, the final distinction.

**Perfect obedience** is that by which the one obeying places their entire "willing and nilling" <note>A common medieval idiom meaning one's entire will, both what they desire and what they reject.</note> at the judgment of their Prelate <term>Prelate: a high-ranking church official, such as a bishop or abbot</term> to perform works,

provided, however, that the command does not oppose the divine will, or holy morals, or the necessities of life, or provided it is not irrelevant to the commands or counsels of the Lord Jesus Christ.

And because obedience concerns both precepts and counsels, the difference between an evangelical precept and a counsel must be noted, as they are distinguished by their opposites. For a **precept** is 

<margin>What a precept is.</margin>
the general teaching of God, binding every person always under the penalty of mortal sin from the very moment they fall away from it. Hence, the blessed who for a time in this life transgressed, sinned mortally during that same time. Just as the damned, through continual transgression, sin continually in hell.

<margin>What a counsel is.</margin>
A **counsel**, however, is a special teaching of God, binding only under the penalty of venial <note>A "venial" sin is considered less serious than a "mortal" sin, not resulting in the loss of the soul's grace, but still requiring spiritual correction.</note> sin, for the duration of this life. <vocab>Obedience: a central virtue Hus is redefining; Precept: a mandatory divine law; Counsel: a voluntary suggestion for spiritual perfection; Thomas Aquinas: 13th-century theologian; Matthew 19, Luke 9, Romans 13, Colossians 3, Titus 3: Biblical citations used to define the limits of authority; Master of Sentences: Peter Lombard's theological textbook; Ordinary Gloss: standard medieval Bible commentary.</vocab>

<summary>Jan Hus defines the limits of obedience to earthly authorities (parents, masters, husbands, and kings), arguing that such obedience is only required in lawful matters. He further distinguishes between "precepts," which are mandatory divine laws for everyone, and "counsels," which are voluntary paths to higher spiritual perfection, such as poverty and chastity.</summary>

<margin>XI.</margin> warn, concerning which it is said in Matthew 23, that the Pharisees say and do not <note>original: "Phariſæi dicunt & nō faciunt." Hus is citing Christ’s warning against hypocrisy.</note>. Therefore he advises the hypocrite, first cast out the beam from your own
<margin>XII.</margin> eye, Luke 6. Nor should worldly anxiety stifle the word, about
which Matthew 6 says: Be not anxious saying, what shall we
eat? etc. Nor fraternal correction <note>term: fraternal correction (correptio fraterna) — the practice of a Christian privately correcting a fellow believer’s sin before taking the matter to the community.</note>, about which Matthew 18 says: If your brother sins against you, go and correct him between you and him alone.
All these twelve similar things in their primary form
will not have to be in necessary effect, but in a secondary sense and form they will be observed, just like eternal mandates; but on the journey of life they are
<margin>The most convenient counsels of Christ</margin> most healthy for life. And would that the Clergy and the spiritually religious, who value the counsels of men, and all others leaning on human counsel, would attend to these counsels of the heavenly physician; because they are undoubtedly a preservative against possible sins, a purgative for those committed, and a conservative for health once attained. Therefore all travelers <note>The "traveler" (viator) is a common medieval term for a living person on their journey toward heaven.</note> are bound to these counsels or some of them in turn under the penalty of venial sin. For the judging of which, each will be the best discrete judge for himself, when he sees that through a defect in the observation of any of these he has fallen into sin; which he does not do quickly, except out of contempt for divine counsel. And it should be noted, that the twelfth counsel, namely fraternal correction, is sometimes a counsel, as when it concerns venial sins, and sometimes a precept, as when one corrects regarding mortal sins <note>In Catholic theology, "mortal" sins are grave offenses that sever one's relationship with God, while "venial" sins are lesser offenses.</note>. And this second mode pertains to all, and binds always, but not for all times, because it is for the place and time when correction is seen to be useful.
<margin>Triple obedience.</margin> Furthermore, it should be noted that human obedience is threefold, namely: Spiritual, Secular, and Ecclesiastical. Spiritual obedience is that which is owed purely according to the law of God, under which Christ and the apostles lived, and all individual Christians ought to live. Secular obedience is obedience owed according to civil laws. Ecclesiastical obedience is obedience according to the inventions of the priests of the church, beyond the express authority of scripture. The first obedience always excludes evil from itself, both on the part of the one commanding as 

<column-break/>

well as on the part of the one obeying. For he who commands according to the law of God, and he who obeys, proceeds rightly; and regarding both the commander and the obeyer, it is said in Deuteronomy 29: You shall do whatever the priests of the Levitical stock teach you, according to what I commanded them <note>original: "Facies quæcūꝙ docu. te ſacerd. Leuitıci, iuxta quod præcepı eis."</note>. Where it is touched upon, that the commander ought to command only things consonant with the law, and the obeyer ought to obey them to that extent, and never against the will of Almighty God. For this point I have elsewhere brought forward Augustine, Gregory, Jerome, Chrysostom, Isidore, Bernard, and Bede, along with Holy Scripture and the Canons. Which, so that I may be briefer, I omit; let only this saying of Isidore be included from Book 11, question 3: "He who is in charge, if he says or commands anything contrary to the will of God, or contrary to what is evidently commanded in the Holy Scriptures, let him be held as a false witness of God or as one committed to sacrilege."

### CHAPTER XVIII

Having already noted some things said about Obedience, I now wish to return to the saying of the doctors, in which it is clear that the Apostolic See of the Roman Church and the prelates are to be obeyed by inferiors in all things whatsoever, where a small good is not prohibited, or a pure evil is not commanded, but a medium thing, etc.
<margin>What the Canonists babble about the Apostolic See.</margin> Where it must be noted, regarding the Apostolic See, about which many—and especially the Canonists <note>term: Canonists — experts in Canon Law, the internal legal system of the Catholic Church.</note>—preach many things, and yet they are ignorant of what the Apostolic See actually is. For some estimate that it is properly a seat made of wood or stone, in which the Pope is accustomed to reside bodily. Others, that it is the Roman Curia <note>term: Curia — the administrative apparatus or "court" of the Holy See.</note>. Others, that it is the seat of Saint Peter in which he bodily resided. Others, that it is Rome. Others, that it is the power of the Pope. Others...

<meta>catchword: that it is the church (ꝙ ſit ec)</meta>

<summary>Hus defines three types of obedience (Spiritual, Secular, and Ecclesiastical) and argues that any command from a superior that contradicts God's law must be rejected. He then begins to critique the confusing and often superficial definitions of the "Apostolic See" provided by church lawyers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hus’s examination of the nature of the Church, moving from the definition of obedience to the definition of the "Apostolic" office. He specifically addresses the etymology of the word "Apostle" and the moral requirements for those who claim the title of "Apostolic," such as the Pope and bishops.</meta>

# 150 J. H. ON THE CHURCH

<sig>p</sig>
<meta>catchword: men</meta>

Whether it be the church or the basilica of St. Peter.

<margin>An Apostle is said to be "one sent."</margin>
But it should be noted that "Apostolic" is derived from an apostolic person, and an apostolic person is named from an Apostle. An Apostle, however, is said to be "one sent" <note>original: "missus"</note> by God. What God has sent, the Savior says in John 3: "I speak the words of God" <note>original: "Verba dei loquor." Hus is paraphrasing John 3:34.</note>. Hence he also says to the disciples in John 20: "As the Father has sent me, so I send you" <note>original: "Sicut miſit me pater, & ego mittam uos."</note>—specifically for the testimony of truth, for preaching the word of salvation, and for showing the way of blessedness to the people through life and doctrine. From this, any priest who does not seek his own glory but the honor of God, the progress of the church, and the salvation of the people—whoever does the will of God and exposes the craftiness of the Antichrist by preaching the law of Christ—he possesses the signs that show God has sent him. Hence, regarding glory, Christ says in John 5: "I do not receive glory from men." And in John 8: "I do not seek my own glory." Regarding the second point, he says in John 5: "I have come in my Father’s name, and you did not receive me; if another comes in his own name, you will receive him." <note>The OCR says "Ioã. vj." (John 6) but this quote is from John 5:43.</note> Regarding the third, he says in John 6: "I came down from heaven not to do my own will, but the will of him who sent me." Christ did the will of the Father because he sought the progress of the church and the salvation of the people. Regarding the fourth, he says in John 7: "The world hates me because I bear witness of it, that its works are evil." And perfectly Christ shows that he was sent by God because he did the works of the Father. Hence in John 10: "If I do not do the works of my Father, do not believe me."

<margin>Which priest should be believed to be sent by God.</margin>
"But if I do them, even if you will not believe me, believe the works." And it is clear that a priest’s holy conversation and fruitful labor in Christ’s word show the people that he is sent by God, because he does the works of the Father. Neither a Pope, a bishop, a priest, nor a preacher ought to be otherwise, unless he is thus sent by God. And hence the Apostle says in Romans 10: "How shall they preach unless they are sent?" On this, Blessed Augustine speaks in the *Sixty-Five Questions of Orosius* <note>A popular medieval dialogue attributed to Augustine, though now considered pseudo-Augustinian.</note>, when Orosius asks: "How can we know who are sent by God?"

<margin>Sent.</margin>
Augustine: "Recognize the one sent by God as him whom the praise, or rather the flattery, of a few

---

# 151 CHAPTER XVIII

<meta>catchword: whose</meta>

men did not elect, but rather him whom an excellent life and character, and the examination of Apostolic priests, or the judgment of the whole people, has approved; one who does not crave to be in charge, and who does not give money to receive the honor of the episcopate <note>term: episcopate — the office or rank of a bishop.</note>. For he who hurries to be in charge—as one of the Fathers elegantly expressed it—should know that he is not a bishop, for he desires to rule rather than to be of use." <note>Hus is playing on the Latin words "praeesse" (to be over/rule) and "prodesse" (to be useful/serve).</note> Thus says Augustine.

<margin>Who is apostolic.</margin>
Having established what an Apostle is in general, what "Apostolic" means can be understood. For one is called "Apostolic" who keeps the way of the Apostle. Just as a true Christian is one who imitates Christ in his character, so a true Apostolic is a priest who follows the doctrine of the apostles, living the life of an apostle and teaching their doctrine. Hence, any Pope is called Apostolic to the extent that he teaches the doctrine of the Apostles and executes it in his work. But if he puts aside the doctrine of the Apostles, teaching the opposite by word or deed, then he is truly called "pseudo-apostolic" <note>term: pseudo-apostolic — a false apostle or one who falsely claims apostolic authority.</note> or an apostate. Hence

<margin>Pseudo-apostolic.</margin>
it says in Distinction 79 <note>Hus refers to the *Decretum Gratiani*, the foundational collection of Canon Law.</note>: "If anyone is enthroned to the Apostolic See through money or human favor, or by popular or military riot, without a concordant and canonical election <note>term: canonical election — an election conducted according to the established laws (canons) of the Church.</note>, let him be held not as an Apostolic, but as an apostate." Since, therefore, there is a greater error in an "active" election when the electors are coerced by the devil to choose a person whom God rejects—which is clearly evident from his deeds and the omission of his spiritual office, being contrary to the life of the apostles—it follows all the more that such a person is to be held not as an Apostolic, but as an apostate.

<margin>The Apostolic See is the praiseworthy life of a priest.</margin>
The "Apostolic See," therefore, according to what has been said, can be called the life of a priest who effectively preserves the life of the apostle, just as the seat of the apostle is the life of the apostle. Hence Chrysostom says in Homily 25: "That virtue of each apostle is his throne, in which he was more perfect than the others. But of Christ, all virtues are as if one seat, because in every virtue He alone is equally perfect." See, this holy man rightly feels that the singular life of any apostle of Christ is his seat, in which he rested here by merit, by reason of...

<vocab>Apostolicus, Pseudapostolicus, Papa, Episcopus, Sacerdos, Ioannes, Augustinus, Chrysostomus, Gratianus, de ecclesia, missus, canonica electio</vocab>

<summary>Jan Hus defines the "Apostolic" office not by title or election alone, but by the imitation of the Apostles' lives and doctrines. He argues that a Pope or priest who fails to live holily or is elected through corruption is an "apostate" rather than "Apostolic."</summary>
<keywords>Apostolic See, true priesthood, Jan Hus, Antichrist, Canon Law, pseudo-apostles, St. Augustine, St. John Chrysostom, holy living, church corruption</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues Hus’s investigation into the true meaning of "Apostolic" authority. Having previously argued that being "Apostolic" requires being "sent" by God to live a holy life and teach the truth, he now refines the definition of the "Apostolic Seat" (Sedes Apostolica). He argues that this "seat" is not a physical throne in Rome, but rather the moral and spiritual authority to teach the Law of Christ—contrasting this ideal with the perceived corruption of the Roman Curia.</meta>

### BOOK III. ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: people</meta>

...whose seat now resides in the glory of Jesus Christ, according to that passage from Matthew 19: "You also shall sit upon twelve seats." Where, according to Augustine, by "twelve seats" is understood the placement of the apostles and of any predestined holy prelates. Since this placement exists in heavenly happiness, it cannot fail or be destroyed by tyrants. The number twelve, however, which is the number of universality <note>In medieval biblical interpretation, the number 12 often symbolized the whole world or the universal Church, representing the four corners of the earth multiplied by the three persons of the Trinity.</note>, does not signify those twelve apostles individually, since at that time [Judas] Iscariot reigned, and Paul would have been subject to him.

<margin>The Seat of Christ</margin>
Truly, the "Seat of the Majesty of Christ" is understood as the establishment of the eternal kingdom, from which he can never be removed. And that Seat of Christ is his internal seat. But his external seat, in which he rests, dwells, or resides through grace, consists of all the holy people.

<margin>The Seat of Satan</margin>
Just as, on the contrary, the "seats of Satan" in which he rests, dwells, or resides, are all the wicked. Hence in Revelation 2, regarding the Angel of the church of Pergamum, he who is like the Son of Man, who has the sword <note>original: "rompheam," referring to the *rhomphaia*, a large double-edged sword mentioned in the Greek text of Revelation.</note> sharp on both sides, said: "I know where you dwell, where the seat of Satan is." There the gloss <note>The "Gloss" refers to the *Glossa Ordinaria*, the standard medieval commentary on the Bible.</note> says: "Understand this to mean those in whom Satan rests; and you hold onto my name, and you did not deny my faith among you where Satan dwells."

But to the main point: the **Apostolic Seat** is the same as the **Chair of Moses**, about which the Savior says in Matthew 23: "The scribes and Pharisees have sat upon the chair of Moses." The Chair of Moses, however, is not Moses himself, nor is it a high wooden or stone seat of Moses, like that of a presiding judge. Nor is it the synagogue. Rather, that Chair is the *authority* of teaching and judging the people. This is shown from the words of Christ when he says: "Upon the chair of Moses." And it follows: "what they say"—that is, what they teach from the authority and doctrine of Moses—"do."

<margin>The Apostolic Seat is the authority of teaching according to the law of Christ.</margin>
Therefore, the Apostolic Seat is the authority of teaching and judging according to the law of Christ, which the apostles taught. In this seat should reside men who are wise and who fear the Lord—men in whom there is truth and who hate avarice. For thus it is written in Exodus 18, when Moses said to his father-in-law Jethro: "The people come to me..."

---

# CHAPTER XVIII.
<sig>X</sig>
<meta>catchword: ruling all</meta>

"...the people seeking the judgment of God, and whenever any dispute happens to them, they come to me so that I may judge between them and show the precepts of God and his laws." Behold the authority of judging and teaching the law of God! To which Jethro said: "Provide from all the people powerful men who fear the Lord, in whom there is truth and who hate avarice." And Moses did so. Regarding this, Lyra <note>Nicholas of Lyra (c. 1270–1349) was a Franciscan scholar whose literal commentary on the Bible was highly respected by Hus and, later, Martin Luther.</note> says: "'Powerful' to judge through wisdom and experience. Hence another translation has 'wise' where we have 'powerful,' and 'fearing God more than men,' in whom there is truth—namely of life, doctrine, and justice—and who hate avarice, because greedy men are easily turned from justice by bribes." This is what Lyra says.

<margin>The Roman seat [turns] into the chair of the Pharisees.</margin>
Would that that Chair now had such men! And where can they be discerned? Certainly in the Roman Curia <note>The administrative and judicial body that assists the Pope in governing the Church.</note>, where they preside over the Chair of Saint Peter—that is, they sit in the authority of the Apostles, which is the authority of judging spiritual matters and teaching the law of the Lord Jesus Christ—if only that avarice, injustice, and pride were excluded, and a holy life flourished. The Savior himself testifies, saying in Matthew 23: "The scribes and Pharisees sit upon the Chair of Moses; therefore, observe and do all things whatsoever they say to you, but do not act according to their works, for they talk but do not act." Behold, here he touches upon a life lacking the works of the law. "For they bind heavy and unbearable burdens and lay them on the shoulders of men." Behold, indiscreet doctrine and injustice! "But they will not move them with their own finger." Behold, a life of luxury! "They do all their works to be seen by men." Behold, vain glory! For they broaden their phylacteries <term>phylacteries: small leather boxes containing Hebrew texts worn for prayer; Hus uses this term to mock the elaborate and oversized Papal "Bulls" or official decrees that spread throughout the world</term> in [Papal] Bulls throughout the whole world, as if they themselves were the most prominent observers of the law of the Lord—there is hypocrisy.

<margin>How remarkably he touches upon the Roman prelates.</margin>
They enlarge the fringes <note>A reference to the tassels on garments required by Jewish law, which the Pharisees made excessively large to show off their piety.</note> with which they cover their mules. They love the first places at feasts for the sake of pleasure and honor, and the first seats in the synagogues—that is, in the gatherings of the churches—because this one wants to be a Cardinal, that one a Patriarch, and that one an Archbishop. And they love greetings with genuflection in the marketplace—that is, in public—and to be called by men "Rabbi," that is, "Our Master" <note>original: "Magister Noster," a specific title for a Doctor of Divinity that Hus frequently criticized for its arrogance.</note>, ruling all...

<summary>Jan Hus defines the "Apostolic Seat" not as a physical location in Rome, but as the moral authority to teach Christ's law. He uses the biblical "Chair of Moses" as a parallel to critique the Roman Curia, accusing the contemporary higher clergy of hypocrisy, luxury, and pride similar to the Pharisees.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hus’s critique of church hierarchy. He has just finished explaining that the "Apostolic Seat" is a spiritual authority found in holy living rather than a physical location. He now turns his attention to those who occupy these seats of power—the Roman Curia—and warns that if they live wickedly, their "seat" is not that of Christ, but of "pestilence" or even Satan himself.</meta>

### I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...so too those ruling the church of Christ, whence they also call the **Roman Curia** <note>The administrative apparatus and court of the Pope in Rome.</note> the mistress of the churches.

And if these things are granted as possible, they themselves are the seats, not of Christ, but of **Satan**, sitting according to their own life in the **Seat of Pestilence**.

<margin>The Seat of Pestilence.</margin>
Regarding this, the Psalmist, speaking of Christ Jesus, says: "Blessed is the man who has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly, nor stood in the way of sinners, and has not sat in the **seat of pestilence**" <note>original: "cathedra pestilentiæ," from Psalm 1:1. While many modern translations say "the seat of the scornful," medieval authors followed the Latin Vulgate's "pestilence."</note>. Where **Augustine** says of our Lord Jesus Christ, the lordly man, it must be understood that he does not sit in the **Seat of Pestilence**. He did not desire an earthly kingdom with the pride that is the seat of pestilence; therefore, it is rightly said again, because there is hardly anyone who lacks the love of ruling and does not crave human glory. **Pestilence** is a disease spreading widely and involving all, or nearly all. However, it will be more fittingly understood as the **Seat** of destructive doctrine, whose speech "creeps like a cancer." These are the words of the Blessed Augustine, calling the **Seat of Pestilence** the lust for power and destructive doctrine, in which Chair the Elders of the church sit when they wish to rule in a secular fashion, and when they teach their own mandates and doctrines more firmly than they observe the mandates of God.

<margin>Those who truly sit in the chair.</margin>
However, he truly sits in the **Chair**—that is, in the authority and doctrine of the law—who teaches the law and carries out the mandates of the law. Whence Augustine, upon that passage of the Psalm, "His will is in the law of the Lord," says: "It is one thing to be **in** the law, another to be **under** the law. He who is in the law acts and moves according to the law. He who is under the law is moved according to the law." Behold a plain explanation of the fact. "Whoever therefore shall do and teach them, he shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven," says the Savior in the fifth chapter of Matthew. Truly, therefore, he sits in the **Chair of Moses** or of **Peter** who lives well and teaches well within the authority of Scripture, and who adds nothing foreign to the law. Nor does he seek profit or pride from the Chair. On the contrary, he sits poorly in the Chair who either teaches poorly or lives poorly, or who teaches good things but lives poorly, or who neither teaches good things nor lives well; and such, alas, are many, who seek those things which are their own, and not those which belong to

<meta>catchword: Jesus</meta>

***

## CHAPTER XVIII
<sig>X ij</sig>

**Jesus Christ**. Of whom our Savior says: "The scribes and Pharisees have sat upon the **Chair of Moses**; therefore, do what they say, but do not do what they do, for they speak and do not act." And in the same place he later says: "Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, who shut the kingdom of heaven before men; for you yourselves do not enter, nor do you allow those entering to go in." Behold the other part; and in the fifteenth chapter of Matthew: "You have made the mandate of God void for the sake of your tradition. Hypocrites, well did Isaiah prophesy of you in the twenty-ninth chapter, saying: 'This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me; in vain do they worship me, teaching the doctrines and mandates of men.'"

<margin>Those who sit poorly in the chair.</margin>
Therefore, they sit poorly in the **Chair of Moses** and of **Peter** or of **Christ** who teach good things and do not do them. Worse are they who neither teach nor do. Worst of all are those who prohibit good things from being taught. And more than "worst of all" are those who live wickedly, forbid the teaching of good things, and teach their own [doctrines]. All such are thieves and robbers. For as the Shepherd says: "All who come [otherwise] are thieves and robbers" <note>original: "fures sunt & latrones," quoting John 10:8.</note>. For all such as those mentioned above have come into the sheepfold apart from Christ; they have climbed up into the **Chair** from elsewhere; they seek their own interests, and therefore they may also be called **hirelings**, not shepherds. Whence the Savior says in John 10, showing who is a hireling and not a shepherd:

<margin>Who are the hirelings in the church.</margin>
"The **hireling** <note>original: "mercenarius," referring to someone who works only for pay rather than out of care for the sheep.</note>, and he who is not the shepherd, whose own the sheep are not, sees the wolf coming and leaves the sheep and flees, and the wolf snatches and scatters the sheep." Where Augustine in a homily upon John says: "The hireling does not bear a good persona here, yet he is useful in some way; nor is he called a hireling unless he receives a wage from the one hiring him. Who then is that hireling, and the culpable hireling? Truly here, brothers, let the Lord himself shine upon us, so that we may understand the hireling and not be hirelings ourselves. What then is a hireling? There are certain **Prelates** in the church of whom Paul says: 'seeking their own, not the things of Jesus Christ.' What then is 'seeking their own'? Not loving Christ freely, not seeking God for God's sake, but pursuing temporal advantages, gaping after profits, and desiring honors from men." These things, when

### I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...loved by the Superior, and for these reasons God is served; whoever is such is a **hireling**, and should not count himself among the sons. For of such people the Lord says: "Truly I say to you, they have received their reward" <note>original: "receperunt mercedem suam," quoting Matthew 6:2.</note>. This is what **Augustine** says. Regarding the fact that such men sit in the **Chair** <note>original: "Cathedra," referring to the seat of authority or the office of a teacher.</note>, he says the same elsewhere with a few things interspersed: "Hear how hirelings are necessary, for many in the church chasing after earthly comforts still preach Christ, and through them the voice of Christ is heard; and the sheep follow not the hirelings, but the voice of the Shepherd through the hireling." Hear how the hirelings were identified by the Lord himself: "The **Scribes and Pharisees**," he said, "sit upon the **Chair of Moses**; do what they say, but do not do what they do" <note>Quoting Matthew 23:2-3.</note>. What else did he say but "Hear the voice of the Shepherd through the hireling"? For by sitting in the Chair of Moses they teach the law of God; therefore, God teaches through them. But if they wish to teach their own things, do not listen, do not do them; for certainly they seek their own interests, not those of Jesus Christ. Yet no hireling has dared to say to the people of God: "I seek my own things, not those of Jesus Christ." Here Augustine concludes at the end of that sermon, saying: "Behold why the hireling is said to flee when he sees the wolf. Why? Because he has no care for the sheep. Why has he no care for the sheep? Because he is a hireling. What is a hireling? He is one who seeks temporal reward; he shall not dwell in the house forever." Here Augustine shows those who, while existing now in the church as hirelings, sit in the **Chair**—that is, in the authority of teaching the law of God. And again, in his commentary on John regarding that final word of John, "Simon Peter hauled the net full of great fish," he says: "The one who undoes with his deeds what he teaches with words is the least." And further on, to show that these "least" are the rejected ones—those who teach good things by speaking the word but undo them by living badly—he says they will not be "least" in eternal life, but will not be there at all. When he had said, "he shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven," he immediately added: "Unless your righteousness exceeds that of the scribes and pha...

<meta>catchword: risees,</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER XVIII</header>

...risees, you shall not enter the kingdom of heaven." Those are certainly the scribes and pharisees who sit in the Chair of Moses. And of them he says: "Do what they say, but do not do what they do," for they speak and do not act; they teach with words and undo with their behavior. This is from Augustine. And he speaks similarly in his commentary on the Psalm *Blessed are the undefiled*, on that verse: "Blessed are they who search his testimonies and seek him with their whole heart."

<margin>What the Chair of Moses or Peter is.</margin>
From what has already been said, it is clear that the **Chair of Moses** or the **Apostolic Seat** <note>original: "Sedes Apostolica." Hus is defining the "Apostolic Seat" not as a physical throne in Rome, but as the authority to teach God's word.</note> is the authority of teaching the law of God, or the lineage of holy Popes, or of the bishops succeeding them. This lineage provides for what it perceives to be most excellent for the honor of God, most useful for the Holy Church, and most healthy for both the leader and the subject—not by appointing the unworthy, not by passing over the more humble, and not by thoughtlessly limiting anyone’s ecclesiastical office through profit, blood relations, or any private affection.

<margin>Which mandates are true and to be received with reverence.</margin>
And it is further clear what **Apostolic mandates** are, as the **Lord of Lincoln** <note>Hus refers to Robert Grosseteste (c. 1175–1253), the Bishop of Lincoln, who famously resisted a papal command to appoint the Pope's nephew to a local position.</note> says in his response to the letters of the Roman Pontiff regarding the appointment of a certain relative as a **Canon** <note>A member of the clergy serving a cathedral.</note> of the church of Lincoln. He responds thus: "To Apostolic mandates," he says, "I obey with filial affection, entirely, devotedly, and reverently. But to those things which oppose Apostolic mandates, I stand opposed and resist with fatherly zeal and honor; I am bound to both by reason of my status as a son by divine command. For **Apostolic mandates** are not, and cannot be, anything other than the Apostolic doctrines and those of our Lord Jesus Christ himself, the Master of the Apostles." Thus says the Lord of Lincoln.

Therefore, a faithful disciple of Christ must weigh, when an "Apostolic" mandate is issued, whether it is expressly a mandate of some Apostle, or of the law of Christ, or has a foundation in the law of Christ; and once that is recognized, he must reverently and humbly obey such a mandate. However, if he truly recognizes that a mandate of the Pope contradicts a mandate or counsel of Christ, or tends toward some evil for the church, then he must boldly resist,

<sig>X iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: resist,</meta>

<summary>Hus uses Saint Augustine and Robert Grosseteste to argue that the "Apostolic Seat" is defined by teaching the truth, not by the office itself. He asserts that a believer is obligated to disobey a Pope if the Pope's commands contradict the teachings of Christ.</summary>

### I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...to stand firm, lest he become a partner in crime by consenting. Wherefore, trusting in the Lord and in Christ Jesus, who powerfully and wisely protects those who profess His truth and rewards them with the prize of eternal glory. [I resisted] the Bull of Pope Alexander V, which was restored—and which Lord Zbyněk <note>Jan Hus refers to him as "Suinco," the Latinized form of Zbyněk Zajíc of Hazmburk.</note>, Archbishop of Prague, acquired in the year of our Lord 1409. In this Bull, he commands that no preaching or sermons be delivered to the people by anyone (even if they are protected by an Apostolic or any other grant <term>indulto: a specific favor or privilege granted by the Pope</term>), except in cathedrals, collegiate,

<margin>The word of God must be preached everywhere following the example of Christ.</margin>

parochial, or monastery churches, or their cemeteries. This mandate is contrary to the deeds and words of Christ and His apostles; it is not Apostolic, since Christ preached to the people on the sea, in the desert, in the fields, in houses, in synagogues, in villages, and in the streets. He commanded His disciples, as stated in the last chapter of Mark: "Go into the whole world and preach the gospel to every creature." They, setting out, preached everywhere—that is, wherever the people wished to hear—with the Lord cooperating. Therefore, this mandate is to the detriment of the Church, for it binds the word of God so that it may not run freely. Thirdly, it is to the prejudice of the Chapels that have been erected and confirmed by the Diocesans, and reasonably privileged by the Apostolic See for the preaching of the word of the Lord within them <note>Hus is specifically defending the Bethlehem Chapel in Prague, where he preached in the Czech language.</note>. For no profit is seen to come from that mandate, but rather a deceptive and inconsistent illusion. This is because places reasonably set apart for divine ministry and privileged for the divine word are stripped of their lawful liberties due to private affection, injurious or persistent petitioning, or for the sake of temporal gain. Therefore, I appealed from that mandate to Alexander himself for his better information; but while I was pursuing the appeal, the Lord Pope recently 

<margin>The unjust tribulation of this author.</margin>

passed away. And with no hearing granted to me in the Roman Curia, Lord Zbyněk, the Archbishop of Prague, acquired processes <term>processus: formal legal proceedings or decrees of excommunication</term> that burdened me. From these, in the year of our Lord 1410, 

<meta>catchword: to Pa-</meta>

***

# CHAPTER XVIII

to Pope John XXIII <note>The Pisan claimant to the papacy during the Western Schism.</note> I appealed. For two years, he granted no hearing to my advocates and proxies, and in the intervening time, I was further burdened by legal processes. Since, therefore, an appeal from one Pope to his successor did not help me, and to appeal from a Pope to a Council is a long and uncertain way to seek help for a grievance, I finally appealed to the head of the church, the Lord Jesus Christ. For He is more excellent than any Pope in deciding a case, since He cannot err, nor deny justice to one who rightly seeks it, nor can He—by His own law—condemn a man without merit.

<margin>The Indulgence Bulls of Pope John XXIII.</margin>

Furthermore, I resisted the ordinance concerning the indulgences made or promulgated in the year of our Lord 1412 through the Bulls of Pope John XXIII, about which I have written enough elsewhere. For the Pope can

<margin>What the Pope can lawfully command.</margin>

command nothing lawfully except for the destruction of malice and the building up of the church; this should be held universally. The Apostle testifies to this in 2 Corinthians 10, saying: "The weapons of our warfare are not carnal, but powerful through God for the destruction of fortresses, by which we demolish counsels and every high thing that exalts itself against the knowledge of God." And in the last chapter of 2 Corinthians, he says: "Lest I should act more harshly according to the power which the Lord gave me for building up, and not for destruction." Wherefore the Bishop of Lincoln <note>Robert Grosseteste (c. 1175–1253), a famous English reformer and scholar often cited by Hus.</note> writes in a letter to the Pope: "The Apostolic See cannot create a schism, for to it was handed all-encompassing power by the Holy Lord of Saints, Jesus Christ, as the Apostle testifies, for building up and not for destruction." And further on, he says: "Nor can your discretion decree anything harsh against me because of this, for all my speech or action in this matter is neither contradiction nor rebellion, but the filial honor due to the Father and Mother—that is, to Christ and the Church—because it is the observance of the divine mandate."

<margin>Appeal from the Pope to Christ.</margin>

Briefly summarizing, I say that the holiness of the Apostolic See can only act for building up and not for destruction; for this is the fullness of power: to be able to do all things for building up. However, these which he calls <insert>Appeal to</insert> punishments are not for building up, but for manifest destruction; therefore the most blessed Apostolic See cannot [command] them. These are the words of Lincoln. To Christ,

<summary>Jan Hus recounts his legal battle against the 1409 papal bull restricting preaching and explains his decision to bypass the Roman Curia by appealing directly to Jesus Christ. He argues, citing Robert Grosseteste, that any papal command leading to the "destruction" rather than the "edification" of the church is invalid.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the duty of the clergy and the fallibility of the Pope. Now, he introduces historical and theological examples of popes who have erred or been divinely judged, specifically focusing on the conflict between Pope Innocent IV and Robert Grosseteste, the Bishop of Lincoln.</meta>

### 160 I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...because he appealed from Pope Innocent to the tribunal of Christ. Wherefore the historian from Chester <note>original: "Cestrensis," referring to Ranulf Higden (c. 1280–1364), a Benedictine monk and chronicler from Chester known for his work *Polychronicon*.</note> narrates in his seventh book how, when Robert of Lincoln <note>Robert Grosseteste (c. 1175–1253), Bishop of Lincoln, a famous scholar and reformer who famously resisted papal corruption.</note> passed away, a voice was heard in the Pope’s court: "Come, you wretch, to judgment." And the next morning, the Pope was found lifeless, as if struck in the side by the point of a staff. Although the man from Lincoln shone brightly with miracles, he was nonetheless not permitted by the [papal] court to be entered into the Catalog of Saints. And it is clear that a Pope can err, and all the more gravely in that he might multiply his sin more abundantly in a possible case, both intensely

<margin>The Pope can err most seriously.</margin>

and "insuperably," as Bernard says in his book to Pope Eugenius <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153) wrote *De Consideratione* to his former student, Pope Eugenius III, advising him on the dangers of the papal office.</note>. His error is most "abundant" and intense if it concerns the removal of spiritual goods related to the care of souls; and it is "insuperable" if no one dares to contradict him—partly because of his alliance with the secular arm <term>secular arm: the use of state or military power to enforce church decrees</term>, partly because of the cloaked censures thundered against the "sons of obedience," and partly because of the ecclesiastical promotions and dignities which he provides for his accomplices. Wherefore, just as a Papal state that benefits the Church deserves more merit, so a perverted Papal state in a man who abuses that office deserves more blame, as it infects the Church more deeply.

<margin>Signs by which the Pope's failings are noted.</margin>

Furthermore, the **first sign** of a Pope’s failure is if, having set aside the law of God and the devout professions of the Gospel, he attends instead to human traditions—an issue for which Bernard rebuked Eugenius.

The **second sign** is if the Pope and the ecclesiastical superiors, abandoning the lifestyle of Christ, are entangled in the world in a secular manner. The **third sign** is if he places "merchants of the world" in the ministry of Christ, and strives chiefly to continue a secular life by burdening poor churches. The **fourth sign** is if, by his mandate or by the placement of inept men in pastoral care, he deprives souls of the word of God, which is necessary for their salvation. Considering this, the man of Lincoln refused to admit a certain relative of the Pope as a canon <term>canon: a priest serving in a cathedral or collegiate church</term> of the church of Lincoln, giving, among other things, a probable reason. "After the sin of Lucifer," he said, "which will be the same at the end of time during the reign of the Son of Perdition, the Antichrist, whom the Lord JESUS will slay with the breath of his mouth, there is not, nor can there be, any other kind [of sin] opposed or...

<meta>catchword: contrary</meta>

<column-break/>

### CHAPTER XVIII 161

...contrary to apostolic or evangelical doctrine—and so hateful, detestable, and abominable to the Lord Jesus Christ Himself, and so ruinous to the human race—as to kill and destroy souls that ought to be quickened and saved through the office and ministry of pastoral care, by defrauding them of that pastoral ministry.

<margin>It is most contrary to the Gospel to shear the sheep and not feed them.</margin>

Those who are established in the power of pastoral care are known to commit this sin (according to the most evident testimonies of Holy Scripture) when they satisfy their own fleshly delights from the milk and wool of Christ’s sheep, and do not provide the necessary ministry of the pastoral office for the eternal salvation of those sheep. For the non-administration of pastoral ministries is itself, by the testimony of Scripture, the slaughter and destruction of Christ's sheep. Moreover, it is manifest that although these two kinds of sins are the worst in different ways, and immeasurably exceed every other kind of sin, it is because they are directly contrary to two things that are (though differently) the very best. For that which is contrary to the best is the worst.

And since in good things the *cause* of the good is better than the *effect*, and in evil things the *cause* of the evil is worse than the *effect*, it is clear that those who introduce such "worst of slayers" [of souls] into the Church of Christ are worse than the slayers themselves. They are closer to Lucifer and the Antichrist, and in this wickedness they excel them by degrees; for, having been given a greater and more divine power for building up and not for tearing down, they are all the more bound to exclude and root out such "worst of slayers" from the Church of God. These are the words of the man of Lincoln.

<margin>The slaughter and destruction of Christ’s sheep.</margin>

He simply means, in short, that the slaughter and destruction of Christ's sheep are two of the worst sins (though they differ), because the quickening of the sheep through grace and their glorification are the two greatest goods for the sheep (though they also differ), to which that slaughter and destruction are opposed. And because slaughter is opposed to quickening, and destruction to glorification, it follows that they are...

<sig>Y</sig> <meta>catchword: sins</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that a Pope is capable of grave error, using the historical conflict between Bishop Robert Grosseteste and Pope Innocent IV as proof. He outlines four signs of a failing papacy, focusing on the neglect of pastoral care as a sin worse than those of Lucifer or the Antichrist.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of the papacy and the definition of the "Apostolic See." On the previous page, Hus discussed how popes can err and how spiritual harm—the "killing of souls"—is the most serious sin. He now moves into the practical ways this harm occurs through the corrupt distribution of church offices and a theological examination of what the "See of Peter" actually represents.</meta>

### 162 JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

...these two sins are more serious to the degree that they oppose the greater goods. And since God is the cause of those goods through Himself, it follows that those who kill and destroy the sheep <note>original: "interemptores ovium," a reference to the congregation being the flock of Christ.</note> are worse than others, to the extent that the killing and destruction of the sheep are worse sins. And it is clear that the worst ministers of Antichrist and Satan are those who kill souls.

<margin>The Pope harms the church through the distribution of benefices.</margin>
From these things, it is held that to rebel against those who have deviated from the path is to obey Christ the Lord. This deviation arises most especially from "provisions" <note>provisions: the practice of the Pope appointing someone to a church office before it even became vacant.</note> that smack of personal favoritism. Therefore, I call the world to witness that the Papal distribution of benefices <term>benefices: church offices that come with an income or land, often granted as political favors.</term> sows mercenaries <note>original: "mercenarios." Hus uses this to describe clergy who serve only for money rather than for the spiritual care of the people.</note> far too widely throughout the church on the part of the Popes. It gives occasion for the magnifying of their vicarious power, for over-valuing worldly dignity, and for affecting a "fantastic" <gloss>imaginary or false</gloss> sanctity. Those doctors <note>doctors: high-level theologians and scholars.</note> who expect temporal rewards from the Pope, or who serve his power out of slavish fear, and thus claim that his power is "incomprehensible," that he is "impeccable" <gloss>incapable of sinning</gloss>, or "incorrigible," and that he can lawfully do whatever he pleases—they are the false prophets and false apostles of the Antichrist.

<margin>What is the apostolic see?</margin>
From what has been said, it is now clear that the **Apostolic See** is the authority of judging and teaching the law of Christ. Or, in a second sense, it is the lineage of holy Popes who succeed one another, as has been said. And it is in this way that the Apostolic See is understood in *Distinction 22, Sacrosancta*, where Pope Anacletus <note>Anacletus: historically considered the third Pope, though the "Decretals" Hus cites were later found to be medieval forgeries.</note> says: "Truly this Apostolic See, the **head and the hinge**, as was stated before, was established by the Lord and by no other; and just as a door is governed by a hinge, so by the authority of this holy Apostolic See, all other churches are governed by the Lord’s disposition." This Pope intended to mean that he himself was the head and the hinge—the head in presiding and the hinge in governing. But he has a rather weak argument to prove his intention. For he argues from a similarity, saying: "Just as a door is held by a hinge, so by the authority of this holy Apostolic See, all churches are governed by the Lord’s disposition." It would be enough to argue that the Pope and the cardinals should rule themselves well, lest they fail to lead.

<column-break/>

### 163 CHAPTER XIX
<meta>catchword: question</meta>

...lest they fail to lead. For just as only one door is governed by one hinge, so by their own doctrine and authority, it would be good if they themselves were governed well, and afterward that the other churches were also governed. For how do those men govern our **Prague church**, except by distributing benefices to the greedy and collecting money? But where is the teaching or the other ministry of power?

<margin>See as power.</margin>
Thirdly, the "See" is taken to mean "power," and it is used in that way in *Distinction 22, Inferior*, where Pope Nicholas says: "An inferior seat <note>original: "sedes," translated here as "seat" or "see" interchangeably.</note> cannot absolve a superior one," and he concludes, though quite impertinently, from that passage in Isaiah 10: *Shall the axe boast against him who cuts with it, or shall the saw exalt itself against him who pulls it?* <note>Isaiah 10:15. The Pope uses this to argue that a lower-ranking priest cannot judge a higher-ranking one.</note> saying:

"These things having been recalled from Divine Scripture, we have shown more clearly than the sun that no one who is of lesser authority can subject him who is of greater power to his judgments, or subdue him by his own definitions." Behold, there he calls the man of lesser authority the "inferior seat" and the man of greater authority the "superior seat."

<margin>The See of Peter is without spot or wrinkle.</margin>
But how is "See" understood when Pelagius <note>Pope Pelagius II (reigned 579–590).</note> says in *Distinction 21*: "Therefore, the first See of the Apostle Peter is the Roman Church, having no spot, nor wrinkle, nor anything of that kind." Behold, there the **See of Peter** is said to be the Roman Church. But of what is it verified that it is "without spot or wrinkle"? Since neither the Pope alone is that see without the church, nor the Pope with the Cardinals (who are not without spot), nor a church made of stone. For certain, I cannot conceive of it in any other way than that the "See" consists of all those who finally imitate the life of Peter according to the law of Christ. For they will first be in their homeland <note>original: "in patria," a theological term for Heaven, the true homeland of Christians.</note> without spot or wrinkle. But whether this was the intention of that Pope, I do not know. Wherefore Augustine, in his commentary on the Psalm *I was glad in these things that were said*, etc., regarding the verse *The seats sat in judgment*, speaks to this purpose: "How did the seats sit there in judgment? A wondrous enigma, a wondrous..."

the question remains, if it is not understood, what the See is, which the Greeks call a "throne." The Greeks call chairs "thrones," as they are honorable. Therefore, my brothers, it is no wonder if people sit in seats or chairs; but how can we understand that the seats themselves "sit"? It is as if someone were to say, "let the Cathedras <note>original: "Cathedræ," the formal chair of a bishop, from which we get the word "cathedral."</note> sit here," or "let the chairs sit here." One sits in a chair, sits in seats, sits in Cathedras—the seats themselves do not sit. What then is the meaning of the phrase: *There the seats sat for judgment*? <note>original: "Sederunt sedes in iudicium," a quote from Psalm 122:5.</note> Surely you are accustomed to hearing the Lord say: *Heaven is my throne, but the earth is my footstool.* In Latin, however, the whole is said thus: *Heaven is my seat.* Who are these but the righteous? Who are the heavens but the righteous? For the churches of the 

<margin>
The righteous are heaven and the seat of God, and sinners are earth.
</margin>

Church itself are so many that they are one. Thus also the righteous: they are so righteous as to be heaven, so that they are "the heavens." In them God sits, and from them God judges. It was not without cause that it was said: *The heavens declare the glory of God.* <note>Psalm 19:1.</note> Moreover, the Apostles were made into heaven. How were they made heaven? Because they were justified. Just as the sinner was made earth—to whom it was said, *Earth you are and to earth you shall go*—so the justified were made heaven. They carried God, and from them God flashed forth wonders, thundered terrors, and rained down consolations. They were therefore His heaven, and they declared the glory of God. For so that you may know they were called "heavens," the same Psalm says: *Their sound went out into all the earth, and their words to the ends of the world.* You ask whose words? And you will find it is the words of the heavens. If, therefore, heaven is the seat of God, and the Apostles became heaven, then they themselves were made the seat of God; they are the throne of God. It is said in another place: *The soul of the righteous is the throne of wisdom.* <note>Hus attributes this to "another place"; it was a common medieval proverb often attributed to Solomon or Gregory the Great.</note> This is a great thing that was said: "The soul of the righteous is the throne of wisdom," meaning that in the soul of the righteous, wisdom sits as if on its own throne, and from there it judges whatever it judges. Therefore, they shall be the thrones of wisdom, and for that reason the Lord said to them: *You shall sit upon twelve thrones judging the twelve tribes of Israel.* Just as they themselves shall sit upon twelve seats, they themselves are the seats of God. Indeed, it was said of them: *For there the seats sat.* How will they sit there as seats, they who *were* the seats? And who are the seats?

## CHAPTER XIX.

Having said what the Apostolic See is, we must now say in
what matters the Apostolic See itself is to be obeyed. And the 
aforementioned doctors say that the inferiors must obey in all things 
where a pure good is not prohibited, or a 
pure evil is not commanded, which—according to the manner, place, time, or person—
can be both good and evil. And they prove this through four
suitable witnesses: namely, the Savior, Bernard, 
Augustine, and Jerome. And because the doctors accepted
<meta>catchword: distinction</meta>

---

<page-num>167</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIX</header>

the distinction of Bernard in his letter to Adam the Monk regarding 
pure good and pure evil. Therefore, it should be noted that after Blessed
Bernard shows that no one is to be obeyed in 
evil, he concludes by saying: "Therefore, to do evil at anyone’s
command is clearly not obedience, but rather 
disobedience."
<margin>Pure goods and pure evils</margin>
Truly this must be observed: that certain things are pure goods, 
and certain things are pure evils. And in these, no 
obedience is owed to men, since the former must not be omitted even when 
prohibited, nor must the latter be committed even when commanded. Furthermore, 
between these are certain "intermediate" <term>media: morally neutral or indifferent actions</term> things, which according to the manner, place, time, 
or person can be both evil and good. And in these the law of 
obedience is placed, just as in the tree of the knowledge of good and evil 
which was in the middle of Paradise. In these matters, it is certainly not 
our place to prefer our own sense to the judgment of the masters. In these, 
neither the command nor the prohibition of the Prelates <note>High-ranking church officials like bishops or abbots.</note> is to be despised. 
Below. Faith, hope, and charity are pure goods, and others of this 
<margin>Pure evils</margin>
kind, and they can neither be forbidden nor not held. Pure evils are 
theft, sacrilege, adultery, and other such things, which certainly 
can neither be rightly commanded nor performed, nor can they rightly not be forbidden or committed. 
No one's prohibition is strong enough to counter the commandments, and no one's command 
<margin>Intermediate things</margin>
prejudices the prohibitions. Then there are the intermediate things, which by themselves 
are known to be neither good nor evil. They can, however, be indifferently 
and equally well or poorly commanded or prohibited—but one must 
not obey the subjects poorly in these matters. From these, for the sake of 
example, I include fasting, keeping watch, reading, and such things. 
It should be known, however, that certain intermediate things pass into the 
category of "impurities" or "evils"; for marriage, while it is permitted to happen or 
not happen, once done, it is not permitted to be dissolved. Therefore, what 
was clearly intermediate before the marriage, in those already 
married, takes on the force of a pure good. Likewise, for a secular 
person to possess property is not an "intermediate" matter <note>Hus means it is generally permitted for laypeople.</note>, since it is also permitted 
not to possess it; but for a Monk, because it is not permitted to possess anything, it is a pure evil. 
Thus says Bernard.

<summary>Hus argues that the Roman See's authority is conditional upon the Pope's adherence to Christ's law, rather than being tied to the physical city of Rome. He then outlines a framework for obedience based on Saint Bernard, distinguishing between actions that are inherently good, inherently evil, or morally neutral.</summary>
