<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s discourse on the nature of the Church and the limits of obedience. Having previously discussed the authority of the Roman See, Hus now transitions into a philosophical and theological classification of human actions (neutral, good, or evil) to establish a foundation for when a believer should or should not obey a superior’s command.</meta>

168 J[AN] H[US] ON THE CHURCH

<margin>a neutral work</margin> It should also be noted, according to the understanding of those speaking about human acts, that a work is called **Neutral** <note>original: "Neutrum," also known as an "indifferent" act in scholastic philosophy—something neither commanded nor forbidden.</note> among them which, from its primary intention, expresses neither a moral good nor the evil of a vice, such as building or weaving.

<margin>Works good or evil by their kind.</margin> Moreover, works are said to be **good or evil by their kind** which describe the substance or nature of a work that is morally good or evil, yet do not imply the circumstances that essentially establish the nature of such acts in the category of virtue or vice. Examples include giving alms and killing a man; for both of these can be done as much for good as for evil, according to the difference of the cause or the intention of the worker. For to give alms for the sake of **vainglory** <note>seeking the praise of others rather than the glory of God</note> is an evil, just as to kill a man by the authority of God, lest he infect the church, is a good. However, there are some composite works which are said to be simply good or evil in a moral sense, such as committing adultery and stealing regarding vice, and to love God and neighbor charitably regarding virtue.

Briefly then: a certain act is **simply good**, such as loving God charitably. A certain act is **simply evil**, such as hating God.
Furthermore, there is that which is **good by its kind**, which is to say it inclines a man more toward judging it to be good than to judging it to be evil, such as fasting or giving alms.
There is that which is **evil by its kind**, which is to say it inclines a man more toward judging it to be evil than to judging it to be good, although it can be 
<margin>Neutral work</margin> done well, as in the case of killing a man. But a **neutral work** is said to be a work that no more inclines a man toward judging it as good than as evil, nor the reverse, such as weaving, eating, plowing, or running. Therefore, a simply good work holds the first rank, a work good by its kind holds a middle rank, and a neutral work holds the lowest rank. An example of these three would be: Loving God, fasting, and weaving. The same applies to their opposites. For a work is **simply evil**, such as hating God; and **evil by its kind**, such as killing a man. But the third, namely the neutral, does not grant
<meta>catchword: evil,</meta>

<column-break/>

CHAPTER XIX 169

evil, because then it would not be neutral. For it is called neutral because it does not incline more toward virtue than toward vice, or vice versa.

<margin>The twofold work: virtuous or vicious.</margin> Furthermore, it should be noted that the immediate division of human works is that they are either **virtuous or vicious**. This is evident because if a man is vicious and does anything, then he acts viciously. And if he is virtuous and does anything, then he acts virtuously, because just as a vice which is called a crime or a mortal sin infects a man's acts universally, so virtue gives life to all the acts of a virtuous man. This goes so far that one existing in grace is said to merit and to pray even while sleeping or while performing any work whatsoever, as the holy doctors say—especially the blessed Augustine, Gregory, Jerome, and others. And that saying is founded upon that word of Christ Jesus our Savior, which he spoke in the sixth chapter of Luke: *If your eye be simple, your whole body will be bright* <note>original: "Si oculus tuus fuerit simplex, totum corpus tuum lucidum erit." This is a reference to Matthew 6:22 and Luke 11:34.</note>; that is, if the intention in grace toward the work be good, then your whole body—that is, the collection of all your works—will be bright, because they are clean before God. But if your eye—that is, your intention—be wicked, criminal, and defiled by vice, then your whole body—namely of your works—will be dark, that is, vicious. Hence the teacher of the Gentiles, the Apostle Paul, commands, saying: *Do all things for the glory of God*, in the first chapter of Second Corinthians <note>The text cites 2 Cor 1, but the verse "Do all for the glory of God" is typically 1 Corinthians 10:31.</note>. And elsewhere in the last chapter of First Corinthians: *Let all your things be done in charity.* From this it is clear that the entire way of living charitably is virtuous, and the entire way of a man living without charity is vicious. From this it follows that just as no one can be neutral regarding virtue or vice—since it is necessary that one is either in the grace of Almighty God or outside of grace—so no behavior of a man can be neutral. In virtuous matters, therefore, a superior must be obeyed; in vicious matters, however, his commands must be boldly resisted.

<margin>The commands of men are not to be cared for as much as those of God.</margin> With these things noted above, every faithful follower of Christ should take care not to believe that if the Roman Pontiff or a Prelate commands anything, that it is necessarily a true mandate of God to be performed, as if that 
<sig>Z</sig> <meta>catchword: Prelate</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus defines three categories of human actions: simply good/evil, good/evil by their nature, and neutral acts like weaving or eating. He argues that a person’s spiritual state (grace or sin) ultimately makes every act either virtuous or vicious, providing a theological basis for resisting the commands of church authorities if they lead to sin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the limits of obedience within the Church hierarchy. He is moving from a general classification of human acts to a specific legal and moral framework: when a believer is required to obey a superior (like a Bishop or Pope) and when they must resist for the sake of their conscience and the law of God.</meta>

170 I. H. ON THE CHURCH

[...the Church] could not err, just as Christ Jesus could not. Second, let it be held regarding the commandments in the law of the Lord, that some things are commanded to us generally <note>original: "confuse," meaning in a general or collective sense</note> and some distinctly. Those things are commanded generally which we ought to do continually and meritoriously, in the way that Blessed Augustine says that all truth is contained in Scripture. However, a commanded work for which there is no reason, or utility for the Church of Christ, is not contained explicitly or implicitly in Scripture. And whether that thing be commanded by the Pope or by another Prelate, the subject is not bound to do it, lest the freedom of the law of the Lord be offended by it.

<margin>God commands nothing irrational.</margin>
Since we ought to accept as faithful people that God commands nothing for us to do unless it is meritorious and reasonable for us, and consequently profitable for salvation.

<margin>When to obey superiors.</margin>
Let this be the conclusion: subjects are bound to obey virtuous superiors, and even the difficult ones <note>original: "discholis," referring to 1 Peter 2:18, meaning harsh or crooked leaders</note>, willingly and joyfully, as long as they command the fulfillment of the mandates of the Lord Jesus Christ. This conclusion is evident. For in Matthew 23, Christ says: "The scribes and Pharisees sit upon the seat of Moses; therefore, do everything whatever they tell you"—namely, all my mandates. Regarding this, Augustine, in his commentary on John as mentioned in the previous chapter, says: "By sitting," he says, "on the seat of Moses, they teach the law of God; therefore God teaches through them; but if they wish to teach their own things, do not listen, do not act." Thus Augustine. And regarding this, Christ also says in Luke 10: "He who hears you, hears me." And consequently they hear God the Father, because such people are not obeyed as men, but as ministers of God, who is primarily obeyed. Therefore, no one ought to obey a man in anything, even the smallest thing, which opposes a divine mandate, which is called by Blessed Bernard "divine counsel." Since in Acts 5 Peter says: "It is necessary to obey

<margin>When to resist prelates.</margin>
God rather than men." Thus, just as we are ordered to obey superiors in things lawful and honest, with the proper circumstances applied, so we are ordered to resist them to their face when they walk contrary to divine counsels or mandates. Since Paul, commanding that we be imitators of him in 2 Corinthians, resisted [Peter] to his

---

171 CHAPTER XIX.

face, as Galatians 2 says, because of a slight fault. Moreover, we are more bound to obey Paul and any author of Divine Scripture than the Roman Pontiff in middle or neutral works <note>original: "operibus mediis sive neutris," referring to actions that are neither inherently good nor evil</note>. And since we are not bound to follow any apostle except insofar as he follows the Lord Jesus Christ—as is clear from the limitation set by the Apostle—it is clear that we are bound to obey no Prelate after the holy Apostles, except insofar as he commands or counsels the counsels or mandates of Christ. Therefore the holy apostle, in 1 Corinthians 4 and 11, when he counsels them to be imitators of him, immediately announces the form of following when he inserts: "As I am of Christ." Hence, a discerning subject ought to examine the commands of a superior when he sees him departing from the law of Christ or his own rule. For not every superior is incorruptible. And for this reason, Christ commanded more frequently to be watchful in our works; hence in Mark 13 he says: "I say to all: Watch." And in 1 John 4 the Apostle says: "Beloved, do not believe every spirit, but test the spirits to see if they are from God, because many false prophets have gone out into the world." And in Matthew 24 the Savior says: "Many false prophets will arise and will lead many astray." And regarding this, St. Bernard speaks very beautifully in his letter to the monk Adam <note>Bernard of Clairvaux’s Epistle 7, which addresses a monk who followed a superior’s command that led him to break his original vows</note>, rebuking him for obeying his abbot indiscreetly against the rule of his vow. Wherefore he says with an exclamation, but mockingly: "O most obedient monk, for whom not even a single jot of any elder's words flies by; he does not consider what kind of thing is commanded, content with this alone—that it is commanded; and this obedience is without delay. If this is how it must be, let him now erase from the book of the Gospel: 'Be wise as serpents.' For what follows would be sufficient: 'And simple as doves.'" I do not say that the commands of superiors are to be judged by subjects where nothing is found contrary to divine institutes. But I assert that prudence is necessary, by which one notices if something is opposed, and freedom, by which it may be nobly disregarded. "Furthermore," that man says, "I must not question; let the one who ordered it see to it." Tell me, I pray, if with a sword given into your

<summary>Jan Hus argues that obedience to Church authorities is conditional, required only when their commands align with God's law. He asserts that believers have a duty to use "prudence" to discern and even resist superiors if they command something contrary to Scripture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that obedience to a superior is not unconditional. He is citing historical and theological authorities to prove that when a command is harmful or contrary to God’s law, the subject—whether a monk or a layperson—has a duty to resist.</meta>

172 I. H. ON THE CHURCH

[...would you allow] his sword to be armed against your own throat and simply acquiesce? If you could prohibit others from doing so and do not, are you not also held guilty of homicide? Come then, see to it that you do not perhaps fall into something more grave under the pretext of obedience; thus says Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a hugely influential Cistercian abbot and mystic.</note>. And he brings forward many testimonies of Scripture, and concludes: You, therefore, against all these and countless other testimonies of truth of this kind, thought that anyone ought to be obeyed. O hateful perversity! The virtue of obedience, which always fights for the truth, is here girded against the truth. Thus says Bernard.

<margin>Five conditions of obedience.</margin>
For this reason, Bernard himself, in his sermon on the Advent of the Lord, sets down five conditions that make obedience right. First, when the work is **holy**, because one must not obey against God. Second, when it is **voluntary**. Third, when it is **pure** according to a holy intention, following the teaching of the Savior in Matthew 6: *If your eye is simple, your whole body will be full of light.* Fourth, when it is **discerned** <note>original: "discretum," implying moderation and wisdom</note>, so that neither excess nor defect infects it. And fifth, when it is **firm**, persevering until the proper end.

<margin>Nothing unless good is to be commanded.</margin>
From this it is clear that a subject, considering an undiscerning command of a Superior <term>Præpositi</term> <note>A general term for any religious superior or overseer.</note>—which is known, or ought to be known, to tend toward the detriment of the church by distracting from the worship of God and the progress of the salvation of souls—ought to resist that superior. For such resistance is true obedience, not only made to God according to the law of brotherly correction, but even to the Superior himself. This is because no Superior ought to command anything except what is good. Since, therefore, the subject is bound by obedience to what is good in general by the Superior, it follows that he obeys him by resisting in this way as he ought, because he does what is good by turning away from evil. From this it is clear that a subject obeying his Prelate in evil is not excused from sin. For the Savior says in Matthew 15: *If the blind lead the blind, both fall into the pit.* That is, if a blind—meaning ignorant or evil—Prelate offers guidance by com-

<meta>catchword: manding</meta>

<column-break/>

173 CHAPTER XIX

manding a work to a blind—meaning ignorant or evil—subject, both fall into the pit of error. Hence Christ appropriately said to the disciples concerning the scribes and Pharisees, who taught that it was a sin to eat bread with unwashed hands when it was not actually a sin: *Leave them alone; they are blind and leaders of the blind.* What does "leave them alone" mean? The Gloss <note>The "Glossa Ordinaria," the standard medieval collection of biblical commentary.</note> says: it means to leave them to their own judgment, for they are blind, meaning they are in the darkness of traditions. Even brute animals observe this rule of Christ, as a horse or a donkey, recognizing a pit ahead, avoids the precipice as much as it can even when urged by spurs. This is also clear regarding the donkey <note>The donkey of Balaam, from Numbers 22.</note> who, recognizing the angel prohibiting it from proceeding on the way Balaam wished to go, rebuked the foolishness of the prophet with a human voice. Hence Bernard, writing to Adam the Monk in an epistle, speaks ironically in this way: *You, that most obedient son; you, that most devoted disciple; you have suffered that father and teacher of yours—whom you lived with for not even a moment or a step’s breadth away—to be distanced from you, so that you did not hesitate to fall into the pit after him, not indeed with blind eyes, but with open eyes like Balaam.* Thus he says.

<margin>A double judgment.</margin>
From these points, when you proceed further, it follows that it is permitted for subordinate Clergy, and indeed even for the Laity, to judge the works of their superiors. This is clear from the fact that there is one kind of judgment of discerning and hidden arbitration in the forum of conscience, and another judgment of authoritative jurisdiction in the forum of the church. In the first way, a subject ought purely to judge himself, according to 1 Corinthians 11: *If we judged ourselves, we would not be judged.* And in the second way, he ought to judge all things pertaining to the common salvation. According to 1 Corinthians 2: *The spiritual man judges all things.* Moreover, it is necessary for a Layman to judge the works of his superior just as the Apostle [Paul] judged the works of Peter by rebuking him, in Galatians 2: *When I saw,* he said, *that he was not walking rightly according to the truth of the gospel, I said to Cephas <note>Saint Peter.</note> before them all: If you, though you are a Jew, live like a Gentile and not like a Jew, how can you compel the Gentiles to live like Jews?* Second, the Layman ought to judge...

<summary>Jan Hus argues that blind obedience to religious superiors is a "perversity." Citing St. Bernard and biblical examples like Balaam’s donkey and St. Paul rebuking St. Peter, he asserts that subjects—including laypeople—have a moral obligation to judge their leaders' commands and resist those that are harmful or contrary to God's truth.</summary>

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...to judge the Superior <term>Præpositum</term> <note>A general term for a religious leader or overseer.</note> in order to flee from him. For in Matthew 7, Christ says: *Beware of false prophets who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves.* But one ought to judge in order to be spiritually guided and physically nourished, or otherwise to do good. Otherwise, the Laity would never choose Clerics as parish priests, confessors, or almsgivers. Therefore, just as it is permitted for the wealthy of this world to examine with diligent judgment which Superiors they will minister alms to, and in what manner, while guarding against ravenous wolves—which, according to the Apostle in Acts 20 and according to Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Early Church Father known for his preaching.</note> in the *Incomplete Work*, Homily 20, is clearly discerned by this: that they seek the money of their subjects more than their salvation—this is contrary to the Apostle in 2 Corinthians 12: *For I do not seek what is yours, but you.* Whence in Acts 20, foreseeing such false apostles through prophecy, he speaks thus:

<margin>Evil Prelates are ravenous wolves</margin>
*I know that after my departure ravenous wolves will enter among you, not sparing the flock.* And because this "wolfishness" is clearly recognized through the plundering of temporal goods and the infliction of punishments to seize those same temporal goods more abundantly, he declares that he himself held the opposite manner, saying: *As you yourselves know, I have coveted no one's gold or clothing; as you yourselves know, these hands have provided for the things I needed and for those who are with me.* Therefore, subjects living piously in Christ ought to pay attention to the life of the Apostles and consider whether their superiors live in conformity with them. For if they are lazy in spiritual ministry, if they are diligent in demanding money, despising evangelical poverty and leaning upon the world, and even openly sowing other crimes, then they shall know them by their works, because they have turned away from the religion of the Lord Jesus Christ.

<margin>Prelates are to be observed.</margin>
Therefore, O heartfelt lovers of the law of Christ, first believe their works, to see if they turn toward the world. Second, attend to their commands, to see if they savor of greed or worldly gain. Third, consult the Holy Scripture

<column-break/>

175

# CHAPTER XIX

consult them to see if they command in conformity with Christ’s counsel, and according to this, believe them—or disbelieve them if it is the opposite. But do not the Parish Priests say to the Laity: "What business is it of yours to judge our life or works?" when our Savior says in Matthew 23: *Do not do according to their works*? And afterward he describes the works of the three types of Prelates, so that those who recognize them may know how to healthfully avoid them. Indeed, to the Prelates who say: "What business is it of yours to judge our life or works? And [to judge whether] you should receive our alms?" <note>Hus is likely arguing that the laity should decide whether to give alms based on the priest's conduct.</note> the Apostle says in the last chapter of 2 Thessalonians: *We charge you in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that you withdraw yourselves from every brother walking inordinately and not according to the tradition which they received from us; for you yourselves know how you ought to imitate us, for we were not rowdy among you, nor did we eat anyone’s bread for free, but worked in labor and toil.* And further down: *For when we were with you, we gave you this charge: if anyone will not work, neither shall he eat.* And it is clear how subjects ought to judge reasonably concerning the commands and works of their Superiors, because otherwise they would be in danger of eternal death; nor would they judge discreetly concerning them—in what they ought to believe them, in what they ought to follow them, and in what things they ought reasonably to obey them according to the law of the Lord. For our best Master, the Lord, forewarned us: *Beware of false prophets* in Matthew 7. And *Beware of the leaven of the Pharisees* in Matthew 16. And in Matthew 23 he says: *Do not believe, do not go out*, and *Do not do according to their works.* He even encouraged the priests and the crowds to judge his own works, saying in John 8: *Which of you convicts me of sin?* and in John 10: *If you will not believe me, believe the works.* And what is an evil for the church of Christ is that our superiors demand more that their approvals and disapprovals be believed in all their judgments, than they demand that the faith of Holy Scripture be believed, which is the Catholic faith. And they punish more for the violation of their own [decrees than for the violation of God's law].

# I. H. ON THE CHURCH

...of traditions, and blaspheming against the primary faith of Christ. Thus of them it can be said, as the Psalmist does: *He who ate my bread has lifted up his heel against me.* <note>Psalm 41:9; original: "Qui edebat panem magnificavit super me supplantationem."</note> For they, as they themselves claim, consume the patrimony of Christ, and yet they value their own commands more than those of the Lord Jesus Christ himself.

### One is not to obey the church or its prelates in all things, even in neutral deeds which are neither good nor evil. Likewise, Excommunication cannot remove the execution of Christ's commands. CHAPTER XX.

<margin>"One must obey" and "he ought to obey" mean the same thing.</margin>
Furthermore, that the Doctors' dual statement might be better understood when they say: "the Roman church and prelates must be obeyed in all things by subordinates," etc. And again: "Therefore they must be obeyed and complied with." I assume, based on correct grammar, that the phrase "one must obey" <term>est obediendum</term> <note>A Latin gerundive construction expressing necessity or obligation.</note> is equivalent to the phrase "he ought to obey," and further, that this word "ought" <term>debet</term> implies a debt of obligation to obedience under the penalty of mortal sin. This assumption is clear from the Doctors' citation of the Savior's words in Matthew 23: *Observe and do whatever they tell you.* For that word of the Lord is a precept. Secondly, the assumption is clear from the words of the Doctors when they say: "Certain members of the clergy in the kingdom of Bohemia refuse to consent, striving, as much as in them lies, to lead the faithful people into disobedience toward prelates, and into irreverence toward the papal, episcopal, priestly, and clerical dignities." Now it is well known that the disobedience and irreverence the Doctors hint at would be a mortal sin. Thirdly, this assumption is clear from the citation of Blessed Augustine, where he says: "If you have not been obedient to your father (understand here not a physical father, but a spiritual one), you have lost all virtues." Now it is plain that

<column-break/>

177

# CHAPTER XX

it is plain that a virtuous man cannot lose all virtues except through mortal sin. And thus, in that authority, disobedience is charged as a crime. Therefore, this statement of the Doctors—that "based on the assumption that the Roman church and Prelates must be obeyed by subordinates in all things," etc.—is equivalent to saying "one must obey under the penalty of mortal sin." Wherefore, according to this sense, they now cry out that I am [dis]obedient <note>The text reads "obediens" (obedient), but the context of the Doctors' outcry and the subsequent excommunication indicates they are accusing Hus of "inobediens" (disobedient).</note> to the Roman church, and for this reason they excommunicate me. And it is clear, from the law of God and the law of the Canons, that no one ought to be excommunicated except for mortal sin, as I have said elsewhere.

<margin>No one ought to be excommunicated except for a mortal sin.</margin>

With this assumption standing, I propose this conclusion: To no Apostolic See of the Roman church—that is, to no Pope with the Cardinals, as the Doctors conceive it—nor to Prelates, must subordinates obey in all things which are neither purely evil nor purely good.

<margin>The Pope is not to be obeyed in all things.</margin>

This is proven: no king, marquis, duke, baron, knight, citizen, or peasant is bound under penalty of mortal sin to obey the Roman church and Prelates in such a way that he should not possess his own property or should enter into marriage; and those things—namely, possessing property and entering marriage—are neither purely good nor purely evil for those persons; therefore the conclusion holds. The consequence is noted, and the minor premise is clear through Blessed Bernard in his letter to Adam the Monk, when he says: "It should be known that neutral things <term>media</term> <note>Hus uses the term 'media' to describe 'indifferent' acts—those not inherently commanded or forbidden by God's law.</note>, which are seen to lie mostly between the purely good and the purely evil, obtain the force of a pure good in the married. Likewise, to possess property is a neutral thing for a secular man, since it is also permitted not to possess it. But for a monk, because it is not permitted to possess it, it is a pure evil." Thus says Bernard. The major premise is thus proven: No Roman church is permitted to command under penalty of mortal sin that a king, marquis, duke, baron, knight, citizen, or peasant should enter marriage, or should not possess property; therefore, also, none of the said persons is bound to obey under penalty of mortal sin. The consequen[ce]

He asserts that excommunication is only valid in cases of mortal sin and challenges the legal definitions of obedience used by his opponents.</summary>

He is challenging the "Doctors" (the theological faculty at the University of Prague) who argued that the Roman Church must be obeyed in all things. Hus is now drawing a sharp distinction between "precepts" (commands that must be followed) and "counsels" (spiritual advice for perfection that should not be forced upon people).</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>The church cannot bind anyone beyond the counsels of Christ.</margin>
<margin>Voluntary poverty</margin>
The consequence is well known from what lies beyond the counsels, and the preceding point is clear, because the Roman Church is not permitted to exalt its own command above the counsel of Christ. But if the Roman Church were to command that a king, a marquess, a duke, a baron, or a knight should possess nothing of his own, it would be exalting its own command above Christ’s counsel. This is the first of the twelve principal <term>evangelical counsels</term> <note>Hus refers to the medieval theological concept of "evangelical counsels"—advice given by Jesus for those seeking spiritual perfection, such as voluntary poverty, as opposed to "precepts" which are mandatory for all Christians.</note>, namely, voluntary poverty consisting in the renunciation of property, which implies a state of need. Christ counsels this but does not command it, saying to a certain young ruler in Matthew 19: *If you wish to be perfect, go and sell all that you have, and give to the poor, and follow me.* <note>Matthew 19:21; original: "Si vis perfectus esse, vade & vende omnia quæ habes, & da pauperibus, & sequere me."</note> Similarly, if the Roman Church were to command that a king, a marquess, or any other secular person enter into marriage, it would be commanding against Christ’s counsel; how then would it not be acting against Christ?
<margin>Matthew 19</margin>
For the observance of virginal chastity until death is the third evangelical counsel of Christ, of which he says in Matthew 19: *There are eunuchs who have made themselves eunuchs for the sake of the kingdom of heaven.* <note>Matthew 19:12; original: "Sunt eunuchi, qui se castrauerunt propter regnum coelorū."</note> Christ does not command this but counsels it, so that whoever it suits may hold to it with a willing heart, saying: *Let anyone accept this who can.* <note>Matthew 19:12; original: "Qui potest capere capiat."</note>

<margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>
Therefore, it would be a great presumption for the Roman Church to bind anyone under the penalty of mortal sin beyond the counsels of its Lord. For this would be to place unbearable burdens on the shoulders of men, as the Savior says in Matthew 23, which the scribes and Pharisees sitting in the seat of Moses do not uphold themselves, but impose upon others. As Christ says in that same place: *Hence the Apostle of Christ, who beheld the secrets of God which the Roman Church has not seen, did not dare to command anyone to marry, nor to remain in continence.* <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul’s advice on marriage and celibacy.</note> Therefore, in 1 Corinthians 7, he says: *Each has his own gift from God, one of one kind and one of another.* <note>1 Corinthians 7:7; original: "Unusquisque proprium donum habet a deo, alius quidem sic, alius vero sic."</note> The Apostle wished to command nothing except what the Lord commanded through him, and thus what was useful for the one obeying. For there are many counsels for others which are not counsels for us due to our frailty or ignorance. For instance, one person may marry in the Lord without mortal sin, even though it would be better for him

<meta>catchword: for him</meta>

---

<page-num>179</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XX</header>

to keep his virginity chaste, but he is ignorant of this, believing the opposite. Therefore the Apostle says, *Each has his own gift from God, one of one kind and one of another.* And it follows: *But if they cannot exercise self-control, they should marry. For it is better to marry than to burn with passion.* <note>1 Corinthians 7:9; original: "Quod si non se continent, nubant, melius est enim nubere quam uri."</note> Thus, some things are more useful for certain people which would be less useful for others. Hence, it would be a notable error to believe that all of Christ's counsels are useful for everyone so that they should fulfill them literally. And for this reason, a son is not bound to obey his father under penalty of mortal sin when the father commands that he possess nothing or that he marry. The same applies to a daughter, whom he cannot lawfully compel to remain chaste until death or to marry.

<margin>They did not obey the mandate of the Roman Church</margin>
Furthermore: If that statement by the Doctors were true—that the Roman Church's Apostolic See must be obeyed by subordinates in all things, etc.—it would follow that King Wenceslaus <note>Wenceslaus IV (1361–1419), King of Bohemia and King of the Romans.</note>, King of the Romans and King of Bohemia, and likewise Sigismund <note>Sigismund of Luxembourg (1368–1437), King of Hungary and later Holy Roman Emperor; Wenceslaus's brother.</note>, King of Hungary, would be continually sinning mortally. This is because they did not obey the mandate of the Roman Church and of Pope Boniface <note>Pope Boniface IX, who was involved in the political turmoil of the Western Schism.</note> along with the Cardinals regarding the resignation of their kingdoms—the former the Kingdom of the Romans, and the latter the Kingdom of Hungary. And it is clear that for the one or the other to resign his kingdom is not an absolute evil, as is evident from the words of Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux.</note>. And since those kings have not yet fulfilled that mandate, nor have they been absolved by Boniface himself, it follows that they still persist in disobedience. But who of sound mind would wish to say this? Since, according to the law of the Lord, Pope Boniface himself ought not to have attempted such a thing.

Likewise, it follows that some of those very Doctors—such as Stanislaus <note>Stanislaus of Znaim, a former friend of Hus who turned against him.</note>, Peter of Znaim, Johannes, Heliae, and others—are continuously under Papal excommunication. This is clear because they have not obeyed the Roman Curia of the Apostolic See to this day. Or, if they obeyed secretly because of legal disputes, they have still not been absolved from the sentence, since a mandate was issued to them by Pope Innocent under penalties

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the Roman Church cannot turn Christ's "counsels" (like voluntary poverty or celibacy) into mandatory "precepts." He uses the refusal of Kings Wenceslaus and Sigismund to resign their thrones as a practical example of how papal commands are not always binding or just.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's argument regarding the limits of ecclesiastical authority. He transitions from discussing the specific legal case of "Master Maurice" to a broader theological discussion on why the church cannot mandate spiritual disciplines like fasting or prayer without considering individual circumstances, citing both Aristotle and the Gospels.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: And hence</meta>

...of excommunication, deprivation of benefices, and disqualification, so that they might effectively grant and assign the place to Master Maurice <note>Likely Mauritius de Praga, a contemporary of Hus and a figure in the university struggles of the time</note> which he desired; and although they were solemnly warned by a notary in the presence of witnesses, they still do not obey that mandate today. Yet assigning a place to Master Maurice is not an **inherent evil** <note>original: "purum malum"; a scholastic term for an act that is wrong by its very nature, regardless of intent or circumstance</note> that is commanded. Although perhaps it is an evil for Master Maurice himself—because they unusually covet that place—and perhaps it is also an evil for the doctors themselves. Because they love the first place in the synagogues, they do not admit Maurice himself. Would that they were not, on both sides, "most humble masters" in that greeting of Christ! Luke 11: *Woe to you Pharisees, who love the first seats in the synagogues.* And Matthew 23 adds: *and to be called "Rabbi" by men.*

<margin>When the church must be obeyed in fasts and prayers</margin> 
Likewise, in acts that are good by their nature, such as fasting and prayer, which are not inherent evils, the Roman Church or Prelates <note>High-ranking church officials</note> should not be obeyed unless it is in a measure balanced by reason. This is evident because it might happen that a subject continues in prayer and fasting to such an extent that he fails by harming himself and the church. But this must be avoided by both the commander and the subject; therefore the premise is true. For it is clear that it would be a "temptation of God" to obey a Prelate, or to vow to him, that I would never eat or drink more than a certain amount, or never have more than a certain amount of clothing or covering, and so on with other clumsily conceived counsels. And it would be much more the foolishness of a prelate to bind a community to such a unique degree. Since, according to the variation of times—sickness and health, youth and old age, heat and cold—such things must vary in the same person; and much more in a community must different "means" <note>referring to the philosophical "golden mean"</note> be adapted to different people. Since, according to Aristotle in the second book of the *Ethics*, the mean is not the same for everyone. For the portion of food for Milo <note>Milo of Croton, a famous ancient Greek athlete known for eating massive quantities of meat to maintain his strength</note>, who wanted to eat an entire ox in a day, would not be a proportional mean for any youth or old person, healthy or sick.

---

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XX.</header>
<sig>Aa iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Truly</meta>

<margin>Christ did not want to force his disciples to fast.</margin> 
And hence the Savior, when his disciples were accused of not fasting, excused them in this way. For in Matthew 9 it is said: *The disciples of John came to Jesus, saying: "Why do we and the Pharisees fast frequently, but your disciples do not fast?"* The Savior responded to these slanderers, who joined themselves with the Pharisees in criticizing Christ, speaking thus for his disciples: *The children of the bridegroom can never mourn as long as the bridegroom is with them; but the days will come when the bridegroom will be taken away from them, and then they will fast. No one puts a piece of unshrunk cloth onto an old garment, for it pulls away its fullness from the garment and a worse tear is made. Nor do they put new wine into old wineskins; otherwise the wineskins burst, the wine is spilled, and the skins are destroyed; but they put new wine into new wineskins, and both are preserved.* <note>Matthew 9:14–17</note> Here the Savior excuses the disciples from the observance of fasting. First, because the bridegroom of the church himself **dispensed** <note>granted a legal exemption or excuse</note> with his children at that time. Second, because that physical fasting was not suitable for them then, as Lyra <note>Nicholas of Lyra (c. 1270–1349), an influential Franciscan biblical commentator</note> says. Where the Bridegroom says: *The children of the bridegroom can never mourn*—that is, be saddened by afflicting themselves with fasting—it is as if he says: "No, since fasting is not suitable for them now." *But the days will come*—namely, of the Passion—*when the bridegroom will be taken away from them* through death, as far as his bodily presence is concerned, *and then they will fast*—namely, with a fast of sorrow, according to that saying in John 16: *You will weep and lament*—and then they will fast when such a fast is suitable for them. And then, through a double example, the Savior proves that physical fasting was not suitable for them then. Moreover, in Luke 5, "observances" are touched upon, where it says: *Why do the disciples of John fast frequently and make "observances," and likewise those of the Pharisees, but yours eat and drink?* To whom he said: *You cannot make the children of the bridegroom fast while the bridegroom is with them*—as if to say, beyond the will of the bridegroom, you cannot lawfully make his children fast.

<summary>Hus argues that ecclesiastical mandates regarding fasting and prayer must be tempered by reason and individual capacity, using Aristotle's concept of the mean and Christ's defense of his disciples to prove that forced spiritual rigors can be harmful.</summary>
<keywords>fasting, obedience, Master Maurice, Aristotle, Milo of Croton, Nicholas of Lyra, Pharisees, discretion, moderation</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of religious authorities who impose burdens on the faithful that they themselves do not carry. He draws a sharp contrast between the "sweet yoke" of Christ and the "heavy burdens" of contemporary church officials, arguing that obedience is only mandatory when it aligns with divine law.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>
<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: from the divine</meta>

<margin>
Christ imposed a light yoke, but the church’s is heavy.
</margin>

Truly, Christ is a pious Prior and abbot who does not weigh down his disciples. Instead, imposing a **sweet yoke and a light burden**, he says of the Pharisees and scribes sitting upon the **Chair of Moses** <note>The "Chair of Moses" refers to the authority to teach and interpret God's law</note> that they impose heavy and unbearable burdens on the shoulders of men, but they are unwilling to move them with even their own finger. So it is with modern Prelates <note>High-ranking church officials like bishops or abbots</note> and penitentiaries <note>Priests authorized to hear confessions and assign penance for grave sins</note>; they impose many fasts, many prayers, and other things that weigh down the people, while they themselves do not do the least of them. Hence they often say, "Let us eat, and let the **rascals** <note>original: "ribaldi"; here referring to commoners or those perceived as lower-class</note> fast for us." Therefore, since the Savior calls such mandates of theirs "unbearable burdens"—because they weigh men down beyond the mandates and counsels of Christ—what wise person would say that a subject is bound to obey his Prelate in such matters under the penalty of mortal sin?

Likewise, **eating with unwashed hands** is a neutral act <note>original: "opus neutrum"; in scholastic theology, an action that is neither inherently good nor inherently evil</note>, neither purely good nor purely evil, and yet Christ's disciples were not bound to that act by the mandate of those sitting in the Chair of Moses; therefore, the same applies now. The logic is clear, since the reasoning is the same for traditions of this kind that are not founded in the law of the Lord. The second part is evident from **Matthew 15**, where the Pharisees and scribes said to Jesus: *Why do your disciples transgress the traditions of the elders? For they do not wash their hands when they eat bread.* But He, rebuking them for the transgression of God’s mandates, showed that His disciples did not sin in not keeping their mandates, saying: *To eat with unwashed hands does not defile a man.*

What reason is there now, then, that any subject should be bound to obey his Prelate in every neutral or indifferent act? For it may happen that a Prelate is a fool who might weigh down a subject with such neutral acts in a very indiscreet manner. Hence, as was said above, **Bernard** <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux, a highly influential 12th-century mystic and abbot</note> correctly set down the conditions of obedience, among which one is that the work commanded must be **discreet** <note>meaning balanced or reasonable</note>, since excess or defect ruins it. Therefore, no human command or counsel is valid or to be observed, unless it is derived from the divine...

---

<page-num>183</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XX.</header>
<sig>Bb</sig>

<margin>
When obedience is meritorious.
</margin>

...from the divine example.

And hence it is that no obedience performed for a superior is profitable for merit, except insofar as it inclines one toward obedience to the mandates and counsels of the Lord Jesus Christ. This is evident because the obedience owed or performed for Christ is, in itself, the reason for merit; as it increases or decreases, so does the merit. Indeed, nothing is more religious than obedience performed for God, as the **Decree** <note>The Decretum Gratiani, a collection of Church law</note> teaches in Case 8, Question 1, in the chapter "One Must Know." There it is noted what the prophet Samuel says in **1 Samuel 15**: *Obedience is better than sacrifice, and to resist is like the sin of witchcraft, and to refuse to submit is like the crime of idolatry.* <note>Hus is quoting the Latin Vulgate's version of 1 Samuel 15:22-23</note> Only obedience, says the Decree, is the virtue that possesses the merit of faith, without which anyone is proven to be unfaithful, even if they seem to be a believer. In sacrifices, it says, the flesh of another is slaughtered, but through obedience, one's own will is slaughtered. Here it is clearer than light that it speaks of the obedience owed to God. For in 1 Samuel 15, Samuel says to Saul: *Because you have rejected the word of the Lord, the Lord has rejected you from being king.* And Saul said to Samuel: *I have sinned, because I have transgressed the word of the Lord and your words, fearing the people and obeying their voice.*

Thus it is clear how much the Prelates of the people blaspheme when they claim this same obedience for themselves based on Scripture or Church law. It is clear, secondly, that the authority of **Augustine** which the Doctors <note>Academic theologians</note> cite for themselves in Sermon 86—where he says: *If you fast days and nights, if you perform prayer, if you are in sackcloth or in ashes, or if you do anything else except what is commanded in the law, and you seem wise to yourself, and you are not obedient to your father (understand not a physical father, but a spiritual one), you have lost all virtues*—actually proves that he who does not obey **God**, the spiritual Father, has lost all virtues. And hence Augustine adds: *Wherefore obedience is worth more than all other moral virtues.* Therefore, the proof the Doctors try to deduce from this authority to support their claim is far-fetched.

Furthermore, I propose this conclusion: Notwithstanding a pretended excommunication...

<summary>Hus argues that religious obedience is only valid when it aligns with God's law, critiquing leaders who impose heavy traditions while ignoring their own spiritual duties. He emphasizes that "neutral" acts, like ritual washing or specific fasts, should not be coerced under threat of sin.</summary>
<keywords>obedience, Jan Hus, legalism, Pharisees, mandates of Christ, neutral acts, St. Augustine, merit, clerical authority</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that divine law takes precedence over human ecclesiastical commands. He is specifically addressing the issue of excommunication and whether it should stop a priest from fulfilling his duty to preach the Gospel.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>
Excommunication should not hinder Christ’s commandments.
</margin>
Even in the face of a threatened or already imposed alleged excommunication, a Christian must carry out the commands of Christ. This is clear from the conclusion of Saint Peter and the other Apostles in Acts 5: "We must obey God rather than men." From this, it follows that a priest of Christ, living according to His law and possessing knowledge of the Scriptures and a desire to build up the people, must preach despite any alleged excommunication. This is clear because preaching the word of God is a command given to priests. As the Apostle Peter testifies in Acts 10: "He commanded us," he says, "to preach to the people and to testify." And in Matthew 10: "Jesus sent these twelve, commanding them and saying: 'Do not go into the way of the Gentiles...'" and further down, "As you go, preach, saying, 'The kingdom of heaven is at hand.'" The same is clear in Luke 9 and 10.

<margin>
Augustine: It is the duty of priests to teach.
</margin>
It is also clear through Augustine in the prologue of his sermons, where he says: "There are few priests who preach the word of God justly, but there are many who are silent to their own damnation. Some do so out of ignorance, refusing to teach; others out of negligence, despising the word of God. But neither the former nor the latter can be excused for the fault of silence, since those who do not know how to preach should not be in positions of authority, nor should those who do know how to preach remain silent, even if they do not hold office." <note>Augustine argues that the ability and duty to preach are tied to knowledge and divine command, not just official rank.</note>

<margin>
Jerome:
</margin>
Likewise, according to Saint Jerome on the text of Ezekiel 3: "If, when I say to the wicked, 'You shall surely die,' and you do not warn him, nor speak to turn him from his wicked way so that he may live, that wicked man shall die in his iniquity, but his blood I will require at your hand." Jerome says here that a priest is bound to preach; let him see to it that human fear does not make him silent. There is great danger in keeping silent about the words of God for three reasons: namely, because of fear, because of laziness, or because of flattery. Likewise, according to Gregory in his *Pastoral Care* <note>A famous manual on the responsibilities of the clergy</note> chapter 15 and distinction 43: "Let the ruler be," where he solemnly proves this through many authorities of Scripture. Among other things, he says: "It is written that a sound should be heard when he enters the Sanctuary in the sight of the Lord, so that he does not die." For a priest
<meta>catchword: entering</meta>

---

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER 20</header>

entering or leaving dies if a sound is not heard from him, because he provokes the wrath of the hidden Judge against himself if he walks without the sound of preaching. These are his words. The same is clear through the blessed Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar</note>, who says in the third book of *The Supreme Good*: "Priests are damned for the iniquity of the people if they do not instruct the ignorant or rebuke the sinners." These are his words.

<margin>
Excommunication should not hinder the office of preaching in a priest.
</margin>
Since, therefore, from what has been said, anyone who enters the priesthood receives the office of preaching by command, it is clear that he must execute that command, regardless of an alleged excommunication. Furthermore, no true Catholic should doubt that a person sufficient in doctrine is more obligated to teach the ignorant, counsel the doubtful, punish the unrestrained, and forgive those who do wrong, than he is to perform any other work of mercy. Since one who is able to provide physical alms <note>Charitable gifts to the poor</note> is bound to do so under pain of damnation, as is clear in Matthew 25, how much more is one bound who is able to provide spiritual alms? From this it is clear that for a priest to preach, and for a rich man to give alms, are not neutral acts <note>original: "opera media"; actions that are neither good nor bad in themselves</note>, but commands. Furthermore, it is clear that if the Pope or another superior commands a priest—disposed as described—not to preach, or commands a rich man not to give alms, the subject ought not to obey. Therefore, relying on this command of the Lord, I did not accept the command of Pope Alexander <note>Pope Alexander V, who issued a bull in 1409 banning preaching in private chapels, aimed at Hus's Bethlehem Chapel</note> regarding not preaching. For this reason, I humbly tolerate excommunication, trusting that I shall obtain the blessing of my God, to whom the Psalmist says: "They shall curse, but You shall bless." He also blesses, saying in Matthew 5: "Blessed are you when men shall revile you and say all manner of evil against you falsely for my sake. Rejoice and be glad, for your reward is great in heaven."

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of the institutional Church’s demands for absolute obedience. On the previous page, he argued that a priest's duty to preach God's word supersedes any human decree of excommunication. Here, he examines the specific biblical passages often used by Church authorities to justify their power.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

Now, we must briefly address the authoritative texts that the doctors <note>Hus uses "doctors" to refer to the university-trained theologians of the institutional Church who defended the Pope’s absolute authority.</note> have brought forward to bolster human obedience. For first, they say that the Roman Church and its Prelates <note>High-ranking church officials like bishops and archbishops.</note> must be obeyed by those beneath them in all things, according to the words of the Savior in Matthew 23: **"Observe and do everything they tell you."**

At this, I wonder why the doctors have so clearly cut off the Savior’s words. For they did not include the preceding part: **"The scribes and the Pharisees have sat on the seat of Moses,"** nor did they add what follows: **"But do not do according to their works."** Instead, they only put forward the middle part, saying: **"Observe and do everything they tell you."** Here <margin>Prelates are indignant if their evils are made public.</margin> it seems to me they did this because the Pope and the other prelates of the Church do not wish to be compared to the scribes and Pharisees; they are indignant if anything is said about their evil works. On the other hand, the doctors and masters flatter such men, and thus the prelates "accumulate for themselves teachers to tickle their ears, turning their hearing away from the truth," while the masters offer their flattery.

Therefore, the Apostle’s prophecy is fulfilled in both groups. For in 2 Timothy 4, the Apostle charges Timothy, saying: "I testify before God and Jesus Christ, who will judge the living and the dead at his coming and his kingdom: preach the word; be urgent in season and out of season; reprove, entreat, and rebuke with all patience and teaching. For the time will come when they will not endure sound doctrine, but according to their own desires they will accumulate for themselves teachers to tickle their ears, and they will turn their hearing away from the truth and be turned toward fables." It is no wonder, then, that the Prelates gratefully receive the sayings of the aforementioned doctors, because they have smeared all those sayings with the oil of flattery, and have placed no word of correction to restrain their malice. But the Master, the most just Bishop and Judge, will come; He will strictly weigh both the fawning of the doctors and the malice of the prelates. He is the one who said: **"The scribes and Pharisees sit on the seat of Moses. Therefore, observe and do everything they tell you, but do not do according to their works; for they say, and do not do."**

<meta>catchword: do,</meta>

---

<page-num>187</page-num>
## CHAPTER 21

**...do.** Truly, this Master <note>Referring to Christ.</note> did not stroke the malice of the prelates; He spoke the truth of the doctors, taught His faithful, and refuted the scribes sitting on the seat by pointing to their evil works. He spoke and taught the truth—the truth because they sat "on the seat of Moses" (of which we spoke in Chapter 17), which refers to the authority of judging and teaching the law of God. Of this seat, Moses spoke in Exodus 18: "They come to me to judge <margin>Prelates say and do not do.</margin> between them, and that I may show them the statutes of God and His laws."

Therefore, **"Observe everything they tell you"** <gloss>pertaining to the Seat</gloss>—that is, observe it in your heart—**"and do it"** in your actions. **"But do not do according to their works"** <gloss>hold to the doctrine, not their lifestyle</gloss>, for they say and do not do. 

Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his preaching.</note> says: "They preach faith, but act unfaithfully; they give peace to others, but do not have it in their own minds; they cry out the truth, but love a lie; they chastise greed, but love covetousness." Augustine says on the same point (cited in Chapter 17): "By sitting on the seat of Moses, they teach the law of God; therefore, God teaches through them. But if they wish to teach their own things, do not listen to them, do not do them."

It is clear, therefore, that Christ's saying and command is most true. It is plain that He does not command us to observe and do *all* the precepts of those sitting in Moses' seat, for otherwise He would not have said afterward: "For they bind heavy and unbearable burdens," which consequently ought not to be carried. And it was shown in the last chapter how He excused His disciples from the command to eat with unwashed hands and from certain fasts. 

Then, as for the authority of Augustine which the doctors immediately attach: this was discussed near the end of the previous chapter. Regarding the saying of Saint Jerome on the explanation of the faith, this was discussed in Chapter 16, assuming it had been addressed to Pope Damasus. However, having examined many ancient books, we find that he wrote to Saint Augustine, whom he frequently <margin>Saint Augustine was a Pope.</margin> calls "Pope" in his letters. For Augustine was a true "Pope" <note>original: "Papa"</note> in one sense: holding the seat and the faith of Peter, as is clear near the beginning of Chapter 13. We also refer to Saint Bernard, where he speaks of the "purely good," the "purely evil," and the "middle ground," as discussed above in Chapter 19. And in...

<sig>Bb ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: And in</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that Christ's command to obey those on "Moses' seat" is limited to when they teach God's law, not their own burdens or hypocritical examples. He accuses contemporary theologians of flattering corrupt prelates by quoting Scripture selectively.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Hus, obedience, Moses' seat, Matthew 23, hypocrisy, prelates, Augustine, Chrysostom, flattery, church reform</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding obedience. He is now moving into the distinction between "middle acts"—actions that are not inherently good or evil—and how the obligation to obey a human superior compares to the absolute obedience owed to God.</meta>

<page-num>188</page-num>
# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

And in these middle things <note>Hus is referring to "indifferent" actions—those not specifically commanded or forbidden by Scripture, such as picking up a straw or, as he later illustrates, picking strawberries.</note>, a law of obedience has been placed, just as it was for the tree of the knowledge of good and evil which was in the middle of Paradise. In these matters, it is certainly not our place to impose our own sense upon the judgment of the masters. In these things, neither the command nor the prohibition of prelates is to be looked down upon. However, it must be noted that the adverb of likeness, "as if," indicates a certain similarity, but not a total one. For in Genesis 2, the law regarding the tree of the knowledge of good and evil was established by God, who can neither deceive nor be deceived. And this law was established under the penalty of death, for God said to Adam: "Of the tree of knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat, for on whatever day you eat of it, you shall die." Therefore, we must weigh the commander, the command itself, and the condition of the person who must obey. The commander is God, who cannot err. The command was very useful, and the man who heard it heard God Himself commanding it; therefore, eating from the tree of knowledge of good and evil after a negative command was purely evil. 

Against this, let us suppose that a prelate, Peter, commands John, his subordinate, to pick strawberries. Let it be weighed whether the one commanding cannot err in this, and how useful that work is to the one obeying, and whether the subordinate is as disposed to that work as Adam was to God's mandate. It is clear that in all three aspects, there is no equal similarity. For the prelate *can* err, the work is not so useful, and the subordinate is not so naturally disposed to that work as Adam was to the Lord's mandate.

<margin>A middle work</margin>
Therefore, Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential mystic and abbot whose writings Hus frequently cites to support his views on church reform.</note> says that a "middle work" is one which, according to the mode, place, time, or person, can be either good or evil. Here the saint posits circumstances on the part of the commander, the work itself, and the one obeying. Thus, when he says it is a "middle work," by "mode" he implies the proper measure of reason, so that the commander does not deviate from divine counsels. For if a prelate commanded Peter, a subordinate who is a priest learned in the law of God, to feed sows <note>Female pigs.</note> on a Sunday, and God at the same time advised him 

<meta>catchword: fuleret (advised)</meta>

***

<page-num>189</page-num>
# CHAPTER 21

to perform a work of supererogation <note>term: Supererogation — In Catholic theology, these are "extra" good deeds that go beyond what is required for salvation, such as voluntary poverty or special acts of mercy.</note> that was incompatible with that act, then Peter the priest is bound to obey God’s counsel more than the prelate’s command. This is evident because the one who is more worthy of reverence is the one to whom every subordinate is more obligated in obedience, and the act joined to it is more useful. Now, the act enjoined by the prelate—namely, feeding sows on Sunday—is indifferent to merit, whereas the act enjoined by God possesses the nature of merit in itself.

<margin>God's counsel is to be preferred to man's command.</margin>
Thus, I would wish for St. Bernard to answer this case: if St. Benedict had commanded him to feed sows, and at that same time God had advised him to provide for the salvation of souls by giving counsel to those seeking it in the church, I believe that the authority of the Counselor and the greater utility of that counsel would have compelled Bernard to obey the divine counsel rather than St. Benedict's command, for the honor of God and the salvation of those seeking advice.

And from this, it seems to follow that we are more obligated to any divine counsel than to an incompatible human command. Second, it seems to follow that no one is bound to obey a private command unless it encourages one toward a divine counsel or command from a pure heart. And it is clear that in the "mode" of the command, the proper measure of reason and the quality of the command are implied, which both the one obeying and the commander ought to discuss. For what reason would there be, at the mandate of a dull and fat Bishop <note>Hus uses this biting imagery to contrast worldly, unintellectual, or gluttonous clergy with the spiritual needs of the people.</note>, for a priest to feed sows and leave the sheep of Christ—whom He redeemed with His own blood—without pasture?

<margin>The circumstance of place must be weighed in a command.</margin>
Similarly, the circumstance of place must be weighed; for if a prelate commands a subordinate to appear in a place full of enemies plotting the subordinate’s death, the subordinate is not bound to obey. Thus, Pope Clement V <note>Clement V (r. 1305–1314), the first of the Avignon popes.</note> says in the *Clementines*, in the decree *Pastoralis*: "For who would dare, or by what reason is anyone bound to dare to undergo the judgment of such a consistory <note>A formal meeting of church officials.</note>, to place himself in the lap of his enemies, and to offer himself voluntarily to a death to be inflicted by violent injury rather than by justice?" This is indeed established by law.

<sig>Bb ij</sig>

<summary>Hus argues that human obedience is conditional. Using the example of a priest ordered to feed pigs instead of saving souls, he asserts that God's counsel and common sense must override unreasonable or harmful commands from Church prelates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument concerning the limits of obedience. He previously discussed "middle acts" (actions neither good nor evil in themselves) and is now applying these principles to legal summons, specifically arguing that a person is not required to obey a summons that violates natural law or puts them in danger from their enemies.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>
# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: because [of the chapter] Nemine</meta>

is rightfully feared, for this is the custom of such visits, which human reason flees and nature abhors. Therefore, anyone who believes that a citation of this kind <note>original: "citationem huiusmodi"</note> binds the person summoned would be acting foolishly. Nor should the power of defense, which arises from natural law, be taken away; for even an Emperor is not permitted to remove those things which exist by natural law.

<margin>Words of Pope Nicholas.</margin>
Likewise, Pope Nicholas to Emperor Michael <note>Nicholas I (r. 858–867) writing to the Byzantine Emperor Michael III regarding the trial of Photius.</note> (in the Decree, Case 3, Question 5): "Because suspect and hostile judges ought not to be allowed, as reason itself dictates and is proven by many examples. For what more pleasing or delightful gift can one give an enemy than to commit someone to him for an attack whom he perhaps wishes to harm?" It is known that the sixth chapter of the Canons of the Synod of Constantinople prohibits this very thing. In the same chapter, Pope Gelasius, a most powerful conqueror of heretics, says: "I ask," he says, "where can the judgment they claim be carried out? Before the very ones who are themselves the enemies, witnesses, and judges? Human affairs should not be committed to such a judgment." If human judgments should not be committed to a trial where the judges are also the enemies, how much less should divine—that is, ecclesiastical—matters be? Let him who is wise understand.

And indeed, the pious Emperor Justinian is known to have promulgated this in his laws, saying: "Let it be permitted for him who considers a judge suspect to refuse him before the lawsuit begins, so that recourse may be had to another." For it is in a way natural to avoid the traps of judges and to wish to flee the judgment of enemies. Thus, Saint John Chrysostom <note>original: "Ioannes os aureum" or "John of the Golden Mouth."</note> refused to enter the assembly of the Council gathered against him. Gratian concludes there: "A defendant is by no means to be led outside of his own province." Hence Pope Fabian (Case 3, Question 6) says: "Let the case always be conducted there where the crime was committed, and let him who fails to prove what he charged suffer the punishment he intended for the other."

Likewise, Pope Stephen (Case 3, Question 6): "The license to accuse shall not proceed beyond the boundaries of the provinces, but every accusation shall be heard within the province." The same is evident (Case 3, Question 6) from the Roman Synod.

<column-break/>

<page-num>191</page-num>
# CHAPTER XXI
<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<margin>No one ought to be summoned to the Roman Court.</margin>
In the chapter "Nemine" <note>A reference to a specific chapter in Canon Law.</note>. What color of truth or what reason of obedience is there, that a person summoned over three hundred miles, unknown to the Pope and denounced by enemies, should travel so anxiously through his enemies and come before judges and witnesses who are his enemies? Should he waste the goods of the poor on great expenses, or, if he lacks funds, travel miserably in thirst and hunger? And what is the fruit of this comparison? Certainly, it is the neglect of the labor enjoined by God regarding his own salvation and that of others. And he will not be taught there how to believe well, but how to litigate—which is not permitted to a servant of God. There he will be plundered in the consistory, grow cold in holy character, be incited to impatience through oppression, and if he has nothing to give, he will be condemned, even if he has justice on his side. And what is more serious, he will be compelled to adore the Pope as a god on bended knees.

<margin>It is necessary to adore the Pope at Rome.</margin>
Blessed be God, therefore, who said in Genesis 18: "I will go down and see whether they have finished the work according to the cry that has come to me; and if not, I will know." Blessed be the Son of God, who descended from heaven to seek and to save that which was lost (Luke 19). He who went about all the cities and villages, teaching in their synagogues and preaching the gospel of the kingdom, and healing every sickness and every infirmity. Seeing the crowds, he had compassion on them because they were harassed and dejected, like sheep without a shepherd (Matthew 9). Blessed be Christ himself, who commanded Peter in Matthew 18, saying: "If your brother sins against you, go and rebuke him between you and him alone." 

Hence the Pope will not find any other places—unless by doing the opposite—showing that Christ visited people for such citations. For if the Popes weighed that law of Christ in Matthew 7: "All things whatsoever you would that men should do to you, do you even so to them; for this is the law and the prophets," I estimate that they would not reasonably wish for men to summon them and force them to undertake such a dangerous and unknown journey. Why then should they urge others without a clear and rational cause? Why should they not weigh the exemplary life of Christ the High Priest, who piously visited those erring and oppressed by the devil?

<summary>Jan Hus argues that natural law and church canons protect a person from being summoned to a distant court (like the Roman Curia) where the judges are their personal enemies. He contrasts the Pope's practice of summoning subjects with Christ's example of personally visiting and healing the people.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument concerning the limits of obedience and the validity of legal summons. <page-num>192</page-num>
# JOHN HUS ON THE CHURCH

<margin>He who wishes to correct another must be irreproachable.</margin>
...not by citing, nor by excommunicating, nor by imprisoning or burning, although he commanded Peter—and in him, any of his Vicars—saying: "If your brother sins against you, go and rebuke him," etc. <note>Matthew 18:15. Hus is emphasizing the personal, pastoral approach over legalistic or violent punishment.</note>

Where a Vicar of Peter should note, first, that when he wishes to rebuke a brother, he must first keep himself irreproachable, since charity must begin with oneself. For how can a Prelate who is entirely drenched in the <term>Simoniac heresy</term> <note>Simony: the illegal buying or selling of church offices or spiritual things.</note>, pride, luxury, or avarice, lawfully rebuke a brother? To such a one the Lord says, "Hypocrite, first take the log out of your own eye, and then you will see clearly to take the speck out of your brother’s eye" (Luke 6). Or how could he condemn anyone to death when the Savior says in John 8, "He who is without sin among you, let him be the first to throw a stone at her." Indeed, if this law of Christ were weighed carefully, a Prelate would rarely be found in these times who could lawfully correct someone or condemn them for heretical depravity.

Secondly, the Vicar of Christ should note that the Savior commanded, saying, "Go." Here he commands that judges ought to visit their subjects in the places where a crime is said to have been committed in order to rebuke them, as the <term>canon laws</term> <note>The body of laws and regulations made by Church leadership.</note> also proclaim; for Christ did so, and likewise the Apostles. And Christ will do so in the final judgment, as he alone foretells in Matthew 25. Thirdly, the Vicar of Peter or a Prelate should note that he must be prudent, diligent, and intent in his manner of rebuking, so that he does not excommunicate before the end of a third correction. Fourthly, he should take note of a multitude of faithful witnesses for himself, by whom the brother's crime may be established. And fifthly, he should "tell it to the church" as the greater authority. For so the Lord commanded Peter: "tell the church."

<margin>Why the author refused to appear in Rome.</margin>
From what has already been said, I conclude that the pretended excommunication does not affect me, or rather does not bind me, because hostile judges and witnesses reside in Rome, and the judge is personally involved in the case. For me, the distance is long, surrounded on all sides by German enemies <note>original: "Teutonicis". Hus’s conflict had a strong ethnic dimension between Czechs and Germans in the Holy Roman Empire.</note>; I see no fruit in appearing, but rather the neglect of the people in the word of God. I hope that Christ protected me when he said in Matthew 10:

<meta>catchword: Behold</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>193</page-num>
## CHAPTER XXI

"Behold, I send you out as sheep in the midst of wolves; therefore be wise as serpents and simple as doves. But beware of men, for they will hand you over to councils, and they will scourge you in their synagogues." And in Matthew 24: "Behold, I have told you beforehand. So if they say to you, ‘Behold, he is in the desert,’ do not go out; ‘Behold, he is in the inner rooms,’ do not believe it; ‘Behold, here is Christ or there,’ do not believe it." Therefore, I have committed myself to Christ himself, so that my life may be ended either under the pretended excommunication of men, or outside of it, whether by a natural death or a violent one.

<margin>The circumstance of time must be considered in the injunction of obedience.</margin>
Then, regarding the circumstance of time: there is no doubt that it is required to know both the one commanding and the one obeying, specifically when an act that is good in its kind, or <term>neutral</term> <note>original: "neuter". In scholastic theology, these are "indifferent acts"—things neither commanded nor forbidden by God, like walking or picking up a straw.</note>, ought to be performed. For if on the feast of Easter a prelate should command a subordinate to fast, or on Good Friday command him not to fast while he is in good health, would it be lawful for the subordinate to obey against the approved custom of the church and against the subordinate’s own resisting conscience? Or if at midnight he should command him to wander through the woods among cruel wild beasts without necessity? There are many such commands that are contrary to reason. Nor should it be drawn into a precedent if somewhere in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "Vitifpatrum" (Vitae Patrum), a famous collection of the lives and sayings of the Desert Fathers.</note> subordinates are found to have obeyed in such irrelevant or neutral works. This is like certain of the holy fathers—as Hugo of Saint Victor <note>A prominent 12th-century mystic and theologian.</note> says in his booklet titled *On Those Things Which Cannot Lawfully Be Done*—where we read that certain holy fathers commanded their subjects to do many things that are foreign to human reason in order to test the virtue of their obedience, such as watering dry sticks until they produced sprouts, softening hard rocks unnecessarily, or taming fierce beasts by a word of command.

<margin>The circumstance of the person is to be considered in obedience.</margin>
As for the circumstance of the person, it is clear that all reason is more direct both in a work that is good in its kind and in a neutral work. In a work good in its kind, if a Prelate commands a subordinate to give alms while impoverishing his own children, or to undertake a penance of fasting that he cannot competently endure...

<sig>Cc</sig>
<meta>catchword: or many</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the Pope's authority to excommunicate or summon is limited by biblical standards of justice, specifically citing his own refusal to travel to Rome due to the presence of hostile enemies and the lack of a fair trial. He further discusses the limits of religious obedience, arguing that even "neutral" commands must be reasonable and not violate a person's conscience or well-being.</summary>
