# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

<margin>He who wishes to correct otherwise must answer here.</margin>
...or to perform many prayers, just as confessors impose burdens upon men. Certainly, in such cases, even the Pope is not to be heard <note>original: "audiendus" - meaning "obeyed" or "hearkened to" in a legal and moral sense.</note>, since a parent is more bound to nourish his children than to give alms to others, and he is not bound to take on unbearable burdens. And the same reasoning applies to **neutral works** <note>These are "indifferent" acts (neither inherently good nor evil), such as building a house or choosing a trade.</note>. For if the Pope commanded me to play the flute, build towers, mend or weave garments, or stitch together a falsehood, should my reason not judge that the Pope commands foolishly? Why should I not prefer my own sense in this to the judgment of the Pope? Indeed, even if he commanded those things to me along with all our doctors, reason would judge that their judgment was foolish.

Likewise, if the Pope, by his own impulse, should even by command grant a Bishopric to one who is unfit because of a **vicious life** and ignorance of the law of God, as well as because of inexperience in the language of the people he ought to rule, should that man obey him by accepting it? It is clear that he should not. Similarly, those people should not accept him, since they would not even want to appoint someone as a shepherd of pigs or goats who was unable to feed those animals. And 
<margin>Christ is to be heard first, then his vicar.</margin>
it is clear that every faithful disciple of Christ must look to the first exemplar, Christ, and only listen to a prelate insofar as he commands His law, and commands things that are reasonable and edifying, and thus lawful for a subject. For the martyr **Cyprian** <note>St. Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258 AD), an early Christian bishop and theologian.</note> says in Distinction 8: "If Christ alone is to be heard, we must not pay attention to what someone else before us thought should be done, but what Christ, who is before all, did first." This most firm rule is set aside by the **Satraps of the Antichrist** <note>Hus uses "Satraps" (originally Persian governors) as a pejorative term for high-ranking church officials who serve the "Antichrist" (his term for the corrupt institutional hierarchy).</note>, who say that disobedience to Papal statutes is to be punished most severely, and thus Christ and His law are set aside. Whence, assuming 
<margin>Why the author refused to appear.</margin>
that the Pope and prelates must be obeyed in all neutral matters, the Pope could, by making the law of Christ difficult, decree that no Christian may perform any neutral work unless he himself later ratifies it with approval; and consequently, he could order his satraps to summon anyone...

### CHAPTER XXI

...anywhere to appear and answer in his judgment, and thus they could harass the people at will and fine them, as they do in absolutions, in reservations, and in dispensations. And believe me, they would practice this even more, except they fear the people would rebel upon perceiving their craftiness. For God now enlightens the people so they are not led away from the ways of Christ. For in **Daniel chapter 9** <note>Hus cites Daniel 9, though the following prophecy is primarily found in Daniel 11:31-33.</note>, he prophesied saying: "And arms shall stand on his part, and they shall pollute the sanctuary... and they shall set up the abomination unto desolation, and the wicked shall deal deceitfully against the covenant. But the people who know their Lord shall prevail and act." The **arms of the Antichrist**, which stand and pollute the sanctuary of God, are the evil prelates, who are an abomination because of their crimes, and a desolation in their rejection of the imitation of Christ. Regarding this abomination, Christ says in **Matthew 24** <note>The OCR reads 23, but the quote is from the "Little Apocalypse" in Matthew 24.</note>: "When you see the abomination of desolation standing in the holy place." And in **Mark 13**: "Standing where it ought not," which was spoken of by Daniel the prophet. And when the prophet adds, "And the wicked shall deal deceitfully against the covenant," it is because they say they hold the covenant of Christ, but they do not, because they will <gloss>gloss</gloss> <note>To "gloss" here means to interpret or explain a text, often in a way that twists the original meaning.</note> it perversely for their own excellence and to excuse their sins. But the people who know their Lord—that is, recognizing Christ through the gift of God’s grace by imitating Him—shall prevail and keep the commands of the covenant of the Lord Jesus Christ. But because the persecution of death meets those who teach these things, Daniel further says: "And the learned among the people shall teach many, and they shall fall by the sword, and by flame, and by captivity, and 
<margin>Those who contradict evil prelates are publicly killed.</margin>
by the ruin of days. And when they fall, they shall be helped with a little help, and many shall join them deceitfully." The experience of facts explains the understanding of this text, because the simple laity and priests, learned through the grace of God, teach many by the example of a good life, and publicly speaking against the lie of the Antichrist, they fall by the sword...

He identifies corrupt prelates as the "arms of the Antichrist" foretold by the prophet Daniel and warns that those who teach the truth will face violent persecution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the limits of obedience. He has just argued that even the Pope should not be obeyed if his commands are foolish or contrary to the law of Christ. Here, he expands on the historical cost of such resistance and the logical framework (the "eight circumstances") for determining when an act is truly virtuous and when obedience is required.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

by the sword, as is clear regarding the laymen John, Martin, and Stascone <note>These refer to three Prague artisans executed in 1412 for protesting the sale of indulgences; they were treated as martyrs by the Hussite movement.</note>, who, by contradicting the lying disciples of the **Antichrist** <note>Hus frequently used the term "Antichrist" to refer to the corrupt institutional Church hierarchy when it acted in opposition to Christ's teachings.</note>, fell by the sword. Others, however, offering their necks for the sake of the truth, were martyrized, captured, imprisoned, and slaughtered; yet they did not deny the truth of Christ, both priests as well as laypeople and women. But those who applied themselves to them deceitfully went away, for, terrified by the censures and imprisonments of the Antichrist, they turned back toward the opposite way. Nevertheless, the Lord still multiplies the children of the Church, who are patient in suffering and announce the truth of Christ the Lord. Blessed be God and the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, who has hidden the way of truth from the "wise" and "prudent" and revealed it to simple laypeople and "little" priests <note>original: "paruulis sacerdotibus" - likely referring to lower-ranking clergy who remained faithful to the Gospel over the hierarchy.</note>, who choose to obey God rather than men. In acts that are good by their nature and in neutral acts <note>referring to 'adiaphora' or indifferent matters</note>, having the life of Christ before their eyes, they obey their superiors only insofar as those limited acts, governed by their circumstances, can be reasonably traced back to the edification found in the imitation of Jesus Christ.

<margin>A virtuous act is made right by eight circumstances.</margin>
For they truly understand that eight circumstances make an act virtuous, which are listed in this verse: *Who, what, where, how much, how many, why, how, when.* **Who**, therefore, refers to the person who ought to obey. **What**, namely, what he ought to do when commanded. **Where**, because it is appropriate to obey in one place regarding a good or neutral act, but not in another or just anywhere. **How much**, namely, how far he ought to obey, because he is bound only so far as something is commanded for edification and is reducible to a mandate or counsel of Jesus Christ. For no one is bound to obey his private superior infinitely, as the foolish babble, saying that the power of the Pope extends to commanding infinitely, and that every individual Christian must obey him to that extent.

**How many**, namely, which acts he can lawfully do when it is not necessary—for instance, if a subordinate, at the command of a priest enjoining penance,

<page-num>197</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXI</header>

gives two *grossos* <note>original: "grossos" - a common silver coin of the period.</note> or pennies, or fasts three days a week. Whether he should give as many coins or fast as many days as the **idiot** <note>original: "idiota" - here meaning an unlearned or simple-minded superior.</note> commands or limits, unless he again satisfies the confessor; for example, that he should give as much for the building of the Church of St. Peter as would be necessary if he were there, and as much to the Pope’s chamber as he would consume on the journey—and so it goes with the other "taxes" invented by the devil.

<margin>The end is the measure of those things which lead to the end.</margin>
The faithful person must also weigh the circumstance of the purpose, namely **Why**: for what end he ought to obey in the act he perceives. If it leads to the honor of God and directly to the benefit of the Church, then the end is good. If, however, another end is intended, then it is against that word of the Apostle: *Whatever you do, do in the name of Jesus Christ.* For the end measures all the means that are performed for its sake. Hence, Aristotle concludes in the second book of *On the Soul*, saying: "It is just that all things be named by their end," so that if the end is good, the means toward it is also good.

Another circumstance is also added when it is said, **How**. For it is not enough to do something good by its nature, but it is required that it be done *well*. Nothing is done well by a man unless it is done in **charity** <note>original: "charitate" - Christian love or grace.</note>; therefore, the Apostle says: *Let all your things be done in charity.* And that nothing is done well by a man without charity, the Apostle proves in 1 Corinthians 13, saying: *If I distribute all my goods to feed the poor, but have not charity, it profits me nothing.* And the reason is that the branch cannot bear fruit unless it remains in the vine, as the Savior says in John 15: *You cannot bear fruit unless you remain in me*—namely, through charity. Hence it is said generally, as a certain philosopher put forth as a maxim <note>original: "phantasma protulit" - likely referring to a common academic saying or insight.</note>, that "God is a rewarder of adverbs, not of nouns." <note>This means God judges the *manner* (how) an act is done rather than just the *name* of the act itself.</note> And it is clear that for true obedience, grace or charity is required; and then the other circumstances are implied in that adverb, namely "how," because the person obeying must execute the commanded work charitably, humbly, prudently, joyfully, bravely, and with readiness.
<meta>catchword: The last...</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that obedience to church authorities is limited by the "eight circumstances" of a virtuous act and must be rooted in charity and the imitation of Christ. He explicitly criticizes the collection of church taxes and the claim of infinite papal power as devilish inventions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hus’s examination of the conditions of obedience. On the previous page, he discussed how acts must be evaluated based on their nature and circumstances. Here, he addresses the specific condition of "when" an act is required and uses logical puzzles to demonstrate that a superior's command is only valid if it aligns with divine reason.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: command-</meta>

The final condition is **When**, namely, it is appropriate to execute the commanded work, as was said before regarding time; for without doubt there are many good acts, both in their kind and neutral <note>referring to *adiaphora*, or things neither commanded nor forbidden by God</note>, which it is not expedient to command at every time, and consequently it is not expedient to obey in them at any time whatsoever.

<margin>It is argued.</margin>
But for that point it is further argued thus: suppose that the **Pope** <note>original: "Papa"</note> enjoins upon any cleric subject to him, by virtue of holy obedience and under the pretext of absolution from punishment and guilt <note>original: "a pœna & culpa" — a common phrase in the sale of indulgences</note>, or some other spiritual support, that he first meet a cleric who is fully obedient to the Pope; and he enjoins upon any Layman in a similar form that he first meet a person disobedient to the Pope, and let this injunction be made under the gravest **anathema** <note>the most severe form of excommunication</note>; and suppose with this that every Cleric or Layman subject to this Pope was at first fully obedient to him, and that every Cleric first specifically meets a Layman, and vice versa. And it seems a contradiction follows; for given that Peter the Cleric and Paul the Layman first meet each other, I ask: is Peter at the moment of meeting obedient to the Pope? If so, then in this case one must say that Paul at that moment is disobedient to the Pope, because since he first meets Peter, who is fully <insert>disobedient</insert> to the Pope, and it was enjoined upon him that he first meet one disobedient to the Pope, it follows that Paul incurs the mark of disobedience. And thus if Peter, in this case and for the moment of meeting, is disobedient to the Pope, then one must grant that Paul is obedient, and consequently Peter is as well, because before the meeting both were obedient, and Peter does not become disobedient from the meeting, but his obedience is confirmed according to what was said. Nor is it valid to deny the case because of such things. First, because the command is only neutral or possible; Second, because a **Prelate** <note>a high-ranking church official</note> can command something simply impossible and entirely irrational; therefore, by the same logic or even more so, he can command this. Therefore, no response remains except the truth: that because of his commanding, the cleric or layman incurs neither more nor less punishment or reward. Because it is necessary

<column-break/>

<page-num>199</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXI.</header>
<meta>catchword: person</meta>

that the command to be obeyed be first reasonable in the eyes of God. And then it would be done, even if no one commanded it under human obedience, since otherwise a man would become disobedient to reason. And it is clear that, just as in this case there is no reason to wait for full remission or anathema, so generally it is the same in the judgment of the people, except for as much as one has deserved before God. For it is necessary that this logical objection be solved. Similarly, suppose that **Prior Peter** has with him two groups of twelve cloistered monks <note>original: "duodenas clauſtrales"</note> all obedient to him, and he commands the more stable group of twelve that they not speak with the rest, unless perhaps to lead a disobedient one back to obedience. And it is clear that the second group of twelve does not speak with the rest, except for the purpose of confirming obedience to Peter. And suppose Paul of the first twelve begins to speak with Linus of the second twelve, both intending in this to observe obedience, so that continuously before that conversation both were rightly obedient to Peter; a contradiction will be evident.

Item, as **Bernard** <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential mystic and church reformer</note> says in his letter to Adam the monk: that "middle things" <note>indifferent matters</note> can equally and indifferently be commanded or forbidden both well and poorly. <margin>A Prelate is to be corrected charitably when he commands poorly.</margin> Therefore, when a Superior commands or forbids poorly, the subject, when he recognizes him commanding or forbidding poorly, ought out of the law of charity to rebuke him as a brother, since if by commanding or forbidding he sins against God and against his brother. This is clear through that rule of **Jesus Christ** in **Matthew 18**:
> If your brother sins against you, rebuke him between you and him alone.
Nor does it stand in the way that one more excellent in virtue may rebuke one inferior in character, even if he is superior in status; for otherwise this law of Christ would perish, which ordains that any Christian Prelate, when he has sinned, is to be corrected by another. For this law says universally:
> If your brother has sinned against you, rebuke him.
Hence if, through the impossible, Christ had sinned, since he is our brother (according to **Hebrews chapter 2**), he would have had to be corrected by the church. Hence he hinted at this, saying in **John 8** to the crowds:
> Which of you convicts me of sin?
Hence the Church appropriately sings in the person

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument regarding the limits of obedience and the necessity of moral accountability. On the previous page, he used a logical puzzle to show that commands cannot be obeyed if they lead to a contradiction or a violation of spiritual duty. Here, he moves to the controversial claim that even the Pope is subject to correction by those of lower rank if he errs.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

the person of our Savior: "My people, what have I done to you, or in what have I burdened you? Answer me," <note>original: "Popule meus quid feci tibi..." — a quote from Micah 6:3, often used in the liturgy of Good Friday</note> meaning, answer by bringing a charge against me. And in the first chapter of Isaiah: "Cease to do evil, learn to do good, seek justice, come to the aid of the oppressed, defend the orphan, plead for the widow; and come, and let us reason together, says the Lord." <note>Isaiah 1:16–18</note>

<margin>A superior can be corrected by an inferior, even the Pope.</margin>
It is clear, therefore, that every "wayfarer" <term>wayfarer: a common medieval term (viator) for a human being currently living on Earth, traveling toward eternity</term> who sins ought to be corrected by a brother; otherwise, the law of Christ would be failing by not providing a remedy against a stain upon his Bride <note>The Church</note>. Paul confirms this in Galatians 2, when he resisted Pope Peter to his face for a minor fault, and left this in writing for posterity so that they might do similarly to their brother in a similar case. Therefore, it is treacherous to assert that a superior state cannot be corrected in matters of morals by an inferior subject. Thus, in certain cases, a son may lawfully correct a father, a daughter a mother, a subject a Prelate, and a disciple a teacher, according to the rule of charity.

But an objection is raised against this: the Pope acts as the representative of the Lord Jesus Christ on earth, and no one was permitted to correct Christ, as is clear in Matthew 16. There, because of his attempt to correct Christ, Peter is called "Satan"; therefore, it is not permitted to correct His representative. But this sophistry limps badly, for it would require one to say that every Vicar of Christ is impeccable <note>sinless</note> just as Christ was. However, it follows logically that neither the Pope nor anyone else should be rebuked or corrected *insofar as* he follows the head, Christ. But if a Bishop or a confessor, acting as a representative of Christ, attempts an act of lust with a virgin or a chaste wife, should she not vehemently rebuke him as an **Antichrist** and a treacherous enemy of her soul? For in that illicit act, he does not represent Christ, but the true Antichrist and the devil, most wickedly tempting the woman. And it is clear that the saying of Bernard <note>Bernard of Clairvaux, a 12th-century mystic and doctor of the church</note>—which the doctors cite—stating that in "middle" <note>neutral or indifferent</note> matters it is certainly not right for us to prefer our own sense to the judgment of the Masters or Prelates, and that their commands or prohibitions are not to be despised, must be understood according to the circumstances attached to an act of due obedience:

<meta>catchword: as said</meta>

---

<page-num>201</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXI</header>

as was said, according to the manner, place, time, and person. For often a disciple reasonably does not obey in an "indifferent" act <note>an act neither commanded nor forbidden by God</note>, or even a good act of its kind, by showing a reason why it is not expedient to obey. This has happened to me frequently: while I was commanding, I received instruction from a disciple on a better way and, gratefully accepting the information, I obeyed the disciple. The same logic applies to a Prelate, since in our times Prelates, frequently raving in their commands because of ignorance, must be charitably rebuked by their subjects for the benefit of the church.

<margin>An equal has no authority over an equal.</margin>
But there is still an objection against this. For "an equal has no authority over an equal." Since the Pope exceeds every living person, and any superior exceeds his subject, it seems that it is not the business of any wayfarer to correct the Pope, nor for any subject to correct his Prelate.

To this it is said: since the "antecedent" <note>the initial premise</note> is a necessary truth—as God the Father necessarily has no authority over the Holy Spirit, and yet they are equal persons—the "consequence" <note>the logical result</note> does not follow. Furthermore, God the Father necessarily has authority over the Son according to His assumed humanity, and yet they are absolutely and necessarily equal. Therefore, if it were true that no equal has authority over an equal, the Catholic faith would suffer an injury. Again, there is a "vicarious authority," which is the nature of all human authority, of which the Apostle speaks in 1 Timothy 6: "Command the rich of this world not to be arrogant." But there is another authority that is of itself or originally authentic over every creature. And it is clear that this argument is hollow in both matter and form. For by this principle, it is understood that an equal does not have *authoritative* power over an equal in the specific sense that they are equal to the one commanding. But what prevents one who is more excellent in virtue from rebuking an inferior in morals, even if that person is superior in rank?

<margin>No one wished to judge the Pope.</margin>
But it is further objected from the Canon Law <note>Gratian’s Decretum, Distinctio 21, "Nunc autem"</note>, where it is said that none of the holy Bishops dared to pass judgment on Marcellinus

<sig>Dd</sig>
<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that moral accountability supersedes ecclesiastical rank, asserting that even a Pope can and should be corrected by an inferior if he sins. He refutes the idea that a leader's status as Christ's representative grants them immunity, noting that a sinning leader represents the Antichrist rather than Christ.</summary>
<keywords>correction of the Pope, hierarchy of obedience, Antichrist, moral authority, Pope Marcellinus, Bernard of Clairvaux, vicarious authority, clerical ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues Hus’s argument from page 201 regarding the duty to correct erring superiors. He is finishing a historical example concerning Pope Marcellinus, who was famously said to have abdicated after being confronted by his bishops for a lapse in faith.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH</header>

...[that they did not dare] to pass sentence on the Pope. But they said, "Judge your cause by your own mouth; you shall not be subject to our judgment." <note>original: "Tuo ore iudica causam tuam..." — Hus is citing a legendary account of the Council of Sinuessa, where Pope Marcellinus was urged to judge himself for having offered incense to idols.</note>

Here it is said that this statement by the Bishops cannot set aside the law of God, by which He rebuked Pope Peter through Paul. <note>Referring to Galatians 2:11, where Paul confronted Peter in Antioch.</note> Secondly, it is said that it would be entirely superfluous for them to rebuke him in such a case, since they perceived from his contrition that he had been fully rebuked by the Lord. Thirdly, it is said that he was rebuked sufficiently by them when they said: "Judge your cause by your own mouth, not by our judgment." And again: "Do not be heard in our judgment, but gather your cause into your own bosom." And once more: "Since," they said, "by yourself you shall be justified, or by your own mouth you shall be condemned." Certainly, that is a great rebuke, because those rebuking him threw the sentence of correction back upon the Pope himself. Wherefore, hearing these things, Marcellinus dictated the sentence of deposition against himself.

And it is clear that a subordinate, with prudence and according to the rule of charity, can correct an erring superior and lead him back to the way of truth. For if a superior were traveling by a devious path toward the plunder of robbers or into the danger of death, it would be lawful for a subordinate to pull him back and thus preserve him from danger. Therefore, by an even stronger argument, this is lawful when a superior, through a devious path of morals, runs into the plunder of demons and into the danger of the worst death of sins. If, therefore, the superior would rejoice in the first case <note>physical danger</note>, why should he not rejoice even more in the second <note>spiritual danger</note>? If he employs a guardian in the first, why not in the second?

<margin>How the saying of Augustine—that subordinates should not examine their superior—is to be understood.</margin>
Nor does that saying of the Blessed Augustine in *The Conflict of Virtues and Vices* stand in the way: "What sort of men those who command ought to be is not for subordinates to examine." This is true and plain: that they ought not to examine rashly what sort of men they should be; yet reason dictates to them that they ought to be good, that they ought not to live outrageously, and if they live badly, subordinates, considering this, ought to beware of their evil works. Wherefore Augustine says: "If human command is to be obeyed, it is necessary to be subject to divine mastery." For He Himself says: "He who hears you, hears me; he who spurns you, spurns...

---

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXII</header>

...spurns me." <note>Luke 10:16</note> And afterward he adds: "But yet, because He foresaw that not all would be such, assuming the persons of all subordinates in the disciples, He said as a forewarning: 'The scribes and Pharisees have sat upon the chair of Moses; whatever they tell you, do; but what they do, do not do.'" <note>Matthew 23:2–3</note> And it is clear that out of zeal for a good intention, subordinates ought to examine or weigh the life of their elders, so that if they are good, they may imitate them; if they are evil, they should not follow their works, but in a humble spirit pray for them, and observe when they command good things. Otherwise they would not examine, they would fall with a blind leader into a pit, and it would easily happen that they would worship the Antichrist as God and, like the Jewish people consenting to the Prelates, would conspire against Christ the Lord.

### How the author of this work was unjustly excommunicated. Also, what excommunication is, when it is to be inflicted, and how it is incurred. CHAPTER XXII.

Finally, the doctors state in their writing as follows: "At last, because the proceedings against Master Jan Hus have been received by the community of the Clergy in Prague, and they have obeyed them, therefore they must be obeyed, and especially since no pure good is prohibited there, nor is any pure evil commanded. But according to the style of the Church of the Roman Curia, customary and observed before the fathers of our fathers, only the 'middle things' <term>middle things: Latin 'media'—actions that are neither inherently good (like prayer) nor inherently evil (like murder), but depend on context and authority</term> are commanded there between pure goods and pure evils, which according to the manner, place, and time, can be either good or evil; and in these middle things, according to evangelical doctrine and according to Bernard, one must obey," etc. And they add: "Nor is it for the Clergy in Prague to judge whether the excommunication of Master Jan Hus is just or unjust," etc.

Regarding this, in the proceedings which I am presently considering—namely, excommunication, suspension, and interdict <note>interdict: a collective punishment where a community is banned from church services and sacraments</note>, about which...

<summary>Hus argues that subordinates have a moral duty to examine and, if necessary, correct the conduct of their superiors to avoid spiritual ruin. He then begins to address the specific legal proceedings and excommunication leveled against him by the authorities in Prague.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus's defense against the theological faculty of Prague. Having previously argued that subordinates can correct superiors, he now addresses the specific legal "logic" used by the doctors of theology to justify his excommunication.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

### CHAPTER 22

I will speak briefly. First, I will discuss how very poor the reasoning of the Doctors is. They argue: "Because the proceedings against Jan Hus were accepted by the community of the Clergy in Prague, and they obeyed them, therefore they must be obeyed." But if one were to argue this way: "Because the proceedings by the community of the Clergy in Jerusalem against Christ were accepted—claiming He is a deceiver, an evildoer, a blasphemer, excommunicated, and guilty of death—therefore the Doctors themselves ought to obey those proceedings." The logic holds by a similar comparison of the cause, because those proceedings were also accepted by the Clergy. The Doctors of Theology, and especially Stanislaus <note>Stanislaus of Znojmo, once Hus's teacher and friend, who became one of his fiercest theological opponents.</note>, since he is the better logician among them, should be ashamed of that logic. 

<margin>Learning logic from the high priests and Pharisees.</margin>
Perhaps they learned that logic from the High Priests, the scribes, and the Pharisees, who formed a similar argument. For in John 18, when Pilate said to them, "What accusation do you bring against this man?" they answered and spoke, forming this logical conclusion: "If he were not an evildoer, we would not have handed him over to you." <note>original: "Si nō esset hic malefactor, non tibi tradidissemus eum."</note> And John 19 presents this justification when Pilate said, "I find no case in him." They replied: "We have a law, and according to the law he ought to die, because he made himself the Son of God." <note>original: "Nos legem habemus, & secūdū legē debet mori, quia filium dei se fecit."</note> 

In the first logical conclusion of the Jews, the Doctors implied that they themselves could not err, saying, "If he were not an evildoer, we would not have handed him over to you"—that is, "Because he is an evildoer, therefore we have handed him over to you." Similarly, our Doctors imply in their logic that the community of the Clergy in Prague cannot err; otherwise, if they could, then their logic would not hold. And because that community *can* err in accepting legal proceedings—just as it also errs in acquiring them and maliciously executing them—therefore the Doctors’ reasoning is not sound.

And I wonder at that "enormous tail" <note>Hus uses "cauda" (tail) to refer to a concluding qualifier or an "appendix" to an argument that he finds ridiculous or hypocritical.</note> of the Doctors, with which they wish to cover the shame of their statement, when this "tail" is added: "Nor is it for the Clergy in Prague to judge whether the excommunication of Master Jan Hus is just or unjust." If those proceedings regarding excommunication must be obeyed,

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER 22

<page-num>205</page-num>

then they must be obeyed as if they are just, and not as if they are unjust. And since the Clergy, along with the Doctors, obeys them and has accepted them, they therefore obey them as just and have accepted them as just; consequently, the Doctors and the Clergy have judged that the proceedings are just. And yet their "tail" says that it is not for the Clergy in Prague to judge whether the excommunication of Master Jan Hus is just or unjust—and a manifest contradiction confirms this. The Clergy in Prague shouts, confirms, and asserts that the excommunication of Master Jan Hus is just; therefore, the Clergy in Prague *does* judge that the excommunication is just, while the "tail" of the Doctors says that it is not for the Clergy in Prague to judge whether that sentence is just or unjust. It is clearly evident that this "tail" contradicts the actual deeds and judgment of the Clergy in Prague.

Furthermore, if it is not for the Clergy in Prague to judge whether that excommunication is just or unjust, yet the Clergy approves it and acts according to the proceedings, then the Clergy in Prague does not know whether it acts justly or unjustly, nor does it hope that it acts justly. For judgment must precede hope.

Also, the Doctors themselves judge that the excommunication of Master Jan Hus is just. This is clear because they judge that the proceedings must be obeyed—not as if they were unjust, therefore as if they were just. Consequently, the Doctors judge that the excommunication commanded in the proceedings is just.

Furthermore, the Doctors say that the proceedings must be obeyed, especially because in them "pure good" is not forbidden, nor is "pure evil" commanded, but only "middle things" <term>middle things: also called 'adiaphora,' these are actions neither commanded nor forbidden by God, where Hus's opponents argued total obedience to the Church was required.</term>, in which, according to Gospel doctrine and according to Saint Bernard, one must obey. Therefore, the Doctors judge that the things commanded in the proceedings are just—among which is the excommunication of Jan Hus. Therefore, the Doctors themselves judge that the excommunication of Jan Hus is just. And they themselves are the Clergy in Prague; therefore, that "tail" of theirs rebukes the Doctors themselves. Likewise, they judge that the excommunication which is [commanded] in the proceedings...

He mocks their legalistic qualifiers (the "tail") as a hypocritical attempt to avoid moral responsibility for their actions.</summary>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

### CHAPTER 22

...taken, is a middle ground between pure good and pure evil, and while it is prescribed according to mode, place, time, and person, it then passes into pure good, because it falls under the precept of the Pope and Prelates. Therefore, the doctors judging thus about excommunication judge that it is just. And yet, they ought not to judge that it is just from its "tail" <note>Hus uses "cauda" (tail) to mean the external result or the mere fact that the decree was issued, rather than the internal justice of the cause.</note>. In doing so, they do what they ought not; indeed, they do not even know what they are doing. For they claim it is not the place of the clergy in Prague to judge whether the excommunication of Jan Hus is just, yet they themselves judge it to be just. It is laughable how the Doctors of Law <note>The "Jurists" who specialized in Canon Law.</note> consented to that "tail"—they who are supposed to judge the Decrees <note>The *Decretum Gratiani*, the foundational collection of Church law.</note>, the decretals <note>Papal law-letters.</note>, and the legal processes to see if they are just, if they were carried out justly, or if the people involved are just. They ought to explain the Decrees and decretals reasonably and discern whether processes are just or unjust, and advise others based on the evidence whether a process should be admitted and upheld, or if it should be rejected, or if it is lawful to appeal against it. It is clear that both groups of doctors have unreasonably shut themselves off from the judgment of reason.

<margin>Master John of Jesenice, a venerable doctor of Canon Law from the University of Bologna, made it clear in the schools through a public determination that the processes thundered against me are null and erroneous. And because, as I said, such processes principally command excommunication, suspension, and interdict, I will speak briefly about them.</margin>

<margin>What excommunication is.</margin>
Noting first, that excommunication is defined as being "placed outside of communication" <note>original: "extra cōmunicationem positio"</note> [Canon Law citations: 11. q. 3. *Nihil*; and *Canonica*; and 7. q. 1. *Viduas*; and 23. q. 4. *Cum sacerdos*; 11. q. 3. *Omnis Christianus*, and the following chapter]. And because excommunication is better understood through its opposite, which is communication or communion—since every good thing is known through its opposite, and likewise the reverse—it must be noted that good communication or communion is threefold. The first is the participation in divine grace, which makes one pleasing—

<column-break/>

# CHAPTER 22

—participation. This is what the Apostle <note>St. Paul.</note> wishes for the Corinthians, saying in the last chapter of 2 Corinthians: "The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God, and the communication of the Holy Spirit be with you all." And this communication is the "communion of saints," who are the mystical body of Christ, whose head is Christ; we believe in this communion when we say, "I believe in the communion of saints." 

<margin>Second: of the sacraments.</margin>
The second communication or communion is the participation in the sacraments. Ephesians 4: "One Lord, one faith, one baptism." Specifically, however, it is taken for the participation in the body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, 1 Corinthians 10: "The cup of blessing which we bless, is it not the communication of the blood of Christ? And the bread which we break, is it not the participation of the Lord’s body? Since there is one bread, we who are many are one body, for we all partake of the one bread and the one cup."

<margin>Third: of suffrages.</margin>
The third communication or communion is the participation in suffrages <note>In medieval theology, "suffrages" refer to the shared spiritual benefits of prayers, masses, and good works performed by the faithful for one another.</note>. In this, the just man glories in the Lord in the Psalm: "I am a partaker of all those who fear Thee and keep Thy commandments." <note>Psalm 119:63.</note>

And besides this threefold communication, there is a communication that is the conversation <note>In the sense of social interaction or dwelling together.</note> of Christians, both good and evil, just as the first three belong only to good men; and men of the world weigh this fourth type more heavily.

Second, it should be noted that in this matter I am speaking of Excommunication in a way that corresponds to the fourfold communication already mentioned. First, it is a separation from the participation in divine grace that makes one pleasing. Second, from the worthy participation in the sacraments. Third, from the participation in suffrages which prepare one for eternal life—this is the opposite of the threefold communion. And from the fourth, it is the public exclusion from the conversation of Christians by the censure of a spiritual or secular judge.

<margin>Excommunication is not incurred except on account of mortal sin.</margin>
From these things, it follows first that excommunication from the first three types never occurs, nor can it occur, except through mortal sin. This is evident because sin, <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the thought on the next page.</note>

<summary>Jan Hus critiques the legal logic used to justify his excommunication and begins a systematic theological definition of "communion." He identifies four types of communion: grace, sacraments, spiritual suffrages, and social interaction, arguing that true excommunication from the first three can only be caused by mortal sin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s theological examination of excommunication. Having previously argued that the clergy’s judgment is often irrational, he now defines the two types of excommunication—major and minor—and asserts that only the individual's own sin, not a priest's decree, truly separates them from God's grace.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

### CHAPTER 22

...because no one is ever separated from the communion of saints—which is the participation in God’s grace, the sacraments, and the suffrages <note>Intercessory prayers and good deeds performed by the Church for the collective benefit of its members.</note> preparing one for life—except through mortal sin. This alone divides or separates a man from such communion, just as it separates him from God Himself. Nor can it happen otherwise, because as long as a man is in grace, he remains a participant in the threefold communion mentioned above, according to his current righteousness. And since God is the most just judge, He cannot harm a man regarding this participation except through the man’s own demerit; therefore, the conclusion is true. Second, it follows that no judge can ever excommunicate someone unless that person first excommunicates themselves through a crime. Third, it follows that no judge ought to excommunicate anyone except because of a crime, specifically because of mortal sin. And this is evident in the Church Law <note>original: "xj. q. iij." referring to Case 11, Question 3 of the *Decretum Gratiani*, a primary source of medieval Canon Law.</note>, where it is said: "Let no Bishop deprive anyone of ecclesiastical communion under anathema without a certain and manifest cause of sin." Because an anathema is the damnation of eternal death, it should not be imposed except for a mortal crime, and only on someone who could not be corrected in any other way. Similarly, in Case 23, Question 4, in the section "His ita respondetur," it says: "With God, it is not the sentence of the priests that is sought, but the life of the accused. For a sentence is not to be noted where the stain of sin has not infected the person."

Likewise, Lyra <note>Nicholas of Lyra (c. 1270–1349), a famous Franciscan theologian known for his literal interpretation of the Bible.</note> says on the end of Hosea 4: "Leave Israel, O Judah, because of their malice, for their feast is separated," which means it is excommunicated. And in Case 2, Question 1, "Multi," Saint Augustine says: "One ought not to be excommunicated except for a crime."

<margin>Major and minor excommunication.</margin>
It is commonly said that excommunication is twofold, namely Major and Minor, as seen in the decrees on sentences of excommunication <note>original: "de Sent. excom."</note>. There it is held that minor excommunication removes one from the receiving of the Sacraments. However, major excommunication separates one from the communion of the faithful. Minor excommunication is the separation through mortal

<column-break/>

209
### CHAPTER XXII
<meta>catchword: trium (three)</meta>

sin from participation in spiritual goods, by which a man makes himself unworthy through his crime to receive the sharing of grace; and no one can inflict this upon a man who persists in the grace of God. Major excommunication is the separation which the Prelates <note>High-ranking church officials, such as bishops.</note> of the church publish against a man as a public sinner, through which they exclude him from the daily company of Christians and from participation in the Sacraments. And it is by this major excommunication that they now mark me in legal processes and public denunciations, excluding me from all human interaction. But blessed be God, who did not give such power to that excommunication that it could take away virtue or justice from a righteous man, provided he suffers it humbly; nor can it bring sin upon him. Rather, as long as he sustains it patiently, it serves to purge him—just as earth cleans iron or fire cleans gold—and it accumulates the reward of blessedness, as the Lord says in Luke 6:

<margin>An unjust excommunication is meritorious to a person.</margin>
> "Blessed shall you be when men shall hate you, and when they shall separate you, and shall reproach you, and cast out your name as evil, for the Son of man's sake. Be glad in that day and rejoice; for behold, your reward is great in heaven. For according to these things did their fathers to the prophets."

<margin>Excommunication ought to be a medicine.</margin>
Now, this excommunication ought to be medicinal; that is, it should be a remedy for healing a man in spirit, for bringing him back into the fold of Christ, and it should be ordained toward eternal life as its ultimate end. Hence Saint Augustine says in his *Homily on Penance* (cited in Case 2, Question 1, "Multi"): "Excommunication ought not to be mortal, but medicinal." And in the Sixth Book of Decretals <note>original: "li. vj." referring to the *Liber Sextus*, a late 13th-century supplement to the Canon Law collections.</note>, in the chapter "Cum medicinalis": "A notorious sinner, because of a crime, after a threefold warning or public citation, if he refuses to be corrected, should be avoided in communication, according to the Savior's command in Matthew 18, where He said to Peter: 'If your brother sins against you, go and correct him between you and him alone; if he hears you, you have gained your brother. But if he does not hear you, take with you one or two, so that in the mouth of two or...'"

<summary>Jan Hus defines major and minor excommunication, arguing that true separation from God occurs only through mortal sin, and that an unjust excommunication by the Church is actually a purifying trial that earns heavenly rewards.</summary>

And although this excommunication is called "minor" because it is not performed solemnly and publicly by a prelate, nevertheless I fear it more than the "major" excommunication with which the Prelates now afflict me. But I fear the "greatest" excommunication even more, which the Supreme Pontiff <note>Christ, as the ultimate High Priest and judge.</note> sitting in judgment before all angels and men will use to excommunicate the damned from the participation in eternal happiness, saying in Matthew 25: "Depart, you cursed, into eternal fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels." A judge ought to weigh this excommunication and take care lest he excommunicate someone unjustly. For whoever excommu-

For he ought only to excommunicate one whom God has already excommunicated because of a crime which the judge knows the person committed; and this must be done after a threefold warning, out of charity for the honor of God, and for the salvation of the person he excommunicates, for the benefit of others, and so that they might fear and not be infected by him. This is what Paul did in the last chapter of first Corinthians, commanding that the public fornicator be purged out, lest he infect them, and so that his spirit might be saved. From these considerations, a faithful person would recognize how many Prelates, Clergy, and Laity are excommunicated by God—namely, all those who wander from the Lord’s commandments. And how many excommunicate themselves while they carry out or proclaim excommunication against others: and especially the clergy who pray almost daily in the morning service <note>original: "in Prima," referring to Prime, the first canonical hour of prayer.</note> saying, "Cursed are those who wander from your commandments." 

These are brief points on excommunication, about which a solemn proposition was made by a man of holy memory, a pious Christian and a great zealot and searcher of the law of Christ, Master Frederick Epinge, a Bachelor of Canon Law, treating the first article: "No Prelate ought to excommunicate anyone unless he first knows that person is excommunicated by God," about which I have also written elsewhere. And if you do not wish to believe it, learn at Bethlehem <note>The Bethlehem Chapel in Prague, where Hus preached and where texts were sometimes painted on the walls for the public to read.</note> on the wall; there you will find how an excommunication does not harm a righteous man, but profits him, and why even a righteous man ought to fear an unjust or "Pilate-like" <note>original: "Pilaticam," referring to the unjust judgment of Pontius Pilate.</note> excommunication. We respond that this is so, first, so that it might not come from elsewhere <note>Possibly meaning from a lack of vigilance.</note>. Second, because of the danger to the one excommunicating unjustly. Third, because of the harm to the brothers which could happen from foolish applications of the censure. Fourth, lest those retreating from the truth be scandalized. <meta>This page continues Hus’s discussion on excommunication. The catchword "possibile" connects the end of page 212 to the start of page 213. Hus is transitioning from the concept of "holy excommunication"—where the righteous are cast out by the wicked—to the legal definitions of suspension and interdict, arguing that human penalties are only valid if they mirror God's judgment.</meta>

<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

...it must be said, for the wicked excommunicated Christ and the man born blind, as is clear in John 9. And a distinction could also be made regarding its opposite, which is participation in evil, about which it says in Ephesians 5: "Do not participate in the unfruitful works of darkness" <note>original: "Nolite communicare operibus infructuosis tenebrarum"</note>. And in 2 John: "He who says 'hail' to him participates in his wicked works." And in Ecclesiasticus 13: "What fellowship has a holy person with a dog?" as if to say, none at all. Hence, everyone who exists in grace according to <term>present justice</term> <note>The state of being right with God at the current moment through his grace.</note> is excommunicated by the iniquitous. And this is a "holy excommunication," by which the righteous person is said to be excommunicated—that is, placed outside the communication or participation of malice. Whence in Revelation 18, John says: "And I heard a voice from heaven saying, 'Come out of her, my people, so that you do not participate in her sins and do not receive her plagues, for her sins have reached up to heaven.'" Let us pray to the Lord that he may deign to keep us in his communion and guard us from that which is illicit.

->What suspension is, what an ecclesiastical interdict is, and when and how they should be applied. Also, the author complains of his own unjust excommunication and the imposition of the interdict, etc.<-

### CHAPTER XXIII.

<margin>What suspension and an ecclesiastical interdict are.</margin>
Now we must speak of suspension. Here it should be noted that, in this context, "to suspend" means to prohibit a ministry or some benefit because of a crime. Thus, what the ancient decrees call "to suspend," the new laws and <term>decretals</term> <note>Letters of the Pope that formulate decisions on points of canon law.</note> call "to interdict." In such cases, they call it an <term>ecclesiastical suspension</term> from office, or from an ecclesiastical <term>benefice</term> <note>A permanent Church appointment for which property and income are provided in respect of pastoral or spiritual duties.</note>, or an <term>ecclesiastical interdict</term> from performing the office of the Church. Having established the definition of suspension just mentioned, it must be noted that just as it is proper to God alone to excommunicate a person by himself, so it is with suspension. <margin>The Pope can suspend no one unless God has suspended them first.</margin> Therefore, it is impossible for a Pope or a Bishop to justly suspend anyone unless God has first suspended them. Just as it is impossible

<column-break/>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXIII.</header>
<sig>Ee iij</sig>

possible for the Pope to think anything justly unless God causes that thought first. Hence the Apostle rightly says in 2 Corinthians 3: "We are not sufficient to think anything of ourselves as if from ourselves, but our sufficiency is from God." And the Supreme Bishop himself says in John 15: "Without me you can do nothing." From this it is clear that a suspension pronounced by a <term>prelate</term> <note>A high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop.</note> has only as much power as Almighty God gives to it. Thus, God's active suspension extends to priests, kings, and any survivors whom he removes from office or withdraws from life as a punishment. 

Hence he suspends someone from the dignity of the priesthood, as is written in Hosea 4: "Because you have rejected knowledge, I will reject you, that you may not perform the office of the priesthood for me" <note>original: "Quia tu scientiam repulisti, ideo te repellam ne sacerdotio fungaris mihi"</note>. And in Isaiah 1: "Offer no more vain sacrifice." And in Malachi 1: "I have no pleasure in you, and I will not accept an offering from your hand." And the Apostle of Christ suspends all those living in crime from the ministry of the body and blood of Christ, saying in 1 Corinthians 11: "Whoever eats the bread or drinks the cup of the Lord unworthily will be guilty of the body and blood of the Lord." 

Likewise, in 1 Samuel 2 <note>Hus cites this as 1 Regum, following the Vulgate numbering.</note> we read of the heavy suspension of Eli and his family, because he did not properly discipline his sons. For God said to Eli: "Why have you kicked at my sacrifice and my offerings which I commanded to be offered in the temple? And you have honored your sons more than me, so that you might eat the firstfruits of every sacrifice of my people Israel." Therefore the Lord God of Israel says: "I had said indeed that your house and the house of your father should minister before me forever." But now the Lord says: "Far be it from me! But whoever glorifies me, I will glorify him; but those who despise me shall be held in low esteem. Behold, the days are coming when I will cut off your arm and the arm of your father’s house." And further down: "This shall be a sign to you that shall come upon your two sons, Hophni and Phinehas: in one day they shall both die, and I will raise up for myself a faithful priest, who shall do according to my heart and my soul."

<summary>Jan Hus defines ecclesiastical suspension and interdict, arguing that such penalties are only valid if they align with God's prior judgment. He uses the biblical examples of Eli and the man born blind to show that the wicked often excommunicate the righteous, while God suspends the corrupt from their spiritual offices.</summary>
<keywords>excommunication, suspension, interdict, Jan Hus, De Ecclesia, justice, grace, priesthood, Eli, Hophni, Phinehas, legal authority</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s discussion of ecclesiastical penalties. In the previous page, he defined "holy excommunication" as being excluded from the fellowship of the wicked. Here, he moves to the concept of "suspension," specifically how God Himself "suspends" corrupt rulers and clergy from their rightful authority, regardless of human legal standing.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>
<header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>
<meta>catchword: suspended</meta>

<margin>A king is suspended by God</margin>
Likewise, regarding the suspension of a king, we read in 1 Samuel 15 <note>The Latin text cites "1 Kings 15" following the Vulgate numbering; in modern English Bibles, this is 1 Samuel.</note> concerning Saul, who, contrary to the command of God, spared the enemies of his God: "Because you have rejected the word of the Lord, the Lord has rejected you from being king." <note>original: "pro eo q̃ abiecisti sermonem domini, abiecit te dominus ne sis rex."</note>

<margin>Multiple types of suspension.</margin>
And it is clear how suspension varies: for some suspension is from office, some from <term>benefice</term> <note>A church office that provides an income or living for the holder.</note>, or from some good, as when a sinner is justly suspended because of a crime. Furthermore, one type of suspension is *de facto* (in practice), and another is *de iure* (by law), along with other subdivisions. However, as has been said, God must primarily originate and regulate suspension *de iure*. Regarding suspension *de facto*, God sometimes causes it through good ministers, and sometimes through evil ministers; but by Himself, God suspends every criminal <term>Prelate</term> <note>A high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop or abbot.</note> from his office or ministry while he is actually in a state of crime. This is because by the very fact that he falls into mortal sin, he sins in whatever he does; consequently, he is forbidden by God from acting in this way, and as a result, he is suspended by God from that office. Whence in the forty-ninth Psalm <note>Psalm 50 in modern numbering.</note> the prophet says: "But to the sinner God said: 'Why do you recite my justices, and take my covenant in your mouth? For you have hated discipline and have cast my words behind you. If you saw a thief, you ran with him, and you placed your portion with adulterers.'" <note>original: "Peccatori aūtē dixit deus, quare tu enarras iusticias meas, & assumis testamentum meum per os tuum? Tu uero odisti disciplinā & proiecisti sermones meos retrorsum, Si uidebas furem currebas cum eo, & cum adulteris portionem tuam ponebas."</note>

<margin>God suspends sinners because of these sins.</margin>
"Your mouth has abounded in malice, and your tongue has framed deceits; sitting, you spoke against your brother, and you laid a scandal against your mother’s son." <note>original: "Os tuum abundauit malitia, & lingua tua concinnabit dolos, sedens aduersus fratrem tuum loquebaris, & aduersus filium matris tuæ ponebas scandalū."</note> Here God enumerates the sins for which He suspends a sinner from the narration of His covenant, which is the law of truth. The first sin is disobedience to God; the second is the rejection of His words; the third is theft; the fourth is adultery; the fifth is malice of the mouth, which is divided into lying, blasphemy, false testimony, perjury, deceit, detraction, vain talk, cursing, foul language, and so on. The sixth sin is on account of the scandal of Christ. From this it is gathered how rare are the judges, preachers, and others who narrate God's covenant to the people, who are not 

<column-break/>

<page-num>215</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXIII</header>
<meta>catchword: truth</meta>

suspended by God from the narration of that very covenant.
Therefore, let the faithful person note in the aforementioned suspension from the office of narrating God's covenant, and from the three-fold example mentioned above, whether our Prelates and Clerics are suspended by the Lord. First, if they repel from themselves the knowledge of scripture and the work of evangelization, for then they shall be suspended by the Lord, as is read in a lesser case in Hosea, chapter four. For our Prelates have, in both respects, greater material and form for preaching, and more reasons why they should preach, than the priests in the Old Law, and yet they exercise that office less. Therefore, since a greater reason exists, and the same Lord is present—who cannot ultimately withhold vengeance for a greater crime, as His justice demands—it is clear that our Prelates, if there be any such, are more gravely suspended. They are all the more gravely suspended because they ought to stand firm for the law of Christ more fervently in the times of the <term>Antichrist</term>.

<margin>How much a Prelate is obligated to his subjects.</margin>
Regarding the second suspension, performed by the Lord upon Eli, let the faithful note whether our Prelates either do not punish, or punish more culpably, their spiritual sons than Eli’s natural sons were punished by him. And in order to discern the magnitude of that fault, the faithful ought to note these two things. First, that a Prelate is more obligated to a spiritual son than by the reason by which he is obligated to his natural son. And second, that a punishment for the sake of extorted money—by which sin is "sold"—is more detestable than a punishment that is withheld out of natural love to avenge an injury to God, as Eli seems to have spared his sons.
Regarding the third suspension, occurring both in prelates and in kings and other secular princes, let the faithful note that they themselves defer more to the public enemies of the Lord for the sake of private gain than Saul deferred to Agag <note>Hus uses "Amalech" (Amalek) to refer to the king of the Amalekites, Agag.</note> because of a desire for their temporal goods. If this is so, there is no doubt that the same God, who cannot bypass the same justice, will punish the more serious offenders. Hence, there is an evident sign concerning the gravity of the penalty.

<summary>Hus argues that God is the ultimate source of legal authority and can "suspend" kings and clergy from their offices for disobedience or moral corruption. He uses the biblical examples of Saul and Eli to demonstrate that many contemporary church leaders are technically suspended by God, even if they still hold their titles.</summary>
<keywords>suspension, King Saul, Eli the Priest, clerical corruption, moral authority, divine justice, Antichrist, preaching, spiritual vs natural kinship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that God is the ultimate source of legal standing. <header>I. H. ON THE CHURCH</header>

<margin>Why God does not always punish with this penalty [in this life].</margin>
Why do we not see that God delays punishment until after death, and does not punish them otherwise in this life, but permits them to wander in worldly prosperities like uncorrected reprobates? But alas, men do not notice this triple suspension <note>Likely referring to suspension from office, from financial benefit, and from the state of grace.</note>, and especially the high-ranking of this age; they truly ought to reflect more often on how they will be suspended perpetually from both their office and their <term>benefice</term> <note>A church office that provides an income.</note>. Hence, regarding that word in Numbers 25: "The Lord, being angry, said to Moses: Take all the leaders of the people, and hang them against the sun on gallows." Origen, in his 20th homily, speaks thus: "The Lord said to Moses that he should take all the leaders and display them to the Lord against the sun. The people sin, and the leaders are displayed against the sun—that is, they are brought forth to be examined,
<margin>Superiors shall give an account for their subordinates.</margin>
so that they may be rebuked by the light. You see what the condition of the leaders of the people is: they are not only rebuked for their own particular crimes, but are also forced to give an account for the sins of the people, lest perhaps the fault be theirs that the people sinned, lest perhaps they did not teach, or perhaps they did not warn, or were not diligent in rebuking those who gave the beginning of the fault, so that the contagion would not spread to many. For it is the duty of leaders and teachers to do all these things; for if the people sin while the leaders are not doing these things or showing concern for the common people, the leaders themselves are displayed and brought to judgment. Moses—that is, the law of God—rebukes them as negligent and idle, and the wrath of God is turned upon them and ceases from the people." If men thought on these things, they would never desire or seek after leadership over the people. For it is enough for me that I am rebuked for my own sins and crimes; it is enough for me to give an account for myself and my own sins. Why is it necessary for me to also be displayed before the sun for the sins of the people—before whom nothing can be hidden, obscured, or concealed? And he adds: "Therefore the leaders are displayed, and if the fault is in them, the anger of God turns away from the people." Thus says Origen, showing how leaders are
<meta>catchword: severely</meta>

severely rebuked for the sin of <term>lust</term> <note>original: "luxuria"</note> which the people committed. Woe, therefore, to modern spiritual and secular leaders who practice lust themselves, giving an evil example to their subordinates, and do not correct their subordinates—or if they do correct them, they do it for the sake of greed. Such men are, without a doubt, suspended from office by the Lord. For it is written from Papal law in the third book of the Decretals, concerning the "Life and Honesty of Clerics," in the chapter *Fraternitati tuae*: "We command that you take care to studiously warn the clerics of our jurisdiction who, being in the <term>subdiaconate</term> <note>The lowest of the major orders in the medieval church.</note> or above, have kept concubines, so that they might remove them from themselves, never to admit them again. If, however, they choose to ignore this, you shall suspend them from their ecclesiastical benefices until they make worthy satisfaction; and if, while suspended, they presume to keep them, you shall see to it that they are perpetually removed from those same benefices." Since the law is not about deeds but about those who would practice it, we know that under the same title the Pope says: "As for <term>prelates</term> <note>High-ranking church officials like bishops.</note> who presume to keep such men in their iniquities, especially under the pretext of money or some other temporal advantage, we will that they be subject to the same

This suspension, according to the Archdeacon <note>A common title for the famous canon lawyer Guido de Baysio.</note>, ought to be perpetual, equivalent to <term>deposition</term> <note>Formal removal from the clerical state.</note>, because of the difficulty of gathering Bishops together to depose such a Bishop who has thus <term>simoniacally</term> <note>The sin of selling sacred things or church offices for money.</note> sold, or is selling, justice. And because the Metropolitan <note>A high-ranking Archbishop.</note>, as the Roman Pontiff is, could be sluggish toward his own Cardinals in the execution of this holy institution, the laws have thirdly ordained a remedy for this confusion: namely, that a mass should not be heard from a priest by anyone who knows notoriously that the priest is thus committing fornication, nor
<margin>Consider these things, O priests who keep concubines.</margin>
should they administer the goods of the church to him to foster his crime. And since the same or even greater judgment applies to any greater spiritual fornication, it seems that an inferior ought to be suspended by a stricter Prelate for any more serious spiritual sin. And since it is certain that "crucifying" pride in a Prelate, negligence in preaching the gospel, and the greed of Iscariot are more serious sins than carnal fornication, it is manifest that the supreme prelate, Jesus Christ—to whom these more serious sins are perfectly known—does not turn away from suspension because of any excuse worthy of blame. From these points, when the status of the church is questioned, it is gathered that from the Pope down to the lowest priest, rarely is anyone excluded for a given time from suspension, except for the one who irreproachably follows the Lord Jesus Christ. For it has already been said how fornicators are suspended. Likewise <term>Simoniacs</term> <note>Those who buy or sell church offices or spiritual things; named after Simon Magus.</note>, in Canon Law <note>original: "1. q. j." referring to the Decretum Gratiani.</note> "Whosoever" through Gregory, and the chapter "They are found" through Ambrose. Likewise, Clerics lingering over shameful profits and gains are suspended, in distinction 88, "Consequently." And since all such persons, according to the law of Christ, minister unworthily to the church, it is clear that irregularities and profanations are multiplied and folded within the Clergy of the church.

<margin>The soul is the most noble temple.</margin>
Regarding profanation, I spoke in the treatise *Against the Hidden Adversary*, showing how any wicked priest profanes—that is, violates, execrates, and contaminates—the spiritual temple of God, which without doubt, according to the saints, is more worthy in its nature than all

material temples, which shall not endure after the day of judgment. And woe to me if I remain silent, not attacking greed or the manifest luxury of the Clergy. For in distinction 84 it says: "An error which is not resisted is approved, and truth, when it is least defended, is oppressed." Indeed, to neglect to rebuke the wicked when you are able is nothing else than to foster them. Nor is one free from the suspicion of a hidden alliance who ceases to oppose a manifest crime. In the same place: "For what does it profit him not to be polluted by his own error, who provides consent to those who err?" In the same place: "He seems to consent to the one erring who does not run to cut back those things which ought to be corrected." Whence Blessed Gregory, in chapter 15 of his *Pastoral Care*, bringing forward that passage from the prophet in Lamentations 2: "Thy prophets have seen false and foolish things for thee, and they did not lay open thy iniquity, to provoke thee to repentance," speaks thus: "Prophets in sacred speech are sometimes called teachers, who, while they judge present things to be fleeting, manifest those things which are to come. Whom the divine word rebukes for seeing false things, because while they fear to correct faults, they uselessly flatter the delinquents by promising security, and they by no means open the iniquity of the sinners. Because they are silent from the voice of rebuke, for the word of correction is the key of opening. Because by rebuking, it uncovers the fault, which often even the one who committed it does not know." These are his words. And it is held in distinction 43: "Let the ruler be." And would that our teachers would take notice of these things; then they would not stroke the life of the Prelates, nor would they be sluggish in opening their iniquity to them, so that they might provoke them to repentance. They themselves have seen in how many ways one person consents to the crime of another. For he consents who cooperates, defends, gives counsel, and authorizes, or who does not threaten or reprehend. Now concerning the <term>interdict</term> <note>An ecclesiastical penalty that prohibits certain groups or regions from participating in certain rites.</note>, by which because of one man

Moreover, they heap up all those three censures upon "disobedience" in this manner: that whosoever does not obey them according to their will, him they excommunicate and suspend from office; and while to their will

...steadfastly resists, they place an **interdict** <note>An "interdict" was a severe penalty where the Church suspended all public worship and sacraments in a town or country to pressure a ruler or population.</note> among the people, forbidding them from performing the divine office, the exhibition of the Sacraments, and burial—even for men however righteous—so that through that devised aggravation they might obtain their own will. Moreover, it is an evident sign when such Censures proceed from the Antichrist—which they call **"Fulminations"** <note>original: "Fulminationes," literally "thunderbolts." This refers to the formal, public announcement of an ecclesiastical curse or excommunication.</note> in their legal proceedings—when they are struck against those who preach the law of Christ and rebuke the wickedness of the Clergy.

<margin>Disobedience to Prelates is punished more severely than disobedience to God.</margin>
The second sign is that those Censures are multiplied more because of disobedience toward themselves than because of disobedience toward God; and thus, more for their own injury than for the injury of our God. For in this way, the ancient enemy, practiced in malice, proceeds so that he exalts the obedience of the Antichrist above the obedience of Christ. Thus, he usurps excommunication for disobedience toward himself, which Christ instituted for disobedience toward God. He proceeds, moreover, in this manner: First, he defames the disciple of Christ; afterward, he accuses him; then he cites him, excommunicates him, and suspends him. If he cannot deliver him to prison or to death, he then invokes the **secular arm** <note>The "secular arm" refers to the use of civil or military force by the state to carry out the Church's judicial sentences, such as imprisonment or execution.</note>. And not being able to conquer even then, he heaps up an interdict through his malice. He proceeds in this way most principally against those who strip bare the wickedness of the Antichrist, who has usurped the Clergy for himself above all else. Whence, he extends those Censures on behalf of his Clergy: such as for those litigating out of greed for **Benefices** <note>A "benefice" was a church office, like being a priest of a specific parish, that came with a guaranteed income or land.</note>; or when the people have not given the tithe according to his wish; or if a prince of the Clergy has arrested or seized temporal goods; or if any cleric—even a most wicked thief or otherwise criminal—has been captured and held in custody by secular authorities; or if a priest has been wounded to the point of shedding blood; or even when the people have

<margin>Christ did not impose an interdict as his vicars do.</margin>
rightfully withheld obedience from those very Prelates. Christ, however, the Supreme Pontiff, when the Prophet <note>John the Baptist.</note> was detained—than whom no greater has arisen among those born of women—did not impose an interdict, nor even when Herod beheaded him. Indeed, when He Himself was being stripped, struck, and blasphemed by the soldiers, the scribes, the Pharisees, the magistrates, and the high priests, He did not then bring down a curse, but prayed for them, saying in Luke 23: *"Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do."* And that

<column-break/>

<page-num>221</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXIII 221</header>

doctrine he delivered to his members, saying in Matthew 5: *"Love your enemies, and do good to those who hate you, and pray for those who persecute and calumniate you, that you may be children of your Father who is in heaven, who makes his sun to rise upon the good and the bad, and rains upon the just and the unjust."* Whence, following this doctrine in word and deed, the first Vicar of Christ, the Roman Pontiff <note>Saint Peter.</note>, in 1 Peter 2, teaches the faithful, saying: *"Unto this you are called, because Christ suffered for us, leaving you an example, that we should follow his footsteps; who did no sin, nor was deceit found in his mouth; who, when he was cursed, did not curse back; and when he suffered, he did not threaten."* And Paul, traveling the same path, says in Romans 12: *"Bless those who persecute you; bless, and do not curse."* Other saints also followed this doctrine, who did not "fulminate" excommunication or suspension during their persecution, nor did they impose an interdict; rather, when a heavier persecution came, they insisted all the more on the divine ministries.

But after the millennium—when Satan was loosed and the Clergy were fattened

<margin>When the interdict took its origin.</margin>
with the filth of the world, and elevated in pleasure, pride, and impatience—the Interdict took its origin. For Pope Hadrian <note>Hadrian IV, the only English Pope (r. 1154–1159).</note>, who began in the year of our Lord 1154, placed all of Rome under interdict because of the wounding of one Cardinal. Oh, how "patient" was this Pope! Yet not like Christ, Peter, Paul, or the apostle Andrew. Afterward, Alexander III, in the year of our Lord 1159, interdicted the kingdom of England (as found in *Of Marriages*, chapter 2, *It is not for you*). Pope Celestine III, who began in the year 1191 <note>The OCR reads "M. lxxxij" (1082), but the text refers to Celestine III, whose reign began in 1191. This is likely a printer's error in the source.</note>, says something about the interdict in the chapter *He sought*, regarding *Superiority and Obedience*. Afterward, Innocent III, who began in the year of our Lord 1199, published the interdict through many **decretals** <note>Letters or decrees by a Pope that formulated church law.</note>, as in the chapter *In the Lateran Council*, regarding *Prebends*, Book 3 of the Decretals. Later, Boniface VIII, Innocent IV, and Clement V, in the *Sext* and the *Clementines* <note>These are standard collections of Canon Law used in the Middle Ages.</note>, expanded interdicts of this kind. And so, with the Clergy continually burning with greed, secular pomp, and impatience...

He provides a brief history of how popes like Hadrian IV and Innocent III introduced these legal penalties after the "loosing of Satan" (the corruption of the church).</summary>
