<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument against the "Interdict"—a form of collective punishment where the Church would stop all religious services in an entire region. Hus is arguing that this practice is unjust because it punishes innocent people and children for the sins of a single person (often a political ruler), which contradicts both the Bible and the writings of Saint Augustine.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH
### CHAPTER XXIII.

<margin>There is no reason why a general interdict should be imposed.</margin>
...justice, such interdicts are multiplied.
Therefore, I always seek the foundation or the reason for a general interdict, by which the righteous are deprived of the Sacraments—such as Communion, confession, and the rest—without any demerit of their own, and sometimes even infants are deprived of baptism. Similarly, I ask why the ministry of God is diminished among righteous men through an interdict because of one man’s position. It would be quite a marvel if service were taken away from an earthly king by all his good servants, because of just one of his servants who was acting against him. And even more so if, because of one who was a good and faithful servant of the king, a vassal wishing to bend that man to his own will should forbid all the king's servants from performing their ministry to the king himself. How, therefore, does a Pope or Bishop so thoughtlessly, without scripture or revelation, so easily forbid ministry to Christ the King? For when a general interdict is placed on a city 
<margin>Evil, not good, results from the interdict.</margin>
or a diocese, sin is not diminished, but increased. For the righteous are denied burial, contrary to the scripture in Ecclesiasticus 7: "Do not withhold grace from the dead." <note>original: "Ecclesiast. vij. Mortuo nō p̄hibe as gratiam." This refers to the duty of providing a proper burial as an act of mercy.</note> For who doubts that burying the righteous dead is a work of mercy? For in Tobit 12, the angel Raphael spoke to Tobit in this way: "When you prayed with tears and buried the dead, and left your dinner, and hid the dead in your house, and buried them by night, I offered your prayer to the Lord." <note>original: "Thobiæ. xij." Hus uses this biblical example to show that burying the dead is a holy act that the Church should not forbid.</note> Who also doubts that hearing confession, giving counsel for salvation, and preaching the word of God are works of mercy? Likewise, offering the Sacrament of the Eucharist to a devoted people, and baptizing. What reason is there, then, to withdraw these things from the people of God without their having earned such a punishment?

Wherefore Saint Augustine writes to Bishop Auxilius <note>The text says "Maximus," but this refers to Augustine’s famous Letter 250 to Bishop Auxilius concerning a man named Classicianus.</note>, as recorded in the Decrees <note>original: ".xxiij. q. iij." A reference to Canon Law (Gratian's Decretum), Case 23, Question 3.</note>: "If you have," he says, "a settled opinion on this matter based on certain reasons or the testimonies of the scriptures, may you deign to teach us how a son may be rightly anathematized <note>Anathematized: Formally cursed or cut off from the Church.</note> for the sin of his father, or a wife for that of her husband, or a servant for that of his master, or anyone in a household not yet born, if they are born at a time when the whole house is bound by an anathema, so that the help of the washing of rebirth <note>original: "lauacrū regenerationis," a common theological term for Baptism.</note> cannot come to them in the danger of death? For this was a physical punishment by which we read that certain 

<column-break/>

despisers of God perished among the holy people along with all their kin who were participants in their same impiety. At that time, indeed, mortal bodies were destroyed for the sake of terrifying the living, though they were destined to die eventually anyway. But spiritual punishment, regarding which it is written, 'Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven,' binds souls, regarding which it is said: 'As the soul of the father, so the soul of the son is mine. The soul that sins, it shall die.' <note>original: "Anima quæ peccauerit ipsa morietur." A quote from Ezekiel 18:4, emphasizing individual responsibility rather than collective guilt.</note> You have perhaps heard of some priests of great name who anathematized someone's entire house for the sin of one person; but perhaps if they were questioned, they would be found 
<margin>O Augustine, they find no one like you!</margin>
unable to give me a proper reason. But as for me, if anyone asks me whether this is done rightly, I find nothing to answer him. I have never dared to do these things regarding the crimes of certain individuals committed together against the Church, unless I was most gravely warned. But if the Lord has revealed to you how this may be justly done, I by no means look down upon your youthful age and your newness in the ecclesiastical burden. For though I am an old man and a bishop of many years, I am ready to be taught by a youth and a colleague who has not yet been in office a single year: how I can give a just reason to God or to men, if I punish innocent souls with a spiritual penalty for someone else’s crime—a crime they did not inherit from Adam, in whom all have sinned. For even if the son of Classicianus contracted from his parent the guilt of the first man, which must be expiated by the sacred font of baptism, yet who doubts that whatever sin his father committed after begetting him—in which the son himself was not a participant—does not belong to him? What shall I say of the wife? What of so many souls of the whole family? Wherefore, if one soul should perish by departing from the body without baptism because of this severity by which that entire house was anathematized, the death of innumerable bodies (even if innocent men were violently dragged from the Church and killed) cannot be compared to this loss. If, therefore, you can give a reason for this matter, please write back to us so that 
<meta>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with the catchword "and we" (& nos).</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the "Interdict" is an unjust collective punishment that denies essential sacraments like Baptism and Burial to innocent people. He cites Saint Augustine to prove that spiritual punishment should only apply to the individual who commits a sin, not their entire family or community.</summary>
<keywords>Interdict, Sacraments, Baptism, Saint Augustine, Collective Punishment, Canon Law, Ezekiel 18:4, Works of Mercy, Clerical Authority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument against the "Interdict" and collective excommunication. He is drawing on the authority of Saint Augustine and the legal scholar Gratian to prove that punishing many for the sin of one is a violation of divine and canon law.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

...and we, even if you cannot do what is required of you because of a reckless commotion of the mind. Whence, if you are questioned, you are not able to return a right response. <note>Hus is quoting Saint Augustine's Letter 250 to a young bishop named Auxilius, advising him against hot-headed spiritual punishments.</note> These are the words of Augustine. From these, Gratian <note>Gratian was a 12th-century jurist who founded the study of Canon Law; Hus is citing his major work, the *Decretum*.</note> concludes thus: as is demonstrated by the most evident authority, it is unlawful for anyone to be excommunicated for the sin of another. Nor are those supported by any reason who mark someone with an unlawful excommunication; it does not harm the one so noted, but rather the one performing the excommunication.

<margin>Many should not be excommunicated because of one.</margin>
Where it must be noted, regarding the words of Augustine, the gloss <note>A "gloss" is a standard legal commentary written in the margins of law books.</note> of the decree says in the chapter "Summario" that this Bishop had excommunicated the entire household of Classianus for the sin of Classianus himself. This Bishop thought he could rightly do so, both because someone is sometimes punished with physical penalty for the sin of another, and because certain priests of great name had excommunicated some for another's sin. In the first part of the chapter, Augustine asks him for the cause and reason for his opinion. In what follows, he teaches that neither of the aforementioned reasons is sufficient to support his sentence. Third, he descends to the specific facts and proves the sentence passed against the household of Classianus was unjust. Finally, he advises the Bishop that if he does not wish to provide a reason for his opinion, he should leave his error behind and imitate the truth. This is what the gloss says. 

Would that those who excommunicate and those who place a general interdict upon a church or city because of one man would weigh this saying of Blessed Augustine along with that gloss! Why do they afflict a community with excommunication and interdict when the community is not at fault? They entirely deprive good and devoted priests of their divine ministry, and God’s devoted people of the sacraments. They deprive God Himself of the honor then shown to Him, and the dead of burial, and—most often—infants of baptism, who depart without it and are damned, according to the opinion of Augustine. <note>In the medieval view, which Hus cites here, infants dying without baptism were thought to be excluded from heaven.</note> Whence the gloss of the decree says regarding that word: "If one soul, because of this severity by which this whole house was anathematized, should perish by departing from the body without baptism, the death of innumerable bodies—even if innocent men were violently dragged from the church—cannot be compared to this loss." The gloss states the argument: It is a greater sin if one soul perishes through the sin of unfaithfulness than <column-break/> if the bodies of innumerable martyrs are killed for God; and this seems to be meant literally. If the innocent are dragged from the church, and if the bodies of martyrs perish, it is not a "loss" in the same spiritual sense, but is said to be like common homicide. Also the gloss says: He sins more gravely whose fault causes the soul of a child to depart this life without baptism, than if he had killed many innocent people by dragging them from the church.

# CHAPTER XXIII.

<margin>Note the malice of the clergy.</margin>
But alas, the clergy, blinded by malice, does not notice all these things. For the sake of non-payment of a tiny amount of money—sometimes even by a poor man who is unable to pay—they deprive the people of the sacraments of the church through an interdict, as has been said. Christ did not teach this; He especially taught the clergy not to contend in judgment, saying in *Luke 6*: "To him who strikes you on one cheek, offer the other also; and from him who takes away your cloak, do not withhold your tunic either. Give to everyone who asks of you, and from him who takes away what is yours, do not demand it back." <note>original: "Lucæ. vj. Qui te pcutit in unā maxillā, prębe ei alterā..."</note> But the impatient clergy, hearing that most wholesome doctrine of Christ, will deride it. It is no wonder, for the Savior says afterward in *Matthew 7*: "Everyone who hears these words of mine and does not do them will be like a foolish man who built his house on the sand." Who indeed is a more foolish man than a clergyman who, founding himself on the dung of the world, holds the life and doctrine of Christ in derision? For the clergy is now so far ruined that they hate those who frequently preach and name the Lord Christ; and whenever someone alleges Christ on their own behalf, they immediately say with a wide and rancorous mouth, "Are you Christ?" and in the manner of the Pharisees, they defile and excommunicate those who confess Christ.

<margin>The unjust excommunication of the author.</margin>
Wherefore, because I preached Christ and His Gospel, and exposed the Antichrist—wishing for the clergy to live according to the law of Christ—the Prelates first arranged with Lord Zbyněk, the Archbishop of Prague, <note>Hus uses "Suicone" referring to Zbyněk Zajíc of Hasenburg (1376–1411).</note> for a Bull from Pope Alexander, <note>Pope Alexander V, whose bull in 1409 sought to suppress preaching in private chapels like Hus's Bethlehem Chapel.</note> so that the word of God should not be preached to the people in chapels. I appealed against this Bull, and was never able to obtain a hearing. Whence, having been cited, I did not appear for rational causes. Because of this, they orchestrated an excommunication through Michael de Causis <note>Michael de Causis was a priest and lawyer who became one of Hus's most bitter prosecutors at the Council of Constance.</note> after an agreement had already been made, and now finally they have procured...

<summary>Jan Hus argues that general interdicts are spiritually dangerous and illegal, particularly because they risk the salvation of infants. He concludes by describing his own legal persecution and excommunication by the Prague authorities and the Pope for his commitment to preaching the Gospel.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Hus, excommunication, interdict, Saint Augustine, Gratian, infant baptism, Bethlehem Chapel, Archbishop Zbyněk, Michael de Causis, clerical corruption</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues Hus’s critique of the "Interdict"—a decree that suspended all public worship and sacraments in any location where he was present. He is specifically challenging the "doctors" (theological authorities) who defend the Roman Curia's legal practices.</meta>

# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

...interdict, by which they burden the people of Christ without fault. 

I would wish, therefore, that the doctors—who say that in these legal processes "pure good" is not prohibited, nor is "pure evil" commanded, but only "middle" things—would prove these claims. First, they should prove that an interdict so general that it deprives the innocent of the sacraments and burial, hinders divine service, and brings about no good except scandals, detractions, and hatreds, is indeed a "middle ground" between pure good and pure evil. And how will the doctors be able to show that it is lawful to excommunicate the people of God from the sacraments, from burial, and from divine services—a people for whom, as has been said, the most powerful doctor of the Church, Saint Augustine, rebuked Bishop Auxilius? <note>Referring to the letter cited on the previous page where Augustine argues against punishing a whole group for one person's sin.</note> The proof of these doctors, combined from hypocritical cloaking and peasant craftiness, would not satisfy Augustine as a rational argument when the doctors say:

<margin>The Style of the Roman Curia.</margin>
"But it is according to the style of the Church and the custom observed by the Roman Curia." <note>The "Curia" is the administrative apparatus of the Pope.</note> Behold the hypocritical cloaking used before the fathers of our fathers. Behold the peasant craftiness: "let only middle things be perceived there." O, Doctors! To which church does that "style" belong? Is it perchance the Apostolic Church? Tell me, which Apostle’s style is that, or which saint’s after the Apostles? Is it perhaps the style of Christ, that head of the Holy Church, in whose style every truth useful to the Church is contained? But I ask: where is this saying contained: "In every place, city, town, village, or borough, exempt or not exempt, to which the same Master John Hus shall turn, and as long as he shall be there and make his stay, and for three natural days continuously following his departure from there—by these writings, we subject [the place] to such an ecclesiastical interdict, and we will that divine services cease in them." <note>Hus is quoting the specific legal language used in the papal decree against him.</note> Perhaps that "style" is founded there: *It is necessary to pray always and not lose heart* (Luke 18). Or there: *Praise God, all you nations.* Or there: *In every place, praise the Lord.* But what will the authors of this "style" say if it should happen that John Hus comes to the holy city of Jerusalem, where the Cherubim and Seraphim do not cease to cry out daily with one voice, saying: "Holy, Holy, Holy, Lord God of Sabaoth"? Will they, on account

<column-break/>

<page-num>227</page-num>
### CHAPTER XXIII
Gg ij
<meta>catchword: Blessed</meta>

of this lightning-bolt decree, cease from divine things there? Will Christ, the just advocate, not intercede with God the Father for his faithful members, or will the angelic choir not cry out, "Holy, Holy, Holy, Lord God of Sabaoth"? Will that voice cease, of which John says in Apocalypse 5: *I heard the voice of many angels around the throne, and the living creatures and the elders, and the number of them was thousands of thousands, saying with a loud voice: Worthy is the Lamb who was slain to receive power, and divinity, and wisdom, and strength, and honor, and glory, and blessing, and every creature which is in heaven, and on the earth, and under the earth, and in the sea, and those that are in it.*?

<margin>Every rational creature is subject to the Roman Pontiff.</margin>
The doctors do not say that this is irrelevant, for all rational creatures, according to the "style" of the Roman Curia, are subject to the mandate of that same Curia. For "every human creature is subject to the Roman Pontiff," as the "style" of the same says in the *Extravagant* of Boniface VIII: <note>Referring to the famous Papal Bull "Unam Sanctam" (1302) by Pope Boniface VIII, which claimed supreme authority over all people.</note> "Furthermore, we declare, say, and define that it is absolutely necessary for salvation for every human creature to be subject to the Roman Pontiff." Similarly, the angelic creature is subject to the Roman Pontiff himself, as the "style" of the Curia says in a Bull of Pope Clement: <note>Hus is likely referencing a controversial bull attributed to Pope Clement VI regarding the power to command angels to carry souls to heaven.</note> "We command the angels of Paradise that they introduce his soul, being entirely absolved from purgatory, into the glory of paradise." Since, therefore, according to that "style" of the Curia, every rational creature, both angelic and human, is subject to the Roman Pontiff in mandate, and the "style" in the legal process of the same Curia says that to whatever place, exempt or not exempt, John Hus shall turn, and as long as he shall be there, we subject it to ecclesiastical interdict—it follows that if (which is maximally possible by the absolute power of God) 

<margin>The Pope places an interdict in heaven.</margin>
John Hus should turn by death to the heavenly Jerusalem, that place itself ought to be subjected to an Ecclesiastical interdict. But blessed be Almighty God, who commanded that the angels with all the saints in that heavenly Jerusalem should not be subject to an interdict of this kind.

<summary>Jan Hus critiques the Roman Curia's practice of placing entire cities under interdict because of his presence, arguing that such legal "styles" contradict Scripture and logically imply the absurdity of the Pope having power to silence the angels in Heaven.</summary>
<keywords>Interdict, Roman Curia, Papal Authority, Boniface VIII, Unam Sanctam, Clement VI, Heavenly Jerusalem, Canon Law, Excommunication</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s critique of the "doctors" of the Church who prioritize the legal traditions and "style" of the Roman administrative office (the Curia) over the spiritual laws of Christ. He is arguing that the Church's life does not depend on a human Pope, but on Christ.</meta>

## II. H. ON THE CHURCH

Blessed also be Christ, the Highest Roman Pontiff, who gave grace to his faithful, so that even when no Roman Pontiff exists for a given time, they are able to reach the heavenly fatherland under the leadership of Christ the Lord.

<margin>Agnes, the female Pope.</margin>
For who would say that during the time when Agnes, a woman, was the apparent Pope for two years and five months, no human being would be saved? <note>Hus refers to the medieval legend of "Pope Joan" (here called Agnes), who was said to have reigned in the 9th century. He uses this as a logical argument: if the papacy was "invalid" or "empty" then, and people were still saved, the papacy is not strictly necessary for salvation.</note> Similarly, after the death of a Pope and in the intervening time between a Pope's death and the election of a successor, no person dying then would be saved. Blessed also be God Almighty, who ordains that His **Militant Church** <term>Militant Church: The community of Christians currently living on Earth, struggling against sin.</term> should live in such a way that when a Pope dies, she is not thereby decapitated or dead. For her life depends not on the Pope, but on Christ the Head; and when a Pope is rendered foolish or made a heretic, the Militant Church remains the faithful bride of the Lord Jesus Christ.

<margin>Three Popes at one time.</margin>
Blessed also be the Lord, who, being the one Head of the Church, preserves her in unity so effectively that even when there is a so-called "three-fold head" among the Popes, she remains the unique bride of the Lord Jesus Christ. <note>This refers to the Western Schism (1378–1417), where three different men claimed to be the rightful Pope simultaneously.</note> For now, Balthasar (called John XXIII) is in Rome; Angelo Corraro (called Gregory XII) is in Rimini; and Pedro de Luna (called Benedict XIII) is in Aragon. Why does one of them—called the "Most Holy Father"—not compel the others and their followers by the "plenitude of power" to submit to his jurisdiction, since the "style of the Roman Curia" says that he has the **plenitude of power** over every person on earth? Therefore, it is quite a weak foundation for proof to hold that anything should be assumed unbreakably simply by the "style of the Roman Curia." For in the *Sixth Book of Decretals* regarding constitutions, it is given as a rule that if two people have letters from the Pope regarding the same provision on the same day, the one to whom the Pope granted a **Canonicate** <term>Canonicate: A legal position or office within a cathedral or collegiate church.</term> is to be preferred over the one to whom he did not, provided the executor was ignorant of who petitioned first. But if they are equal in grace according to the form of the "Papal style," the one who presented his claim first will be stronger in his right to the **Prebend** <term>Prebend: The portion of the church's revenues granted as a salary to a canon.</term>. And thirdly, if they are equal in these three things, then the Canons to whom the appointment belongs, or the majority of them, are bound to elect...

<meta>catchword: the other</meta>

***

# CHAPTER XXIII

...the other, who lacks the "fruit of grace," unless it appears expressly from the tenor of the Papal letters that the Pope wished to provide for both of them. But this three-part "Style of the Curia" seems to be a principle fundamentally against Christ. For supposing—as happens in the majority of cases—that the one preferred in the canonicate, or in the timing of the presentation, or in the showing of grace, or thirdly in the election of the Chapter, is significantly more unworthy before God than the other, then by this "maxim of style," judgment must be given against the judgment of Christ. From which it follows that this maxim of style...

<margin>Not everything the Roman Style does is immediately right.</margin>
...is against conscience, and consequently against Christ. What kind of proof is it, then, to say: "The accustomed and observed style of the Roman Curia concedes or asserts this, therefore it is in harmony with the law of Christ and must be accepted by Catholics"? That is the "peasant craftiness" which the doctors put forward: "it was so believed or observed before the time of our fathers." That argument would lead to the conclusion that the doctors themselves should believe and observe the erroneous customs of the pagans and Jews—indeed, that they ought to worship Baal, just as the Bohemians did when they were pagans. They have against them that word from Ezekiel 20: *“Walk not in the statutes of your fathers, neither observe their judgments.”* <note>original: "in praeceptis patrum vestrorum nolite incedere nec iudicia eorum custodiatis"</note> What a shameless proof from the doctors! "Before the fathers of our fathers this was believed, observed, or held; therefore it ought to be believed, observed, and held by us." For foolish men make such tasteless arguments to find "excuses for their sins" (Psalm 141:4). He who says in Psalm 105 is not of their number: *“We have sinned with our fathers, we have acted unjustly, we have committed iniquity. Our fathers in Egypt did not understand your wonders; they were not mindful of the multitude of your mercies.”* <note>original: "Peccavimus domine cum patribus nostris, iniuste egimus, iniquitatem fecimus. Patres nostri in aegypto non intellexerunt mirabilia tua, non fuerunt memores multitudinis misericordiae tuae."</note> And if by chance the doctors say that in their statement they mean by "fathers of fathers" the holy Prophets or Apostles...

<margin>Fathers of fathers</margin>
...or later saints, then they should have expressly cited their writings, against which it would be unlawful for anyone to rebel. Then their insistence would cease—the insistence that breaks logic or reason when they say that in legal processes, according to the accustomed and observed "style of the Church and Roman Curia" before the fathers of our fathers, these things are commanded...

<meta>catchword: there so</meta>

<summary>Jan Hus argues that the Church's survival does not depend on the Papacy, citing the legend of Pope Joan and the current Western Schism with three rival Popes. He critiques the "Style of the Roman Curia," arguing that legal traditions and precedents are invalid if they contradict the laws and judgments of Christ.</summary>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan Hus’s argument that the presence of a "bad person" does not invalidate the sacraments or the Church’s divine work. He is specifically attacking the "interdict"—a legal tool used by the medieval Church to stop all religious services in a city as a form of punishment—arguing that neither Christ nor the Apostles ever practiced such a thing.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>
# JAN HUS ON THE CHURCH

<margin>It is perverse that divine things should cease at the presence of a bad man.</margin>
...there is only a middle ground between pure goods and pure evils. For which of the holy fathers, prophets, Apostles, or other saints ever commanded that wherever even the worst man might turn, divine services must cease there? For you see that Christ, even with the most disobedient Judas and his own betrayer present, did not cease from divine things at his Great Supper. On the contrary, while Judas was present, he performed the divine rites and gave him his most holy and divine body to eat. He also firmly warned the disciples to watch and pray with him, so that they might not enter into temptation because of the violent invasion of the scribes, Pharisees, and <margin>Neither Christ nor the saints found benefit in an interdict.</margin> high priests. Nor did the most pious High Priest <note>Referring to Christ as the "High Priest."</note> cease from his most divine prayer while he was being blasphemed and crucified; rather, he prayed for his crucifiers, saying, "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do" (Luke 23). When he prayed with a cry and tears, he was heard because of his reverence (Hebrews 5). Thus, his true and manifest Vicars, the Apostles and other saints, imitated him in this. First among them was Stephen, who said in the sixth chapter of Acts: "Lord Jesus, do not hold this sin against them, for they know not what they do."

And it is a wonder why an interdict <term>Interdict: A decree by which the Church prohibited public worship and the administration of sacraments in a specific territory to pressure its leaders.</term> is not issued because of the Jews who deny that Christ is God and thus reject his entire law, or because of manifest Simoniacs <term>Simoniacs: People who buy or sell church offices or spiritual authority, named after Simon Magus from the New Testament.</term>, who are the chief heretics—cursed, excommunicated, suspended, and interdicted by the writings of the holy Apostles and other saints, and estranged from the holy priesthood. The reason is that those <margin>The simoniacal trade of the clergy.</margin> Simoniacs buy and sell excommunications, suspensions, and interdicts. They nourish and defend their Simony with these things as if they were the most powerful weapons. Nor is proof necessary, since this simoniacal trade is obvious to the eye, even to the peasants who are burdened, vexed, oppressed, and plundered by these very Simoniacs. For the Simoniacal and Gehazi-like <note>original: "Giezitica." This refers to Gehazi, the servant of Elisha who was struck with leprosy for taking money for a miracle (2 Kings 5). Hus uses it as a synonym for corrupt greed.</note> heresy has grown so strong that they drive men, even against their will, into this kind of crime—not slowly, but with relentless importunity—for the sake of trade. And every trade of this kind issues from the "style" <note>The "Style" refers to the official legal and administrative procedures and paperwork of the Roman bureaucracy.</note> of the Roman Curia <term>Roman Curia: The administrative and legal body that assists the Pope in governing the Catholic Church.</term>, which otherwise...

<column-break/>

<page-num>231</page-num>
## CHAPTER XXIII

...subjects the courts of the Bishops to itself in this same Simoniacal and Gehazi-like style. This is clear in the "letters dimissory" <note>Formal letters sent from one bishop to another regarding ordinations or permissions.</note> for confirmations, absolutions, admissions, and in other things invented for the sake of draining away money.

Now, something should be said concerning the condemnation of the forty-five articles. <note>The 45 Articles were a list of propositions by the English reformer John Wycliffe that were condemned by the authorities in Prague. Hus defended many of them.</note> But I say briefly that, up to this day, the doctors of the Assembly <note>original: "Prætorij." Refers to the official legal or university meeting place in Prague.</note> have not proven that every one of them is either heretical, erroneous, or scandalous. But I wonder why the doctors do not now teach in the Assembly that the article concerning the seizure of temporal goods should not be put into practice—namely, this one: *Temporal lords can, at their own discretion, take away temporal goods from churchmen who are habitually delinquent.* Now they are silent, along with the High Priests and Pharisees, and they do not gather at the Assembly to condemn this article. For certainly, what they feared has happened to them, and will happen in the future: they shall lose their temporal goods, but may God grant that they preserve their souls.

The doctors used to say that once the condemnation of the articles was carried out, there would be peace and harmony. But their prophecy has turned into the opposite. For while those who condemned the articles were rejoicing, those who pay the taxes were mourning. They condemned this article: *Tithes are pure alms.* <note>Hus argued that tithes—the 10% church tax—should be considered voluntary gifts ("alms") rather than a mandatory legal tax. This would allow people to stop paying corrupt priests.</note> And many people coming to the Assembly requested that their taxes, which are alms, should not be taken away. But certain lords of the Assembly responded, saying: "Behold, you previously condemned the idea that tithes are pure alms, and now you are saying that they *are* alms, and thus you are attempting something contrary to the condemnation." And that is enough for the present; the other matters are left to be deferred until later.

---

### HERE ENDS THE TREATISE OF MASTER
### JAN HUS, which he collected in the year of our Lord 1413.
### And it was pronounced publicly in the City of Prague.

->This work was printed by types in the month of<-
->August, in the year 1520.<-

<summary>Jan Hus concludes his treatise by arguing that the Church should not stop its divine work (interdict) because of "bad" individuals and critiques the Roman Curia's financial corruption. He also defends Wycliffe's 45 articles, specifically the idea that tithes are voluntary alms and that secular rulers have the right to seize property from corrupt clergy.</summary>

IN THE YEAR 1518, in the month of March.
This book, which when I had very recently
acquired, I have now, Reader, not without
great vigils, immense labors,
and finally innumerable annoyances,
as the magnitude of the work itself
testifies, brought into this state, that is,
printed with the utmost fidelity and dili-
gence, as I was able, you ought rightly to
consider. But
since you will find not a few things
in it which can in many ways hinder you from
this purpose, which is ours,
and force you to err in them, and entangle
you, as it were, in a certain labyrinth from
which there is absolutely no exit, as the
first page itself demonstrates,
therefore, so that you might not struggle in
such an error, and that we might not have
undertaken our labor in this part of the work
in vain (since it was not composed by the author
of the work itself in such a way as to
satisfy everyone everywhere, which has been
found by me, diligently examining his
volumes for several years), I thought, reader,
that you should be admonished in this place not
to give credit to this work so easily. Nor do I
say this because I intend to
despise its author, but because you will find
some things in it which, if examined by the
scale of truth, will be found to contain some
controversy, especially in those things
which pertain to our Faith and morals.
These, assuredly,
must be examined according to this true, Catholic,
and Apostolic Roman Church,
which if we were to establish from his,
that is, John Hus', works,
there is no doubt that we would deviate
so much from this true, Catholic,
Apostolic, and Roman Church
into something else, that we would be
forced to admit not one but innumerable
errors of our faith and morals. For
indeed, if anyone, in what pertains to this
Roman Church, should give
it credit, he certainly cannot but err
and stray from this true, Catholic, Apostolic, and Roman
Church. And this
you will find not only in what pertains
to the Roman Church, but also in several
other things which, if I am not mistaken, will
be able to become clear to you from reading
this work, which the very first
page of the first work, I say, the
book itself, demonstrates to you.
Wherefore, Reader, if you apply your
mind to this work, diligently peruse its
first page beforehand, which,
if I am not mistaken, will show you how
you will deviate from the truth through this
operation. If you do
this, I do not doubt that you will more easily
free yourself from the error of its author,
and from his error in the elaboration
of this work, and will be able to return to
the path of truth. Farewell.
